 THE PROPHETIC MARRIAGE


 PART ONE

 Chapter One

Tod, playing some mysterious jungle game under the grubby laurels and
aucubas that hedged the side walk leading to No. 13 Spaniards Row, heard
two respectable loiterers passing judgment upon his father.

“That silly fool Western will never make any money.”

Tod was a combative child and very clannish, and he pushed a solid round
head through the laurel leaves, and retorted upon these Pentworth
financiers.

“Boo! Only a silly fool wants to make money!”

The elder of the two, it was Mr. Joab Twain of Twain’s Stores, turned an
enormous and angry face on the child.

“Who’s that?”

“Me,” said Tod.

“The Western brat! Sort of language you’d expect from a little atheist.”

“I’m not a brat,” said Tod, “and three of the eggs Mother bought of you
yesterday was rotten.”

He made a rapid, backward retreat through the shrubbery, scrambled over
the little, low red-brick wall, descended into the garden, and found his
father in his shirt-sleeves preparing to beat the parlour carpet.

Tod flew at him and then stopped dead, solemn as a professor of
political economy.

“I say, Daddy——”

“My son——?”

“Jus’ heard ol’ Twain say you was a silly fool and couldn’t make money.”

Western threw up his head and laughed.

“It’s true—confound him. We haven’t the penny-three-farthing soul, Tod,
my boy. Come on and beat carpets.”

If ever a child loved his father, and had good cause to love him, that
child was Tod Western. In later years he never lost the very vivid
memory of the man, the memory that many such incidents had left him,
glimpsing him in the mirror of a boy’s devoted hero worship, a tall man
with a Spanish air, head up, black hair waved back, blue eyes a little
fierce and ironical. The old velvet coat, soft shirt and collar, cherry
coloured tie, knickers and stockings, were part of the beloved picture.
For William Western was an original, in that he had not bowed the knee
to the gods of Pentworth town, had refused the black coat and the
commercial credo, and dressed himself in the Lincoln green of the merry
men of Sherwood. He was generous and he was always short of money. He
had the mischievous and affectionate heart of a boy, and a genius for
getting the last drop of happiness out of the simplest things of life.
Smugness and the dull materialism of Pentworth bored him, but he was
never conquered by them. Tod could remember occasional strange rages,
times when that boy father of his seemed to mount a big horse and go
galloping around and flashing a sword at queer, intangible beasts of
slime and shadow. Then he would come down off that horse and laugh, kiss
Tod’s mother, and perhaps chase Tod round the garden.

There were two whacking the dust of that parlour carpet. William Western
using a hollystick, Tod a little bamboo cane, when Tod leant forward on
all fours, regarding a frayed patch in front of him.

“Here’s where your chair has the fidgets, Daddy.”

“Get’s excited, does it? ‘Wish this chap wouldn’t talk so much—and such
tosh!’ It’s a respectable, middle-class chair, Tod, and likes
comfortable sermons on a Sunday before the roast beef and Yorkshire
pudding.”

“My chair fidgets a bit,” said Tod.

“There are signs of it elsewhere, my boy.”

Then the other voice chimed in:

“Oh, Tod, where did you do that?”

Tod twisted round to face his mother—mother with the gold brown hair
and eyes that were so calm and yet always ready to smile.

“Done what, Mummy?”

“Frayed your particular bit of carpet, my son,” said his father.

Tod ran a tentative hand over a certain portion of his figure.

“Yes, that’s a fragment of shirt, my child.”

“Must have happened sliding down the wall by the church steps,” said
Tod, very solemn.

“Oh, Tod!” said his mother.

Tod got up and kissed her.

“Couldn’t you put in a nice bit of leavver, Mummy? I’m so sorry.”

Western struck the carpet a resounding smack.

“That’s what a wanton destruction of good shoddy deserves! Great
Jehovah, I’ve forgotten to develop Miss Trilby’s plates!”

“I’ve done it, Will,” said his wife with a mother smile.

Western arose and imitated his son.

“There never was anyone like your mother, Tod,” he confessed.

No man knew better than William Western that it was his wife who had
held the gates and walls of this Château Gaillard of a home.

Western’s Photographic Studio was the only temple of its kind in
Pentworth town, and Western was a most irresponsible and autocratic
priest. The shop had been built in front of one of the little old red
houses in Spaniards Row, and its two bulging bow-windows with the white
door in between looked like a pair of high-powered spectacles perched on
the blunt nose of Western’s house. Tod’s father had a reckless sense of
humour. He could take a good picture, if the subject pleased him; he
loved children and would spend an hour playing with some shy child till
he had surprised it in a moment of naturalness. The adults of Pentworth
gave him no such romantic possibilities, and he did not ask for them.

“Jam ’em in chairs up against a bit of rockery. That’s all that’s
wanted. See they’ve got their cuffs down and their gold chains showing.”

And that was all he did. He plastered his walls and windows with the
obviousness of Pentworth, its staring stolidity, its bony head and
bucolic gawkishness, its absurd Sabbath clothes all put on for the
occasion. There were various mayors of Pentworth in robes and chains;
family groups looking thoroughly stiff and uncomfortable; girls sitting
in the same Louis Quinze chair with young men posed rather sheepishly
behind them; other young men trying to look negligent and at ease while
perched on a flat-topped bit of rustic furniture; old ladies with dogs
and cats, and their laps and bosoms very much in the foreground; young
women who simpered or looked sternly stolid; boys with cricket bats;
brides and bridegrooms who did not seem quite sure whether they belonged
to each other.

“Slap ’em up against a bit of rockery. Jam their heads in a vice and let
’em stare. Give ’em something to hold—a prayer book, a Malacca cane,
somebody else’s hand—anything. Give ’em something obvious, something
they needn’t trouble to understand.”

Now and again he caught a temperament, a face, a naturalness that piqued
him, and then he made a picture of it, and would spoil a dozen plates in
the adventure. Perhaps it was a little country girl with something of
the Tess about her, or a sailorman who had forgotten to worry about
being respectable, or an old shepherd who lived with his sheep and the
weather. He painted pictures too, rather wild water-colours of marsh,
and downlands, and mysterious thundercloud smitten woods. They hung in
the studio, but nobody ever bought them. He also catalogued himself as a
painter of miniatures, and he had painted several of his wife, and one
of Tod. Miss Timperley of Gore Hall had once given him a commission and
had then accused him of making her squint.

“But you do squint, madam,” he had said in self-defence.

Such sincerity is not made for this world.

The house in Spaniards Row held the heart of Tod’s childhood. It was a
queer house, a Puck’s palace that played tricks on you and always got
the best of the game. The front of it was all red, the back all white,
but it did not deign to be divided so dogmatically. It had queer gables,
juttings out of odd wings and outhouses, little brick excrescences,
unexpected chimneys, windows that winked at you goblin fashion. There
were two back doors, a side door and a front door, all painted a blurred
bluish green. No. 13 delighted in little yards and passages, and funny
poky outhouses and cellars that just existed on mystery and did nothing
for a living. There was a pump, a rain-water cistern that suggested all
sorts of subterranean and watery horrors if you lifted the stone slab; a
brick baking-oven fit to hold forty thieves; a coal-cellar that any
decent dragon might have coveted. The interior of the house was just as
mysterious and unexpected. It was full of passages and cupboards and odd
bits of staircase, and every room had a different mood. The shabby old
furniture that Nancy Western had brought with her marriage, the white
chintz curtains, faded rose-bud wall-papers, were part of the same
picture. Tod used to play at Bogey by hiding in the oak chest on the
landing, or he would barricade the attic stairs with a couple of old
trunks, a derelict towel-horse, and an assortment of cardboard boxes,
and await attack by his father. His father would advance up the stairs
with the fur rug from the parlour over his head and shoulders, and armed
with a battered dish-cover and rolling pin. Tod held the stairs with
paper darts and a curtain pole. There would be great excursions, shouts,
battle cries, clashing of dish-cover and curtain pole, and some
disorganisation of the lumber forming the barricade.

“I’m King Borrabarroo, the terrible—terrible sabbige, Wahoo,
Borrabarroo! I smell the blood of Tod—the Englishman.”

“I’m Tod, the giant-killer. I’ve cut off the heads of ten sabbiges, and
I’ve killed hundreds of Frenchmen.”

Sometimes the fort was stormed, but King Borrabarroo never survived the
adventure.

Then there were those rambling pilgrimages afield, when Tod learnt about
wild birds, and trees, and flowers. There were half days in the
Pentworth woods, and Tod could remember spending one whole night under
the stars, cuddling close up to his father, with Western’s old brown
mackintosh drawn over him beside a gipsy fire.

Mummy had looked rather grave next day.

“Will, you shouldn’t!”

“It was dry as a haystack, dear. And we had a fire to frighten the bears
and wolves. Hadn’t we, Tod?”

“And we cooked pertaters in it, Mummy.”

Nancy Western had kissed both her savages and fed them on tea and boiled
eggs.

It was not given to the Tod of those days to understand the beloved soul
of his mother. Finance did not affect him and if there were worries at
No. 13 Spaniards Row, they were not clouds to the child. Nancy Western
was a happy woman. Her irresponsible, lovable boy of a man was worth the
little contriving frowns that his originalities sometimes placed upon
her forehead. She was his mother and his mistress and his wife. She
managed his house, performed miracles with his money, made good his
casual attitude towards the commercial part of Pentworth, and yet kept
him as a lover. Western was no fool, but a very human and wise rebel. He
knew what life had given him in his wife’s shining eyes, and her soft,
full-hearted laughter. He knew that she had had every right to be
exasperated—how much she forgave, and how greatly he loved her for that
smiling, understanding, human soul of hers. She was Romance, and had
never ceased to be Romance, and his home had never been sordid.

Tod had glimpses of his father as a lover. Those summer Sundays were the
great days. The picnic basket would be packed the night before, and they
would catch the train that always started so adventurously and
inconveniently early and landed them at Yerrow or Wivelsea in time for a
second breakfast. There were sea days and woodland days, days on the
chalk hills with lunch and tea under some windblown thorn. Sometimes it
rained, but Western was a genius with a couple of mackintoshes and an
umbrella, and life was always a bit of Crusoe’s island. Tod noticed that
his mother laughed more, and looked pinker, and brighter about the eyes
on those days. She wore her pretty frocks—the white frock with the pink
ribbons, or the green one edged with white. Most of the time Tod’s
father had an arm round her, or he was sitting so that he could touch
her or give a playful tweak to the hair. Tod remembered a day in the
Yerrow woods when he had trundled off to look at an ant-hill near the
camping ground, and had come back to find his mother in his father’s
arms and being kissed, as he—Tod—was never kissed. Tod had sneaked off
again, and spent half an hour stirring up the ant-heap with a stick.

Six days out of seven Mummy was cooking, or cleaning, or mending
clothes, or shut up in that funny little dark-room where Daddy messed
about with bottles and dishes and things. Tod never remembered seeing
her out of temper, except on one of those Saturday afternoons when Uncle
Remus drove over from Lydhurst. Tod came on them in the garden, down by
the three old apple trees, and his mother was very white and Uncle Remus
very red.

“Never dare to say that to me again.”

“You needn’t flare out like that, Nan. There’s no denying that Will’s a
poor hand at business.”

“I didn’t marry him for that, did I? He’s the best man in the world, and
he has made me happy.”

Tod did not like Uncle Remus. He was long and meagre, with a figure that
had concavities where bulges should have been. He had weak, shifty eyes,
a fringe of grey hair, a neck like a turkey’s and a wonderful blue and
everted lower lip. That lip used to fascinate Tod, especially when Uncle
Remus ate peas with a knife.

“Doesn’t it get cut, Uncle?”

“Get cut, boy? What?”

“Your lip.”

Uncle Remus made some sort of remark about a strap and its uses.

He was a farmer, pretty well-to-do, and he wore a hard felt hat, green
with age, and black clothes that looked rusty. The last thing Uncle
Remus ever did was to part with money. He avoided the sacrifice, dodged
it, walked round it, ignored it unless it was too obviously part of the
day’s business. Never once did Tod get a sixpence out of him, and Uncle
Remus got nothing out of Tod. He thought him a bold and cheeky little
beast, and Tod christened his uncle “Old-bug-in-the-Bed,” though how he
had come by the phrase no one ever knew! His father roared when he first
heard it; his mother went red, began a reproof, and then laughed like
her husband.

Even at the age of seven Tod had some understanding of his father’s
attitude towards the world of Pentworth town. Pentworth thought William
Western an eccentric fool; it also disliked him, because it had an
unpleasant suspicion that Western managed to laugh at Pentworth’s
opinion. When you have labelled a man as a fool, it is most improper for
him to turn round and laugh at you. There had been occasions, of which
Tod, of course, knew nothing, when his father had got up at public
meetings and said the most ironical, scathing and truthful things with a
happy smile on his face. Pentworth, especially the commercial part of
Pentworth, did not like that sort of man. He was an uncomfortable
neighbour, the kind of person who upsets the middle-class “Christian,”
who talked about money-changers and scourages, and who reminded fat
people that their God’s feet had been washed by a harlot.

That his father was at war with Pentworth Tod guessed quite shrewdly,
but the spiritual inspiration of the feud was beyond the grasp of the
child.

Tod remembered quite a number of Western’s queer sayings, and they stuck
in his memory, to be pulled out and used in after years.

“I’m not a penny-in-the-slot machine.”

“Christ was born before the commercial traveller.”

“Black-coated, slimy slaves!”

“Up with Ruskin, down with the robbers.”

“You never need fear playing dirty tricks so long as you keep them out
of the newspapers.”

“This country with a penny-three-farthing soul.”

 Chapter Two

Tod had been out stalking imaginary tigers in a jungle growth of runner
beans, artichokes and gooseberry bushes, his weapon the deadly
spring-gun that would eject a mild pea at a velocity calculated to make
a dog think that a fly had tickled him. It was growing dark, and Tod had
tracked an elephant out of the artichoke forest. The tracks led Tod to
the brick wall and the front shrubbery, that most dismal and dangerous
spot in all Pentworth, where a boy had to be infinitely daring and
infinitely cautious or lose his life. Tod’s boa-constrictor lived in the
shrubbery, and fed upon the various Pentworth worthies whom Tod’s
imagination consigned to it. The Rector of St. Jude’s, Mr. Joab Twain,
the Police Inspector, old Mrs. Clutterbuck who kept the sweet shop, had
all been devoured by the great snake. Tod’s father had pretended to
wonder what the snake could have done with Mrs. Clutterbuck’s boots and
the Police Inspector’s buttons.

Tod had his head above the wall when the two men came out of the house
and stood talking in the alley-way between the railings and the shrubs.
One of the men was Tod’s father, the other a rather wild-eyed, hunted
looking little man who kept a bootshop, and whose name was Mr. Jacks.
The street lamp had been lit, and Tod could see that Mr. Jacks was
strangely excited. He held his hat in one hand, and a long black
forelock had fallen over his forehead, and he stammered slightly when he
talked.

Tod’s father had taken some white things out of his pocket, bits of
white paper that crinkled.

“It’s made to be used, old man.”

Tod’s father handed the white things to Mr. Jacks, and Mr. Jacks seemed
extraordinarily upset about something. He pocketed the paper, grabbed at
Western’s hand, and made funny noises in his throat.

“God bless you, Western. God bless you.”

Tod saw him cram his hat on his head and make a dash for the street.
Western re-entered the house.

Tod forgot the boa-constrictor, scuttled back, and went in by the garden
door. His father had just strolled into the parlour where his wife was
laying the table for supper. Tod hung back in the hall and listened.

“Poor Jacks,” said his mother.

“Poor devil! Such a good chap, and so fond of those kiddies. What an
accursed and sordid slaving this commercialism of ours can be!”

“Did you help him?”

“Yes”

“Much?”

“Ten pounds,” said Tod’s father laconically.

Tod saw his mother’s blue eyes look at his father with a moment’s tragic
dismay.

“Oh, Will!”

“I had to, dear. The poor beggar was so——”

Tod’s mother came quickly round the table and kissed Tod’s father.

“You dear? I didn’t mean——”

Western caught his wife’s hands and held them against his mouth.

“No wonder I’m always in love with you, Nan.”

No. 13 Spaniards Row had its periodic financial crisis, and Western’s
generosity to Mr. Jacks inaugurated a season of lean months. Tod noticed
various things. His father spent more time in the studio and in the dark
room, and even consented to paint the portrait of Miss Glandinning’s fat
fox-terrier. The daily glass of beer disappeared from the dinner table.
Margarine replaced butter, and jam and margarine were not allowed to
meet on the same piece of bread. The Sunday excursions were cut down to
two a month, and Tod had to wait for a new summer suit. Yet Château
Gaillard held out bravely and lost none of its happy good temper. His
mother sang or whistled softly while she worked, and she found time to
give Tod his lessons. Western had bought an old bicycle and was touring
the country, taking pictures of Heddingly Castle and Bramber Abbey,
“Sunset on Downs” and “Dawn over the Weald.” He had ideas about
developing the picture postcard trade. It was a little less obvious and
boring than photographing Pentworth in its Sunday clothes.

About that time a Mr. Hogg obtruded himself on Tod’s notice. He was a
square, red-faced man who walked with his feet set rather wide apart and
his toes turned in, rather like a duck. He had little, alert, evil eyes,
and a nose like the nose of one of those indiarubber faces that you can
flatten as you please. The young animal in Tod did not like the smell of
the man. Mr. Hogg was always turning up when Tod’s father was away, and
hanging about in the shop or the studio, making googly eyes at Tod’s
mother.

One autumn evening, just about dusk, Tod heard his mother calling him.

“Tod—Tod——”

Tod was in the garden, and there was a cry in his mother’s voice that
made him race for the house. He found her in the parlour, lighting a
candle with hands that shook a little; something black and heavy and
straight lay on the table. But Tod’s mother was not alone. A man seemed
to have fallen over one of the rush-bottomed chairs; he was picking
himself up, and as his head appeared Tod saw that his forehead was all
bloody. But it was his mother’s face that astonished and bewildered Tod.
She was angry, with an anger that was new to the child; her face was all
stark and white and blazing, and her eyes looked like two bits of
shining glass.

“This man has insulted your mother, Tod. Never forget it.”

Then Tod’s father appeared out of the sudden dusk, like Ulysses from out
of the sea.

“Hallo, what’s this, Nan?”

“We owe this man money, Will. He has got to be paid.”

“Of course, he’ll be paid.”

Tod’s mother blazed at them both.

“To-night, at once—now! He came here and asked me to pay him
with—myself. Tod, dear—come to me.”

Tod saw his mother’s anger leap to his father and set him afire. He had
never glimpsed his father like that before, and it made him afraid. He
ran to his mother, and was caught up and carried out of the room.

The affair ended there, so far as Tod was concerned. He heard strange
noises in the house, a voice that howled, the sound of heavy blows. The
street door banged. Then there was silence.

Tod’s father had lit the lamp when Tod and his mother returned to the
parlour. A broken chair lay in a corner, and the cloth had been dragged
from the table. Western was whistling softly. Tod saw his eyes light up
strangely directly his mother entered the room.

“That was all my fault, Nan. And, by God, I shan’t forget it.”

Tod felt rather forgotten for a moment. His mother was weeping, and
Tod’s father was saying all manner of strange things, and holding her as
though he never meant to let her go again. Tod noticed that the knuckles
of his father’s right hand were all red and raw.

“Now, what about supper? Sit down, dearest. Come along, Tod, my man;
we’ll lay the table.”

In the pantry Tod asked his father a question.

“What did the man say to Mother, Daddy?”

“I hope you will never know, my boy. Now then, hand out the bread from
that pan.”

Various familiar pieces of furniture disappeared from No. 13 Spaniards
Row during the next two weeks. The oak chest on the landing was carried
away by two men in dirty white aprons, and placed in a cart. The grand
father clock went with it, looking very moon-faced and melancholy and
ready to break into tears. Tod lost his temper over the rape of the poor
grandfather clock, and challenged the two men.

“What are you doin’ with our furniture?”

“You go and ask that respec’ble father of yours, cocky,” said one of the
men roughly.

Tod glared at him.

“Your teeth’s all black,” he retorted, “why don’t you clean ’em like a
gentleman?”

His mother looked rather sad for two days, but Tod noticed that his
father was very loving with her, and could hardly bear her out of sight.
Tod’s father had all sorts of pet names for his mother, and some of the
names seemed to come out of the Bible—at least Tod thought so. His
mother’s eyes would grow all strange and deep and shimmery when Tod’s
father called her by these names: Sometimes she would answer him with,
“You dear old Quixote,” and Tod tried to find out what a Quixote was.

He asked Mrs. Clutterbuck at the sweet shop, the lady who had been
devoured by the imaginary snake, but Mrs. Clutterbuck did not help him.

“Aven’t got any. We don’t stock it.”

Which was a subtle and innocent gibe at the psychology of Pentworth.

The first tragedy of Tod’s life followed swiftly upon the brown heels of
the autumn. Western, pedalling that old bicycle of his down Castle Hill,
collided at the crossroads with Pentworth’s latest mechanical sensation,
the motor-car from Pavitt’s Brewery. William Western was rushed home to
Spaniards Row in the car that had smashed him, and that night he lay in
the long, low room that overlooked the street, dying. Tod had not seen
him. The dazed and uncomprehending child had been seized on by Mr. Jacks
and carried off to that gentleman’s bootshop. Mr. Jacks seemed very
upset about something, and kept pulling that forelock of his and
uttering strange blasphemies. He stodged Tod with sweets, and talked all
sorts of comforting and mendacious nonsense to the child.

“Yes, Daddy’s all right. See him to-night, Tod, old man. Best man in the
world your daddy; best chap that ever was.”

About seven o’clock somebody rang Mr. Jacks’ bell, and Mr. Jacks went to
answer it.

“Western wants to see his boy,” said a voice.

“I’ll bring him myself,” said the keeper of the bootshop.

Tod did not realise what was happening to the life of No. 13 Spaniards
Row. Mr. Jacks held his hand and appeared unable to speak coherently
when he delivered Tod to his mother. Nancy Western bent down and kissed
Tod, and her mouth felt very cold. Her eyes had a strange look, as
though they were frozen, and her face was all stiff and expressionless.
Tod did not understand how great and piteous his mother’s courage was.

She took Tod to his father, and Tod was a little frightened. His
father’s face was the colour of linen, and the big eyes all big and
misty. He lay with the clothes turned back off his chest, and just
breathed and looked, and there was a queer spasm in his breathing.

“Oh, Daddy!”

His mother held him back.

“You must be very quiet, dear. Sit down. Daddy wants to talk.”

The dying man’s eyes were all anguish and tenderness.

“Let him kiss me, Nan.”

Tod kissed his father, and Western spoke to him in a small and husky
voice.

“Take care of Mummy, Tod; there never was such a mother as Mummy. She’s
been the Faery Queen to me. I’m going away on a long journey, Tod, and
you’ve got to be a man.”

Tod choked a little.

“I’ll look after Mummy till you come back, Daddy.”

“Nan, I can’t bear it,” said Tod’s father, and Mother took Tod away.

Mr. Jacks was waiting, and trying to light a pipe that kept going out.

“Will you keep him to-night?”

“Anything,” said Mr. Jacks; “much better if it had been me. Silly sort
of world, anyway. Tod, ol’ man; let’s go and have some toffee.”

“I can see Daddy in the morning,” said Tod.

“Yes, dear.”

Mr. Jacks carried him off, seeing that Tod’s mother was breaking, and
that her thoughts were with her man.

Tod would eat neither sweets nor supper that night, and sat in the
shopkeeper’s own chair, asking interminable questions. “Where’s Daddy
going? Will he be away long? What’s Daddy in bed for? Has he broken his
leg? What’ll they do to the man who did it?” Mr. Jacks had always known
himself to be a coward, but he felt more afraid of hurting that child
than he had feared anything in his life before. Little Tod Western would
have to be told the truth, and Mr. Jacks saw himself breaking the news
to the boy.

Tod fell asleep at last in the arm-chair, and Mr. Jacks spent the night
on the hard and shining black sofa, dozing off and waking with a start,
to find the child still sleeping. It was one of the longest nights of
the man’s life. He crept out of the room before daylight, and pottered
about in the kitchen, lighting the fire and getting breakfast ready.
About seven someone rang the bell, and Mr. Jacks went to the side door.

“Mr. Western’s dead,” said a voice.

“Is he?” said Mr. Jacks blankly.

“Mrs. Western wants you to tell the boy.”

“Damn,” said Mr. Jacks. “Yes, I’ll do it.”

He kept Tod in the sitting-room, and his own children in the kitchen.
Tod ate quite a good breakfast, and Mr. Jacks gave him buttered toast,
sardines, and an egg. Tod kept asking when he might go and see his
father, and old Jacks, seizing the desperate courage of a tender-hearted
man, lit a pipe and dragged Tod on to his knee.

“Tod, ol’ man—damn this pipe! The smoke gets into a chap’s eyes. Tod,
ol’ man, your father has gone away.”

“Gone away? Where’s he gone to?”

“Heaven, Tod, ol’ man. And you’ll go there some day and see him.”

The child’s eyes were round and staring. He did not speak for a moment,
and then he clutched one of Jacks’ hands.

“Daddy’s dead!”

“Yes, dear; he’s gone to heaven.”

Before Mr. Jacks could grapple the crisis, the child was off his knee
and out of the door.

“Tod, ol’ man, Tod——”

But the boy did not heed him. The street door stood open at the end of
the passage, and Tod dashed out into the misty November sunlight, a
strangely silent child with the face of an old man. Mr. Jacks faltered
at the street door.

“He’s gone to his mother,” he said to himself; “she’ll want him.”

But Tod had not gone to his mother. He ran out of the town and down the
hill to the meadows along the river. The grass was all wet and grizzled
with early frost, and the great elms were dropping their yellow leaves
to a light breeze. And Tod ran wild along those meadows, silent no
longer, but wailing and calling his father. “Daddy, Daddy, Daddy. Come
back, Daddy, oh, come back.” His child’s anguish seemed tireless and
unending, and even when the movement of grief ebbed in him, he lay on
the wet grass under an elm tree and sobbed.

Tod remained lost all that day, but about dusk someone saw him toddling
like a little ghost up the lane to Spaniards Row. He went in by the
garden door, and there were no lights in the house. His hands fumbled at
the door of the parlour and opened it, almost without a sound.

Someone was sitting by the window, a shadowy figure, with a white face.
It was Tod’s mother, and she was alone, and of a sudden Tod’s little
heart went out to her like a bird to its mate.

“Mummy!”

“Tod!”

He ran to her, and was caught in her arms. She asked no questions, but
held him to her and kissed him again and again. Her tears were on his
face, and her breath came in great gasps. Tod had never been kissed in
such a way before. Somehow it made him think of that day in the Yerrow
woods when he had seen his father kissing his mother like that. Strange,
passionate kisses, beyond the soul of a child.

“Mummy!”

Her broken words fell on him like her tears.

“His boy! You are him. O God, how can I bear it!”

 Chapter Three

A week after William Western’s death, an old lady took up her abode in
the kitchen, and Tod’s mother went out of it and accepted the
responsibilities of the studio and the shop. She had grown familiar with
the technique of the business during her husband’s lifetime, and a neat
little notice posted up in the window warned Pentworth that it could be
photographed as before. Nancy Western had inherited two five hundred
pound mortgages which gave her forty pounds a year. She meant to make a
fight of it in Pentworth for the sake of her boy, and not to be pushed
off the narrow shelf of her commercial opportunity.

Tod would not be parted from his mother those days. He slept in her bed,
and more than once he woke at night to find her clasping him and kissing
him with passionate tenderness.

“Oh, little Will.”

Tod’s arms clung round her neck.

“I’m a man now, Mummy,” and so he was, in spite of his years.

He had developed a sudden new self-consciousness and a new ideal. He
stayed with her in the shop, sat with her in the dark-room, watching and
learning with an intense seriousness that helped the broken woman beside
him back to her smiles. Mrs. Western was looking very pale and comely
and pathetic in her black dress, and other people noticed this, as well
as Tod. Quite a considerable part of Pentworth expressed its sympathy by
coming to be photographed. Mr. Jacks headed the procession, and the
Mayor and Mayoress gave the impulse public recognition.

Nancy Western had as much as she could do. Tod, the child, was
challenged and worried. He ran about after her like a dog, but a very
intelligent, quick-eyed dog, who soon learnt to fetch and carry. His
devotedness overtopped all difficulties. He learnt where every thing was
to be found; he brushed out the shop and dusted the shelves and counter,
tied up parcels, went on errands, even insisted on being allowed to make
solemn, round-handed entries in the ledger and day-book. He began to
pick up the technique of the dark-room where his mother worked with the
light from the little red lamp shining on her soft hair and pale face.
And he developed a quaint and autocratic fatherliness towards her that
issued orders and saw them obeyed.

“Mummy must come to dinner.”

And Mummy went.

“Mummy’s eyes are tired. Mummy must come and lie down on the sofa.”

How much his queer and authoritative little tendernesses helped his
mother, Tod never guessed. His child’s love stanched the blood-flow of
those bitter weeks, and turned her anguish into a duller pain. He made a
complete and splendid conquest of old Mrs. Bunce who functioned in the
kitchen. She would have let him turn her rolling-pin into a cricket bat,
or play football with her Sunday cap.

“That there child’s a marvel,” she announced to her friends; “there must
’ave been a lot of good, somehow, in William Western.”

A year passed away, and Nancy Western was able to look about her and
estimate her success. She found that it had been very considerable, so
considerable that she was able to pay a young woman to help her in the
business, and to send Tod to school. Uncle Remus, who still drove in
regularly from Lydhurst, and whose contribution to Nancy’s assistance
had been a great deal of pessimistic advice, a few potatoes, and a
bundle or two of acid rhubarb, suggested the Grammar School at
Marychurch.

“A solid sort o’ school,” he called it; “nothing new-fangled about it.
Turn a boy out sensible and solid.”

“And stupid,” added Tod’s mother.

She pointed out to Uncle Remus that the Grammar School at Marychurch
meant Tod’s board, and that she could not afford it, and Uncle Remus did
not offer to accept any responsibility.

Moreover, Tod absolutely and utterly refused to be parted from his
mother, and her heart was with him in his obstinacy. He was such a
sturdy, spirited child, she could find no signs of the incipient milksop
in him. A Mr. Chipperton conducted a private school in Pentworth, and
Nancy Western called on Mr. Chipperton.

He was a tall man of forty or so, with a high bumpy forehead, pince-nez,
and an air of vivacious benignity. Mr. Chipperton “conducted” his
school; any other method of expressing his mastership would have been
vulgar, and Mr. Chipperton was never vulgar. He was a good soul who had
concentrated in his person all the ideals and prejudices of middle-class
Pentworth, and to whom everything in life was either “nice or nasty,”
“proper or improper.” He studied refinement, and kept a rather bleary
and mistrustful eye on all girls. Sex scared him to death, made him
chatter all sorts of fanatical and pathetic nonsense. Mr. Chipperton was
English middle-class prudishness stuck on a stool and wagging a long
forefinger. He was a bachelor; Miss Victoria Chipperton, his sister,
kept his house and helped him to guard the world’s virtue.

“My dear madam, the note of my school is refinement—refinement, and a
religious education. I try to remember, madam, that character comes
before the multiplication table. I try to be a father to my boys.”

Nancy Western, judging him by her dead husband, thought Mr. Chipperton
an honest but harmless sort of fool. She felt quite capable of looking
after Tod’s character; she wanted Mr. Chipperton to educate him, and she
said so.

“Certainly, madam, our curriculum is up-to-date in every department. We
can give a scientific and commercial training. I am not a bigoted
classic.”

“I want him to learn what is sensible and useful.”

“And poetry, madam, music, and lit-er-a-ture?”

“I’ll see to the romance,” said Nancy Western.

So Clarence Todhunter Western trudged daily to Prospect House and sat
under Mr. Chipperton and Mr. Chipperton’s two assistant masters. Tod
began by being rather shy and solemn, and he seemed to know very little
about arithmetic and grammar, and his knowledge of Latin was nil. He was
placed in the lowest class, and though he was no Paladin in the
class-room, he was a force to be reckoned with in the playground.

“Western, how do you spell pork?”

“Pig’s meat, sir?”

Mr. Chipperton inclined his head in the affirmative.

“P-a-l-k, sir,” said Tod.

The master blinked.

“Very ingenious, Western, but incorrect. Think of fork and not chalk.”

Tod heard about pork in the playground. Little Cocky Walker, that
snub-nosed, pallid, supercilious bit of infallibility, selected Tod for
the day’s jest.

“Come here, Clarence Todhunter, and spell chalk.”

“C-h-a-l-k,” said Tod.

“Rot. It’s c-h-o-r-k, you cuckoo.”

“I’m not a cuckoo,” said Tod.

“If I say you’re a cuckoo you’re a cuckoo.”

“Then you’ll say it the other way round.”

And Tod closed with Master Walker, and after rollings and contortions
that lasted three minutes, remained triumphantly uppermost, holding the
infallible one’s face hard against the gravel.

“Say ‘Western’s not a cuckoo.’”

Further contact with the gravel persuaded Master Walker that it would be
wiser to propitiate Tod.

Tod let him up.

“Cocky’s licked at last,” said one of the interested crowd, and Tod’s
imaginative spelling ceased to be a subject for public criticism.

Prospect House was a very ordinary school with the usual class-rooms and
the lifeless uninspired routine. The natural curiosity of the child did
not survive in that pot-shop where all the pots were alike and filled
with the same stodge. One or two boys remained keen, and followed Mr.
Chipperton into the wilds of algebra, and found adventurous delights in
Latin grammar. Nine-tenths of the school were bored, and determined to
avoid learning anything that could be safely left unlearnt. So long as
you could steer safely past “impots” and a whacking, what did it matter?

Tod, too, was bored, and it was his first experience of boredom. He did
not understand it, and he was inclined to blame himself. The great fact
that weighed with him was that his mother had sent him to this school to
learn, and she was paying money for it, and he decided that it was his
business to learn. He fought his boredom as though it were a deadly sin,
and Mr. Jukes the junior master began to appreciate Tod.

Mr. Chipperton was an innocent and unconscious dispeller of ennui. He
was the serious, impressive, dramatic clown, and the little devils
mocked him. Mr. Chipperton was something of a poser, but he did not pose
effectively.

“You must lead boys; you must be a boy with boys,” such were his
inspirations.

He played cricket with the school, going out in baggy grey flannel
trousers, a white shirt, and an absurd little red and black striped cap
that refused to sit down decently on his bald head. He was very
energetic and conversational in the field, but he let most of the balls
through and muffed nearly all his catches. The school hung on the edge
of giggles when he was batting. He sweated, and poked and flourished,
and was generally bowled in the second over by some quite futile googly.

Then there were those “Christian evenings” on Fridays in the big
schoolroom. Tod attended on several occasions until he disgraced himself
by laughing, and was severely admonished by Miss Chipperton.

The process never seemed to vary. Mr. Chipperton would seat himself on
the music-stool, lift the lid of the keyboard, and let his fingers
patter over the keys. There were mice in the piano—perennial mice.

Mr. Chipperton’s handkerchief would come out.

“Dear me, nasty little animals!”

He would wipe the notes excitedly.

“Haven’t we got a cat, my dear? Disgusting little animals. Now, boys,
‘Onward, Christian Soldiers’——”

Tod grew in stature and in understanding that summer, and came by a new
and rather disturbing knowledge of the great facts of life. In spite of
Mr. Chipperton Tod discovered sex, and it was uncovered to him
nastily—by a member of a clique of dirty little boys who had wriggled
behind the mock curtain with which the English middle-class drapes the
mystery.

Tod was horrified, disgusted, incredulous, because these urchins dragged
his parents into the squabble, and attacked him through his mother.

“You go and ask your ma how you were born.”

“I will,” said Tod.

And he did.

It was one of the moments of anguish in Nancy Western’s life, for the
boy came to her shrinkingly, his eyes asking to be given a convincing
picture of the old fairy tale. He had been shocked, and his mother had
to shock him a second time; nor need it have happened, and Tod’s mother
blamed herself. Had she been thoughtless or a coward?

“God did give me to you, didn’t he, Mummy?”

“Yes, dear.”

“How did I come, then?”

It was dusk, and she drew Tod to the sofa and, feeling her face like
fire, told him the truth. She had to tell him; she knew now that she
ought to have told him before. The nastiness should never have been. It
was England, the old England of the “little Bethels.”

The child shrank from her a little, and she felt his shrinking in every
fibre of her woman’s soul. It could not end there, and she stripped her
love and her courage to the child, and fought to regain the glamour of
the lost fairy tale. She told him the whole story, simply, a little
tragically, describing the long waiting, the pain, the joy. Truth and
emotion will always touch and convince a child.

In the end the boy’s arms were round her neck.

“Oh, Mummy, Mummy, what a lot of pain I gave you.”

She had won.

One of the backyard clique challenged Tod in the playground next
morning. He was an unwholesome, spotty boy of the name of Blimmer.

“Hallo, Western, did you ask your mother?”

Tod went very white.

“Don’t you speak of my mother; she’s an angel.”

“Shucks,” said the other.

And then Tod flew at him and with such a fury that its fierceness
impressed itself on all the boys who saw that fight.

Blimmer was bigger than Tod, but he was flabby, and the smaller boy’s
fury scared him. Even when he flinched and tried to edge away, Tod
followed him with relentless, hammering fists. It ended with Blimmer
lying face downwards on the ground, snivelling, to be rescued by Mr.
Chipperton who stalked with jerky solemnity across the playground, and
pulled Tod off by the collar.

“For shame, Western.”

Tod blazed at the master.

“Then he shouldn’t speak of my mother. I’ll kill him another time.”

From that day all the decent boys loved Tod dearly.

Coincidences gathered to thrust a more intimate conception of the great
mystery upon the boy. He overheard things, casual bits of gossip, words
that passed perhaps between Mrs. Bunce and her friends.

“Pretty Mrs. Western.”

They called his mother that. Yes, of course she was pretty.

“She ought to marry again.”

“There’s gents who’re ready to ask her.”

And then the figures of Mr. Plumpton and Mr. Parfit loomed up over Tod,
and threw their heavy, black-coated shadows across his life.

Mr. Parfit was a decent little man with a brisk and nervous manner. He
was very black and sallow, had very small hands and feet, a hollow chest
and high shoulders. His obvious niceness did not recommend him to
anybody who was easily bored. He was very polite to Tod, and even
brought lumps of sugar for Tod’s latest friend, Gruff, a mongrel
terrier. Mr. Parfit appeared to be intensely interested in artistic
photography, but Tod soon realised Mr. Parfit wanted his mother.

Mr. Plumpton was a very different sort of creature. He kept the grocery
shop in Stone Street, and was big and clumsy and domineering. He had a
fine, egg-coloured moustache that spread and drooped like the wings of a
swallow tail butterfly. When he looked at you his swollen blue eyes
seemed to be saying, “We make no allowance for broken eggs.” Tod had
heard his scolding in the shop, and seen him turn with sudden shining
and sweating urbanity to serve someone who was worth while.

“Gooday, madam, be-ootiful weather; and what can we do for you to-day,
madam?”

Mr. Plumpton was a widower and he had a family of his own, half a dozen
cheese-faced children with loud voices and hard eyes. The grocer may
have wanted someone to manage his house—anyway he began to bulk big
round about No. 13 Spaniards Row, and to make love to Tod’s mother. The
bigger he bulked, the more Tod hated him, and the less he saw of Mr.
Parfit. Mr. Parfit was crowded out and became a negligible shadow.

Tod had never hated anything as he hated Mr. Percy Plumpton.

“Hallo, Clarence; learnt to spell yet?”

He tried to be jocular and friendly in an arrogant sort of way. The boy
had to be humoured, but Tod remained glumy and _farouche_.

“We’ll cure you of that—some day,” said Mr. Plumpton to himself.

The man was a mountain of self complacency, a strongly sexed beast who
had had his way with women. There was something greasy and unclean about
him, as though he was always hot and never washed. But Tod hated him
most for the way he looked at his mother; she might have been something
he wanted to eat.

Tod noticed that his mother remained quite calm and quiet when Mr.
Plumpton was talking to her. She did not seem to be in the least afraid
of him; she treated him just like other people.

Tod was worried, bewildered, frightened. He had moments of anguish,
moments when he yearned to rush at his mother and cry, “O, Mummy, Mummy,
don’t.”

His mother puzzled him. Mr. Plumpton might have been an old woman in a
black bonnet for all the change he wrought in her. She had the same soft
laugh; the same steady, tender eyes; the same pale quick-coloured
prettiness. Tod realised of a sudden how pretty his mother was, far
prettier than Mrs. Coghurst of the Brewery, and Mrs. Coghurst was
supposed to be Pentworth’s Astarte. Tod grew almost afraid of his
mother’s prettiness. He saw Mr. Plumpton’s big red hands making a grab
at her, and the vision filled him with sensitive horror.

Tod grew reserved and shy those days. He kept apart, thought a great
deal about his father, and was often in that corner of the churchyard
where William Western lay buried. His hatred of Mr. Plumpton was a great
cloud over his life, a cloud that threatened to burst.

And burst it did, and the dog Gruff was the cause of the deluge.

It appeared that Mr. Plumpton had invited himself to tea. He came
swelling in, making the floor boards creak, and with his usual
complacent clumsiness, trod on Gruff’s tail. Now Gruff was a
quick-tempered dog, and his teeth tried the texture of Mr. Plumpton’s
grey Sunday trousers.

“Damn the dog!”

Next moment a fat leg swung out and a big boot sent Gruff head over
heels into the corner.

Tod lost his whole soul at that. He went at the big man like a little
white-faced fury, kicking and hitting, and his kicks hurt.

“Tod, dear!”

“You brat! I’ll teach you manners.”

Mr. Plumpton got Tod by the ear and twisted it with his big fingers,
while with the other hand he cuffed Tod’s head.

“If your mother can’t manage you, it’s time——”

Then Nancy Western intervened.

“How dare you touch my boy! How dare you!”

Mr. Plumpton stared at her, but he let go of Tod, who would have rushed
in again had not his mother seized him. She was breathing fast, and he
could feel her heart thudding, and her eyes were all ablaze.

“You cad! Get out of my house!”

“Well, I’m damned,” said the man, “and I was going to ask you to marry
me.”

“You! Marry me!” she laughed. “I knew the honour was to be offered me,
and I had to wait to refuse it, hadn’t I? Get out of my house, please.”

And Mr. Plumpton went.

Tod leapt up into his mother’s arms and kissed her.

“You do love me, Mummy?”

“My darling.”

“You won’t give me another daddy?”

She kissed him with passion.

“Never, never, my little Will.”

 Chapter Four

It caused no sensation in Pentworth when Clarence Todhunter Western put
on trousers, and became the little father of the man he was to be, a
sturdy figure, not too long in the leg, and with a close-cropped,
squirish and intelligent head. He was a high-spirited, passionate child,
with his mother’s colouring and his father’s eyes. He looked at you
squarely, and smiling if you pleased him, showing a fine row of strong
white teeth. Girls called him a “brown boy,” and thought him rather
plain.

Mr. Jacks and Gruff were the only creatures who showed any particular
interest in Tod’s trousers.

Mr. Jacks met Tod in Conduit Street, and appeared greatly impressed.

“Well, well, Tod, old man—that’s fine. Funny sort of world anyway.
We’ll be old buffers in ten years.”

Mr. Jacks’ simplicity—sharp people called it foolishness—always
appealed to Tod. He liked to go fishing and bird-studying with Jacks;
they were just boys together; there was no talking up and down about it.

Gruff showed his interest in Tod’s trousers by walking round him and
sniffing at them. Gruff seemed to distrust trousers, and the change in
Tod disturbed him.

“A sign of incipient and respectable dullness, old man,” said the dog;
“you’ll get irritable and kick me some day. Or you’ll be going walks
with a thing in a petticoat, and I’ll be gooseberry. O lord!”

There was no burking the fact that Tod discovered Romance ten days after
that notable advance in costume, and Romance dawned on him in the blue
eyes and yellow hair of “Giggles.”

Tod first saw Miss Millie Hornblower sitting on her father’s garden
wall, and eating strawberries out of the hollow of a rhubarb leaf. The
brick wall was old and mossy, with a mass of green orchard trees behind
it, and a rose or two showing red as fire. The girl was wearing a light
blue linen dress, tan shoes, and brown stockings, and her yellow hair
was all fluffed out like smoke.

“Hallo, boy.”

“Hallo,” said Tod.

She slipped a strawberry between her restless red lips, tossed her hair,
and threw the stalk at Tod. It hit him on the collar and made a little
red mark.

“Like one?”

“Thank you.”

“Please.”

She stretched out the leaf, and then drew it back again with a
mischievous giggle.

“What’s your name, first.”

“Tod.”

“What a silly name.”

“It’s short for Todhunter,” said the boy.

“Toadhunter? Oh, horrid! Haven’t you got a nicer name than that?”

“Clarence.”

She considered “Clarence,” swinging her legs and looking at him with
feline curiosity. The child had a way with her, and a restless,
mischievous yet lean sauciness that promised to mature and trouble many
men. She was very bold at twelve, mainly because she had not learnt to
hide her boldness. Sex cunning and middle-class morality were to teach
her that.

Tod had his strawberry, and a chat with Miss Hornblower. The Hornblowers
had but lately come to Pentworth, where Mr. Hornblower was in charge of
the local management of a coal merchant’s business. It appeared that the
Hornblowers had just bought a new drawing-room carpet—a very gorgeous
affair; that Dad thought of keeping a pony and trap; and that she—Miss
Hornblower—was going to receive piano lessons from the organist of St.
Luke’s. Tod accepted all this information with proper awe and interest.
Miss Hornblower had a fine appreciation of her social position, and she
meant other people to appreciate it also. Humility does not pay in
middle-class England, and Millie had absorbed the wisdom of her parents.

“What’s your father?”

“He’s dead,” said Tod.

There was nothing interesting to her in the fact that Tod’s father was
dead.

“Where do you live?”

“Number thirteen Spaniards Row. My mother owns the photographic studio.”

“Oh, you keep a shop!” said Miss Hornblower.

Her exclamatory tone challenged him.

“It’s not a shop, but a studio. My dad painted pictures.”

“Did he sell them?” asked the girl.

“He painted ’em for pleasure,” said Tod, lured from the strict truth by
her feminine fascinations.

Miss Hornblower offered him another strawberry.

“It’s quite gentlemanly to paint pictures,” she announced. “Where do you
go to school?”

“Chipperton’s. Prospect House.”

“I know some of the boys there, Bertie Blimmer and Tom Cassilis.”

Tod felt that he might have serious things to say to Messrs. Blimmer and
Cassilis.

That was the beginning of Tod’s first romance, and it occurred in spite
of Mr. Chipperton and his mother. The strange part of it was that this
boy was inspired to a sensitive and adventurous chivalry by this vain
and precocious little chit who had learnt all the elemental tricks of
the fully sexed woman. Nature does not intend a man to have ideals, she
adapts her sorcery and makes use of these ideals, and in the great game
of fooling him nearly all the poets and romancers have blindly and
wilfully aided her in her deceit. Tod had taken to reading boy’s tales
and romances, and his head was full of wonderful young women who were
beautiful as angels, sweet as nectar, gentle as white pigeons, and pure
as the forest dew. A boy fought for one of them, suffered for her,
rescued her from all sorts of wonderful perils and iniquities, and in
the end some good old man blessed them, and they loved each other for
ever and ever.

Mr. Chipperton believed in that sort of tale, and in that hypothetical
young heroine, but he was a bachelor, and he regarded the actual thing
in petticoats with immense and cowardly suspicion. He did not like girls
loitering about the playground gate. He approved most thoroughly of the
“caterpillar” formation in which Miss Rumble sent her young ladies to
take their daily exercise. Mr. Chipperton, that most representative
English moralist, having taken up a thoroughly false and artificial
position on the sex question, found himself harassed and outmanœuvered,
and was always being fooled behind his back. The pity of it all is that
these good people teach youth a false and lying philosophy, arm them
with brittle and treacherous weapons that are apt to break when the
great truths of life put them to the test. The man and the woman who
search for sincerity find themselves doomed to re-learn much of
mischievous stuff that was taught them in earlier years. They are lucky
if the “sugar romance” and the “pretty-pretty-happy-forever-after”
school of morals have not already involved them in some sordid and
tragic partnership.

Nancy Western might have helped her boy had she had more knowledge of
this primitive romance, and had she lived longer. She was a woman—one
of the very few women—who had been happily married and had found
complete and human satisfaction in her man. The very rich and tender
memories of her marriage made her perhaps a little slow in the matter of
fearing for her boy. He was a child still, and the man in him seemed a
long way off. She did not want to go forward and meet the man in him.
She longed to keep him as a boy, hers, unshared by any other feminine
thing.

It must be confessed too that Tod’s love for and admiration of his
mother sent him off on life’s adventure with the notion that all women
were like her. He had all the instinctive chivalry of the clean and
healthy boy whose mother had given him a charming and happy childhood.
He thought that all girls were like the girls he read of in those books
of adventure. Wholesome books no doubt, if life were like that, and a
boy were never to become a man. If Nancy Western could have foreseen the
future and thought about it, in a thoroughly, un-English, honest, and
intelligent way, she might have burnt those books, forgathered with
other wise and devoted women and insisted on some new sort of literature
for children. She might have realised that English parents do not face
the great problems, that they shrink from telling their own boys and
girls the truth.

Tod was enslaved by Millie Hornblower’s yellow hair and audacious eyes.
He thought her a very wonderful creature, and fit to take her place in
any of those romantic books. He haunted Bridge Street and Mill Lane, and
was thrilled if he had a glimpse of the lady. He hung about on Sundays
and waited to see her go to church, and even had the courage to take off
his little bowler hat to Mrs. Hornblower and her daughter.

“Who’s that little boy?”

Tod blushed shamefully. Little boy indeed! When he was in a mood to
encounter and to slay dragons.

Pertinacity had its reward. He met Miss Hornblower in Bridge Street one
Friday evening; she had been adopting a rather distant and ladylike
attitude, and the innocent Tod imagined that he must have offended her.

He pushed his school cap over the back of his head, blushed and stopped.

“Lovely evening.”

“It’s quite a nice evening,” said Miss Hornblower, and giggled.

Then she made that amazing suggestion.

“Let’s go for a walk.”

Tod stepped straight into heaven.

They went down over the bridge and along the path across the river
meadows, Tod trying to make gallant and intelligent conversation, and
failing abjectly. He wished a mad dog or a tramp or something would
attack Millie, so that he could express in wild action what he could not
put into words.

“I liked your mother’s dress on Sunday,” he observed, after wondering
whether this wonderful creature was interested in dogs.

“Was it a nice dress?”

“Rather. But not half as nice as yours.”

Millie gave him a quick, challenging look.

“Don’t believe you noticed what colour mine was.”

“Yes I did—white with blue bows.”

She patted his shoulder, and Tod wanted to die for her.

“What a shy boy you are!”

“I’m not shy.”

“Yes, you are. You couldn’t say bo! to Mr. Chipperton if he came round
that corner.”

“Yes, I could,” said Tod.

And surely enough Mr. Chipperton did stalk round that corner, arms
swinging, bumpy forehead rather moist, the serious man taking his
serious exercise. He stared at Tod, and threw a disapproving and
mistrustful look at Millie.

“Evening, Western.”

“Good evening, sir,” and Tod lifted his cap.

His enchantress showed a sudden haughtiness when Mr. Chipperton had
passed.

“I don’t like cowards,” she said.

Tod was grieved, desolated, angry with himself, but not in the least
angry with her.

“I couldn’t be rude to him, could I?” he argued. “And old Chips is quite
a decent sort.”

“He ought to have taken his hat off to you,” said Miss Hornblower, “when
he saw you were walking with a lady.”

She turned back with all the airs of a very superior young woman who had
been piqued and offended, and Tod could find nothing to say to her.

She left him in Bridge Street.

“Good night, Mr. Western.”

“Good night,” said Tod, jerking his cap, and thinking that girls were
most amazingly sensitive creatures and most easily offended.

Tod was a boy with plenty of sentiment and very little pocket-money, and
it never occurred to him that pocket-money could count with so proud and
peerless a creature as his lady. The Dorotheas and Lornas and Dianas in
the story books never stopped to wonder whether young John Splendid had
a shilling with which to buy them cakes and ale. They expected him to be
strong and bold and adoring, to loiter under walls and in gardens for a
glimpse of them, to treasure gloves and bits of ribbon, to throw flowers
and little love letters into their windows, to smite all villains
fiercely and repeatedly, to be devoted, sentimental, tender.

Poor Tod followed his new romantic instincts and the teachings of the
books. He became the very son of his father, a little Quixote, who made
up bunches of roses, and clove pinks and sweet peas, tagged a label to
them and then tossed them with fear and trembling over Mr. Hornblower’s
garden wall.

“To the most beautiful _and_ lovely Queen of Pentworth.”

“A little token from your loyal and devoted knight and worshipper, Tod
Clarence Western.”

“To the noble lady whom I love.”

Also he tried his hand at poetry, and sent fragments of it with flowers.

 “Your hair is like the brightest gold,
 Your eyes are bluer than the sea.
 But why is your sweet heart so cold
 When I am sunk in misery?”

This particular appeal fell into the hands of Mr. Hornblower, who
happened to be forking potatoes, and who made dreadful fun of it at the
tea-table.

“Tell the little gentleman to take a Gregory powder, Millie. Your hair
is like the brightest gold, is it? Gosh, he’s got it badly!”

Millie giggled, but she rather liked Tod’s very flattering despair.
There were other boys who had fallen under her spell, but they were not
so romantic and devoted as Tod. She relented, and allowed Tod an
assignation at the gate under the scrubby old laurels at the end of the
garden.

Tod was not so eloquent as his flowers or his poetry; he was ever rather
a shy boy with girls and women, and this sudden nearness to his princess
filled him with awe.

“Awfully good of you to let me come and talk to you,” he said.

“You rather like talking to me, then?” sang the siren.

“It’s music,” said Tod; “and isn’t your hair wonderful?”

She shook it at him.

“Would you like a little bit to keep?”

“Would you give it me?”

“If you’ll swear on your honour.”

“What do you want me to swear?” Tod asked.

“That you’ll never look at any other girl, and that you’ll fight any
other boy who wants to talk to me. There’re lots, you know.”

“I’ll do that,” said Tod. “Give me your hand to hold.”

She gave him her hand through the bars of the gate, and Tod removed his
cap, knelt down, and made his vow.

“I’ll always love Millie Hornblower,” he declaimed, “and I’ll defend her
from all ruffians, dogs and rivals, with all my heart and strength. I’ll
be her knight and her servant till I die, please God, Amen.”

With that he kissed her brown little fingers, and got up—blushing.

“You’ll give me the lock of hair now,” he said. “I’ll put it in a little
bag and carry it over my heart.”

“I’ll have to get some scissors.”

She slipped away, and Tod stood there in a prayerful and exultant mood,
a mood that was suddenly disturbed by the appearance of another boy, a
boy with a snub nose and freckles, wearing a disgustingly clean collar,
and a silver watch chain displayed across his black waistcoat.

It was Tom Cassilis, a very sulky looking Tom Cassilis, with green
jealousy in his eyes.

“Hallo.”

“Hallo,” said Tod, beginning to bristle.

“What’s the joke?” said Master Cassilis.

“What joke?”

“Bet me she’d have you here, and I owe her sixpence. You’re a
gooseberry, you loony.”

“It’s a lie,” said Tod.

“Call me a liar.”

“Liar.”

They did not trouble to take off their coats, but plunged into battle
like two good paladins fighting for the love of a fair lady. Tod felt a
particular rage against Master Cassilis’s clean collar, and the swagger
of that silver watch chain. He made haste to ruin both, while Miss
Hornblower, returning with a bit of yellow hair wrapped up in a piece of
pink tissue paper, watched the fray through the slats of the gate, and
felt sweet and good and pleased with her own beauty.

“Oh, you bad boys!”

The boys took no notice of her; they were too busy with each other. Tod
had made Master Cassilis’s nose bleed. He closed with his rival and,
getting the under hold, threw the other boy and destroyed the last
vestiges of vanity that challenged him in the other’s person. Tom
Cassilis rolled and kicked, and began pinching, but Tod dropped that by
a throttle hold that clinched his victory.

“Say ‘I’m a liar.’”

“Shan’t,” gulped Cassilis.

He let Master Cassilis up when that gentleman had recanted.

“It’s my gate,” said Tod. “Don’t you ever come here again, or I’ll
thrash you worse.”

The beaten one was on the edge of tears, and Tod felt a sudden pity for
him.

“Couldn’t help it, Tom, could I? Shake hands.”

“Shan’t,” blubbered the other boy, walking off, dabbing his bleeding
nose with a dirty handkerchief.

Miss Hornblower looked on Tod with favour after that stout proof of his
devotion. He fought three more boys in the next ten days, vanquished
them, and was vexed by no more rivals.

Giggles allowed him to buy her a little German-silver ring and to put it
on her third finger.

“You’re mine, you know, now,” said Tod, “mine—for ever and ever.”

 Chapter Five

Tod held his place as Miss Millie Hornblower’s devoted knight and
servant till the coming of the summer holidays brought him an unexpected
rival. Tod was not a greedy boy; he never had much pocket-money, and he
had failed to understand the secret worldliness of his enchantress’s
affection. He gave her himself when she sometimes had a desire for
caramels and chocolate creams. She liked looking at the tarts and pinky
iced things in the pastrycook’s shop, but Tod was no plutocrat and he
rated her soul above jam puffs and penny ices. He once had brought her a
paper bag full of raspberries from his mother’s garden, but Giggles had
turned up her nose at them.

“Raspberries are nasty, grubby things.”

And Tod had felt hurt.

The summer holidays brought Sydney Plumpton back to Pentworth from some
superior sort of school where they had a sixth form and Olympian young
men who were called Prefects. Sydney Plumpton was a true son of his
father, a heavy, silent, boorish animal with a big wet mouth, staring
eyes, and sandy hair. He walked about Pentworth with his hands in his
pockets, a tuft of hair sticking out under the peak of his cap, and he
never lacked sixpences or something to suck. Chipperton’s thought him a
very great man. He talked about his school’s footer team, how he was
going to get his “cap,” also a scholarship at one of the Cambridge
colleges. He hinted, too, that his house-master’s daughter was always
wanting him to kiss her, and that she gave him her father’s cigarettes
to smoke.

This Plumpton boy happened upon Miss Hornblower somewhere in Pentworth.
He was new and he wore a stand-up collar, and carried a little stick.
Miss Hornblower smiled at him, and Sydney Plumpton thought her “the
stuff.”

They happened on each another day when young Plumpton was buying
sixpennyworth of chocolate creams at Mrs. Clutterbuck’s sweetshop. Miss
Hornblower was impressed. This young man could spend money.

Sydney Plumpton waited for her outside the shop.

“Come for a walk,” he said with the air of the master.

Giggles went with him, the sexed thing acknowledging its lord.

Tod became aware of a chilliness during the next ten days. Millie grew
sarcastic and patronising and talked over his head of the things real
men did when they entertained a lady, yet Tod had no suspicions till he
met her one evening with Sydney Plumpton. They were eating sweets out of
a bag.

Tod was thunderstruck. He had always hated young Plumpton, partly
because he was his father’s son, but his hatred blazed with double
strength because of this treachery. He sensed it at once as treachery,
from the way Giggles looked at him.

The place of the meeting was the path at the foot of the Castle Mound.
The mound was planted with trees, and there was an open space at the top
where lovers used to sit. Tod jerked a thumb in the direction of the
mound.

“Come up there and be licked,” he said to Sydney Plumpton.

They went up and found the place deserted. Millie Hornblower accompanied
them. She had no prejudice against gladiatorial shows, especially when
they were in her honour, though she protested half-heartedly.

“You mustn’t fight. It isn’t nice or gentlemanly. What a nasty temper
you have, Tod Western. Mr. Plumpton hasn’t done anything to you.”

Tod gave her a blank and tragic look. Did women forget so quickly?

Now young Plumpton was two years older than Tod, a good head taller, and
very heavy and strong. He did not take the affair very seriously until
he felt Tod in action, and saw the determination in the smaller boy’s
eyes. They fought for a full ten minutes, savagely, stubbornly, till
they were both so done that their attempts to punch were ludicrous.
Tod’s right eye was bulging and his lip was cut, but he had marked young
Plumpton’s left eye for him and given him a bloody nose. Both boys were
dead pale, panting, unsteady on their feet, when two men pushed through
the trees and stopped the fight.

“Well fought, little ’un.”

“Why don’t you take on one of your own size, Fatty?”

“I’ve got to beat him,” said Tod, holding himself up by the man’s arm.

“So you have, old sport. He’s hooking it; he’s had enough.”

“Rot; I let him down easy,” said Sydney Plumpton thickly. “Come on,
Giggles.”

Then that most amazing thing happened, for Miss Hornblower, who had been
eating young Plumpton’s sweets, hooked an arm in his and offered him her
handkerchief.

“Little savage,” she said, looking destroyingly at Tod; “he never had
any manners.”

Tod stared after them, with his romantic world toppling into ruins.

“But she’s got my ring,” he said aloud.

“No, I haven’t,” retorted the voice of the lady tartly. “Cheap German
silver! Sydney’s bought me a real gold one.”

Tod was a sensitive child, and he took that little tragedy of his
greatly to heart, never having heard as yet of a thing called cynicism.
He was humiliated, bewildered, and unable to rid himself of the belief
that this trashy little yellow-haired chit with her middle-class greed
and cunning was really something fine and wonderful. Those words of hers
about “German silver” had reduced Tod to horrid shame. The sensitive and
generous nature is always at the mercy of a bold and ingenious egotism.
The girl had thought him mean, and Tod looked for the blemish in
himself. Perhaps he was mean, naturally mean like Uncle Remus. He
remembered the incident of the raspberries, and saw himself as a mean
little boy whom no girl could love.

Tod waited till it was dusk before returning to Spaniards Row. He had a
horror of meeting anyone, even his mother; he wanted to get into some
lonely corner and lick himself clean like a dog. He felt that everybody
would be laughing at him and saying, “There’s mean little Tod Western
who gave a girl a German-silver ring.”

He sneaked in by the garden door, and, of course, fell straight into the
arms of his mother, who was lighting the lamp in the passage.

“Oh, Tod!”

“It’s all right, Mother.”

“But your poor eye and lip, dear.”

“I’ve had a bit of a fight,” said Tod, “but I gave the other chap much
what he gave me.”

“And what were you fighting about?”

Tod looked embarrassed, abashed, and thoroughly miserable. He had never
told his mother about Millie Hornblower, and he did not mean to tell her
now.

“Oh, just a row with Sydney Plumpton.”

“That big boy! Why he is two years older than you are, Tod, dear. Come
and let me bathe that poor lip. Was young Plumpton trying to bully you?”

“Bully me! It was an affair of honour,” said Tod with dignity.

“I see,” said Nancy Western.

She did not catechise her boy, but took him to her own room, and made a
sweet fuss of him, and in the end Tod told her everything. He was very
solemn and serious about it, and his mother’s heart went out to him. She
happened to know more about Miss Hornblower than Tod imagined.

“I like my boy to be brave and honourable,” she told him. “But Tod,
dear, lots of little girls, and women too, are just vain and greedy.
They want presents and flattery out of men, and nothing else.”

“I’ve done with girls,” said Tod gravely, “and I don’t want to read any
more of those silly books.”

He meditated, while his mother combed his hair.

“Suppose there are girls who grow up to be like you, Mummy?” he asked
her suddenly.

“Like me, Tod?”

“Yes. You’re the most wonderful woman in the world, Mummy.”

She kissed him.

“Oh, my dear, I’m not; I loved your father very much, and I love you.
That’s all. But I want you, Tod, I want you more than you think. You’re
the one thing I have got in the world.”

Tod turned and looked at her with a sudden shine in his eyes.

“What a silly ass I’ve been! There’s no one I love like you, and no one
so pretty as you.”

“I shan’t be pretty long, Tod.”

“Yes, you will, always and always.”

She sat down suddenly on the old sofa and put an arm round him. Her face
had grown very serious and sad.

“I’ve got to tell you, Tod, dear. I haven’t been very well for quite a
long time. I didn’t tell you before——”

Tod’s eyes had grown big and questioning. He looked at his mother as he
had never looked at her before. There was fear in him, a great
tenderness, an accusing selfscorn, a sudden throwing back of his memory
to moments when he had been conscious of his mother as seeming thinner
and paler than of yore.

“Mummy, you are not ill?”

“I don’t quite know yet, dear, and the doctor isn’t quite sure either.
But I’m not so strong as I was, and I get a silly, tired feeling.”

The memory of Millie Hornblower became blotted utterly out of Tod’s
mind. He knelt on the sofa, and put his arms round Nancy Western’s neck.

“I’ve got to leave school,” he said.

“No, dear, I want you to learn all you can.”

“Well, I’ll learn,” he said almost fiercely—“I’ll learn everything. But
I’m going to work in the shop, too, and I’m going to take care of you,
and you’re going to do nothing at all.”

“Tod——”

“And we’ll go to picnics, and I’ll carry everything. And if you get
tired, Mummy, we’ll have a bath-chair like Mrs. Furnival’s and I’ll
wheel you about.”

Her eyes filled with tears.

“Dear God,” she said, “you have given me all that I asked for.”

That was the beginning of a new season in Tod’s life, perhaps the most
beautiful, the saddest and the most significant season of them all. His
mother had not spoken to him without good cause. She was a sick woman,
and before three months had passed a doomed woman, a woman with youth in
her heart and death in her body.

Tod nursed her, at least he called it nursing her, though it was more
like the mingled love of a child and a man than the carefulness of a
nurse. He brought her her tea in the morning, carried up her breakfast,
and then dashed off to school where he tried to work as she wanted him
to work, though his thoughts were in Spaniards Row. He dashed home
again, tenderly irrepressible, and did all sorts of things without being
told or prompted. There were flowers in her room, and Tod put them
there. Once or twice she had asked him for the cut glass scent-bottle on
the dressing-table, and he saved up his pocket-money and bought her
scent. He interviewed Mr. Chipperton and asked to be excused homework,
explaining the situation with a simplicity and a frankness that touched
even that dusty pedant.

“Certainly, Western, certainly. I most thoroughly approve of your—your
devotion, sir.”

But Tod was at his greatest when he waylaid the doctor, and talked to
him with the anxious gravity of a father of a family.

“How is my mother to-day, sir, please?”

“Much the same, Tod, I’m afraid.”

“What about a bath-chair, sir? You would recommend fresh air?”

“Certainly, Tod. I think I can get you the loan of a chair.”

Dr. Vance went out into Spaniards Row and swore at nothing in
particular.

“Wish I had a boy like that,” he said.

But he was so conscious of Tod that he found time to be worried, and he
had to speak of it to Tod’s mother.

“That boy of yours is a trump,” he said, sitting on the chair beside the
bed and looking thoroughly uncomfortable.

Nancy Western answered him with a strange question.

“How long am I going to live? I want you to tell me the truth.”

The doctor felt in all his pockets, scratched his cheek with his
forefinger, and then looked her straight in the eyes.

“About four months, I should say.”

“Thank you,” she said; “I must tell him.”

She told Tod that evening when it was getting dusk, making him lie
curled on the bed beside her, with his head on the pillow. And Tod did
not weep just then. He lay very still, fists clenched, face all screwed
up, his heart full of a bitter wailing that something told him had to be
suppressed.

“I’ve got to be a man—I’ve got to be a man.”

And a man he was and far bigger-hearted and finer than most, fighting
that chocking anguish in his throat and that sense of hopelessness and
bewildering desolation.

“Oh, Mummy, why must God take you away? I won’t be selfish. It’s Daddy
that wants you.”

She was heartbroken when she thought of her boy alone, left to the
negative kindness of Uncle Remus. She was dying ten years too soon, just
when she could have done so much. The business was breaking up, and
Tod’s home was going with it. In another ten years she could have seen
him settled in Pentworth, or realised those other ambitions that had
been so dear to her mother’s soul.

“Uncle Remus will give you a home, dear,” she said; “I have spoken to
him about it.”

Tod did not want to go to Uncle Remus, but he managed to blurt out: “It
will be jolly on the farm.”

Tod wept his soul out that night when he was alone in his room. His
child’s anguish was so clean and poignant that its wet eyes could see
nothing in the future but desperate and utter loneliness. There were
moments when he rebelled, and stood in his white nightshirt at his
window, and flourished a fist at the stars.

“God, you mustn’t take Mother away. If you’re a good God you’ll let Dr.
Vance make her well.”

But God faded very much out of Tod’s life during those next weeks when
his mother began to suffer exquisite pain. The boy’s heart cried out for
tangible help, and in his queer, frank way he renounced a shadowy
benignant divinity whom nobody had ever seen and who appeared to be of
no use in those hours of bitter anguish. Dr. Vance became Tod’s god,
quiet, inarticulate, brown-faced Dr. Vance, with his little syringe and
bottle of blessed balm. Tod utterly refused to go to school. They could
hardly get him out of the house, and if morphia soothed Nancy Western’s
body it was Tod that soothed her soul.

He managed to blind himself to the future by throwing himself with wild
tenderness into the present. Sickness, especially mortal sickness, can
be very wearying and repulsive, but Tod was never tired, and there was
no flinching in his eyes. He washed his mother’s face for her, and
brushed her hair, fed her, tidied the bed, fetched and carried, read to
her, kept the fire bright, or sat and held her hand.

“That there boy’s a marvel,” said Mrs. Bunce; “’e’s better than any
husband.”

The most vivid memory of Tod’s life was the memory of the night when his
mother died. It had been morphia and always morphia. She was thin,
horribly thin, and white as linen. Her lips were dry and pale. She could
hardly swallow a teaspoonful of milk, or move herself in bed.

It was winter, and Tod had slipped out and bought a tin of peaches. He
had two of them all soft and juicy ready in a saucer.

“Tod——”

Her voice was a mere whisper. Tod was with her instantly.

“Tod, you’ve made me so happy, you and your dear daddy.”

Tod held her hand.

“I’ve been so proud, Tod. You are going to be a fine man like your
father.”

“I’ll be a fine man, a great man—for you, Mummy,” said Tod with a gulp.

She lay silent awhile, and then spoke, with a faint wail in her voice,
after a spasm of pain.

“Oh, I’m so thirsty.”

Tod went for the peaches, and, kneeling on the bed, and supporting her
with one hand, fed her with a spoon. She took the fruit almost eagerly
and with a kind of pathetic pleasure.

“That was good, Tod.”

Then she lay quiet again. It was growing dusk, and there was no light in
the room save the light of the fire.

Suddenly she spoke again.

“Tod.”

“Mummy.”

“Come here and hold my hand.”

He snuggled near her. She was on the edge of the great abyss, but he did
not realise it.

“Tod, never marry—till you have been in love ten times.”

“I shan’t marry—never,” he said, “unless I find somebody like you.”

She made a queer little sound, something between a sigh and a laugh, and
lay there holding his hand.

It was about midnight when Mrs. Bunce, keeping vigil by the kitchen
fire, heard that wailing in the room above.

“Dear God,” said she, starting up, “the poor dear’s dead! God save us,
but what am I to do for that boy?”

 Chapter Six

Tod was nearly fifteen when his mother died, and he was destined to
spend two years under the control of the negative virtues of Uncle
Remus.

Nancy Western left her boy the £1,000 she had inherited, the “business”
and the furniture. The £1,000 was safe enough, invested in those two
mortgages; and Uncle Remus, left sole trustee until Tod was twenty-one,
had a solid faith in mortgages. The “business” and the furniture proved
to be very liquid assets, so liquid that the “business” ran away through
Uncle Remus’s fingers.

The commercial spirit of Pentworth was responsible for this
liquefaction.

Mr. Plumpton happened to have an energetic young nephew who was a
photographer’s assistant at Shaston-on-Sea, and about six months before
Nancy Western’s death Mr. Plumpton sent for that young man and offered
to lend him a hundred pounds.

“Look here, Gus; the woman’s done for. There’s no need to make her an
