offer. Set up yourself, and you’ll collar the whole business, without
paying a farthing. When she’s dead, there’ll be a sale.”

“And I can nip in and buy as much of the stuff as I want.”

“Cheap, dirt cheap,” said Mr. Plumpton, remembering a certain scene in
Nancy Western’s house.

That was Pentworth’s idea of commercial morality, but in those days Tod
knew nothing of the Pentworths of England.

The sale of the Westerns’ furniture and shop fittings at No. 13
Spaniards Row left Tod with a few of his father’s books and a little
cane chair that had belonged to his mother. Tod did not go to the sale;
he was resentful and glum about it, yet conscious in a vague way that he
was up against a force that was stronger than his boy’s sentiments.

“You’ve got to have two years more schooling,” said Uncle Remus, “your
mother left it written down. Schooling’s got to be paid for.”

Tod supposed that it had.

Pentworth excelled itself over that sale. It sniffed, and fingered and
sneered, turned the home of the dead Westerns upside down, and snapped
up “bargains.” It was a day of bargains. Mrs. Bunce, who was a
sentimental sort of creature, followed the sale through the first half
of the catalogue and then pushed her way out into the street.

“Downright robbery, that’s what it is,” she said to a neighbour,
“robbin’ that boy. And all the poor soul’s things being treated like
rubbish.”

Mr. Plumpton’s nephew bought the shop fittings and the outfit of the
studio at a very successful figure.

“Smart bit of biz, that!”

Ten years later someone else played the same trick on him, and he asked
if it was “a Christian country.”

No. 13 Spaniards Row ceased to be a little corner of romance. Tod could
never bring himself to go near the house. He tried it but once, and came
by such a lump in his throat and such a misery of loneliness that
Spaniards Row became for him no more than a thing of dreams. He
continued at Chipperton’s as in-boy, living in Mr. Chipperton’s house,
and learning to connect Miss Victoria Chipperton with underdone beef and
pickled cabbage. Tod was never a great desk boy, and he hated church and
the Friday evening hymns almost as much as he hated Miss Chipperton. He
learnt his arithmetic and how to spell; picked up some dud-Latin and a
few oddments of algebra; struck queer, disjointed patches of English
history and remembered a few battles, but little else. He could draw,
and he absorbed some elementary science which the third master threw at
the science class twice a week in a desultory sort of way. But most of
his activities were of the open air. He was the best bat in the school,
and he knew more about birds and trees and wild things than any other
boys at Chipperton’s. As for girls, he had no use for them at all, which
made him rather attractive.

His holidays were spent with Uncle Remus at Burnt Farm, a thoroughly
hideous brick-and-slate house that stood up mournfully on a bare
hillside. Uncle Remus was all for the practical side of life. He
believed in corrugated iron and barbed wire, dirty clothes and clean
land. There were no pigeon-pie, cheese-cake, strawberry-and-cream
touches about Uncle Remus’s farming. He kept a housekeeper and one
strong girl, and the housekeeper saw to it that Tod did not fraternise
with the food in the larder. She was a very tall woman with great bony
hips, a flat chest and a little dab of nose that always looked blue in
winter. Tod used to fancy that she must pull herself out of bed by her
own hair, so tight and thin was it. The “strong girl” was a hoyden who
was always trying to make Tod wrestle, till a new ploughman came along
and married her.

Tod did not love Burnt Farm. It was dreary and penurious and isolated,
and Uncle Remus used to put down seed in winter and shoot the birds.

“Darned little thieves,” he called them.

Uncle Remus shot everything; especially if it could be sold or eaten.
When it was cheaper to trap he trapped, using the steel trap, and this
economy of his led to a row with Tod. For Tod found a rabbit in a steel
trap one morning, down by the ash wood, and after that all Uncle Remus’s
steel traps were missing. Tod had collected them and thrown the lot into
the cow-pond.

He avowed it openly and with anger, and Uncle Remus made an unsuccessful
attempt to whack him.

“You young varmint——”

“Don’t you touch me.”

“What did you do it for—jus’ mischief?”

“How’d you like to be in a trap?”

Uncle Remus stared, and his lower lip hung down.

“We’ll never make a farmer of you, my boy.”

“I’m not going to be a farmer. I’m going to be a doctor, and mend
things.”

Uncle Remus gave one of his rare laughs.

“Start on your manners, young man. You a doctor! Why, Chipperton says
you’re a blockhead.”

“I’m not a blockhead,” said Tod fiercely. “Old Chipperton’s a fool.”

At the end of two years Uncle Remus began to think that it was high time
Tod should earn some money. He was fond of the boy in a queer, grudging,
cold-blooded way, but the little bit of gold exercised a wonderful
fascination over Tod’s uncle. He would spend hours at night, sitting
smoking in his old arm-chair, scribbling figures on the backs of old
envelopes and making eternal calculations. It was Uncle Remus’s great
game, the great game of profit and loss, turnips and haystacks reduced
to the last halfpenny.

“Time a big boy like you was getting to work.”

“I want to be a doctor,” said Tod.

“And where’s the money to come from?”

“There’s the money Mother left me.”

Uncle Remus gave a sardonic grin.

“That won’t be touched so long as I have a say in it. Two good solid
mortgages. Capital ain’t come by every day of the week.”

He was adamant, nor could Tod move him for a moment. Yet the boy was so
obstinate that the old man took the trouble to drive over to Pentworth
and get himself supported by private interviews with Dr. Vance and Mr.
Chipperton.

He came back very well satisfied.

“About this doctoring idea, young man. How long do you think it takes to
learn the trade?”

“I don’t know.”

“Five years. Vance says it would cost the best part of a thousand pounds
to do it well. And at the end of it you might earn a hundred and fifty
pounds and your keep. That’s a monkey’s game. Besides—you ain’t got the
head for it.”

“Who says so?”

“Chipperton. Says you’re a good boy, but you’d never pass the exams.
Money wasted. You listen to me, Tod my boy; business is the thing.
There’s money in a shop—not every sort of shop of course—specially if
you’ve got grit and a little capital. You drop all that black-coat
nonsense. What’s a black coat against ten thousand pounds in good
securities? It’s money that counts.”

Tod had no answer to give to his uncle’s shrewd materialism, no answer
that mattered. Uncle Remus’s opposition was too solid. No money would be
available till Tod was twenty-one, and as the farmer put it: “A young
man can’t loaf for five years.” Moreover, Tod had felt discouraged by
Mr. Chipperton’s scepticism with regard to those exams. Tod still had
some faith in Mr. Chipperton’s intellect, if he had no belief in him as
a cricketer, and Tod was a sensitive boy in spite of all his sturdiness.
A sense of adventure was stirring in him; Chipperton’s school and Burnt
Farm were like sucked eggs.

About that time Uncle Remus brought off one of his diplomatic triumphs;
that is to say, he dispossessed himself of a responsibility without
parting with any time or money. He had a friend at Shaston-on-Sea, a Mr.
Eli Flowerdew who traded in second-hand furniture, books, pictures and
antiques, no hole-and-corner business, mind you, but a two-fronted shop
in Ponsonby Place, a big warehouse, and a reputation. Old Flowerdew and
Uncle Remus had been at school together, and Eli Flowerdew was a “warm
man,” a man with a fine scent for a bargain.

It happened, too, that Uncle Remus was in a position to oblige Mr.
Flowerdew. The reason seemed trivial enough; it would have sounded
meaningless to Tod. His uncle had a fine set of old English pewter,
chargers, plates, ewers, all marked with the mark of a rather famous
pewterer, and Mr. Flowerdew needed that set. He needed it so badly that
he drove over one day in a gig from Shaston and tried to persuade Remus
to sell.

Tod’s uncle “went to market.” He had an idea in his shrewd noddle, and
if he obliged Eli Flowerdew, Mr. Flowerdew should oblige him. He
pretended to be very unwilling to sell that pewter, paraded a quite
unexpected and sentimental pride in it, and boasted of the fact that he
did not want the money.

“It belonged to my grandfather,” he kept repeating; “Ol’ George Barter,
who was the best judge of a bullock this side o’ the Downs.”

Mr. Flowerdew persisted.

“Look here, Remus, this isn’t a matter of one bit of business to me. I
have struck a new client, a wealthy and eccentric sort of fish who’ll
bite at anything old. He’s mad on pewter, and this is the first bait. I
can make hundreds out of him if I get him on the hook, and this pewter
of yours would do it.”

“Oh, that’s it, is it,” said Uncle Remus. He puffed at his pipe, and
refilled Mr. Flowerdew’s glass.

“Money ain’t everything,” he said benignantly. “We’ve been friends these
forty years. Yet I’ve a sort of kind feeling for that pewter.”

The dealer in antiques played sentiment against sentiment.

“That’s true. Anything I can do for you, old man?”

Uncle Remus appeared to search for the thought of something that he
needed.

“No, not as I knows of.”

“Don’t want a bit of furniture?”

“No.”

“Wish I was as contented.”

Uncle Remus smiled like the beautiful and simple old peasant of some
sentimental story. Life was complete; there was nothing on earth that he
greedily desired. And then he removed his pipe from his mouth, pointed
the stem at Mr. Flowerdew, and sat up in his chair.

“Hold on. Why, I never thought o’ that before! You’re a man of
influence, Eli; that’s a fine business of yours, and it’s going to be
finer. You don’t want a lad in your shop, do you?”

“Why, have you got one?”

“Yes. Young Tod Western, my dead niece’s son. He’s leaving Chipperton’s
school in a month or two. He’s a good boy, strong and healthy, and he’ll
have a thousand pounds when he comes of age.”

Eli Flowerdew frowned at his boots.

“I could do with a boy in the shop to help Rook. But he’d have to be
quick and nippy, and have nice manners. We get a lot of women in; you
know, people with style.”

“Tod’s got very good manners,” said Uncle Remus, “and he’d learn what
solid business was with you, Eli.”

“If I like the look of the lad I’ll take him. I’d give him his board to
start with, and then pay him as he shaped.”

Uncle Remus grew quite animated.

“Why, that’s an offer, a generous offer. It just entered my head
sudden-like, and I hadn’t thought about it one way or the other. The
boy’d learn the business, and some day he could start on his own, or you
might go so far as to take him in, with that little capital of his.”

“I might,” said Mr. Flowerdew guardedly, “supposing I see the boy?”

Tod happened to be at Burnt Farm for the half-term holiday. He was found
by Uncle Remus and sent up to put on his Sunday suit and a clean collar.
The farmer was solemn and mysterious about it.

“I’ve opened a new furrow for you, young man. Here’s Mr. Eli Flowerdew,
one of the leading men at Shaston, ready to take you in and teach you
his business. It’s a favour to me.”

“What’s the business?” asked Tod.

“We’ll talk about that later. Mr. Flowerdew wants a young chap with good
manners. Don’t you forget to put on that clean collar.”

So Tod was introduced to Mr. Flowerdew, a tall man who held himself very
straight, and had a sharp, red-brown face and blue eyes that made you
think that he was angry. There were three deep lines on Eli Flowerdew’s
forehead, lines that ran down and met at the root of his nose; the skin
between those lines used to bulge and grow red when anything annoyed
him. Tod noticed that he had a little greyish tuft of hair on his chin;
his lips looked too small to cover his teeth, so that his lips appeared
tight and stretched. When he smiled he showed his fangs like a dog, and
those teeth of his were long and crowded together, and all yellow.

“Here’s young Tod, Eli.”

Mr. Flowerdew held out a hand.

“So this is Tod. How are you, young man?”

“Very well, thank you, sir.”

The “sir” settled it. Tod was of no serious importance to Mr. Flowerdew.
The boy was not an oaf; he had an intelligent face, and looked well
grown and strong. Also he seemed a little shy and impressed. All boys
were much the same to Mr. Flowerdew; he hated the whole genus, and his
attitude towards them was that of a ferocious dog.

“Seems a quiet sort of boy,” he said when Tod had been inspected and
dismissed.

“Takes after his mother. Mr. Chipperton, of Pentworth, thinks a lot of
the lad. He ain’t over quick at his lessons, but they tell me he has
character.”

“I hope he is a religious boy, because if I take him into my family——”

Uncle Remus side-stepped with great adroitness.

“He ain’t a lad as is always after the girls.”

“I’ll take him,” said Mr. Flowerdew, and he also took the pewter at a
price that was very acceptable to Uncle Remus.

About a month later the farmer drove Tod in his market cart over to
Shaston-on-Sea, and left the lad and a little old leather trunk at the
house of Mr. Eli Flowerdew. It was a tall, serious-looking house in
Ponsonby Place, a house that still seemed to be worrying over the fact
that the lower part of its front had been turned into a shop. It stood
abashed under the eyes of the house across the way, a house that
retained the title of a “gentleman’s residence,” a swanking, arrogant
sort of house with a lot of white and green paint, brass-work and bright
curtains. A doctor lived there, on the sunny side of the street. Eli
Flowerdew’s house had its face in the shadow. It looked smutty and
depressed. Its back view was a little more cheerful and prepossessing.
There was a strip of garden that ended in a shrubbery, and from the top
windows you could see the sea.

It was a very grey sea that day. Summer had lost itself for a week, and
a south-west wind played Pan-pipes with the chimney-pots, and drenched
Shaston with occasional scuds of rain. Tod’s stomach felt empty. His
uncle had dropped him at the door of Flowerdew’s shop, and driven off to
dodge the rain-storms.

Tod found a supercilious and cold-eyed young man in the shop. He wore
rimless pince-nez, and had a suave but brutal mouth.

“Hallo, what do you want?”

“I’m Tod Western,” said Tod, lugging that portmanteau of his through the
swing doors.

“Oh, you are, are you! Get that thing round the back. This isn’t a
pawnshop.”

The young man went back to a desk in a far corner, and left Tod to guess
what “round the back” meant. He was more depressed than annoyed by the
young man’s indifference.

“Not that way. Door on the left. And mind the table with the china on
it.”

Tod and his portmanteau bundled themselves into a dismal passage hung
with old prints. He saw a flight of stairs covered with linoleum, and a
landing window at the top, and he was hesitating whether he should climb
the stairs when a voice descended on him.

“Who’s that?” It was a sharp, shrill voice, a voice that was made for
the harrying of servants.

“It’s me,” said Tod inelegantly.

A figure followed the voice, the figure of a girl with a lot of fluffy
hair, and a face that made Tod think of the face of a horse.

“What do you want? That’s the way into the shop.”

“That chap sent me here. I’m Tod Western.”

The girl stared at him. She was extraordinarily like her father, and had
the same hard eyes and ugly mouth.

“Oh, it’s young Western, is it? Your room’s right at the top. Looks out
on the street. You’d better carry that thing up, and don’t you mark the
paint.”

Tod had not the heart to ask Miss Lena Flowerdew for any further
directions. He discovered his room by using the method of trial by
error, butting first into an apartment that was obviously feminine, and
backing out with great briskness. His own room was a dingy little tank
with a stain on the ceiling and a strip of old red stair carpet down the
middle. The chest of drawers and the washstand had once been yellow, but
some enthusiast had streaked them over with a coat of cream paint. The
chest of drawers wobbled on three legs, and was an article of severe
virtue, all its drawers showing a great unreadiness to open to Tod’s
blandishments. There was a text over the bed which cried, “God bless our
Home.”

When Tod had distributed his belongings he sat down on the bed and felt
acutely miserable. This heartache was reinforced by an attack of
sensitive shyness, a panic mood that held him tied to his room. He did
not know whether he ought to go downstairs; he felt that he would be
sure to arrive in the wrong room. Moreover, he did not think he wanted
to meet Miss Lena Flowerdew again, until fate or hunger brought them
together.

Tod sat down for an hour, quite ready to blubber in spite of his
seventeen years, wishing himself back at Burnt Farm, anywhere but where
he was. He had thoughts of making a bolt for it, but that poor old brown
portmanteau appealed against desertion. The thing had belonged to his
father.

And then Tod heard someone coming up the stairs. The footsteps paused
outside the door, and someone knocked.

“Hallo,” said Tod.

Tod had never been called Mr. Western before, and the title sent a
thrill through him. Moreover, the voice was quite a different sort of
voice from that of Miss Lena Flowerdew. It was gentle and soft, and
rather deprecating.

Tod got up and opened the door, and found a girl standing there, a shy
girl with dark eyes, brown hair, and a quick colour. She looked fragile
and pretty, and not much older than Tod.

“Good afternoon,” he said.

“Tea’s ready. We have it at five.”

“Been unpacking my things,” he explained, blushing.

Her brown eyes smiled at him, the first gleam of human sympathy he had
caught in that house.

“I’m Kitty.”

“My name’s Tod, short for Todhunter, Miss Flowerdew.”

“It’s quite a nice name,” she answered, “I’ll show you the way down.”

She did more than that, for she saved him from being overwhelmed by the
solemnity of the Flowerdew tea-table. Mr. Flowerdew was drinking tea as
though he had to catch a train. He showed his teeth at Tod in what was
meant for a smile.

“Sit down, Western. My two girls, Lena and Kitty. Mr. Rook—my
assistant. Pass the butter, Rook will you.”

Tod sat down next to Kitty, and was given tea by Miss Flowerdew. She
also pushed a tin of sardines towards him with perfunctory
suggestiveness, and Tod felt that she was watching how many he took.
Edwin Rook eyed him all the while with cynical curiosity. Mr. Flowerdew
took no more notice of him at all. It was Kitty who saved him from utter
demoralisation and from choking himself over his tea.

She cut bread for him.

“Have some jam, Mr. Western.”

Tod had jam, lots of jam. He began to think that he liked Kitty
Flowerdew very much indeed.

 Chapter Seven

The town of Shaston-on-Sea had shaped itself very much like the Nile,
that Lily of the Desert with its flower-head on the sea and its stem
trailing towards the equator. Shaston had flowered along the seafront in
solid masses of white Georgian and grey stuccoed Victorian houses, and
the Edwardian red villa craze had coloured the fringe of its petals a
hard red. The high, commercial part of Shaston lay in the calyx of the
flower, grouped about King William Street that joined the seafront to
the stalk of the town, and there the simile ended. The Georgians had
planned solidly and well, but the commercial individualists who had
followed them had been men in a hurry to make money. The haste and the
hideousness of it showed everywhere in the long valley behind the town.
Bricks and stucco and slate had been slopped down in great yellow
puddles that had trickled and met and intermingled till the ugliness was
complete. There were rows upon rows of flat-faced, wall-eyed houses,
crowding back to back with miserable little spaces, called “gardens,”
between them. The creators of this base, cynically cheap rubbish heap
may have taken Bermondsey or Bethnal Green as their models. Some
sardonic humorist had flung names about. Blackberry Hill, Alsatia,
Bilson’s Bottom, Rope Alley, Mug’s Lane, Victoria Road, Sea View
Terrace. All this nastiness was within two miles of the Whiteholt beech
woods, and less than a mile from the sea.

Tod Western accepted Shaston-on-Sea as a healthy boy accepts his dinner.
It was there, on the plate, waiting to be eaten, and Tod swallowed it
with relish, not yet having arrived at that analytical stage when a man
worries about his proteids, fats and carbohydrates. The place was new to
Tod, and full of life and mystery. Mr. Flowerdew let the lad out every
afternoon the first week, not from any wish that Tod should enjoy life,
but rather to let him satiate himself with Shaston.

“Let a boy gorge himself with strawberries and he’ll give up wanting
them.”

Tod explored Shaston from the fashionable quarter of St. Margarets in
the West to certain picturesque slums called “The Shingles” and “Red
Ruin” that lay under the shadow of the White Cliff. He went on the pier,
tried the various penny-in-the-slot machines, listened to the band, went
for a sail in one of the pleasure yachts, roared with laughter at Punch
and Judy, got up early and bathed and made friends with a swarthy young
man from Horrox & Horrox’s drapery establishment who promised to teach
him to swim. This Mr. Robert Twine appeared to find Tod very breezy and
refreshing. He was always calling him “You kid” or “You jolly green
apple.” Tod was green, healthily and innocently green. He accepted life,
and he accepted Shaston, putting his penny in the slot of each day and
getting his chocolate or his nougat.

At the end of a week he was whole-heartedly in love with Miss Kitty
Flowerdew. She was a shy girl who had gathered up her brown hair and
lengthened her skirts less than six months ago, and whose prettiness was
the prettiness of a soft dark pansy. She played the piano, and sang in a
pleasant, bird-like way, such songs as “The Carnival,” “Good-bye” and
“Annie Laurie.” She was religious, going to the weekday services at St.
Jude’s, and never missing early communion on Sunday.

Tod fell into such depths of shyness in her presence that he became very
slow over his meals, and was always late for pudding. His adoration
gazed and gazed, and might have gone on gazing in the same blind and
devoted way had not Miss Lena Flowerdew taken it in hand.

“Wake up, moon-calf.”

There was nothing soft or subtle about Lena Flowerdew. She was as ready
to rend Tod’s feelings as though they were calico, and she had no love
for her sister Kitty.

Mr. Rook, who had dinner and tea with the family, seconded her in
de-romancing Tod. Tod did not understand Mr. Edwin Rook in those early
days. The fellow was always quizzing him in a sneering, cold-blooded
sort of way. He was particularly witty if Mr. Flowerdew happened to be
absent.

“Mind you don’t fall into it, Western.”

“Fall into what?” asked Tod.

“Your plate, of course. You ought to look where you’re going.”

Tod glared and blushed, while Miss Flowerdew gave a snigger.

“Expect to see them rolling round the table any moment. Sure they are in
tight, Western?”

“What d’you mean?” and Tod glanced at his buttons.

“Why, your googlies, old chap, those nice, round marble eyes of yours.”

“Shut up,” said Tod.

Kitty would look uncomfortable and a little frightened, and Tod began to
suspect that she was afraid of her sister. He noticed too that she
hardly ever looked at Edwin Rook, but that Edwin Rook often looked at
her. He stared at her in a way that puzzled Tod, his lower lip thrust
out, his pale eyes all big and funny behind his glasses. And once Tod
happened to glance at Lena Flowerdew when Rook was looking at her sister
in that way, and the elder girl’s face shocked Tod. It was a face of
evil hatred, jealousy and scorn, the nostrils pinched and sneering, the
mouth thin and cruel, with its lips stretched tight over Miss
Flowerdew’s prominent teeth.

Quite a number of things began to puzzle Tod.

Why was Kitty Flowerdew so utterly unlike her father or her sister?

Why did Lena Flowerdew hate Kitty? The elder girl was very much the
mistress of her father’s house. She was capable, hard, domineering; she
managed the housekeeping, bullied the little general servant, and paid
the bills. Her voice dominated the place, and the only person whom she
feared was her father. Tod often heard her sneering and gibing at her
sister. She had the most sarcastic, virulent tongue; the tradesmen’s
boys hated her. Tod had caught Kitty with wet eyes, and had felt her
brush past him in a hurry, and he more than suspected that Miss
Flowerdew was responsible.

Edwin Rook puzzled him; he was such a cold fish, such a queer, sarcastic
beast. He showed extraordinary suavity to anyone entering the shop, and
talked with a glibness that fascinated Tod. Rook’s real self came to
life again when the customer had returned to the street. He used to
indulge in little snarling phrases.

“Good day, Mrs. Snobson. Vewy enervating wevver to-day, what! Yes, we
have—ah—a wig-table—ah—don’t ch’you know. Quite the recherché
article—ah—yes. Shall we—ah—pack it in cotton-wool—ah—and dispatch
it to you—madam—in a van—ah?”

As a rule he was more terse and bitter.

“Dirty shoppies, that’s what we are, young Western, and don’t you forget
it.”

“Fleeced the old fool that time! Good biz!”

“Bargains? We bargain ’em. We stuff ’em in worm-holes, or send ’em to
Birmingham.”

Tod was almost equally puzzled by Mr. Eli Flowerdew. There appeared to
be three Mr. Flowerdews—the Flowerdew of the shop, the Flowerdew of the
house, the Flowerdew of the Sabbath. The Flowerdew of the shop was
bland, obliging, persuasive, spouting a Sheraton and Sheffield plate
jargon that Tod had yet to learn. The Flowerdew of the house scolded,
made a noise when it drank, sucked its teeth, was vulgarly hard and
overbearing, kicked the cat, was greedy at meals. The Flowerdew of the
Sabbath came down to breakfast in a very bad temper, put on a frock coat
and a top hat, went to church as though its nostrils were closed against
the smell of original sin, acted as church-warden with an air of severe
serenity, called itself a “mis-er-able sinner,” came home to a hot
dinner, slept on the sofa with a handkerchief over its face, had tea,
went to church again, sang “Rock of Ages,” and spent the rest of the
evening working out the week’s profits. There was yet another and more
compact edition of the Sabbath Flowerdew that was issued on Wednesday
evenings. It appeared at the meeting of the local Social Purity League,
a very fastidious Flowerdew, one of the lily-white angels who guarded
Shaston’s honour.

Tod spoke of his puzzlement to that new friend of his, Bob Twine. They
had just splashed back from the sea, and were drying themselves on the
shingle.

“Can’t make old Flowerdew out at all,” said Tod

“What’s worrying you, kid?”

“He’s such a rum mixture. I go to church with them, you know.”

Bob Twine chuckled. He was a rather _farouche_ young man who had been
born and bred in Shaston.

“Six days with the devil and one with God! Do you know old Flowerdew
killed his wife?”

“What!” said Tod with horror. “You’re rotting.”

“No, I’m not. He made life so sweet for her that she went off her head
and drowned herself.”

“Good Lord!”

“Yes, just about here. That second girl of hers is just like her.”

Tod scratched a bare leg and stared at the shingle.

“But how’d you know?”

“My mother told me.”

“But he goes to church,” said Tod, “and he’s great at those Christian
Purity Meetings, or whatever you call ’em.”

“Oh, chuck it!” quoth Mr. Twine, “the society for getting searchlights
fixed on the pier to prevent a man kissing a girl on the beach after
dark.”

Tod’s last puzzlement was provided by the shop and warehouse. He was
taking the business seriously, and living in a chaos of old books, oak
chests and gate-legged tables, Chippendale chairs, Sheraton sideboards,
Boule tables, bureaux, pictures, prints, old china, pewter, Sheffield
plate, Toby jugs, grandfather clocks, brass candlesticks, warming-pans,
mirrors, fire-backs, dogs, Dinanderie, Dutch tiles, knife boxes,
fenders, wine coolers, samplers in frames, old pistols, blunderbusses,
candelabra, Oriental plates, tall-boys, Welsh dressers. It was all very
queer and confusing. Nothing seemed to have the value which you would
have expected it to have. Ladies of obvious wealth and intelligence came
in and paid what seemed to Tod absurd sums for ugly little pot sheep and
spotted cows and snuff-boxes, and shabby old worm-eaten chairs. Tod
noticed too that the prices varied most queerly. Old Flowerdew and Mr.
Rook seemed to have a game of their own, a game of which Tod was
destined to learn the rudiments.

Mr. Flowerdew had urged him to play the shadow to Edwin Rook.

“Knowledge is money, Western, especially in a business such as this.
Rook’s worth double what he was when he came to me.”

Rook was no very willing instructor, and he did not like Tod, but
necessity and Mr. Flowerdew persuaded him to part with a portion of his
knowledge. He tried to teach Tod the elements of value in the world of
Wardour Street, and he advised Tod to specialise in old books.

“You take that catalogue of Porteous’s and mug at it on your spare time.
Then you’ll know what to watch for and how to grab a prize when the
chance comes. I’ve got enough to do without bothering about books.”

Tod was expected to familiarise himself also with the prices of the
various articles in the shop. Rook started him on furniture, and used to
hold a daily viva-voce examination, running Tod from Sheraton
card-tables to Jacobean chairs. Tod found it rather amusing, and would
have found it more so if Rook had not been such a cold-blooded beast. He
had a suspicion that Rook had never wanted him in the shop, that he was
jealous of him for some reason or other, and Tod wondered why.

As for his love affair it continued to be rather shy and tentative. He
had given up spilling his devotion over the tea-table, largely because
he had realised that Lena Flowerdew and the shopman were ready to use it
as a weapon against Kitty. And Kitty herself gave him a shy hint.

“Tod——”

She called him Tod now.

“Yes——”

“I wish you wouldn’t look at me so much at meals.”

Tod flared scarlet.

“Sorry—I—I didn’t know I did. Of course—you wouldn’t like it.”

She looked at him quickly, approvingly.

“No, it isn’t that, Tod. But Lena—Lena is so quick-tempered.”

“She’s a yellow-faced cat,” said Tod, “but I’d do anything in the world
you asked me to, Kitty.”

Her soft face flushed prettily.

“I think you would, dear.”

So Tod became a model of discretion and of chivalry. There were moments
when Kitty Flowerdew reminded him of his mother, especially in the quick
way her eyes lit up, in the soft wave of her hair, in the poise of her
head when she was working or reading a book. But Kitty Flowerdew lacked
Nancy Western’s strength. She was what our fathers called a “sweet
creature,” sensitive, pliant, easily frightened or suppressed. She was
afraid of her sister, and she was also afraid of Edwin Rook, but she had
no fear of Tod.

Tod had five shillings a week pocket-money, the sum that Uncle Remus
allowed him out of his yearly £40, and nearly all Tod’s pocket-money
found itself being spent in Kitty’s service. He bought her sweets,
flowers, little bottles of scent which she did not dare to use, because
scent always exasperated her father. Her mother had used it.

“Faugh! I won’t have these loose and filthy habits in my house.”

Once or twice she let Tod take her to a concert on the pier, going with
him quite publicly, but not advertising the adventure to the family. The
lad was very much in love with her, and behaved to her always like a
shrewd and big-hearted man. His boyish pride and delight in her would
have touched a few harder hearts than Kitty Flowerdew’s. There is no
doubt that she loved Tod in her shy and rather frightened way, liking
his sturdiness, his brown and intelligent head, and his eyes that always
looked at you as straight as the eyes of a dog, but she was afraid of
her father and her sister, and she was afraid of Edwin Rook.

“I say, how pretty you look.”

“Do I?”

“You’ve got such wonderful hair, Kitty.”

“I don’t think you ought to tell me I am pretty,” she would say, with a
secret and half guilty thrill.

“But it’s the truth. You are.”

Tod went to church with the Flowerdew family, and sat between Kitty and
her father. He had always loathed churchgoing, but even the dismal St.
Jude’s at Shaston was made mysterious and beautiful by Kitty’s presence.
He loved to kneel beside her, feel the soft rustle of her clothes, and
listen to the murmur of her voice when she joined in a public prayer. He
would cast furtive and devoted glances at her soft profile; she charmed
him particularly when her eyes were closed, and her long dark lashes
made sweet and gentle shadows on her cheeks. He almost forgot Mr.
Flowerdew, and the aggressive voice that insisted on reminding God that
he was a “mis-er-able sinner.”

An incident that occurred late in the autumn taught Tod to look at life
and Kitty Flowerdew with a new alertness. They had arranged to meet one
Saturday afternoon and to walk along the cliffs to Melling Gap. It was
one of those innocent little conspiracies so dear to the hearts of a boy
and a girl, and had Tod been challenged to account for it to the
Pharisees of Shaston he might have flared into that fighting mood that
dares prurience to come out into the open.

It was one of those tranquil autumn days and they were sitting on a
rough seat under a windblown thorn tree not far from the edge of the
cliff when a man, who was strolling along the path, stopped and looked
hard at them. He stood there at their backs for fully a minute, grinning
now and again, but not with pleasure. He was about to walk on again,
when Tod happened to look round and so discovered his presence.

“Hallo; it’s Rook!”

Kitty Flowerdew went white, and remained still and motionless for a
moment like a startled bird. Tod felt her shrink from him in a queer,
frightened way.

“Hallo, Rook.”

The shopman grinned maliciously at Tod.

“Thought you were cycling to Pentworth?”

“Did I say so?” asked Tod.

“Mind you don’t fall over that cliff after such a whopper.”

Tod stood up, with a red face, but Edwin Rook walked on.

“Tod——”

He glanced at Kitty and saw a blackness of fear in her soft eyes.

“He’ll tell, Tod.”

“Let him. What’s the harm?”

“Oh, you don’t understand, dear,” she said.

 Chapter Eight

Tod never knew when Edwin Rook blabbed of the affair at Melling Gap. He
did not see the man again till the Monday, and Rook went discreetly
about his business while Mr. Flowerdew was in the shop. There was a sale
at a house in Virginia Gardens, at eleven, and Mr. Flowerdew had decided
to attend it in quest of possible bargains.

Tod was extricating some books from under a mahogany sideboard when a
man’s hand struck him a resounding whack from behind.

“Hallo, Don Juan!”

Tod faced round. He had never known Rook to indulge in playfulness, and
there had been a vicious sting in that smack.

“Chuck that!”

“Well, Don Juan, how’s life?”

“What d’you mean?” said Tod.

Rook looked at him in a queer, mocking way, and began to rearrange some
china.

“Don’t be a young fool, that’s all,” he remarked suggestively.

Tod was getting red and combative.

“You keep your jokes to yourself, Rook.”

“Right-o, my lad, I shall. Why don’t you pick up a kid of fifteen;
that’s about your size.”

“Look here, you shut up,” said Tod, “don’t you say anything about Miss
Kitty Flowerdew.”

Rook turned on him with an insolent laugh, and bobbed a china cow up and
down in front of Tod’s face.

“Moo-moo, cocky! The boy’s on his dignity, by Jove! Why, you cuckoo,
she’s only practising on you.”

“She’s not,” said Tod fiercely. “She’s not that sort of girl.”

Mr. Rook addressed himself confidentially to the china cow.

“Hark at him, old girl. He knows all about you women. He’s a regular man
about town; Beautiful Bountiful Bertie, what! All done by kindness at
the age of eighteen.”

“I’m old enough to punch your head, anyhow,” said Tod.

And then Edwin Rook left him alone.

The man had all the cleverness of a mean beast who was jealous, and in
three days he had Tod helplessly and hopelessly enraged. The lad soon
discovered that there was some conspiracy between Lena Flowerdew and the
shopman. Rook had blabbed to the elder sister, and Miss Flowerdew had
told her father. And Tod saw that it was Kitty who was in disgrace. The
condemnation of that moral household was emptied upon her head, while
Tod was made to feel a silly young fool. The girl had her hair up; she
was old enough to know better.

But the true maliciousness of the two conspirators betrayed itself when
Eli Flowerdew happened to be out to meals. Lena hated her sister and
wanted to hurt her; Rook hated Tod and wanted to disgust the girl with
her lover by making the affair ridiculous and paltry.

“Going to do a little more practising to-day, dear? Why not take Mr.
Western to Melling Gap?”

Rook adopted a jocular tone and pretended to be very much afraid of Tod.

“Western’s a terrible fellow. I’d advise any girl to be careful of
Western. He’s a fascinator. He’ll be the Shah of Shaston, and you’ll
hear him boasting about it too.”

Tod, conscious of Kitty’s miserable and abashed face, and remembering
that he had heard loud voices, and had caught her rushing upstairs in
tears, did all that a shrewd boy could do by keeping the remnants of his
temper, and accepting the business as a joke. His hatred of Lena
Flowerdew was so fierce that he could not save himself from one or two
frank and deadly tilts at her, and perhaps he hurt her more bitterly
than he knew.

“Nice place, Melling Gap, Mr. Western. Suppose you take me there next
Saturday? Mr. Rook will look after Kitty.”

Tod was getting tired of the vulgarity of the thing.

“No, thank you,” he said.

“Why not?”

“I don’t like ugly women.”

Lena Flowerdew gave him one glare of the eyes, and laid that saying to
her sister’s credit.

Tod was alone in the room behind the shop that evening, sorting some
second-hand books that had been bought at a sale, when the door opened
and Kitty slipped in. They happened to be alone in the house for ten
minutes, and Kitty had waited for that chance.

“Tod——”

She stood there, downcast, dismayed, and he could see that she had been
weeping.

“Tod, don’t speak to Lena like that, dear, please.”

“Why not? And I couldn’t help it.”

“For my sake, don’t. You don’t know how horrible Lena can be. And
Father——”

She had more to tell him, but her heart failed her, as her courage
failed her in that moment of trial.

“You don’t understand, Tod, how miserable they can make me.”

Tod’s love could hold back no longer. Here was his most dear lady,
persecuted, humiliated, and all because of her kindness to him.

“Kitty! You know I love you, Kitty. I can’t stand them being such beasts
to you.”

He crossed over to her, his blue eyes alight, and tried to take her
hands, but she put them behind her suddenly, and began to weep anew.

“Tod, dear——”

“Why shouldn’t we be engaged? I’m eighteen. And there’s no one——”

“Don’t, Tod, oh—don’t—please,” she said brokenly. “You don’t
understand—and I—I can’t tell you. Father would be so angry, and they
would just mock at you.”

“I’m not afraid of that, dear.”

“It can’t be. And I have to ask you, Tod, not to look at me or talk to
me much. We can be just friends——”

“I see how it is,” he said sturdily, “they think I’m a silly kid, and
that I can’t be serious. If you would wait for me, Kitty.”

“I can’t, Tod, dear, I can’t. Father wouldn’t let me.”

“I’ll—I’ll make him,” but she fled out of the room and away up the dark
stairs, and Tod could hear her sobbing as she went.

Tod was puzzled, but he suppressed the hot chivalry of his youth and the
desire to do something heroic. The Flowerdews’ house was not a tilting
ground for heroism, and the more Tod pondered the matter, the less
sanguine he felt. Sir Galahad would have been met here with ridicule and
a shower of rotten eggs. The lad began to understand Kitty’s sensitive
dread and her shrinking from the malicious vulgarities of her sister’s
tongue.

Tod made up his mind to dissemble. If it helped Kitty and saved her from
further domestic storms, so much the better. He told himself that he
would wait. In three years he would have that £1,000, and could talk to
Mr. Eli Flowerdew as one man to another. And he found that his
self-suppression, cheerfully and shrewdly advertised, had a very
distinct influence upon the other members of the Flowerdew family. He
talked more to Lena Flowerdew and made it his business to pass her the
butter and the jam, and actually allowed her to take him out to a
tea-party at the Lagos’s who ran a big grocery shop in King William
Street.

Eli Flowerdew seemed to have made up his mind to approve of Tod, and Tod
encouraged that spirit of approval, even though it led him into the
dismal experience of being enrolled as a junior member of the local
Social Purity League. Eli Flowerdew took him to the men’s Wednesday
evening meetings in the Shaston Public Hall, where he listened to sacred
music as rendered by the Shaston Glee Singers, and heard sundry eloquent
gentlemen give flamboyant addresses upon civic and domestic virtue.

The whole crowd made Tod think of a lot of unwholesome people getting
into a stuffy corner and solemnly holding their noses because of the
smell. They were so drearily unhuman and self-complacent. Tod had a
feeling that they rather liked smells, and enjoyed holding their noses,
and gloating self-righteously over the iniquity of the other people’s
drains.

He asked Bod Twine about it, because he had come to regard that swarthy
young man as violently and savagely honest.

“I don’t see that it does anybody any good,” he argued.

“Course it doesn’t kid. It’s like telling a chap who’s healthy that he’s
got a worm. They like it, you know—makes ’em feel warm and good inside
like a glass of gin; they don’t want things to be different, not really.
Get outside; that’s my motto.”

“Outside what?”

“Why, out into the country, you pippin. There’s nothing wrong with the
sea, or the fields, or the open road, is there? And there’s nothing
wrong with a chap wanting to be in love with a girl. If so, what are we
here for? If a man tries to come the pious over me, I always think he
has got dirty feet. Say, when are you going to join our footer club?”

Twine had taught Tod to swim; he also taught him to box, and young
Western began to be one of the pets of the local football club. He had
shaped well as a half-back at Chipperton’s, and before Christmas he was
playing right-half for Shaston Town, and was beloved of the local crowd.
“Worry-’em-Western” was his nickname, and even Mr. Flowerdew approved.
It was an advertisement. “That’s young Western from Flowerdew’s.” The
commercial eye sees a placard everywhere, and pastes up a pious “poster”
whenever it looks publicly upon its God.

Yet even Clarence Todhunter Western had his worries. The Flowerdews
household had made inroads on his youth, and changed him from the
irresponsible boy to the young man who was finding life rather a queer
and puzzling business. His silent and restrained loyalty to Kitty made
him all the more jealous and observant. He began to notice that Edwin
Rook was always sneaking out of the shop and into the house when Eli
Flowerdew was not about. He noticed, too, that Rook’s eyes were for ever
watching Kitty during meals, but that she hardly ever looked at him in
return. Rook seemed to frighten her and to reduce her to silence.
Moreover, Miss Lena Flowerdew grew very snappy and sarcastic towards the
shopman, and Tod caught her watching him trying to ingratiate himself
with her sister. Miss Flowerdew’s face was never very pleasant or
amiable, but on those occasions Tod thought her the ugliest thing he had
ever seen.

Then Edwin Rook took to attending St. Jude’s church and sat in the
Flowerdew pew, displacing Tod, who was pushed to the far end by the
pillar. Tod was annoyed but contemptuous. He had a healthy scorn for
Rook, a fellow who wore glasses, never played any games, and was, Tod
suspected, a physical coward. Tod, the romanticist, blindly refused to
accept Rook as a serious rival, which wild faith of his betrayed how
ignorant he was of the real inwardness of the Flowerdew family.

But it was the shop and the business that were puzzling the sportsman in
Tod. You bought as cheaply as you could and you sold as dearly as you
could; that seemed fairly obvious and reasonable, and yet Tod was not
satisfied. When some old woman received three and sixpence for an
article which Mr. Flowerdew sold to a wealthy stockbroker’s wife three
days later for five guineas, the gap seemed too greedy. Tod began to see
a good deal of this buying and selling, and it left him with an uneasy
and guilty feeling. Also it became very plain to him that in a shop you
were not supposed to tell the truth. You romanced, you exaggerated—in
short, you lied.

“Those candlesticks, madam? Yes, we picked them up only a week ago and
at a fancy price. They won’t remain with us very long; the first
connoisseur who sees them——”

Now Tod knew that these candlesticks had been bought at a sale three
months ago, and that Mr. Flowerdew had given exactly seven shillings for
them. The lady, a visitor to Shaston, bought them for three pounds ten.

Moreover, for subtle reason known only to himself, Edwin Rook set
himself to try and debauch and commercialise Tod. He was cynical,
jocular, and facetious, and he took a delight in airing all the trade
cynicisms, and flaunting them in front of Tod’s eyes.

“You’ve got to watch your customer, my lad. If it’s a ‘regular’ and one
who knows a bit, go easy. If it’s a stranger, why then you can do a bit
of fibbing and push the price.”

“Be jolly careful of duchessy looking ladies in fine clothes. They don’t
swallow things easily. They can be really nasty if they think you have
cheated them.”

“Always back down and eat dirt if you are found out.”

“Expert knowledge! What do we want to know for? Why, it’s the whole
game. You can score off the people who don’t know, get ’em to take
sixpence for something that’s worth a quid. That’s business.”

“Sentiment. There’s no sentiment in a shop. Only oil, my boy, oil.”

Tod disliked Rook all the more thoroughly for these cynicisms of his. At
first, he did not take them too seriously. Rook was a glib talker,
rather pleased with his own cleverness, but as the weeks went by, Tod
began to realise that Rook was telling him the truth. The discovery
depressed Tod very seriously. He felt that he was getting mixed up in a
sordid sort of game, a game in which your main endeavour was to cheat.
Tod called it “cheating” to himself, quite frankly and with no
reservations. If you tried to foul in the football field, the referee
dropped on you, and the crowd, if it was not too bigoted a crowd,
hooted.

And then came the incident of that book.

Mr. Flowerdew had been present at one of those “sales of necessity.” A
doctor had died and left a widow and three children, and a small balance
at the bank. Mr. Flowerdew bought a few lots at the sale, and among them
an odd number of books that cost him thirteen shillings. Tod was sorting
these books two days after the sale when he came across a queer old
volume that sent him running for Messrs. Porteous’s price catalogue of
second-hand books.

There seemed no doubt about it. That particular book was valued at
fifteen guineas.

Tod, full of enthusiasm, announced the fact to Rook.

“I say, there’s been a mistake. We have bought a valuable book mixed up
in a lot of rubbish.”

“Where’s the mistake?” asked the shopman with cynical sangfroid.

“But the governor only paid thirteen bob for the lot, and this book is
priced at fifteen guineas. The people who sold it didn’t know. We shall
have to send it back.”

“Do what?” squealed Mr. Rook.

“Why, send it back. It wouldn’t be fair or sporting to keep it. I don’t
suppose Flowerdew knew when he bought the lot.”

Rook stared at Tod, and then gave a queer laugh.

“Suggest it, old chap. If the auctioneers didn’t do their work properly,
and the governor had the luck——”

“You mean you’d keep it and say nothing?”

“You soft egg!” said Mr. Rook.

Tod was challenged. He said nothing more to Rook, but waited for Mr.
Flowerdew to give a judgment of Solomon. Rook was out when the interview
occurred, and he missed one of the most cynical comedies that had ever
been played in Mr. Flowerdew’s shop.

“Excuse me, sir, but you bought a valuable book the other day by
mistake.”

Mr. Flowerdew was totting up some figures in a notebook, and he looked
at Tod with interested approval.

“What’s that, Western? You are learning to use your wits, are you? What
about it?”

Tod explained, and Eli Flowerdew listened with fatherly satisfaction
until Tod made the extraordinary suggestion that the book should be
returned.

Mr. Flowerdew’s thin lips slipped back over his yellow teeth.

“Say that again, Western?”

Tod said it again.

And then Eli Flowerdew gave Tod a solemn and biblical description of
what business was and how it should be conducted. He expatiated on the
necessity for loyalty between master and assistant, and how that
his—Mr. Flowerdew’s—interest was Tod’s. He trounced Tod’s sentimental
obtuseness, and assured him that commerce and civilisation would be
impossible if people behaved in the way Tod had suggested.

“You have got to get the business spirit, Western,” he said grimly, “if
you want to rise in this world. Competition doesn’t say ‘After you,
please.’ If you hold yourself responsible for other peoples mistakes,
you are a fool, young man. Don’t you mix sentiment and business. And
don’t let me hear any more of this nonsense.”

 Chapter Nine

Towards the end of the winter Tod began to be very restless. Kitty
Flowerdew had been away for a month, and there had been nothing to
relieve the ugliness of the house in Ponsonby Place; Tod was growing a
little weary of serving like a boy Jacob for his Rachael. Edwin Rook’s
cynicisms, and a more intimate knowledge of Eli Flowerdew’s methods of
making money, had killed Tod’s interest in the business. He began to
hate the shop and everything in it, almost as much as he hated the
atmosphere of the Flowerdew house. Some of those queer sayings of his
father’s cropped up like sharp flints under his feet.

“I’m not a penny-in-the-slot machine.” “This country with its
penny-three-farthing soul!” “Pray publicly to God, and cheat your
neighbour!”

There were elements of rebellion stirring in Tod. He lost some of his
frankness, and grew rather morose and thoughtful, and he showed a new
fierceness towards Edwin Rook. He began to take long prowls after
closing time, and forgathered with Bob Twine and two or three other
young men who liked the rush of the sea air on their faces. Tod found
that it was agreed that life as a shopkeeper’s assistant was doomed to
suck all the virility out of a man’s soul. Yet these rebels had no
alternative paths of adventure to suggest, save joining the Army of
running after girls. Mossup, a mild, flaxen-haired, and rather serious
soul who wore spectacles, hoped to attain liberty by acquiring a shop of
his own.

“You’re welcome to it, old man,” said Twine; “you ain’t rogue enough.
You’ll be dodging bankruptcy till it makes you grey. I’m turning
Socialist.”

Tod happened to mention the word “Socialist” to Edwin Rook, and was
puzzled by the shopman’s slimy and secretive smile.

“So you know some Socialists, do you, Western?”

“I know one,” said Tod.

The information was passed on in a casual and inconsidered manner to Mr.
Flowerdew. Tod did not suspect it then, but Rook had decided that
Western was in his way, and Rook set about edging Tod overboard with the
methodical shrewdness of the born sneak and coward. He dropped hints
that Tod was getting into loose company and loafing about the town at
night. He even hinted at “girls.” All girls, save his own, were anathema
to Eli Flowerdew.

Two days before Kitty Flowerdew was expected home, Rook laid a
deliberate trap for Tod. A couple of packing-cases had arrived from
Birmingham, and Rook called Tod to help him unpack them.

“One of our confidential jobs, my son. Now what do you think of that
brass door-knocker?”

“It looks old,” said Tod.

They unpacked a pretty selection of mock antiques in
metal—candlesticks, tinder-boxes, sconces, and the like. Rook chatted
jocularly, and in a cynically confidential way to Tod.

“The fools will have old things, so we have to get ’em.”

“Aren’t these old?” Tod asked.

“Fakes, reproductions, the whole lot of them.”

“But we don’t sell them as old?”

“Oh, don’t we! You’ll sell tons before you’ve done.”

Tod was kneeling, and with two hands on the edge of the case he faced
Rook across it.

“I’m dashed if I will. It’s not honest.”

“Just tell that to the boss.”

“I will, if he asks me. And what’s more, if I see you trying to sell the
stuff as old—I’ll—I’ll——”

“Hoity-toity!” said Mr. Rook.

But he had manœuvered Tod into a desperate position, and he made haste
to let Mr. Flowerdew appreciate Tod’s pose of defiance and its
possibilities.

“Can I have a few words with you, sir?”

“What’s the matter, Rook?”

“It’s about Western, sir. I don’t like playing the sneak, but I’m
worried about Western. Besides, I’m thinking of my loyalty to you and
the business.”

“Come into my room.”

With a certain air of diffidence and unwillingness Rook described Tod’s
rebellious attitude and the threats he had used.

“Of course, I was teasing the chap really, but he’s likely to be a bit
of a problem, sir. He doesn’t understand being loyal, and I think
Western’s getting rather wild.”

“Confound that boy!” said Mr. Flowerdew.

He walked up and down, cleared his throat, and then gave the assistant
an enigmatic look.

“Not a word about this.”

“Not a word, sir.”

“I’ll think it over. We don’t want a row, and a boy with a reckless
tongue might make trouble. Besides, I don’t want to be hard.”

He smiled, and moistened his lips.

“But I appreciate your attitude, Rook. You are always backing me up;
making the business a personal affair.”

Rook gave a simper and appeared embarrassed.

“It’s a fact, sir. I owe you my job. And I’ll admit I’m ambitious.”

“No harm in that, man; ambition in you suits me.”

And then Rook plunged and showed both his heart and his hand. Flowerdew
listened to him with a slight frown, and the stare of a shrewd and
unwinking bird.

“So that’s it, Rook?”

“Don’t take it as impertinence, sir. You have been very kind to me, and
I’m human. And I’m a steady sort of chap; I mean to get on, and if Miss
Kitty——”

Flowerdew patted Rook on the shoulder.

“I’ll think it over, Rook. I’m not so sure you wouldn’t make me a very
sound partner, some day.”

Tod was destined to strike one of those adventurous and pugilistic
passages in his life before he ceased from the Flowerdew household.
Romance caught him, romance and a sudden glimpse of Kitty, Kitty unseen
for a whole month, and drifting in like the spring with an armful of
daffodils. Perhaps it was not wholly by chance that Tod happened to be
there to rush out and carry in her box. This sudden meeting surprised
them both into a flutter of naturalness, a quick and dear delight in
each other that couldn’t be hid.

“Tod——”

He was conscious of her as a creature with brown, shimmering eyes, and a
quick rich colour. She gave him her hand, and he held it a moment. She
looked a little wild and shy, like a bird.

“It’s been such a long month,” he said.

“Oh, Tod, I wish——”

But he never heard what her wish was, for Miss Flowerdew intervened, and
Tod saw Kitty’s happy face close up like a flower.

“Thought you were coming by the 2.30.”

“No, by the four o’clock, Lena.”

“Look at those messy flowers; you have let the stalks dribble all down
your dress.”

“Have I? Well, it doesn’t matter.”

Tod was playing the strong man, struggling up the narrow stairs with
Kitty’s trunk, and telling himself that he was not going to stand it any
longer. It was somebody’s business to rescue Kitty, and Tod decided that
that somebody was himself.

He heard voices that night; Mr. Flowerdew’s voice very hard and
emphatic, much the same voice that Flowerdew used at the meetings of the
Social Purity League. He was speaking to Kitty in the drawing-room and
Tod left his bedroom door open in order to listen. He could not
distinguish what was said, but he could hear Kitty piping occasional
notes like a pathetic and appealing thrush. The duologue ended with a
lengthy, harsh and emphatic grumble from Mr. Flowerdew’s voice. Tod
heard Kitty go to her room with the flutter of a bird escaping from a
rough hand. Later Tod fancied that he could catch the sound of weeping.

Two days passed, wrathful, volcanic days, with Tod ready to erupt. Kitty
went about the house with the strange air of a doomed thing in disgrace.
She hardly spoke, left her food almost untouched, and her frightened
eyes avoided Tod’s. Tod noticed that Rook had a peculiarly sleek and
bland look, like a cat in a good temper. Miss Flowerdew ignored both
Rook and her sister. Old Flowerdew read the paper at meals, and made
occasional snapping sounds such as “Butter,” “More tea,” “Pass the
bread.”

Tod had no business in the garden on that fateful evening just when dusk
was falling. From the back-room window he glimpsed Kitty walking slowly
down the path towards the shrubbery where a few scraggy lilacs were
pushing out green buds and making the euonymus and the holly look black
and fusty. Rook appeared to have left the place, and Tod entered the
garden by the passage that led through from the street. There was a
ramshackle rustic summer-house in the middle of the shrubbery, one of
those bourgeois and urban touches that make the real countryman smile.
Tod saw Kitty enter the summer-house, her hands hanging limply, her
brown head bowed down.

Tod was half-way across the garden when he heard the sound of a
struggle, and Kitty’s voice appealing with a fear that feared to
protest.

“Don’t, oh, don’t!”

“Please let go, please, Mr. Rook.”

Tod went in at the gallop like a true knight errant. He found Rook
holding Kitty in his arms, and thrusting that ugly mouth of his in
search of her averted face. His arms had pinned hers to her body, and
she was trying to free them when Tod interfered.

“Let go, you cad!”

Tod never forgot the change that came over Rook’s face. At one moment he
had been a greedy, gloating thing about to suck the honey that he had
long desired; the next he was looking balked, furious, sheepish, and a
little afraid.

“You dirty cad! Come outside.”

“Look here, young Western, boys aren’t wanted. You clear out.”

“Kitty,” said Tod, “will you please let me——”

“Oh, Tod, dear——”

She slipped out of the summer-house, soft, pathetic, ineffectual, and
Tod faced Edwin Rook.

“Come outside.”

Rook did a thin bluster.

“Look here, kid, I don’t want to hurt you. It’s no business of yours if
I’m engaged to Miss Kitty Flowerdew.”

“It’s a lie,” said Tod, and went in after the rat since the rat would
not come to him.

Rook was a weedy man and no fighter, though he patted and hooked at Tod
like an angry cat. Tod smashed his glasses, split his lip, and bundled
him twice against the rotten wall of the summer-house. Rook closed with
the lad, and they were in an angry and dishevelled tangle on the floor
when Mr. Eli Flowerdew stormed in. Tod was uppermost, with a good grip
in Rook’s collar, and riding his man victoriously in spite of the jerks
and contortions of the shopman’s legs.

“What the—— Get up, Western. Get up, Rook. What the devil——”

Old Flowerdew was very angry, and his voice rasped like a saw.

“The boy’s mad,” said Rook, picking himself up, panting, “he came at me
like an——”

“I’ve thrashed you, that’s all,” said Tod, “for being a dirty cad.”

Flowerdew caught Tod by the collar.

“Silence, you young savage. What do you mean by——”

“Mean?” said Tod, looking quite ready to deal with Mr. Flowerdew as he
had dealt with Rook, “he’d insulted Kitty.”

Mr. Flowerdew let go of Tod as though the lad were hot iron.

“Oh, that’s it, is it! I see. Savage jealousy, I suppose. And may I ask
what it has to do with you, Mr. Tod Western?”

He sneered down at Tod with grizzled and grinning irony.

“I suppose the man who is going to marry my daughter has a right to be a
little—affectionate. You are too big for your boots, my boy. Go
upstairs to bed. I’ll talk to you like a father to-morrow.”

All the fine fury seemed to fall out of Tod’s chivalry. He gave Mr.
Flowerdew a blank and bewildered look, put his hands in his pockets, and
disappeared.

Tod passed a Byronic and very miserable night. It was as though some
Eastern giant had reached down a great hand, lifted the world’s lid, and
shown Tod all sorts of strange secrets and knavish devices. Tod
understood the Flowerdew household as he had never understood it before.
He remembered that saying of Bob Twine’s that Eli Flowerdew had driven
his wife to suicide, and that Kitty was the daughter of her mother. Rook
wanted Kitty, he had wanted her all the while; Tod understood that now,
and why Rook hated him. But Kitty did not want Edwin Rook, and the
inference was obvious. Eli Flowerdew was giving the girl to Rook,
compelling her, even against her great loathing, to surrender herself to
this lover, this cold, weedy beast with his bland “selling” manner, and
his venomous tongue.

And why?

The answer to that question stuck in Tod’s throat, but he was
constrained to swallow it. Rook was a useful man to Eli Flowerdew. He
knew all the chicanery of the business, and practised it like a man who
loved it. It was possible that he knew so much that he had to be part of
the family, part of the conspiracy.

Tod tossed to and fro in bed, bit his pillow, and schemed all sorts of
mad and impossible schemes for the rescuing of Kitty. He felt that he
loved her as he had never loved her before, since he had seen her
twisting in Rook’s arms. Rook shouldn’t have her! He would see her
to-morrow and appeal to her to run away with him. Yes, and on what?
Tod’s shrewdness saved him from being a mere sentimental and adventurous
young fool. He knew that he had about three shillings in his trousers
pocket, no very noble purse for an eloping couple.

No, he would be perfectly straight with old Flowerdew. He would stand up
to him like a man and say, “I love Kitty, and I think Kitty loves me.
I’m young, but I mean to get on. You can’t give Kitty to Rook.”

Yes, and that would entail his staying in the business, and swallowing
all the humbug and the trickery. What a slimy and clever devil that chap
Rook was! Tod realised that he had been driven into a corner, and that
though he had punched Rook’s head, the fellow had him tied up like a
fly. And the beastliness of it all, old Flowerdew of the Social Purity
League, buying a man’s loyalty by giving him the body of his daughter!

Tod went downstairs next morning, red-eyed, and prepared to fight, but
the Flowerdew household surprised and nonplussed him, and smothered the
attack. Kitty did not appear at breakfast; she was locked in her room,
and Tod did not have a glimpse of her that morning. Lena Flowerdew had
never been so smiling or so pleasant; Tod had buttered eggs with his
bacon, and three lumps of sugar in his tea. Miss Flowerdew’s
friendliness held him mute. He did not understand it, or guess that she
was gloating over his trouncing of Edwin Rook. He had not heard that
gibe of hers, “Nice sort of man you are, to be licked by a mere boy.”
But Mr. Flowerdew himself was the surprise of the morning. It was the
Sabbath Flowerdew that appeared at the breakfast-table, solemn,
sententious, oracular, dressed in its Sunday clothes. It was polite and
patriarchal towards Tod, a figure that took him by the elbow and marched
him into an atmosphere of surplices and “mis-er-able sinners.”

“Now, Western, if you please.”

Tod found himself in the Flowerdew drawing-room, sitting opposite to Mr.
Eli Flowerdew, and listening with a feeling of helplessness to arguments
that he could not answer.

“I feel that I have a grave responsibility—a fatherly responsibility
towards you, Western. The fact is, you are not suited to a business such
as mine. You appear to have picked up a lot of queer ideas from
somewhere, ideas that won’t work in this world. You have got to learn to
be a practical business man. Now I don’t want to be unkind to you, my
boy, but I have felt for a long while that you don’t suit me, and
perhaps my business doesn’t suit you.”

Tod was nonplussed.

“Some of it doesn’t go down, sir,” he said gloomily, “but I wanted to
talk to you about——”

Mr. Flowerdew caught him up.

“About my daughter. Exactly. Now let’s talk this over like men, Western.
How old are you?”

“Just eighteen.”

“And my daughter is a year older than you are. And when do you expect to
be able to keep a wife?”

Tod fidgeted on his chair.

“I shall have a £1,000 when I’m twenty-one. I mean to get on, sir. And
she doesn’t, I mean, Kitty doesn’t——”

Mr. Flowerdew smiled at Tod.

“Western, my boy, you are very green and inexperienced. You think that
my daughter doesn’t want to marry Mr. Rook. You mustn’t jump at
conclusions in this world, and I may as well tell you that you did
yourself a very bad service last night.”

“What do you mean, sir?”

“My daughter was pained, disgusted, Western. You whom we had treated as
one of the family, raising a vulgar brawl and assaulting my daughter’s
fiancé! Women are queer creatures, Western; they often pretend to
dislike a man when they really want him. My daughter is much more
attached to Mr. Rook than you imagine.”

“If she would tell me so,” blurted Tod.

Mr. Flowerdew sat forward in his chair, and wagged an emphatic
forefinger.

“Listen to me, young man; do you think I am going to have my girl’s
future happiness compromised by a wild boy who isn’t old enough to know
his own mind? Certainly not, Western, certainly not. I have duties as a
father. Mr. Rook has earned a position of trust. I know Rook—I believe
in Rook; I say, ‘There is a steady fellow who can give a girl a good
Christian home.’ And Mr. Rook is going to marry my daughter. Put all
that nonsense out of your head, my boy. I have had you in here and
talked to you like a man of the world and a father, but of course you
realise that you can’t stay any longer in my house.”

“I realise that,” said Tod.

“Very well, then; that’s a sensible answer. There are plenty of chances
for a steady fellow in a town like this. I’m quite ready to help you,
Western, or you can go back to your uncle for a while.”

“I’ll go back to my uncle,” said Tod.

And that was the end of it. Mr. Flowerdew had his gig out that afternoon
and drove Tod and his box over to Burnt Farm. He had a somewhat lengthy
interview with Uncle Remus, while Tod idled about the garden and
listened in a wild and rebel mood to a blackbird’s singing.

He kept out of the way till Mr. Flowerdew had gone, and then went in to
face his uncle.

“You darned young fool!” was all that the farmer said to him.

 Chapter Ten

A month later Tod Western returned to Shaston-on-Sea and entered the
establishment of John Snape, chemist, in King William Street.

Various people had had a hand in launching this new adventure.

Tod himself had initiated the idea. Balked in a second attempt to
persuade Uncle Remus to let him become a student of medicine, he had
piqued himself with pleasant memories of chemists’ shops, liking the
scent and the cleanness of them and the idea of handing out mysterious
cures to suffering humanity. He imagined that he would find nothing of
the Flowerdew fraudulencies in such a place. Moreover, he had not
forgotten Kitty Flowerdew. He still had dreams of indulging in some
piratical excursion into the Flowerdew household, and of rescuing Kitty
from the clutches of Edwin Rook.

When Tod made his suggestion to Uncle Remus, Uncle Remus said “Humph,
want to mix physic and sell soap, do you? I suppose there’ll always be
fools who want to drink physic and wash. Toothbrushes, too.” Uncle Remus
never used one.

Dr. Vance was consulted, and agreed that the idea was a sound one. He
happened to know John Snape of Shaston, Snape had once dispensed for
him, and he wrote to the chemist and spoke highly of Tod. Mr. Snape
suggested an interview, and confessed that he needed an intelligent
assistant to serve in the shop and learn dispensing. Mr. Snape
interviewed Tod, and then made the further suggestion that Tod should
“see how he liked it” for six months, and that if he suited Mr. Snape,
he could then enter on a three years’ apprenticeship and train for the
examinations of the Pharmaceutical Society. The reference to the
“Pharmaceutical Society” rather impressed Uncle Remus; it sounded so
respectable and Mesopotamian; besides, it was backed and reinforced by
those indispensable articles—toothbrushes and soap.

So Tod went to Mr. Snape, and was found a bed-sitting room, food, and a
smell of domesticity in the house of a certain Mrs. Budd. Mrs. Budd was
a good soul, and she and Tod clasped hands at once. She had a little
house in Laburnum Road, not far from Shaston Gardens, a little
cabbage-rose of a house, buxom and easy, like Mrs. Budd herself. Mrs.
Budd was a widow, and her children were all launched on the world.

“You make yourself at ’ome, dear,” she said to Tod; “I always believe in
plenty of butter on the toast.”

Mr. Snape was not quite so obvious and easy as Mrs. Budd. He was sandy
and pink, and wore gold-rimmed spectacles. Tod thought him something of
a dandy, and he was so polite that you wondered whether he was trying to
be sarcastic. There was an air of prosperity and rosy health about John
Snape and his business, and he looked like a good advertisement for
somebody’s shaving soap, or Rumbelow’s Iron Pills.

Snape gave Tod some shrewd advice and handed him over to a middle-aged
assistant, a laconic, earthy-faced man named Wisdon. Wisdon had a short
brown beard and a moustache; he wore a dicky and removable cuffs, cuffs
that he covered each morning with the clean white pieces of paper that
were used for wrapping up bottles. His black coat was shiny at the
elbows, and he had some special concoction for dealing with this
shininess, but it never grew any less shiny. Wisdon’s inspiration was
the word “economy.” He economised in razors and shaving soap, in his
laundry, in his conversation, in his food. There was something rather
melancholy and pathetic and suppressed about the man; he always looked
tired, and he had a perennial cold in his head.

Tod nicknamed Wisdon “Mr. Any-think-else.” That was the final phrase he
used to each customer on handing over the customer’s purchases.

The other assistant was a young man named Cook. He was a certified
dispenser, and Tod disliked him from the first day. Cook was a shallow,
cocky sarcastic young gentleman who never troubled to hide his contempt
for the snuffy and much worried Wisdon. Tod noticed that Mr. Snape
treated Cook with consideration, whereas he bullied and hectored the
older man, and Wisdom took it like a quiet and depressed old dog.

Tod began by liking Mr. Snape’s shop. He liked the smell of it, the
airiness and cleanness, the rows and rows of mysterious bottles, the
glass cases full of scent and boxes of soap, and the hundred and one
articles connected with a well washed and well groomed body. Even the
phraseology of the craft fascinated him, all that quaint Latinity that
smelt of the Pharmacopœia and suggested old world herbalists and the
romance of alchemy. There was no dust in Mr. Snape’s shop. Everything
glistened. And there was so much of everything. Drawers full of new
white toothbrushes, of chip and cardboard pill and ointment boxes,
stacks of bottles, oceans of soap, sponges by the hundred, scissors,
rows of hot water bottles, and unimagined wealth of all sorts of scent,
toilet powders, manicure sets; cohorts of combs, ointments, razors,
shaving-brushes, siphons of potash and soda water. Then there were all
the patent medicines and proprietary preparations. Tod was less
interested in these, and perhaps from the first he viewed them with a
slight distrust. They advertised themselves rather too blatantly, and
had not the restrained solemnity of the tinctures, extracts, and
infusions on the dispensing shelves. You could look up the latter in the
Pharmacopœia, that Doomsday Book of the drugs, and find out just what
they were and what they pretended to be. The patent medicines were
rather like women; they wore petticoats, and led you to believe that the
world’s most wonderful mystery was hidden upon their persons.

Tod’s thoughts were constantly fluttering round that house in Ponsonby
Place. He made a point of walking past it in the evening, but he had
never a glimpse of Kitty, and he had no desire to meet Mr. Flowerdew or
his elder daughter. They might suspect him of some possible act of
piracy, and take precaution against it. Tod was cautious.

One Sunday evening he went to St. Jude’s Church, and sitting in one of
the back seats, saw Rook and Kitty come in together and take their
places in the familiar pew. Tod shadowed them home to Ponsonby Place.
They walked arm and arm, and Tod felt savage and depressed.

Chance gave him the opportunity he had so long desired. He had been
wandering along the beach in an aimless sort of way, and was watching
some men winding up the bathing machines under the White Cliff, when he
became aware of a girl sitting on the shingle, with a book in her lap. A
big hat hid her face, but she raised her head as Tod passed, and the
face was the face of Kitty.

Tod turned straight towards her, trying to convince himself that it was
not tears that he saw in her eyes. She sat there, staring up at him, as
though she had lost both the power of speech and of movement. It struck
Tod that she looked older, and that she did not flush in her old
sensitive and girlish way.

“May I speak to you, Kitty?”

“Oh, please——” she began.

Tod sat down beside her, and saw her glance towards the town.

“Edwin’s coming, any moment.”

Then Tod rebelled, and talked all sorts of wild and tender nonsense that
brought her to the edge of tears. There was no rebellion to be roused in
Kitty Flowerdew. She had accepted the system, was terrorised by system,
and her poor scared face made Tod feel a beast.

“Oh, don’t, please, Tod. I’ve promised to marry Edwin Rook. It was
father’s wish.”

And suddenly Tod grew very gentle and tender.

“Poor Kitty,” he said; “I don’t want to hurt you, dear. But I wish I
thought you were happy.”

“Of course I am going to be happy,” she protested; “Father has been so
good to Edwin; he is a partner now, and we are going to have a dear
little house near St. Jude’s.”

She showed Tod her ring, a diamond and sapphire hoop.

“Isn’t it pretty?”

“Very,” said Tod, wishing he could kill Rook.

“And we are buying the furniture, such nice furniture.”

She was talking defensively, holding Tod and her own emotion at arm’s
length, and all the while she kept looking anxiously towards the town.
Tod’s heart smote him. He understood at last the hopelessness and the
helplessness of Kitty, and that he was only torturing her by persuading
her to cry for the moon.

“I hope you will be very happy,” he said.

“Thank you, Tod.”

“I think I ought to be going now.”

Her look of relief was obvious to him, and with a tragic glance at her
he got up and went. But he felt very miserable and very bitter, and
betaking himself to Rose Cottage he poured his bitterness into the lap
of Mrs. Budd, not as a confessional, but as the vague yet fiery
discontent of a young man balked of his desire.

Mrs. Budd was a comfortable opportunist. She had rounded off all her
angles, and if the world bumped her it bumped against something soft and
elastic. She had learnt to circumvent by yielding, to flow round
obstructions, to be comfortable even when you had to sit six aside in a
third-class carriage.

“Don’t go looking for trouble, my dear. You’ll get plenty of it in the
world without looking. What, you say people cheat? Of course they cheat,
if it pays ’em. Not all, mind you, but most.”

“It ought to be altered,” said Tod; “my father used to say that anyone
who made too much money was a rogue.”

“Well, my dear, if you take that line, you will be up against the
elephant. The elephant will tread on you. Much better give it buns.”

“You are a coward, Mrs. Budd.”

“No, I’m not, my dear; I’ve used my eyes and my ears. Unless you’re a
gent, it pays to be polite. Never you be too clever. Clever people ain’t
popular. Soft soap and stoopidity; that’s the stuff that gets you along
comfortable in this world.”

Tod remained in a rebel mood, and consorted more than ever with Bob
Twine and Mossup and one or two other young men who did not like life as
it was. Twine roared with laughter when Tod gave a rather shame-faced
account of how he had been bundled out of the Flowerdew household.

“What! Oh, you pippin! Wanted to send that book back, and to tell
everybody that all the worm-holes in the shop were fakes! No wonder that
chap Rook got a strangle-hold on you.”

“Well, wasn’t I right?” flared Tod.

“Too right, kid. I wish half the world was half as right as you. Suppose
there’ll be a second suicide in the Flowerdew family?”

But Tod’s fierce face warned him to gibe no farther.

“Sorry, kid. I’m a coarse beast sometimes. And how do you like old
Snape?”

“I don’t know yet,” said Tod.

“You and I’ll end in the army, kid; that’s what will happen to us.
P’r’aps we’ll emigrate. And there’s poor old Mossup wanting to marry a
girl at the glove counter and shove his little capital into a draper’s
shop in Pondicherry Street. Poor old blighter!”

“Aren’t you going to marry, Bob?” Tod asked.

“Me marry! Not as things are, my boy, thank you. That’s the last knot
the blasted system ties on you. I’m not going to make my woman eat
dirt.”

“My father married, and he didn’t eat dirt,” said Tod.

“No, kid; I dare say he was a better man than I am. I’m the sort of chap
that must have a girl. P’r’aps you’re too young, old man, but you’ll
find out in time. Let’s have a swim to-night.”

“All right,” said Tod.

As to John Snape’s, it was a serious improvement on Mr. Flowerdew’s,
yet, as Tod had said, he wanted to see more of it before he gave it his
approval. Tod had inherited his father’s love of looking facts straight
in the face. There are quite a number of people who look Truth in the
face and then make great haste to get away round the nearest corner. Tod
was not like that. He stood and stared till he was satisfied, and if he
did not like what he saw he said so. Most boys are such irresponsible
creatures, absorbed in the green springtide of their own youth, that
they accept the crookedness and oddities of life as a jest, a suit of
motley that does not matter, but Tod had the uncomfortable knack of
asking questions and of remaining unsatisfied until they were answered.

Now “Mr. Any-think-else” had a grubby little book that he used to poke
his nose into at odd moments. It always lay handy on the shelf below the
desk, and Tod grew curious about that book. He got hold of it one
afternoon and found that it was called “How to be Your Own Doctor.” Tod
was interested. Perhaps Old Wisdon had ambitions and dreams of entering
the great profession!

A snuffy old lady in a black bonnet and dress all covered with crape
toddled into the shop the same afternoon and became engaged in a
mumbling and confidential conversation with Mr. Wisdon. Both Mr. Snape
and Cook were out of the shop, and Tod was wrapping up some bottles in
the dispensing department.

He stood still and listened.
