“My daughter’s got a pain in the chest,” said the old lady, “and she
coughs something terrible. I’ve rubbed her with turps, and she’s ’ad
some of that lung tonic, but it ’asn’t done her any good.”

“Where’s the pain?” asked Wisdon in a confidential voice; “in the front
or the side?”

“Just about the breast-bone.”

“Does she expectorate?”

“What’s that?”

“Spit phlegm.”

“Yes, frothy stuff.”

“Doesn’t perspire at night, does she?”

“Don’t think so. She hasn’t said.”

“I expect it’s a slight attack of bronchitis. There’s been a lot dust
blowing about, and an east wind. Suppose you try a bottle of our
cough-cure.”

“Is it good?”

“Works like magic. It’s modelled on the prescription of a leading London
doctor.”

“I’ll try a bottle. How much it is?”

“Half a crown.”

The old lady paid her half-crown, dropped the bottle of Snape’s Cough
Cure into a string-bag and toddled off. Tod, emerging from the
dispenser’s corner, began to ask “Mr. Any-think-else” ingenious and
interested questions.

“Didn’t know you knew anything about doctoring, Mr. Wisdon.”

“Oh, didn’t you?”

“Do you get it all out of that little book?”

Wisdon gave Tod a worried look.

“It’s a very good book,” he said.

“How do you know the old lady’s daughter had bronchitis?”

“By the symptoms.”

“But mightn’t she have consumption or something?”

“She might, of course.”

“When my mother had a cough,” said Tod, “Dr. Vance listened to her chest
with a thing he called a steffescope. You could hear all the bubbles and
clicks, he said, and all that was going on.”

“Dare say he could,” said Mr. Wisdon, showing symptoms of being bored
with the subject.

“Don’t you think the woman ought to see a doctor, Mr. Wisdon?”

And then the patient, much enduring assistant turned on him with sudden
wrath.

“Look ’ere, don’t you ask so many questions.”

Tod liked old Wisdon, and had no idea of hurting him.

“Sorry,” he said. “I wanted to know—how——”

“You want to know too much, young Western; the less you think about
things in this rotten world the better it’ll be for your peace of mind,
and don’t you forget it.”

Tod had never seen “Mr. Any-think-else” so ruffled and combative and
upset about something. He banged bottles back in their places, kicked a
drawer to, and began to dust the counter with furious energy.

“Think I like it, do you? I have my orders, and I carry ’em out. You
can’t be saucy on eighty pounds a year.”

Then he grew suddenly afraid and went down the shop to make sure that he
and Tod were alone.

“But I’m not saying anything against it. I’m better off here than I was
before. You have to take life as you find it, Western, and don’t ask too
many questions. People don’t like it.”

“You mean Mr. Snape wouldn’t like it?”

“Look here”—and then a customer came into the shop and Mr. Wisdon
escaped from Tod’s inquisition.

Tod’s eyes had been opened, and in the future he placed quite a
different construction on those confidential little interviews that were
always taking place in Mr. Snape’s shop. He saw Cook studying a book
like Mr. Wisdon’s. He began to understand that the fatherly interest
John Snape took in certain customers was not a casual asking as to their
health and a polite passing of the time of day. Mr. Snape talked in a
subdued voice to mothers with babies. There were occasions when he took
sheepish-looking men into his back room and served them himself with an
air of sleek mystery. Tod wondered what Mr. Snape gave these gentlemen.
He had the curiosity to note the particular drawer from which Mr. Snape
took what he wanted. Tod discovered later that the drawer contained
small glass syringes.

One Thursday afternoon when business seemed very stagnant Cook hailed
Tod.

“Here, Western, I’ve got a job for you.”

“What is it?” said Tod.

“You can fill up these bottles with water, cork ’em, dry ’em, and paste
on those labels. Our stock’s getting low.”

Cook had a couple of biggish bottles and a measuring-glass on the
dispensing counter, and a whole phalanx of eight-ounce bottles ranged in
two neat rows. He began to measure some brown stuff and some pink stuff
into a glass, and to pour an equal amount of the mixture into each empty
bottle. He worked with great rapidity and with that cynical and gay air
of his that rather puzzled Tod.

“What’s this?” Tod asked.

Cook gave him a wink.

“This, my boy, is Snape’s Magic Cough Cure, an absolute elixir, priced
at half a crown a bottle.”

“Oh, is it?” said Tod, immensely interested. “What’s in it?”

“What’s in it! That’s a trade secret, but I’ll tell you. You’ve got to
swallow the physic and get used to the taste. We give ’em a little
ipecacuanha and some sugar water coloured pink. It doesn’t do ’em any
harm.”

“Must be rather cheap,” said Tod.

“Costs twopence—including the bottle, perhaps. You can work it out.
Then there are those pretty labels and the corks. Yes, it’s good biz.”

Tod’s blue eyes took on a sulky look.

“I thought it was from the prescription of a big London doctor.”

“Oh, did you! Suppose you heard old Wisdon guffing.”

“Well, I call it a swindle,” said Tod.

Mr. Cook gave him an amused look.

“Better not say that to Snape. He’d kick you out.”

“What about you?” asked Tod.

“Me? Snape can’t play games with me. It wouldn’t be worth his while. I
could make things nasty. Besides, we’re only small fry at that.”

“What do you mean?”

Cook indicated a case full of patent medicines, jerking the hand that
held the empty measuring-glass in their direction.

“What price all that stuff? Some of it’s not so bad; most of it is just
muck. Take Prettyman’s Pills, for instance; twenty pills for two and
six, and they’ve got nothing in ’em but bread and a little aloes.
Prettyman must be a millionaire.”

“Why, it’s thieving,” said Tod.

“No, it’s just cheek and advertising. Besides, Prettyman’s a great
philanthropist. He gave £50,000 to a hospital the other day.”

“It wasn’t Prettyman who gave it,” said Tod.

“Who, then?”

“Why, the people he’d swindled.”

Cook gave one of his gay laughs.

“You’d better get into a monastery, young Western. Anyhow, what about
all those bottles?”

 Chapter Eleven

Tod began to be discouraged. He lost his interest in Mr. Snape’s shop
and in all those mysterious bottles of physic and proprietary
preparations that pretended to heal the sick. It was the profit on them
that troubled Tod. He still had vivid memories of his mother’s last
illness, and he could picture men and women buying those sure cures for
poor souls in a like tragic state. It seemed to him to be heartless
cheating.

“And if some people will always be fools,” as Cook had put it, why
didn’t the wise people try to prevent them being fools? Surely it was
someone’s business!

He put that question to a new friend, a Mr. Burgetrode, one of Shaston’s
eccentrics.

“It’s the Englishman’s privilege to be a fool, young man, his legal
right. Be careful how you interfere with it.”

It was in June that Tod made the acquaintance of Mr. Burgetrode, and
made it in rather a queer way. Tod had had a solitary fit, and had taken
to country walks, fierce and lonely Sabbath rambles with a bottle of
ginger beer in one pocket and some bread and cheese in the other. He was
coming back down a wild, gorse-grown valley that cut deep into the
rolling and wooded country to the west of Shaston, a valley sacred to a
semi-defunct golf club and a number of sheep. It was known as Summer
Valley; through it a stalwart little stream shouldered its way through
masses of blackberry and brier. The bells of the Shaston churches were
ringing for evening service, and down yonder on the green slopes a few
scattered figures were playing golf. Tod leapt an old rail fence, came
round a couple of big thorn bushes, and found an old gentleman sitting
on the grass, with one boot off, and a blue-socked foot looking rather
big and funny.

The old gentleman had a queer, lean face, rather fierce and alarming,
with blue eyes packed away under big eyebrows, and a little tuft of
greyish black hair between his lower lip and chin. He was wearing a
shapeless grey tweed hat, a brown jacket and grey flannel trousers. His
red tie was passed through a big gold ring. Tod had never seen anybody
or anything quite like him before. He had not the Shaston look, either
working-day or Sabbath.

“Hallo, boy!”

“Hallo!” said Tod.

The old gentleman stared at him.

“Damn those church bells, and damn those golfing fools. I have been
shouting and waving. Might as well be on the raft of the _Medusa_.”

“You’ve sprained your ankle,” said Tod.

“Solomon in a straw hat! What do you think made me want to jump that
fence?”

“Curiosity, sir,” said Tod.

The old gentleman bunched his eyebrows together, and stared at Tod still
more fixedly.

“No fool like an old fool! I could clear four feet ten in my day. Well,
what’s to be done, young man.”

Tod considered.

“You can’t walk, or you wouldn’t be sitting there like that. It’s a good
mile to the Lignor road. Tell you what, sir, I’ll run down to that
golf-house and telephone for a cab to come up there. Then I’ll come back
and we’ll do a three-legged walk down to the golf-house.”

The old gentleman said “Ha!” rather fiercely. It was one of his tricks.

“You help me down to the club-house, and I’ll raise the conveyance.
What’s your name?”

“Western. Tod Western.”

“All right, Western. And you will come back and have dinner with me at
the Majestic Hotel. That’s a bargain. My name is Burgetrode.”

Tod helped Mr. Burgetrode down to the club-house, and he had—for
him—the unique experience of dining at the Hotel Majestic. Tod was
rather shy and awkward; the great room was full of wonderful people—men
and women in evening dress—and Tod felt that everybody was staring at
him. Mr. Burgetrode had a little table in a corner near a window. A very
important-looking waiter had helped the old gentleman across the room,
and now stood waiting for orders.

“Dinner for two, sir?”

“Of course, Reynolds, of course.”

The waiter beamed at Tod.

“Thick or clear soup, sir?”

“Whichever you like,” said Tod, rather embarrassed and wanting to return
the waiter’s politeness.

Old Burgetrode looked up sharply.

“Clear, Reynolds, for both. Go straight through. And bring us two ginger
beers.”

Tod remembered that bottle in his pocket, and a surreptitious hand
discovered the stopper and stone top protruding for all the world to
see. He suppressed that bottle and carefully turned down the flap of his
pocket.

Then Mr. Burgetrode began to talk. He talked rather jerkily, and with a
sort of playful and biting ferocity that puzzled Tod. He asked Tod all
sorts of questions, queer, unexpected questions that came at you all
ways and danced round you like a Chinese cracker. Tod noticed that the
waiter was extraordinarily polite to Mr. Burgetrode. What with eating
his dinner and answering Mr. Burgetrode’s questions, Tod had enough to
do. Presently a very gorgeous and charming lady in black who had been
sitting at another table rustled across and spoke to Mr. Burgetrode on
her way out.

“I do hope you have not sprained your foot very badly, Mr. Burgetrode?”

Tod saw Mr. Burgetrode’s eyes turn all red.

“I’ve sprained it; that ought to be good enough, I think.”

The gorgeous lady purred over him.

“How witty you are. We are having a little concert in the
recreation-room to-morrow night——”

“Have it, madam, have it. I shan’t stop it. Put me down for a guinea.”

The gorgeous lady looked at Tod as though she were ready to kiss him,
and then floated away.

“Sausage and Schnapps!” said Mr. Burgetrode, irrelevantly, “and her
second husband is still alive.”

He dismissed Tod in a queer, curt, yet friendly way.

“You will find me near the bandstand between six and seven, Western, on
most evenings.”

“We don’t close till seven, sir.”

“Then make it eight till nine.”

“Thank you, sir.”

That was all that Mr. Burgetrode said to him. In Tod’s ears it had
sounded strangely like an order, but Mr. Burgetrode seemed to be the
sort of person who had an inherent right to issue orders. Most people
were afraid of him. Tod was not.

Mr. Burgetrode came into Tod’s life at a moment when young Western was
fighting with a series of disillusionments. On the way home from the
Hotel Majestic he met Kitty Flowerdew and Edwin Rook walking arm in arm
along the Parade, and Kitty refused to see him. Not so Edwin Rook. The
shopman nodded at Tod in a very complacent and patronising way, and when
he had passed them Tod heard them laughing. He did not know that there
was pain and shame in Kitty’s laughter. The jest rankled. He thought of
the way in which Mr. Burgetrode had snubbed that gorgeous lady who had
come and purred round his table. Perhaps Mr. Burgetrode had no use for
women and believed that anything in petticoats was made for the
humiliation and enslaving of men?

Tod spent a very difficult Monday in Mr. Snape’s shop. Mr. Snape was in
a very bad temper about something or other, and Tod happened to be the
person who got in his way. It began by Tod breaking the glass in one of
the big cabinet doors just when one of the richest women in Shaston was
buying scent from “Mr. Any-think-else.”

“You clumsy young brute,” said Snape, when the lady had left the shop.

Tod coloured up.

“Don’t you speak to me like that. I’ll pay for it.”

“I guess you will,” said Mr. Snape sarcastically, “and I don’t want any
impertinence from my assistants, thank you.”

Tod fell into trouble again in the afternoon. Cook was dispensing; Old
Wisdon had gone to unpack some drugs, and Tod was left in charge of the
shop. A woman came in and asked him if Sowerby’s Sea Salts were any use
for sore feet. Tod said he didn’t know. Mr. Snape, emerging from his
back room, happened to overhear Tod’s crass and futile confession of
ignorance.

The woman was leaving the shop when Snape appeared behind the counter.
He let her go, and then fell heavily on Tod.

“What do you mean by answering a lady like that?”

“Like what?” Tod asked. “I wasn’t rude.”

“You told her that you didn’t know whether Sowerby’s Salts were good for
sore feet.”

“Well, I don’t know,” said Tod.

Mr. Snape grew very pink.

“Don’t let me ever hear you say I don’t know again in my shop.”

“What am I to say?” asked Tod.

“Use your wits, you young idiot. You are here to sell things. You can
come and ask me or one of the others, can’t you?”

“All right,” said Tod, restraining himself; “I’m learning.”

He described the incident to “Mr. Any-think-else,” and appealed to him
for sympathy.

“I only told the truth,” Tod argued. “How was I to know whether the
stuff was any good for people’s feet? Is it?”

“Don’t know,” said Wisdon sulkily.

“Well, would you tell the truth?”

“Tell the truth! Great Scott, Western, I’ve got a wife and three
children.”

Tod said no more, but he thought a great deal about the problem of old
Wisdon and his wife and children.

He fell in that evening with Arthur Mossup from Horrox and Horrox’s, and
Mossup, who had a shy man’s affection for him, named him for an
adventure.

“Like a walk, Western?”

“Right-o! Where are you going?”

Mossup showed him a handful of keys.

“House-hunting. It’s the only time I’ve got, and an agent friend of mine
obliged me with these. I’m after a little place, and we have two hours’
daylight.”

“What fun,” said Tod.

They walked in the direction of Blackberry Hill, and for the first time
in his life Tod felt the lure of houses. There was something very
fascinating in poking about in those back streets, finding your house
all empty and waiting, in unlocking its door and exploring all its
possibilities and its secrets. Tod developed a keenness and an
enthusiasm that surprised Mossup. They measured rooms, inspected rusty
kitchen stoves, ferreted about in lugubrious back yards, took note of
cupboards, coppers, sanitary arrangements and the invariable absence of
a bath. Mossup was a serious and prematurely old young man. He would
stand and meditate like a stork on one leg, his weak eyes glazed behind
his spectacles, a tuft of flaxen hair standing up on his head like a
plume. His ambitions were very modest. His romance ran to a rental of
£20 a year, with the furniture for one bedroom and a sitting-room, and
the stocking of a kitchen. There was something pathetic about his
cautiousness, and the way he kept consulting a little notebook in which
the adventure was tabulated to the last penny.

They were poor little houses, obvious little houses, cheap, shabby, and
hopelessly ugly. All the windows looked at other houses of exactly the
same type. One or two of them had a very grimed and greasy air. An
occasional whiff of sewer gas sent Mossup sniffing into dark places like
a solemn and suspicious dog. There seemed a lamentable absence of fresh
whitewash everywhere, and Tod could not see any appetising corner where
you would care to keep food. The larders and little sculleries
were—well, you just said “Poof!” Yet the fascination of the quest
gripped Tod. Mossup interested him. He was equally interested in the
house in which Mossup might choose to live.

“We have to be very careful—very careful,” said Mossup, jotting down
notes in his book.

“I don’t like any of these houses.”

“Don’t you, Western?” and Mossup looked pained.

“They’re dirty and stuffy and ugly. Where’s the gardens, too? I don’t
call any of those cat-walks gardens.”

“Can’t afford more than twenty pounds. You have to put up with things,
sometimes.”

“I don’t see it,” said Tod. “Why can’t things be arranged differently.
They could be, if somebody bothered.”

Tod spent two more evenings in house-hunting with Arthur Mossup. At last
they found a little old house, in a blind lane off Paradise Row, one of
those solid and unaffected little houses that had lived in the country
until the splurge of the Shaston suburbs had swallowed it. It had a
green latticed porch, green window frames, a tiled roof and a bit of
garden. Mossup grew greatly excited. He wanted to rush down to the
agents and take the place that very night.

“Somebody else may get it. Olive will just shout when she sees this
cottage.”

“Plenty of time,” said Tod, “go round first thing in the morning. Rather
wish I was a house agent. Must be quite jolly finding homes for people.”

The idea fermented. It was working in Tod’s head when he strolled along
to the bandstand on Wednesday evening in search of Mr. Burgetrode. He
found him in a bath-chair by the parade railings, and looking as though
he hated it. The bath-chair man was leaning over the railing, smoking a
pipe.

“Smith,” said Mr. Burgetrode.

“Sir?”

“Go and get a drink; go and get two drinks, and come back in half an
hour. Here’s the money.”

Tod perched himself on the railings.

“How is your foot, sir?”

“My foot doesn’t interest me, Western; let it alone. We’ll leave it with
the doctor. I expected to see you before, young man.”

Tod looked down unabashed into Mr. Burgetrode’s fierce brown face.

“Sorry, sir; I’ve been house-hunting.”

“What?”

“House-hunting with a friend who is getting married.”

“Why doesn’t he do something else! And did you find a house?”

“After three evenings at it,” said Tod; “we looked at all sorts of
mouldy places where you wouldn’t expect to find anything but
blackbeetles and cats. Houses are very interesting, sir, don’t you
think?”

“Almost as interesting as men, Western, and not quite such fools. Houses
are alive.”

“Yes, aren’t they?” said Tod. “I have begun to feel I should like to
live with houses, know all about houses, put people into houses.”

Old Burgetrode gave a quick lift of his head.

“You don’t like your present place, then?”

“No,” said Tod.

“Why not?”

“They don’t want you to tell the truth.”

“Ha!” said Mr. Burgetrode fiercely.

Tod edged himself nearer to the bath-chair. He had a feeling that he
could talk to Mr. Burgetrode.

“They want you to lie about things. It was the same in the other shop I
was in, and I got chucked for that—and something else.”

Mr. Burgetrode was watching him with great intentness.

“So you don’t like it?”

“I loathe it,” said Tod. “Why should you have to lie to people?”

“It means money, Western, more money.”

“Well, we ought to get rid of money. Or supposing there was so much
money that it was cheap as the shingle down there, people wouldn’t tell
lies to get it.”

“That’s a problem, young man. You seem to have discovered the trick of
running your head against problems.”

The Shaston Corporation Orchestra who inhabited the bandstand, woke to
sudden life after an interval of twenty minutes. Mr. Burgetrode grew
impatient.

“Take me away from that noise, will you?”

Tod descended from the railings and trundled the bath-chair in the
direction of the Hotel Majestic.

“That will do,” said Mr. Burgetrode; “it will take my friend Smith
another ten minutes to find us. Do you play any games, young Ajax?”

“Cricket,” said Tod, “and I played right-half for Shaston last winter. I
can swim a mile, and box a bit.”

“Keep it up, Western. What did you want to be before you got trapped in
a shop?”

“A doctor.”

Mr. Burgetrode did not seem prejudiced in favour of such a career. He
gave Tod to understand that there were doctors and doctors, and that if
you wanted to become a first-class surgeon you might count on earning
very little money for fifteen years. He talked rather strangely about
doctors trying to patch the roof, when the foundations were rotten. He
said that the doctors were not to blame, and that you could not get at
the foundations, because the Vested Interest fence kept you out.

“What’s a Vested Interest?” Tod asked.

“You have got to find that out,” said Mr. Burgetrode.

Then he made a sudden suggestion.

“Would you like to take up houses, Western?”

“I think I should, sir.”

“Study them inside out, and from top to bottom, how they are built, and
how they shouldn’t be built?”

“That’s it,” said Tod.

Mr. Burgetrode looked hard at him with those queer, fierce eyes of his.

“Very well, Ajax. I shall be at that hotel for another month. If you
want me, you will know where to find me.”

 Chapter Twelve

Someone had once nicknamed Mr. Burgetrode “Old Mischief.” That was ten
years ago, and if Shaston had been asked to place and describe Mr.
Julian Burgetrode, Shaston would have stared at you uncomprehendingly,
or would have asked whether you were referring to “that old chap who
stayed at the Majestic?” Even the Hotel Majestic knew very little about
Mr. Burgetrode. He was sarcastic, and fierce and laconic. He had an
abundance of money, and he appeared to despise money, judging by the
size of his tips. People gossiped about him. He was mysterious,
eccentric, fabulously wealthy. The old ladies who sat in the
drawing-room and read Marie Corelli, and Mr. Garvice and “The Rosary,”
spun all sorts of romances about Mr. Burgetrode. They said he was a
misogamist, an atheist and other unwholesome and unnatural things. His
wife had committed suicide, or run away with another man, and he had
chronic dyspepsia, and took drugs. Mr. Burgetrode did not care at all
what the old ladies said of him. He disliked old ladies, disliked them
as much as a fierce dog hates cats, and he never attempted to
compromise.

“Reynolds,” he once said to the head waiter, “I’ll give you ten pounds a
week if you promise not to put one of those minute guns within ten yards
of me.”

“Minute guns, sir?”

“Old women, Reynolds, old women.”

“Rather difficult, sir; you see——”

“Yes, I know; it rains old women and caps and artificial fringes, and
bead bags and novels, in a place like this. Put something young, or
something in trousers at those tables, something that doesn’t want to
grin at you like a Cheshire cat.”

“I’ll see what I can do, sir,” said Reynolds.

The queer part of it was that the “staff” of the Hotel Majestic liked
Mr. Burgetrode. They rather sympathised with him about the old ladies.
The maid who looked after his bedroom and private sitting-room laid it
down as an axiom that she would rather “do” for ten Mr. Burgetrodes than
for one of those old cats. “He’s a sport,” she asserted; “he’s got a
tongue on him, but if you’ve got anything straight to say, he don’t mind
you answering back.” The lift boys were Mr. Burgetrode’s devoted slaves.
Nor did largesse account for it all. There was something fiercely human
about the old man; he could be extraordinarily severe and
extraordinarily kind.

There were, however, one or two people in Shaston-on-Sea who knew who
and what Mr. Julian Burgetrode was in the larger world up north. He came
south to escape from the roar and the smother of the factory and the
market, Shaston was a sort of toy-town to him, and he strolled about and
looked down on it like an amused Colossus. Yet there was a memory behind
it all, a smell of the blue sea, a glory of golden broom and white
black-thorn, a girl and a boy honeymooning in one of the old white
Georgian houses under the cliff. Ten days of marriage, and then death,
death in the blue sea on a sweet spring morning. In one of the Old Town
churchyards there was a stone that told how Elsie Burgetrode had been
buried there nearly forty years ago.

Such was Shaston’s sentimental hold on Julian Burgetrode. He liked the
slothfulness of the place after the hard shallowness of the North. He
came south when he wanted to think, and went tramping for miles over the
beech-covered hills and into the soft blueness of the Weald. Away in the
north men were dominated by the idea of the machine. They had forgotten
to wonder how green life started in the dim slime of primeval seas.
Burgetrode was lord of many machines, but never the slave of one of
them. He could still jump a fence, delight in Anatole France, and sit up
thinking with Bergson.

Shaston was his toy-town. How much of it was his and what a grip his
brown hands had on it, very few people suspected. One lawyer knew; so
did a bank-manager; so did Rathbone Cragg of Rathbone Cragg and Son,
Estate and House Agents. And when Julian Burgetrode limped into the
office of Rathbone Cragg in Caroline Place, old Cragg fell from his
perch of supercilious greed and flopped and squawked most unctuously
upon the floor.

“Good morning, sir. Will you please come in my private room, sir.”

“I want a few words with you, Mr. Cragg.”

“Quite so, sir. Thomas, will you see that we are not disturbed?”

Burgetrode detested Mr. Cragg. The man was such a Lord Chief Justice to
little people, yet he was so easily mastered by a dog bigger than
himself.

“You would be able to find room in your office for a youngster of
eighteen or nineteen?”

“For instruction, sir?”

“Yes, in the ordinary way of business. He would start at the bottom and
work his way up. I happen to be interested in a boy whom I want to
train. When he had spent two or three years with you, I should put him
to a builder.”

Mr. Cragg nodded.

“It shall be done, to oblige you, Mr. Burgetrode. I presume——”

“The boy is not to know that I have any ultimate interest in his future.
I want you to understand that, Mr. Cragg. Any breach of confidence——”

“You can rely on me, sir, absolutely.”

“Very good. The youngster’s name is Western. He will call on you one day
with a letter from me. I want you to treat him as you would treat any
other clerk in your office. Good morning.”

Tod guessed nothing of Mr. Burgetrode’s sudden interest in him. It had
not struck Tod that he was something of a phenomenon, a lad in a shop
who was sturdily set against the telling of lies. Mr. Burgetrode had
been handling men all his life, dull men, grumbling men, good men, bad
men, men who were contented provided they got beer, men who would have
been discontented if they had been drowned in that beverage. Then there
was the new type of man, the keen-witted, implacable revolutionist, who
knew what he wanted and meant to get it. Mr. Burgetrode was making a
special study of that type of man, for he saw in him the man of the
future. He needed mellowing; he needed a little more honest pudding in
his stomach, and less poison under his tongue. At present the new man
was ugly, and Mr. Burgetrode detested ugliness.

He had been impressed at once by the unusualness of Tod. He liked the
lad’s intelligent square head, his brown face, and alert unfearing eyes.
The boy was sensitive too, and not in the least arrogant. He looked at
life and asked questions, the very questions that a thinker like Julian
Burgetrode knew to be the great questions that the world had to answer.
The boy’s sincerity was a thing of wonderful significance. It was like
one of those magic swords, hammered out by goblins in the underworld,
and then belted to some blue-eyed, innocent, fate-predestined hero.

Meanwhile Tod was feeling very unheroic in John Snape’s shop. The
regular dispensing of prescriptions was but a small part of the
business. There was plenty of honest stuff to sell, from photographic
plates to toothpicks, but Tod could never forget those quack medicines
and the sham doctoring that was carried on by Snape and his assistants.
Tod foresaw that his days were numbered. He was getting discredited.
Some day there would be a row, and Mr. Snape would suggest his returning
to the farm.

Tod felt the storm gathering. Mr. Snape had taken to bullying him, and
using a dry sarcasm on every possible occasion. For a pink, well-fed man
he had a most ingenious and spiteful tongue.

“Is that the way you wrap up bottles, Western? Here, let me show you.
You’ve got no snap or neatness in your fingers, bodgering a bottle in
paper till it looks as though it were dressed in a dirty nightshirt!”

“A place for everything, Western, and everything in its place. For
goodness’ sake get some method in that head of yours. And your hair
wants cutting.”

“You had better oil that voice of yours, young man. This isn’t a school
playground.”

Tod took all this heckling as a good boxer takes punishment, but it was
providing him with the justification for getting in a good straight
punch to Mr. Snape’s jaw. He was not like poor old Wisdon with his wife
and three children and his eighty pounds a year. Wisdon was not a
certified dispenser, he was just so much casual labour, living in
perpetual fear of being out of work. Snape bullied Wisdon, and daily cut
the throat of his hired man’s self-respect, and Wisdon went like a sheep
to the slaughter, offering the neck of his man’s pride so that his
children might live.

Tod saw Mr. Burgetrode on several evenings, and entered into long
discussions with his new friend. He put forward the case of “Mr.
Any-think-else,” arguing hotly that a man of Wisdon’s age should not be
so placed in life that he could be bullied and sneered at by a man
younger than himself.

“Money again,” said Mr. Burgetrode; “your Mr. Snape seems something of a
swashbuckler.”

“I couldn’t stand what old Wisdon has to stand,” said Tod; “I’m just
waiting, and one day I’ll land Snape one and walk out of the shop.”

“Just for love, young Ajax?”

“No, sir, I’m waiting till he comes at me crooked over something I
should have lied about, and then I’ll hit out straight.”

“Ha!” said Mr. Burgetrode in his fierce way; “come and tell me about it
when it happens.”

It happened the very next day. About half an hour before closing time a
pale woman with tired eyes walked into the shop, and it fell to Tod to
serve her. Cook had left early; old Wisdon was in one of the stock rooms
upstairs looking out trusses; Tod knew that Mr. Snape was in his office
at the back of the shop. Tod noticed that the pale woman looked rather
shabby, and that her face made him think of his mother as he remembered
her six months before her death.

“Have you got Bartrup’s Consumption Cure?”

“Yes,” said Tod.

“Is it—is it any good?” asked the pale woman.

Tod happened to know something about Bartrup’s Consumption Cure. Cook
had told him that he had seen the analysis of that very popular remedy
and that the best thing to do with it was to pour it down the drain.

Tod looked straight into the woman’s tired eyes.

“It’s rubbish,” he said.

“Rubbish!” echoed the woman.

“I’d see a doctor,” Tod added, “before I touched any drugs.”

The woman thanked Tod and left the shop, and it was then that Tod became
aware of Mr. Snape standing at the far end of the counter like a little
pink Napoleon who had caught one of his sentries giving secret
information to an enemy patrol.

“So Bartrup’s Consumption Cure is rubbish, Mr. Western, is it?”

“I have been told so,” said Tod stubbornly.

“And who gave you that valuable information?”

“It’s not my business to tell.”

John Snape lost his temper, and came and stood very close to Tod, so
close that he spluttered in Tod’s face.

“You’re a damned fool, that’s what you are, Western. Nice sort of idiot
for a man to have in his shop. Get me discredited—discredited! D’you
understand? Supposing Bartrups were informed that their preparation had
been called rubbish in my shop? You’d get me tabooed by Bartrups; they
might take legal action——”

Mr. Snape drew breath, and Tod waited, blinking a little because he did
not like Mr. Snape’s splutter.

“Where’s your loyalty to me? Didn’t I take you in here to give you a
chance, and all you can do is to blab silly lies over the counter!”

“They’re not lies,” said Tod; “half the quack stuff we sell is just
cheating.”

“Cheating!”

Mr. Snape gasped, and took a deep breath, but Tod got his blow in first.

“I’m leaving this evening,” he said, “this sort of business doesn’t suit
me.”

“Well, of all the impertinent young cubs——!”

“You needn’t call me names,” said Tod; “I’m not going to be responsible
for Bartrup and Co., that’s all.”

 Chapter Thirteen

The intervention of Mr. Julian Burgetrode initiated one of the good
seasons of Tod’s career. Some big hand seemed to push open a heavy door
and draw back a curtain, and Tod stepped easily into a new and more
spacious life.

The office of Rathbone Cragg and Son, Auctioneers and Estate Agents, had
a certain Victorian calmness, a deliberate and leisurely way of doing
things that was decent and almost pontifical. The Craggs, both father
and son, were very polite people, and with a politeness that suited the
more civilised end of Shaston and the ritual beloved of the symbolists
who attended the church of St. Alban the Martyr. Mr. Rathbone Cragg,
lean, bald-headed, round shouldered, with that emaciated and
cartilaginous throat of his, and the head of a benignant vulture, served
as a sidesman at St. Alban’s Church, and also as a Justice of the Peace.
His son, a younger and more anæmic sort of vulture, with a queer
axe-shaped head, its edge fronting forwards in chin and nose, clove his
way into the intellectual life of West Shaston with an indefatigable and
supercilious seriousness that made more full blooded young men hate him
as buoyant savages must hate your irrepressible missionary. Frederick
Rathbone Cragg was for ever improving himself. He knew too much; facts
and figures rolled off him like turnips off an overladen cart. He was
the sort of young man who sat solemnly and grimly at breakfast with a
part of Handsworth’s Encylopædia in front of him, munching eggs and
bacon with teeth of virgin gold.

Tod discovered a new atmosphere. If you walked down Beacon Hill with the
western sun behind you, you had a vision of a fresh and vivid Shaston,
Shaston as the Georgians had schemed it, shorn of its base speculative
excrescences in yellow brick and grey stucco, a white town strung
between the blue of the sea and the green of the hills. In the beyond
loomed the White Cliff, a little hazy with the smoke of the Old Town,
the yellow sand making a dim gleam between it and the sea. There were
the masts of the Shaston fishing fleet, black and crowded like the ships
of the Greeks beached before Troy. Above the White Cliff the lighthouse
painted a white streak against summer skies. The parade in front of the
tall houses had breadth and dignity, and could stand and answer the roar
of a rough sea. On the hills above the town the beeches and holm oaks
and wind-beaten pines of Effingham Park, broke up and softened the firm
white lines, and spread a rolling frieze between the white houses and
the yet whiter clouds.

The Craggs had their office in Caroline Crescent, a bow of solid houses
with the parade as its bowstring. Caroline Crescent suggested the
Regency, gentlemen in gay coloured coats and white breeches,
high-stepping horses, “tigers” hanging on to the backs of yellow-wheeled
dog-carts, young gentlewomen with side curls, Pickwickian worthies with
Hessian boots, and solid, round, white-breeched stomachs. The houses had
balconies with long and steeply sloping green roofs, and from any of
these balconies one of Marcus Stone’s sentimental young ladies might
have thrown a red rose at some quite impossibly handsome and romantic
young man on a horse. Such things had ceased to happen in Caroline
Crescent. A little colony of doctors lived there, two solicitors and a
dentist. Brass plates were as thick as gold teeth in the mouth of a
nigger.

The Craggs were very discreet and restrained, and confined themselves to
gold letters on the lower windows of No. 10. Blatancy did not pay in the
west end of Shaston. Notice boards, posters and advertisements of houses
to be let and sold were to be found only in the big hall and in the main
office. Once upon a time there had been litigation in Caroline Crescent
over the keeping of a cockatoo that screamed. The Craggs did not scream.
You were careful of your manners when you practised your profession in
the company of gentlemen who felt pulses or drafted people’s wills.

Tod felt all boots and hands on that first morning when he entered the
establishment of the Craggs, and stood in the middle of the big office
with Mr. Burgetrode’s letter of introduction between thumb and
forefinger. A young woman with a high forehead and pince-nez was typing
at a table. Two clerks were busy at their desks, one of them making
entries in a ledger, the other sorting out a chaos of keys. Tod stood
and waited till the younger of the two clerks chose to discover him.

“Yes?”

“I’ve a letter for Mr. Cragg.”

“All right. Hand it over. Any answer?”

“I’ll wait and see,” said Tod.

The letter and the clerk disappeared through a glazed door on which “Mr.
Rathbone Cragg” was painted in black letters. Tod noticed that there was
a second and similar door with Mr. Frederick Rathbone Cragg’s name upon
it. He stood and stared at the bills on the walls, at the maps and
photographs of houses, at the typist clicking sedulously on her machine,
until she stopped and stared at him in turn. Tod veered away, and felt
uncomfortable. The young woman behind the table made him absurdly
conscious of the fact that he was a creature that wore clothes.

The clerk reappeared with a new geniality towards Tod.

“Mr. Cragg will see you at once, Mr. Western.”

That interview puzzled Tod. Mr. Cragg stood up behind his desk and shook
hands with him, and Tod felt embarrassed. This hard-eyed old gentleman
who gobbled in his throat like a turkey, and was so jerkily and
parentally polite, made Tod wonder what Mr. Burgetrode had said
concerning him.

“Sit down, Mr. Western. So you want to be an estate agent?”

Tod sat down.

“I’m interested in houses,” he said.

Mr. Cragg appeared to approve.

“Enthusiasm is the great secret of success. I have this little note here
from Mr. Burgetrode, and it is possible that I may be able to oblige
him. I understand that you are an orphan, Western.”

“Yes, sir.”

Mr. Cragg tilted his head like a benignant bird.

“A great deprivation, Western. And—about your education?”

“I was at Chipperton’s school at Pentworth for five years, sir.”

“An excellent school too. Well, let me see, you wish to enter my office
immediately?”

“If you can take me, sir.”

“I think I may say that I can.”

It all seemed so absurdly easy. There were no clouds, no complications,
nothing but geniality and sunshine and an avoidance of all awkward
questions; no reference to Mr. Flowerdew or Mr. Snape. Mr. Cragg
suggested terms that made Tod feel a plutocrat. And then Mr. Frederick
Rathbone Cragg was called in and presented to Tod as though he were a
friend of the family.

Frederick Cragg gave Tod’s hand a hard and earnest squeeze. He stared
impressively at people when he was introduced to them, and he stared
very hard at Tod.

“Very glad to meet you, Western.”

Tod felt ten years older. He began to wonder whether he ought not to
have come in a bowler hat.

Two months later Tod had become an enthusiastic student of houses,
houses of all sorts and descriptions, stolid houses, frivolous houses,
snobbish houses, houses that were disreputable yet happy. And Tod too
was happy though he was in danger of being sucked over the edge of his
youth into that respectable and genteel world that is death and
destruction to any adventurous soul. Mr. Frederick Rathbone Cragg had
taken control of Tod; he had a penchant for cultivating boys and young
men as though they were puppies, and trying to teach them his own
tricks. He was interested in houses, but his interest was different from
Tod’s. He was a bit of commercialised intelligence, yet in spite of his
fish blood and flat feet he mimicked all sorts of strange people, like a
vain and clever ape who copies a gesture without understanding its real
meaning. Young Cragg was an intellectual. He had read Browning and Henry
James, Nietzsche and Bernard Shaw. In fact he had read a little of
everything, and he was the secretary of the St. Margarets Literary
Society. He posed. He liked to hand out queer and startling opinions.
Nothing pleased him better than to drag around a youth like Tod, let off
intellectual fireworks and see the little fellow skip and open his eyes
very wide.

Tod, in his innocence, was flattered. He was impressed by “Young
Supercilious,” as some of the scoffers called Mr. Frederick Cragg, the
energetic person who had an answer to everything. He trailed Tod round
with him and talked mediæval architecture with the dogmatism of a
Ruskin, and was so much the philosopher and the man of the world that
Tod had ears and no tongue.

“You always want to know the right people, Western; it’s just the same
as going into the right sort of house.”

Very soon he was criticising Tod’s clothes, and telling him with an air
of great impressiveness that such and such things were not worn.

“You mustn’t do it, old chap. No pink or red ties, and don’t wear brown
boots on Sunday. Good form, you know; it counts, it counts with the
right people.”

Mr. Burgetrode had gone north again, and Tod was left to the earnest
egotism of Mr. Frederick Cragg. Life in the office was not unpleasant,
and Tod’s growing enthusiasm flattered the pride of the firm. Mr.
Burgetrode had hinted that Clarence Todhunter Western was an original
child, and with such a sponsor life in the house of Cragg and Son was
bound to be easy. They had been very ready to make the best of Tod, and
Tod the learner was keen and indefatigable.

“That boy has got a future,” Fred said to his sister.

Miss Bertha Cragg was always tripping up the toes of her brother’s
impressiveness.

“Because old Burgetrode has taken a fancy to him? Nice sort of wet nurse
you make, Fred!”

“You women don’t understand.”

But he continued to make a sort of East End Settlement of Tod and to
cultivate that young heathen. Tod went to Hanson’s the gentleman’s
tailor, and had a suit of clothes made to measure. His ties grew more
conservative and refined. He ceased to wear a cloth cap on the back of
his head. Frederick Cragg rescued him from the bathos of Mrs. Budd’s
cottage, and persuaded him to take a bed-sitting room in St. Helen’s
Terrace in the house of an impoverished gentlewoman who read Tennyson
and genuflected regularly at the church of St. Alban the Martyr. He lent
Tod books, went for country walks with him, declaimed Keats and
Rossetti, and talked a lot of nonsense that sounded very clever and
rather shocking.

Caroline Crescent was teaching Tod all sorts of things about real
estate. Tod had no immediate vision of the financial aspect of the
business, and he had lost some of the distrust that the world of
Flowerdew and Snape had roused in him. The Craggs were polite people.
They were always and ever your most obedient servants, and they appeared
to let or sell you a house for the pure social joy of doing you a
service. They taught Tod about leases and agreements, the writing up of
houses, the making of inventories, the valuing of fixtures, the business
of insurance and of rent-collecting; and all the ritual of a successful
sale. They had little to do with the cottage property of old Shaston.
They confined their activities to the gentlemen’s residences and the red
villas of St. Margarets and the Grand Parade; moreover, they were the
“county people” and much county property had passed through their hands.

Young Cragg had taken Tod in to tea at “Clovelly,” the Craggs’ house in
Salisbury Road, but Tod’s real emergence from the caterpillar state
dated from the day when he was invited to tennis.

“My mater wants you to come up on Saturday, Western, and play tennis.
Just a little party, you know.”

Tod confessed that he knew nothing about tennis.

“You’ll soon learn,” said his mentor with his usual impressiveness;
“come up half an hour before the other people, and I’ll put you up to
the main points.”

This was very gallant and chivalrous, but Tod was worried about clothes.

“Suppose I had better buy a racket?”

“Yes, you ought to have a racket, Western. And shoes.”

“And shoes?” echoed Tod.

He wanted to ask Fred Cragg about the various details of his toilet, but
shirked the confession of ignorance and in shirking it challenged his
own imagination. What sort of shoes ought he to buy—white or
brown?—and what should be the colour of a tennis-player’s trousers and
shirt? And did one wear a belt or braces? That was a devil of a dilemma.
He was so pestered and baffled by the whole problem that he dashed up to
the St. Margarets Tennis Club that evening, and lifting his chin over
the top of a wooden fence studied the details of dress as exemplified by
the gentlemen of that club. Braces were not worn. You fastened your
white flannel trousers with a coloured scarf or a leather belt. The
shoes were white, so were the shirts, and any old coat that was not
black seemed fit to complete the picture. Tod gave a gulp of spiritual
relief. If he had turned up at the Craggs’ wearing those red braces of
his, what a scandal he would have created!

Tod spent quite a respectable sum on a racket, buckskin shoes, two pairs
of white trousers, two white shirts and soft collars, a chocolate and
light blue scarf, and a straw hat with a black band. He began to dress
about half-past one on the Saturday afternoon, and the business lasted
an hour. He went downstairs, swinging his new racket, and met his
landlady, the gentlewoman who read Tennyson and genuflected daily at the
church of St. Alban the Martyr.

She liked Tod. He was such a fresh, clean, and considerate youngster,
and rather shy.

“Tennis, Mr. Western?”

“Tennis,” said Tod.

“How nice. You ought to have those new clothes marked before they go to
the wash.”

“Suppose I ought.”

“I will do it for you.”

And Tod went on his way, feeling cheered, and of the opinion that life
was quite good and that people were awfully decent.

At “Clovelly” a maid showed him down to the tennis lawn where Frederick
Cragg was sitting under a young lime tree and reading Zola’s “Paris” in
French. Fred was a very shaky French scholar, but it was rather
impressive to leave a book like that about.

“Hallo, Western.”

He looked Tod over comprehensively.

“Yes, that’s quite all right. But just one thing, old chap, you won’t
mind——”

He got up, and gave a little jab to the rear portion of the brim of
Tod’s straw hat; for Tod had tilted that hat too much on the back of his
head.

“Like that, Western; excuse me, you know.”

“Don’t mention it,” said Tod, flushing, but the remedying of that defect
dashed his self-confidence for the rest of the afternoon.

It was the first function of its kind that Tod had ever attended, and it
reduced him to a state of stubborn shyness. There was that big red villa
“Clovelly” in the background, a mysterious house half hidden behind a
great pergola and masses of flowering shrubs. A curly path and a flight
of stone steps led to the tennis lawn, and down this social highway a
succession of people and things descended upon Tod, girls in summer
frocks, elderly women who rustled, young men who looked surprisingly
cool and unflurried, old Rathbone Cragg in a grey flannel suit and a
white Panama hat, a gardener who carried deck-chairs, and later a couple
of maids with tables and tea. Tod was introduced to one or two people by
Frederick Cragg.

“Mr. Western, Miss Lavibond.”

“Pleased to meet you,” said Tod, holding out a hand, and then
remembering to take off his hat.

He stood by Miss Lavibond’s chair and tried to think of something to say
to her. The frocks and the chatter and the puzzling and refined
correctness of the business had him stifled. The Craggs were the leaders
of a definite social circle, a coterie of well-to-do middle-class people
whom a connection with trade had segregated in that little world of
gentlemen’s residences. There were old Horrox’s two daughters, the
Playles, who owned the biggest grocery in Shaston, Vintners the wine
merchants, and many more. It is possible that they were a little more
correct and polite and stiffly self-conscious than the class just above
them, for they were not such old hands at the game, and they had to take
great care of their manners.

“Weren’t the rhododendrons lovely this year,” said Miss Lavibond to Tod.

Tod didn’t know what rhododendrons were but he did not say so.

“Yes, very fine, weren’t they? Do you like the band we have got this
year?”

“Just a little too much brass, I think. I heard them murder a thing of
Grieg’s the other night.”

“Hard luck!” said Tod, and wondered why Miss Lavibond stared.

He decided that it was time to get away from the lady before she
discovered that he was conversing in the dark. He felt she was
dangerous, that she would denounce him if he launched into any more
conversational ventures, and he seized the chance of going to hunt for a
tennis ball that had disappeared in a bank of laurels. Tod took a
preposterously long time finding that ball, and when he did find it he
was confronted with Mrs. Rathbone Cragg and the ordeal of tea.

“Now you are going to help me, Mr. Western, aren’t you?”

She was a very large woman, a duchess of a woman, all black silk and
white lace, and she had ideas about putting Tod at his ease. She sat at
a wicker table with two big silver teapots and a crowd of cups in front
of her, and all around him on other tables Tod saw a bewildering wealth
of cakes, thin bread and butter in rolls, cucumber sandwiches, potted
meat sandwiches, biscuits, little dishes of sweets, strawberries, cream
pots, sugar bowls and plates.

“What’ll I do, please?”

“If you will hand round the tea-cups as I fill them, the maids will take
round the sugar and milk.”

Tod disgraced himself over that tea. He spilt one of the cups over
somebody’s dress and over his own flannel trousers, and ended by wishing
that he was dead. He was glooming in the background, and contemplating
making a bolt for it, when Miss Cragg swept in from somewhere and
rescued him from himself.

“Do come and have some tea, Mr. Western.”

“I’m not at all hungry,” he said politely.

“But I am, and I haven’t had tea yet. Let’s get a little table all to
ourselves and be greedy. Nearly all the other people have finished.”

Her frankness and her smiling brown eyes recovered the man in Tod. She
was a dark girl of about five-and-twenty, neither pretty nor plain, the
type of girl that is called a “good sort,” the girl whom no man marries.
Moreover, she had a brain, and a pair of eyes that were learning to
watch life with a rather wistful shrewdness. She had begun to realise
that she had no lure for the average men of her class, nor had they any
very great attraction for her. They were so obvious, and so very
complacent, and so up-to-date and cheap. They had all the tricks and
catchwords of the season. The weather was always “top hole” or “the
limit”; “priceless” was the adjective in vogue for the moment and it was
applied to everything, frocks, plays, flirtations, boots and drinks. If
you were keen on tennis or golf you were a “tennis merchant” or a “golf
merchant.” Everything that was unpleasant was “rotten.” Bertha Cragg had
been listening for a new and original note from some unconventional
male.

Tod had appealed to her. He was shy and unhappy, and there were
possibilities about him that were lacking in the more complacent young
men as exemplified by her brother. Bertha had a secret contempt for her
brother, that infallible young man whose greatest joy was to create an
impression.

“You haven’t had a game yet, have you?”

“No,” said Tod, “I can’t play tennis.”

“Let’s take this table. You’d soon learn; you are good at games. I
remember seeing you play football last winter.”

Tod blushed.

“Did you?”

“You saved the match for Shaston. Yes, let’s have some of these mixed
cakes, and we’ll take a teapot all to ourselves.”

Tod lost much of his palsied shyness. This girl was restoring his
self-respect; she had seen him do something finely and well, and she did
not make him feel a consummate and ignorant ass. He liked her frank
brown eyes, and her broad white teeth when she smiled, and that simple
white dress of hers. She was not so terribly fluffy and ornate as a lot
of those other women.

“Do you like games?”

“I like anything that’s natural.”

“So do I,” said Tod; “I like my swim each morning, especially when it’s
a bit rough. You feel you have beaten something.”

“I know just how you feel.”

Tod accepted her goodwill in the frank way that it was given. He was
grateful to Bertha Cragg, nor did the vanity of the self-pleased young
male suggest that the girl was “making up to him.” Tod was not fashioned
in that way. In half an hour he was talking to her without any trace of
self-consciousness, and towards the end of the afternoon he was playing
tennis as her partner, and getting the feel of his racket with the
natural quickness of a boy who had always been good at games.

They combined so well, and grew so merry and light-hearted over it that
Tod managed to forget the incident of the tea-cup and the brown stains
on his flannel trousers. And Bertha Cragg was kind to him with a new and
subtle motherliness that was very pleasant to a rather plain girl of
five-and-twenty. She liked Tod. The woman in her liked him very much
indeed.

Her brother blundered against her at the close of the afternoon. “Thanks
for looking after the kid, B. He was making rather an ass of himself.
One can’t always be running round can one?”

Bertha Cragg bent down and retied her shoe lace.

“He was shy, that’s all. You are never shy, Fred.”

“Can’t say I ever felt that way.”

“Yes, it’s rather a pity.”

 Chapter Fourteen

Caroline Crescent and “Clovelly” had taken possession of Tod Western.

Through all those rapidly moving months he was being peacefully
persuaded towards middle-class culture. The women had conceived an
interest in Tod, Miss Truebody of St. Helen’s Terrace who lent him her
Tennyson and persuaded him to go occasionally to St. Alban’s Church,
Mrs. Rathbone Cragg, Bertha Cragg, even the girl who typed in the
office. Tod had become attractive to women. He piqued the sex in them,
and with an innocence that was all the more provoking in that he was not
acutely conscious just then of the sex in himself.

Tod was finding life very interesting. He was so busy doing things that
he did not drift into any of those sauntering moods that are apt to end
in the sentimental discovery of petticoats and ribbons. He had grown to
love his work, and so keen was he that he procured books on architecture
and building construction and sat up at night reading. His day began
with a swim or a brisk walk. He played football that winter for the West
Shaston Sports Club and in the summer he spent the evenings at tennis.
There were no gaps in his day, no dawdling interludes. Sex was dormant
in him, waiting like a cunning woman for the moment when the young male
should tire of merely hitting a ball and developing his strength.

Sex too had relieved Tod of Mr. Frederick Rathbone Cragg’s mentorship.
The impressive Fred was cultivating the elder Miss Horrox, and he had no
time now for the tutelage of Tod. His sister had slipped unobtrusively
into his place. Tod went very often to “Clovelly,” and came to look on
Fred’s sister as a jolly girl without any nonsense. The sex in her never
touched him, and Bertha, with that unusual instinct of hers, hid her sex
and played the man. She was in love with Tod, and she waited.

Yet her secret tenderness, the love of a clever yet rather plain girl
for a healthy, game-playing boy, watched over Tod jealously and followed
him into all the corners of his life. She knew more about Tod perhaps
than Tod knew about himself. She knew how he spent his day, and how he
passed his evenings; Miss Truebody told her, for Miss Truebody owed much
to the Craggs. And she was at one with Mr. Julian Burgetrode in her
sensing of the unusual in Tod. She knew that he was quite unlike any
young male thing she had ever met; there was a directness about him, a
kind of delightful simplicity, the look of going a far journey in his
blue eyes, a promise of something clean and adventurous in his squarish
head and brown face. For Tod was clean, and it was his cleanness that
appealed to Bertha Cragg, who was a sensitive, a spiritual “sport” in
that very material and middle-class world.

Tod found it very easy to talk to her. She was a comrade, just like a
man friend, and he had not discovered the woman behind the frank and
game-loving girl. She had very pleasant brown eyes, white teeth, and
rather a shapely figure, and she could play a fast game at tennis and
swim almost as well as Tod. They made a pleasant, laughing,
unselfconscious pair, and Mrs. Rathbone Cragg folded her hands over it,
and did not interfere. Tod had a future and some capital. Mr. Julian
Burgetrode loomed big in the North.

Tod’s quaint franknesses were not unlike the sayings of his father, and
Bertha caught them in her lap and then hid them away in her heart.

“What do people want to make money for? It’s much jollier doing things
for love.”

“I should like to be able to swim across the Channel.”

“I can’t stand cheats.”

“Wasting your time with a lot of girls!”

She laughed over that saying, with a little twinge of self-pity—yet she
knew that he had spoken the truth. He did not spend his evenings loafing
round the bandstand, playing with a lot of fluffy young flappers or
young women with red necks and chests and sun-scorched faces. There were
no huddlings into dark corners, giggles and a curiosity that might end
in a little mock protesting scream. And she was glad.

Tod saw very little of old Shaston, the Shaston of the Flowerdews and
the Snapes. Kitty Flowerdew was married to Edwin Rook, and was expecting
a baby, but Tod knew nothing of that. Mossup was married too and settled
in that little white cottage of his, and Tod went and had tea with them
one Sunday afternoon. Mossup took him all over the house and garden, and
simpered with pride.

“Nice little kitchen, isn’t it? Olive’s a wonder. Did you ever see such
pots and pans? Come and see the chickens. Yes, nothing but egg-boxes,
old chap; the house took six at ninepence each, not so bad. Eggs? Oh,
yes, we’ll get plenty of eggs.”

After tea he led Tod into the garden and became mysteriously
confidential.

“I’m setting up on my own this autumn. Little shop in Duke Street; just
two windows and a door in between. Olive’s going to do the hats. She’s a
wonder at hats. So if you can send anyone along, old chap——”

Tod promised to tell all West Shaston and St. Margarets of Mossup’s
shop. Mossup was such a good chap, even though he did not play games.

One evening Tod met Bob Twine. He was strolling back from the St.
Margarets Tennis Club, a brown young man in very white flannels,
meditating deeply on the improving of his forehand drive.

“Hallo, Tod.”

“Hallo, Bob, old man. Haven’t seen you for years.”

Bob Twine looked shabbily smart, and that black chin of his seemed
blacker than ever.

“Say, you are some knut, kid! How’s life?”

“Quite good,” said Tod.

Bob looked him over with the eyes of a connoisseur. There was no doubt
about it, Tod was “it.” His flannel trousers were nicely creased, his
buckskin shoes white as milk, his Oxford shirt, soft collar and tie the
best productions of Messrs. Mummery, the gentleman’s outfitters in
Pomeroy Place. Tod’s hat was at the correct angle. He had collected and
absorbed all the subtle details pertaining to costume, and he carried
them with that utter lack of self-consciousness that puzzles and piques
the more florid bounder.

“They are making a pukka gent of you, old man. Quite the Burlington,
what!”

“Shut up,” said Tod.

“It’s a fact. Real West Shaston china, the genuine article. Got a dress
suit, I suppose?”

“Not yet.”

“It will come, old man. Play bridge and go to dances, and eat
strawberries and cream in people’s gardens.”

“Shut up. Come and have some supper one night.”

Twine refused, and refused with a kind of awkward sulkiness.

“Rather full up just now, kid. I’ve got a girl, and she takes some
satisfying. Well, don’t forget us all.”

“I shan’t,” said Tod, “but why not bring your—your fiancée along with
you.”

“That’s some word, old man. But she isn’t that—yet; that’s the trouble.
Well, so long.”

They parted, Tod wondering why he thought Bob Twine rather a coarse
beast, and whether he—Tod—had changed as Twine said he had. Twine
loafed away, smiling a queer, sensual, slightly cynical smile.

“They’ve got him,” he thought, “he’s going up into the real respectable
pew. Would I swop? Damn it, no! You can’t be respectable and have such a
time with the girls.”

Ten minutes later he met a fair-haired, and richly coloured young woman
on the parade. She had been waiting in one of the shelters, eating
sweets and reading a paper novel. And the face of the young woman was
the face of “Giggles,” Miss Millie Hornblower, to whom Tod had once
given a German-silver ring.

The greater part of a year had passed since Mr. Julian Burgetrode had
last played the Colossus in his toy-town, but he reappeared one day at
the Hotel Majestic, and two evenings later Miss Truebody found him on
her doorstep. The bell of No. 11 St. Helen’s Terrace had been rung with
unnecessary vigour, and Miss Truebody’s instinct was to protest. The
maid was out, and she answered the door herself.

“Yes? What do you want, please?”

She had meant to remark that the inmates of No. 11 were not deaf, but
people did not use sarcasm on Mr. Burgetrode. The fierce blue eyes, and
the red tie, and the masterful brown face were so very vivid and
arrestive. Mr. Burgetrode was always treated like a big and rather
doubtful tempered dog, and Miss Truebody forgot about the bell.

“Is Tod Western in?”

“Mr. Western? No I think he is at the tennis club.”

“Will he be long?”

Mr. Burgetrode’s fierce manner quite agitated Miss Truebody.

“Oh, no, he may be in any minute; you see, he always comes home and has
a little supper and studies before going to bed.”

“Ha!” said Mr. Burgetrode, and Miss Truebody winced.

“He studies quite hard, you know, such a nice, gentlemanly, quiet
fellow.”

“I’ll come in and wait.”

“Oh, certainly. Are you——”

“My name is Burgetrode. Will you show me his room, please?”

Miss Truebody continued her “agitato” on her way up the stairs.

“Quite a healthy, sunny room, I’m sure. I have got him a nice arm-chair;
the windows look south, you know, and he has his meals in my
sitting-room. Yes, it has been very hot to-day, hasn’t it?”

“Very,” said Mr. Burgetrode tersely, wondering if the lady was married
and had a daughter. He did not believe in a man marrying before thirty,
and he did not want Tod to marry any girl in Shaston. Mr. Burgetrode had
unusual views on sex and marriage.

Sheer nervousness kept Miss Truebody holding the door handle of Tod’s
room and making polite conversation.

“I expect he’ll be playing tennis with Miss Cragg, Mr. Burgetrode. Such
a very nice girl, and Mrs. Cragg is one of the kindest and most
Christian of women. Yes, he does smoke, just a little, poor dear boy,
but such mild tobacco, I’m sure.”

Mr. Burgetrode had picked up a brier pipe, and he took not the slightest
notice of Miss Truebody. Believing in allowing such clockwork ladies to
run themselves down, he idled round Tod’s room, looking at the books and
the furniture with the interest of a man who had vivid memories of his
own youth. Tod’s blue bathing suit was hanging over the back of a chair,
and Miss Truebody, with great decency and presence of mind, threw a
towel over it, and then “chasséd” towards the door.

“You’ll find that chair quite comfortable, Mr. Burgetrode.”

“I have no doubt of it, madam,” he said, removing the towel from the
blue bathing suit, and staring at it fixedly till Miss Truebody had
closed the door.

He was very interested in Tod’s room. The Truebody part of it was quaint
and old maidish and precise, but Tod’s share was that of the healthy
young male. There were glass lustre candlesticks, and a little white
marble cross on the mantelpiece. The furniture was mahogany of the most
obviously Victorian pattern. It appeared that Miss Truebody had dabbled
much with the paint brush at some period of her life, and the splasher
above the washstand showed a bunch of white Madonna lilies. A couple of
tambourines that hung one on each side of the overmantel had been
decorated with very wooden and wall-eyed tom-tits balancing with a grim
determination on bits of twig. But the room was snug and clean and full
of Tod and his possessions, tobacco tins, books, a pair of trousers
thrown on the bed, a collar and tie and a pair of mauve socks on the
chest of drawers, a little table in the window with ink, writing paper
and notebooks. Julian Burgetrode picked up some of Tod’s books, and
found that he was reading architecture, and practical building, and an
elementary handbook on sanitation. Mr. Burgetrode smiled. The Craggs had
sent him very flattering reports on Tod.

His protégé arrived and found Mr. Burgetrode sitting side-saddle on the
window-sill, and enjoying that evening glimpse of old Shaston and the
sea.

“Well, Master Ajax?”

Tod was pleased to see Mr. Burgetrode, and Mr. Burgetrode was pleased
with the pleasure on the boy’s brown face.

“I’m sorry you had to wait, sir; I would have stayed in if I had known.”

“Not a bit of it. You’ve grown.”

“Have I?” laughed Tod.

“Let’s see, how old are you?”

“Nearly twenty, sir.”

“Wish I could be twenty again. You are coming out to dinner with me,
Tod, yes, just as you are. This seems a clean and comfortable sort of
place.”

“Miss Truebody is very kind, a good sort,” said Tod.

“And religious?”

“Very religious, sir. People are very kind—and I am having a good time,
and I like the work.”

“Houses?”

“I never get tired of houses, sir, all sorts of houses. I’m reading
quite a lot.”

“Excellent!” said Mr. Burgetrode, with a queer gleam of the eyes. “Now
come along to dinner.”

Tod dined at the “Majestic,” and Mr. Burgetrode saw that the lad had
changed. He had developed poise, and he was no longer embarrassed by
waiters and people in evening dress. He had put on new social clothes.
But the real Tod was there as of old, frank, fearless quaintly direct
and eager, a happier Tod perhaps, a boy who had the makings of a very
big man.

He talked away to Mr. Burgetrode, and if the “Colossus” asked a few
questions they were very significant and pertinent questions, and Tod
responded to them with ingenuous readiness.

His talk was all of houses, not only of houses as they were, but houses
as they might be and ought to be. He was interested in the conceiving
and building of houses and cottages, and in the atmosphere of their
surroundings. Tod had seen some of the “county” houses during the year,
and the beauty and the soft serenity of many of them had left a vivid
picture in his brain. He had not forgotten that house-hunting adventure
with Arthur Mossup, and those stuffy and dreary little grey boxes at the
back of Shaston town, and Tod, with the natural idealism of youth,
believed that such mean beastliness in brick should never have been
perpetrated.

“There ought to be gardens and trees,” he said, “and plenty of places to
play in. Why should people live in rabbit-hutches?”

“The speculator’s idea of economics, my boy.”

“Well, it oughtn’t to be,” said Tod; “and all those dirty little houses,
each with a dirty little kitchen, and making more dirt and mess. And no
baths.”

“You would like to make a new town all out of your own head, with
gardens and baths, and playing grounds, and concert halls and fine
cookhouses?”

“I should,” said Tod, his eyes alight, “but who’d pay for it?”

“Exactly!” said Mr. Burgetrode; “we don’t like paying for other people’s
health and comfort.”

It was rather obvious to Mr. Burgetrode that the Craggs had not pushed
Tod against the financial thorns of the business. He had not got himself
pricked and exasperated as he had done in the shops of Messrs. Flowerdew
and Snape. The Craggs were respectable people. They did not perpetuate
the more flagrant cynicisms, and West Shaston was too comfortable and
well fed and wealthy to bother about elemental economics. The Craggs had
made money rather easily; they had not developed the clawing hands, the
fierce masticating muscles and the canine teeth of the average
Flowerdew. Tod had not yet discovered “commercial competition,” that
devil god of the industrial age, the god of the unprincipled and the
greedy.

Mr. Burgetrode even persuaded Tod to betray his views on girls, and
discovered, more by inference, that Tod was not interested in them for
the moment. Mr. Burgetrode had introduced the Craggs into the
conversation, and he heard all about Fred Cragg the impressive, Mrs.
Cragg whose lap was full of charity, and Bertha Cragg who could swim
like a man and place a back-hand volley down the side line with fine
precision.

“She’s a good sort,” said Tod; “no nonsense, just like a man, you know.
Can’t stand the giggling sort, all ribbons and hat brim. The Craggs have
been very kind to me, sir, very kind indeed.”

“I’m glad to hear it,” said Mr. Burgetrode, beginning to wonder whether
Miss Cragg was not a rather dangerous young woman. He did not want Tod
to marry for many years to come, and he did not want him to marry a girl
of the middle classes.

 Chapter Fifteen

Tod made another friend that summer, a Mr. Lavender, who kept a quiet
little tobacco shop in Greek Street, behind Caroline Crescent. Mr.
Lavender made a living by selling pipes and tobacco, but in the life
that mattered he was a naturalist and a lover of birds. He was a big,
fair, quiet man with a soft voice and blue eyes that were always ready
to smile, a great placid, lovable creature, a born countryman and hunter
of the woods and uplands, an idler by rivers, one of the few who cared
more for life than for money. As an ornithologist he was no mean
authority, and it is probable that he was the best read man in Shaston.
He read because he loved good books, and not with the spirit of Fred
Cragg, who wanted to collect information, borrowed stones and brilliants
with which to dazzle fools. Mr. Lavender had lost his egotism in the
great lap of Nature. He knew things that Frederick Cragg would never
know—because Life was his book, Life that cares nothing for the
intellectual vanities of the fussily clever people.

Tod bought his first pipe from Mr. Lavender, but their friendship began
when Tod discovered by some chance that Mr. Lavender had known his
father.

“I should think I did know him! We had a tramp’s holiday together
twenty-five years ago. Your father was one of the rare birds.”

“In what way?” Tod asked, intensely interested.

“Cared for the real things. Money was dirt to your father, useful dirt,
just what it should be.”

“My mother used to say that my father ought to have been a great man.”

“He was a great man, but he wasn’t a successful one according to modern
standards. There’s a difference.”

Tod supposed there was; and in the course of a few weeks he and Mr.
Lavender became close friends.

Tom Lavender spent each Sunday in the country. He would take the early
train for Bidhurst or Willoborough, and walk five and twenty miles
before sunset, carrying a haversack, a pair of field-glasses and a stout
ash stick. He would lie in a ditch or under a tree for an hour, watching
some bird, and sometimes he turned into a village church and joined in
the service. There was not a wild flower that he could not tell you the
name of, and he could spin all sorts of fascinating yarns about the life
of an ant or a bee. Nor was he a mere naturalist. He had a sense of
colour and of atmosphere, a love of landscape and an eye for all the
strangeness of beauty.

He made a disciple of Tod, and they spent many Sundays together tramping
the lanes and the field-paths and watching the life of the woods. Tod
learnt many things from old Tom Lavender: the tricks of the squirrel,
the notes and plumage of shy and elusive birds, how the woodpecker earns
his living, where bumble bees live, how trees should be grown, how to
tell an adder, where to look for plovers’ eggs, where trout might still
be found; but the most precious picture of all was the picture of old
Lavender himself. Tod was the better for having realised the gentleness
and the wisdom of the old man. Lavender gave his youth a new outlook on
life, a new choice, a new understanding. Here was a man who loved things
for themselves, who despised money and pitied greed, a man who was not
feverish, and to whom the world could offer no bribe.

“It’s up to all of us to choose,” he told Tod. “There are two ways of
owning things—by loving them and not wanting to put them in your
pocket, or by grabbing Life and trying to sell it. It’s up to all of us
to choose.”

“I don’t want a lot of money,” said Tod.

“Somebody else may.”

“Who?”

“The woman you marry.”

Tod had not thought of that.

“Why should she?”

“Women like a man to be successful. In the old days you got a horse and
knocked over other men, or you went on a great adventure. Success and
money are synonymous nowadays.”

“I shan’t marry a girl like that. Besides, my mother loved my father.”

“She wasn’t a money fool. She thought of the inside of her man before
she thought of the furniture.”

“That’s true,” said Tod.

So life promised well for Julian Burgetrode’s protégé. He had his feet
on the upland track and his eyes on the clear sky ahead of him. He was
healthy in body and mind, and he seemed in a fair way to escape the two
things that damn a young man most, cheap women and cheap money. He
played a good deal of tennis that summer, and he played much of it with
Bertha Cragg. He had joined the local club and had become something of
an expert.

He was known among the gossips as “Bertha’s boy.” The middle-class young
women were making a jest of it, but Tod was the most innocent thing on
earth just then.

Bertha knew.

But Bertha Cragg was not an average young woman. There was a fineness
about her, a reticence, a shrewdness that could watch and wait. She
understood Tod, glimpsed him as a healthy wholesome, delightful boy
whose whole attention was concentrated on the life outside him, on doing
things, and on doing them well. He was deep in his book of adventure,
his Crusoe story, and there was no woman in it. Some day a ship would
heave-to off Crusoe’s Island, the ship of sex, and Bertha Cragg hoped to
be the young mistress of that ship.

She loved Tod very dearly, but she hid her love so well that he never
caught the sheen of it. She wanted him to come to her, to open his eyes
to her and find that she had played herself into his life.

“Dear Bertha is growing quite pretty.”

Old ladies made that remark to her mother, and it was true.

She had a mass of fine dark hair, good teeth, very pleasant eyes, and
the bloom of her sex was upon her. She took great trouble with her
dress, wore the neatest of shoes, and was never gaudy.

Yet Tod remained blind. He liked her better than any other girl in
Shaston, and he liked her so well and in such a fashion that she
wondered whether he would go on liking her in that way till—— But
Bertha always shrank from the thought of the other girl who might swoop
in and carry Tod off in a night’s flirtation. She bore with the beastly
gossip and the sniggers and the knowledge that she was being watched by
the merciless eyes of competing flappers. She was always frank and merry
with Tod. She was his playmate, his sports-woman; and her dream was that
some day he would look into her eyes and discover the woman.

They entered at the local tournament as partners in the mixed doubles,
and they won.

Tod had been so keen on winning. Their last match was an uphill affair
against a pair who were the local experts. Tod, over zealous, had struck
an uncertain and eccentric patch, and was smashing into the net and
putting his drives out of the court. It was the girl who steadied him
and pulled them through.

“You’re worrying too much——”

“I know. I’m a silly rabbit; I want you to win.”

“I shan’t be cross if we don’t. Let’s stonewall for a while.”

They did, and Tod’s serenity came back. Bertha was returning everything
with that beautiful forehand drive of hers, and finding the side lines
with nearly all her shots.

“Set all, four all!”

“We are going to beat them, Tod.”

She wanted to call him “dear.”

And beat them they did, Bertha making the finishing stroke with a crisp
volley down the centre of the court. Tod had turned to her with an
exultant, happy face, “Oh, great shot!” and had tossed his racket in the
air.

He was almost the lover as they walked off the court together, and the
woman in her thrilled.

“You won it, you know. I should have been all over the place but for
you.”

She laughed softly and coloured.

“We never annoy each other, do we?”

“Rather not! I say, you must be tired. Come over here and sit down, and
I’ll get you something to drink.”

She was very happy when he made her put on her white blanket-coat.

She was very happy, too, when they took the prize, and Tod made an
ingenuous and half-shy little speech.

“My partner did it. She was the best player on the court.”

And some chit remarked to a friend:

“I believe she’s hooked him at last.”

But Tod remained as platonic and as innocent as ever, her playmate and
nothing more. The winter arrived, and he bought a dress-suit, took
dancing lessons and learnt to play bridge. He was popular and had nice
manners, and it leaked out that some very influential person was
interested in him. The tennis club carried on weekly subscription dances
during the winter, and Tod danced a great deal with Bertha Cragg. He
danced with other girls also, and she made a point of being very gay
whenever Tod had other partners. There were certain girls whom she
hated, mischievous little wretches who made eyes at Tod, and were quite
ready to make Bertha look a fool.

In fact, there was a game played by several of them, and they called it
“Bagging Bertha’s boy.” It did not prove to be a very successful game,
because Tod appeared to be so preposterously unsentimental and so loyal
to his comrade.

“You dance better than anyone else,” he told her; “most girls seem to be
such silly fools.”

Perhaps it was inevitable that Mrs. Rathbone Cragg should intervene. She
had eyes and ears and a maternal pride, and she was very fond of her
daughter. Nor had she any spite against Tod; she was quite ready for him
to become engaged to her girl. That was just it: the relationship needed
labelling, giving proper social significance.
