She spoke to Bertha, and there was a quarrel. Mrs. Cragg had not thought
of quarrelling, but it happened. Some women are very sensitive.

“But, my dear, it can’t go on like this. You must realise that you are
getting talked about. It’s not nice. I know the boy is thoughtless——”

“I’m in love with him,” said the daughter with a queer, hard frankness.

“My dear child, that may be, but you ought to make him realise——”

Bertha flared out with sudden passion.

“You mean, I ought to do what most girls would do, grab him for myself
and land him on the sentimental hook. I know it could be done; he’s such
a dear boy, and sensitive. But I won’t.”

“You prefer to make a fool of yourself? Really, you are old enough——”

Bertha stood up.

“Mother, I have got a sort of pride that you don’t understand. I’m not
going to be clever with Tod; I love him in a better way than that. I
want him to come to me, if he is to come at all. As for all the beastly
gossip, it is not going to make me a little cad. I’m his friend; I can’t
help it—if I am not yet anything else.”

She was much moved, and almost in tears when she reached the door. Her
mother, a good soul in spite of all her conventionality, jumped up and
held out her hands.

“Bertha dear, we ought not to quarrel. You always were such a proud
child.”

“I can’t help it, Mother.”

“Don’t you think it may be that Tod doesn’t realise? I know you have
never helped him to realise, and he has seen so much of you. If you were
to make a kind of little break—like taking something away from a child
so that he missed it and found out how much he wanted it?”

Bertha looked straight into her mother’s eyes.

“Perhaps. To please you, I’ll try. Supposing I go and stay with Aunt
Ethel for a month?”

Mrs. Cragg kissed her.

“The very thing,” she said.

If Tod had had more personal vanity he might have discovered Bertha’s
secret. She went away to her aunt who lived at Surbiton, and Tod managed
to see her off at the station, a piece of boyish courtesy that clouded
the issue. He was not conscious of any gap in his life when Bertha Cragg
had gone. He did his work, played his games, took his food with the
usual zest, and went out with Thomas Lavender on Sundays, for the
country was never dead to old Lavender; he loved it wet and he loved it
fine. Tod danced once a week, and Mrs. Cragg had him to “Clovelly” to
play bridge. She was rather motherly and mysterious with Tod, but Tod
remained the most healthily unsubtle young idiot, and most ingenuously
blind.

The Horroxes were giving a big dance after the New Year, and Bertha
wrote to Tod to ask him if he would go with her. Each girl was taking a
man. Tod accepted, and thought no more about it. He liked dancing with
Bertha Cragg because it always seemed so easy, and he had not to
chatter; they both loved dancing for itself, the quick glide of their
youth together and the lilt of the music. There had never been any
mystery in it to Tod, no quiver of madness in the nearness of her, in
the whiteness of her shoulders or the smell of her hair. She had not the
spark to set him alight, and that was her woman’s tragedy.

Bertha did not return to Shaston till the day before the dance, and she
did not see Tod until he came up to “Clovelly” to share her cab. She had
brought a new dress with her from town, a most beautiful rose silk
thing, and she had spent more than an hour in dressing. It was her
“Flaming June,” her challenge of the youth in him, the conscious and
romantic assertion of the sex in her. She had scented her hair, and
fastened a spray of roses in her dress, and as she came down the stairs
a little pair of silver shoes flashed in and out under the rose-red
skirt.

Tod was waiting for her in the hall. He was glad to see Bertha, but it
was a sort of brother gladness, a cheerful “Hope-you-have-a-good-time”
welcome. There was no mystery about him. He took her black velvet cloak
and placed it over his shoulders.

“Very good of you to ask me to come,” he said; “the cab is waiting. Do
you think you’ll be warm enough in that thing?”

“Quite,” she answered very calmly, and smiling at her mother who had
come into the hall to see them off.

“Mind you take great care of her, Tod.”

“Of course I shall,” said Tod.

“I think we were lucky to get her back safely from Surbiton. She was
rather gay there.” And Mrs. Cragg smiled mysteriously and conspiringly
at Tod.

Bertha betrayed a moment’s impatience. She loathed being advertised.

“Time we were off. Come along, Tod.”

It is probable that that was the most unhappy evening the girl had ever
spent. Tod sat like “good dog Tray” in the cab, and talked like a
brother. The dance was a very sumptuous affair, for old Horrox had
plenty of money and knew how to spend it. He liked “display,” and the
Regent’s Hotel knew how to cater for people with social vanity. The
string band had been brought from town, and the room was decorated with
rambler roses. To do the unexpected and the expensive thing in the
middle of winter was an extravagance old Horrox loved. He had been an
expert at window-dressing in the days of his youth.

“Aren’t the roses lovely?”

“They are just the colour of your dress,” said Tod.

That was the only suggestively personal remark he made to her during the
evening. The interlude had taught him nothing. She was still his
playmate and nothing more, and he was as much at his ease with her as
though she were a man. She had hoped perhaps for a sudden and subtle
shyness in this boy, a sense of strangeness, an unfamiliar “What has
happened to you?” in his frank eyes. She gave him five dances, and he
always welcomed the return of their partnership, but he did not come to
her with the look of a lover, but with the air of a man calling her to a
game.

She was strangely silent as they drove home in the cab, lying well back
in her corner with a kind of languor. Tod was puzzled by her silence. He
did not understand.

“Tired?”

“A little.”

“It’s nearly three, you know, and I’m called at seven.”

“Poor Tod!”

She was strangely gentle with him; all her bitterness was against
herself. A sort of mother pathos softened her mood. She would not let
him leave the cab when it reached “Clovelly.”

“No. You need not fuss about me. You can drive straight on to the
terrace.”

“It’s been a jolly evening, hasn’t it?”

“Splendid! Good night!”

The cab rolled on, and Tod felt a healthy pleasure in the prospect of
going to bed. Bertha Cragg was in her room, bending forward and looking
at her face in her mirror. It was a plain face—a little drawn, tired,
colourless, for the joy of life had left it. She felt that she hated
that face of hers, hated it as she had never hated anything on earth
before.

“You fool!”

Her hands went to the shoulder straps of that exquisite dress of hers,
and ripped it relentlessly from bosom to waist.

 Chapter Sixteen

Three weeks later Tod met Millie Hornblower, the lady of the
German-silver ring.

It was a sunny February afternoon, with a cold north-east wind blowing,
the sea a deep blue ridged with white. Tod had been “writing up” a house
on the seafront, and he was walking back along the Colonnade, and had
stepped aside to let a perambulator pass, when some one spoke to him.

“I don’t suppose you remember me, Mr. Western?”

He turned and found himself looking into the china-blue eyes of a girl
who was wearing a set of black furs. Her gloves were black and so was
her hat, but beneath it her hair shone out like a red-gold wreath, a
flash of colour that made him forget her sombre clothes. The “Giggles”
of Pentworth had grown into a perilously handsome girl. She had one of
those soft, round, glowing faces, with a complexion that old-fashioned
ballad writers called “cream and roses”; moreover, it was natural. She
was of middle height, deep of bosom, with a beautiful plump throat and
well-shaped shoulders. But her mouth was the live flame of her beauty.
It was a disastrous mouth—beautifully sensual and provoking, very red,
the upper lip slightly lifted at the centre, a mouth that made men look
at her with eyes of desire.

“It’s Miss Hornblower,” said Tod.

He had given her a little grudging lift of the hat, and he stood
considering her with a shy reserve. Yes, he remembered her very well,
for had she not killed the wonder-heroine of those books of adventure
and most basely sold his love for a bag of chocolate creams?

“So you are at Craggs’,” said the girl, sensing those memories in the
stare of his eyes.

“Yes.”

“Quite the man-about-town.”

“Oh?” said Tod, abrupt and uneasy.

He could not think of anything to say to her, though he was acutely
conscious of her as a compellingly pretty girl. He thought that she had
changed, and that her face looked softer and pleasanter, and that the
memories of Pentworth were no longer fit to be remembered. Physical
beauty is so convincing to a young man whose ideals have not been
tarnished, and who does not know that a little harlot may have the face
of an angel.

“You have changed a good deal,” said the girl; “I wonder if you would
have spoken to me if you had seen me first?”

“Of course I should,” said Tod; “that’s to say if I——”

He got bogged there, and she did not try to help him out. His
awkwardness made him stiff and difficult, and she felt that he was
putting on side.

“Oh, don’t explain. You’re in a hurry, I expect. Of course, Wednesdays
don’t affect you.”

“So you’re in a shop?”

It was an innocent bit of blurting idiocy, and he knew at once that he
had said something disastrous, though to the self in him it had meant
nothing at all. She gave a little lift of the head, looked at him
queerly, lips pressed together, the pupils of her eyes small and very
black.

“Thank you. Good afternoon, Mr. Western,” and she set her face westwards
and walked on.

Tod saw that he had hurt her, and Tod had always hated hurting people.
He walked back to the office with a furious sense of soreness inside
him, and the growing realisation of the fact that the girl must have
assumed him to be a snob. A remark of Bob Twine’s recurred to him:
“Suppose you will be getting too big for us shoppies.” Tod had been
indignant, but his present indignation was a chivalrous rage. The mood
was miraculous. Miss Hornblower became in a moment a vision of softness,
accusation and pathos. He remembered noticing that her gloves were
shabby, and then he realised the suggestiveness of her black clothes and
hat.

And so Tod landed on Calypso’s island, and his Calypso had a tragic
mouth and grieved blue eyes.

He spent the whole of the following week in trying to cross paths with
Millie Hornblower, and to win a chance of wiping that rage out of his
heart. His chivalry went nightly down the parade into old Shaston town,
and into every likely and alluring street. He tried two churches on the
Sunday, and discovered no grieved and blue-eyed girl in either of them;
but Tod had a good deal of obstinacy, and the smart did not wear away
from him as quickly as it melted from the hearts of most people.

When at last he did meet her she was walking down Duke Street with a
girl friend, and though Tod lifted his hat and broke his step with the
hesitation of a man who wanted her to stop, she swept past him, a proud
young ship in full sail. She had given him a nod of the head and no
smile.

“Who’s that?” asked her friend.

“A boy I used to know.”

“He wanted you to stop.”

“He can want it a little more. I’m on my dignity, old thing.”

Tod was piqued and troubled. He was such an innocent man thing in the
Garden of Eden that, unlike Father Adam, he accused himself and not Eve;
and he behaved as though this particular Eve was made like a male.
Bertha Cragg had clouded his conception of the other sex. She played the
game of life like a good man, never cheated, and had hidden all her
subtlety, showing him only the face of a frank and straightforward boy.
If Tod had taken up his picture of Miss Hornblower where his Pentworth
memories had left it, and not let sex smudge it, he would not have been
the dear fool of a sensitive imagination. When a girl uses both her sex
and her wits, the only opponent who can do her justice is a cynic or a
_roué_.

Mr. Thomas Lavender had no Tod trudging with him over the Sussex hills
on that first warm Sunday in March. There was spring in the air, an
adventurous blueness in the sea, suggestion in the soft swish of the
waves along the shingle, a “Come and find me” in the mood of the day.
And Tod went forth like a pagan, with no thoughts of Miss Truebody and
church bells, and the blood of St. Alban the Martyr, a young man
patrolling the parade with a vague foresense of adventure tingling in
his spine. In one of the glass shelters half hidden behind a hedge sat
my Lady Propriety, reading a book with an air of careful and ladylike
isolation. Her blue eyes looked vague and inattentive, and virginal as
the sea and the sky. She was aware of nothing and of everything, and she
did not appear to see Tod until his shadow was at her feet.

“Good morning!”

“Good morning!”

Her upward glance was surprised and critical. She waited, her book open
on her lap, a virgin fortress with gates closed and flag flying.

Tod had to attack.

“May I sit down a moment?”

“It’s not my seat.”

He sat down a good yard away, very uneasy, the young male thing
unconsciously full of its maleness.

“I’m afraid I offended you the other day?”

Her blue eyes held him baffled with the opaque and defensive stare of a
moment.

“Oh?”

“If I did, it was quite——”

“Unimportant.”

She supplied him with the word, and then returned rather obviously to
her book. A moment later, like the _retiarius_ of old, she made a quick
throw of the net.

“I work in a shop, you know.”

“So do I,” said Tod.

“That’s news. I shouldn’t have called an estate office a shop. St.
Margarets rather fancies itself.”

“Do you mean I’m a snob?”

He had said it. He had sounded his trumpet, marched out his men and
offered battle. The field was his.

“One can’t help one’s impressions, Mr. Western. I have learnt to take
people as I find them. After all, it doesn’t matter.”

“Yes it does,” said Tod obstinately.

She gave him an ironical glance.

“Oh, why?”

“Because I’m not a snob.”

“Am I to take your word for it? I suppose I was a silly, impulsive ass
to speak to you that afternoon.”

“It was very nice of you to speak to me. And you went off in such a
hurry.”

She turned over a page and smiled.

“Perhaps I’m rather touchy. I just did the natural thing, and you looked
over your collar like a shocked curate. I’m not used to being snubbed.”

“But I didn’t mean to snub you.”

“Well, I felt snubbed, anyhow. And a girl’s rather touchy when things
have gone wrong, and she is beastly poor.”

Tod sat there in silence for a while, tapping the toe of a boot with his
cane, a very serious and generously sentimental Tod. She had set the
youth alight in him, and though he was still unconscious of the fire
that was to be, he had no suspicion that all this was strategy and part
of the campaign. Millie Hornblower had seen Tod in Shaston long before
he had seen her, and the promise of the man in Tod had pleased her. He
was a brown, strong, quick-moving youngster, with alert eyes and an air
of adventure. And there was a glamour about Tod. He was supposed to have
a little money, and what was more important, inside influence. He was
going up. Millie Hornblower had learnt quite a number of things about
men and life, and the sort of future a girl in a shop might expect. She
intended to get out of that shop with éclat and romance, and not as the
wife of some dull and semi-paternal middle-class husband vaguely fat and
forty.

“I’ve been trying to see you ever since that day,” said Tod at last.

“Have you?”

“You wouldn’t stop that afternoon in Duke Street.”

“Did you want me to stop?”

“Yes, I felt I’d offended you—hurt your feelings.”

“Thank you,” she said. “I don’t know that I’m asking for anybody’s
pity.”

Tod fought stubbornly, like a man who was being outboxed and punished in
every round.

“It’s not pity. And I’m not a snob. I was in two shops before I went to
the Craggs. Besides——”

“Well?”

“I want to be friends.”

She gave him a quick side-glance, and then looked out to sea. She had
her suppliant obstinately chastened, but those Pentworth memories needed
final contradiction, and her desire was to appear somewhat
unapproachable and proud, no casual girl to be picked up just as a man
pleased.

“We’re not a couple of kids—now,” she said seriously.

“Of course not.”

“I was a mischievous little wretch at Pentworth, wasn’t I? I don’t make
friends now in five minutes; if a girl has any self-respect she keeps
herself to herself, especially if she’s in a shop.”

Tod’s answer came like an echo.

“That’s it,” he said; “that’s why I feel that it’s awfully good of you
to let me sit here and talk. I’m not a cad, you know, and I’m grateful.”

She let herself smile at him.

“You used to be rather a nice boy—Mr. Western, otherwise I shouldn’t
have let you—— Oh, well, I’m not very fond of men; they are such mean
beasts.”

“I should not have thought anyone could be mean to you,” said Tod.

So Tod was forgiven and his conscience satisfied. He sat there with blue
sea in front of him and the blue eyes beside him, and made polite
conversation that tended towards the inevitably personal plane. Miss
Hornblower put her book aside and allowed Tod to possess all her
attention, but she was tantalisingly cool and correct, the very
antithesis of the “Giggles” of Pentworth. Tod was not to be allowed to
think that he could open the gate and walk into the garden just as he
chose.

By one of those curious coincidences Bertha Cragg, the elder Miss Horrox
and Frederick Cragg passed that shelter while Tod was absorbed in
propitiating Millie Hornblower. Tod did not see them, nor did he guess
what was happening in another woman’s heart. Bertha had glanced at the
other girl’s eyes and mouth, that mouth that was like the red mouth of
the woman in Rossetti’s picture of “Fazio’s Mistress.” The thing had
happened. Tod had met that other sort of woman, the woman who did not
rush about in the sun, but who just sat and waited for young Adam to
steal her the apples.

 Chapter Seventeen

The morning dip in the sea, the smiting of a white ball down a length of
green grass, marching into Sussex village with Mr. Tom Lavender, spring
woods and primrose time and the watching of birds; work in the office,
houses, favourite books, all the healthy ritual of a young man’s open
air life, gave place to a loitering at corners, a parading of the
seafront, the game of the moth and the candle. A girl had taken all the
colour of life for her eyes and her mouth and her clothes. She had put
on the veil of make-believe, and the real face of her soul was hidden.

Tod Western had ideals, very clean and sturdy ideals. There was nothing
mawkish or decadent about them, no ladylike pre-Raphaelite milk and
moonlight hyper-refinement. He had never had to analyse what he felt
about women; youth and instinct go hand in hand, and it may be that
instinct has the face of a boy’s mother. It is the man of forty who
stands and analyses, and has the subtle understanding of a mature
wisdom. Tod had a sort of soul-picture of the girl a man should love. He
wanted her to be beautiful and he expected her to be gentle, truthful,
not greedy about money, not cheap or noisy, no player of mean tricks.
All that is inside a man who has had a mother worth loving. It is part
of his blood and his brain. If love and youth did not wish to make
things better and finer than we know them to be, life would be a sorry
business. Christ was a young man. Happy is that man who rediscovers that
young Christ in himself when the hairs of his worldly head are turning
grey.

And “Giggles” played the part of my Lady Propriety, a pink and white
Chloe who exclaimed, “La, sir!” and who was serious, sedate and coy. In
her natural and unpremeditated moments she was one of those girls who
always had a finger in her mouth, or was patting her hair or looking
obliquely at mirrors, shop windows or men. In Millie Hornblower’s case
it was the little finger of her right hand that crooked itself into the
left corner of her red mouth and dragged the lower lip down very
slightly. She had very fat white hands with dimples where the knuckles
should be and fingers tapering to soft, blunt ends. She was very fond of
sweets and of strong scents, but her sensing of the man in Tod taught
her to suppress these predilections and to eschew too much colour.

Some things were not “naice.” She was using the word “nice”—or “naice,”
as she pronounced it—a great deal just then.

“I’d rather not sit on the stands, Mr. Tod.”

“Why not?”

“It’s not quite naice.”

And Tod found himself learning to make all sorts of subtle
discriminations, and the natural and sensitive decency of the lad made
him an apt learner. Miss Hornblower had a quick eye for all the proper
formalities. She liked Tod to wear gloves and a bowler hat, and to take
off his right hand glove before he shook hands with her. They walked
very sedately up and down the Parade, and if they sat on a seat it was
sure to be some very public seat, the sort of seat where a “naice girl”
would sit with a man and advertise the niceness of her virtue to
passers-by.

She softly but firmly suppressed any suggestion of walks in the country;
it was still primrose time, and not quite the month of June. She let Tod
take her once or twice to the pier concerts, but a girl friend went with
them, and on one particular evening Tod had a great experience in trying
to explain away a rather vulgar “turn” in a variety show that was
running there for a week.

Miss Hornblower got up and walked out, and Tod found that the
responsibility was his. She was on her dignity, a pretty prude who
accused him.

“I don’t think it was naice of you to take me to that.”

“How did I know?” said Tod, honestly upset.

“Do you mean that you thought I shouldn’t mind?”

“Good heavens, no, Millicent!”—she had ordered him to call her
Millicent—“I didn’t know there was anything in the show that would——”

“You ought to have known. A man oughtn’t to chance a thing being beastly
and vulgar.”

“I’m awfully sorry. I ought to have gone and seen it first. I’m awfully
sorry.”

She had impressed him. He had liked the way she had got up and walked
out, like a Christian martyr refusing to burn incense before the altar
of a pagan god.

Meanwhile, she was entangling him in a net of confidences—confidences
that make a passionate appeal to a generous boy. There were the
disasters that had fallen upon the Hornblower family. Millie’s father
had died of pneumonia, a drunkard’s pneumonia, but she did not tell Tod
that. He had left no money, and the family had scattered. One of the
boys had gone to sea; two of the girls were in London shops; Mrs.
Hornblower herself was living with a brother whom Millie described as a
gentleman in business. Millie had had to face the hard realities of
life, and to set about procuring herself food and clothes.

“I don’t think there is anything dishonourable in a girl having to
work,” she said to Tod, with touching simplicity.

“Dishonourable! I respect you so much for doing it.”

“And then, of course, there’s Mother,” she explained. “I have to help
Mother.”

She allowed Tod to understand that Mrs. Hornblower’s impecuniosity was
almost a joy to a loving daughter.

“I don’t grudge the little I can give her. Uncle Alfred is quite well
off, but Ma has her pride, and so have I.”

“I think you are splendid,” said Tod, with shining eyes.

But she refused to be regarded as a heroine.

“It’s nothing to boast about. It’s little enough after all the years
Mother looked after me. But it’s beastly being poor.”

“How do you find it beastly?”

“Well, there’s Horrox’s—the shop. You don’t know perhaps how a girl can
get treated in a shop. A naice boy like you wouldn’t believe it.”

“What do they do?” Tod asked.

She looked at her shoes with an air of rich embarrassment.

“Oh, I couldn’t talk about it; it’s too beastly. Some of the men are
such cads; they expect a girl to let them do things.”

“What things?”

Tod was getting up on his horse, the same gallant war-horse that had
been ridden by his father.

“Oh, I couldn’t tell you. Sometimes the shopwalkers, or even the ‘heads’
want a girl to sell herself, and if she’s a naice girl and respectable,
she gets dismissed for putting a cad in his place.”

Tod flamed up.

“I’d like to talk to a cad like that. I’d give him such a thrashing!”

Her blue eyes held his.

“Would you?”

“I should think I would. I never knew there were such cads about.”

“And they are such liars, too. If a girl flouts them they tell all sorts
of horrid lies about her, and what can a girl do?”

“The cowards! But has it ever happened to you?”

She became confused—almost frightened.

“Don’t! Don’t talk about it, Tod. There was someone who—well, I don’t
think he’ll try it again.”

“If he does, you’ll tell me?”

“Perhaps.”

“Promise to tell me; a cad like that wants a lesson.”

“You’d thrash him?” she asked, with flattering simplicity and wonder.

“I’d tear the heart out of him,” said Tod.

Knowing that Mr. Robert Twine still served at Horrox’s Tod had referred
to him as a friend, but Miss Hornblower had spoken discouragingly of Mr.
Twine.

“That black man? Yes, he’s in the glove department, but he’s not my
style.”

“Bob’s not a bad sort.”

“I don’t like him,” she had insisted; “not that I know much about him,
but he is the kind of man a naice girl doesn’t trust.”

Tod had said no more. He remembered having felt that Bob Twine was
rather a coarse beast in his attitude towards women.

Tod was on the way to getting himself talked about in West Shaston. He
was breaking with his old haunts and habits. He had been seen but rarely
at the tennis club, and people noticed his absence from “Clovelly.”
“Bertha’s boy” had become a bandstand and seafront devotee, and the
inference was obvious. Some flapper soon discovered that Mr. Western was
always walking about with one of the girls from Horrox’s shop, a girl
whose beauty had made her undisguisable, for Millicent Hornblower was
one of those sun-women whom everybody notices and whom nobody forgets.
People wondered what the Craggs thought about it, and Bertha Cragg in
particular, Bertha who had been so conspicuously interested in Mr.
Burgetrode’s protégé, but the Craggs satisfied nobody’s curiosity. Tod
lapsed gradually from their circle—that was all.

Bertha Cragg dominated the family during that obscure social crisis, and
impressed her spirit upon them. She bathed and played tennis with more
than her old enthusiasm, and went about with such an air of healthy
cheerfulness that people had no excuse for referring to her as “poor
Bertha.” She snubbed her brother when he thought it his duty to be
confidential and impressive. After all, it was a very trivial matter, a
boy of twenty falling in love with a golden-haired shop-girl, very
trivial and very natural. You did not stand on the seafront and draw
everybody’s attention to the remarkable fact that the sea was coming in.

When she met Tod she showed him the same old frankness, and Tod’s eyes
were never opened to the woman in her. She forgave him because she
realised his innocence, and because she was rather unusual. The bitter
jest was against herself, and she accepted it, making no moan and
showing no malice; but she hated the girl who had captured Tod—hated
her almost as much as she hated her own plain face.

She had been ambitious for the boy—her boy.

“I think I could have made him happy, and I know I could have helped
him,” she said in her own heart.

But that other girl? What would she make of Tod?

So poignant and strong was her curiosity that she went more than once to
the shop of Horrox & Horrox, and contrived to be served by the girl with
the red mouth and the flamboyant hair, the girl whom she had seen
sitting with Tod in that shelter. These two women were antipathetic,
fire and snow, even across a haberdashery counter.

“I want a silk scarf, please.”

“Yes, madam. Any particular colour?”

“A soft yellow.”

Scarves were brought, and Miss Hornblower betrayed all the faults of the
bad saleswoman. She was casual, superior, ready with a vague sneer if a
purchaser asked for something that was less expensive. She was Venus
caged behind a counter, bored, absorbed in the cult of her own
comeliness.

“Madame should never wear yellow.”

Bertha Cragg flushed and let the impertinence pass.

“How much is this one?”

“Nineteen and sevenpence halfpenny.”

“Have you anything cheaper?”

Millie stared at the plain girl.

“Oh, yes, madam, we have a cheap line for customers who don’t want the
best article, but the colours are not so naice.”

“Will you show me some, please?”

Miss Hornblower’s languor was extreme. She went in search of the
“cheaper line,” chattered for a half a minute to another assistant, gave
the glad eye to a shopwalker, and then returned to her customer. Bertha
had been watching the girl, and watching her with a jealous intentness,
and her imagination saw Tod lying in the lap of this glowingly selfish
and red sexed thing, this little haberdashery Delilah whose soul was a
box of sweets and a sealskin coat. Bertha Cragg was too subtle a woman
to be deceived. The scent was so pronounced, the colour so vivid. This
was no case of a pretty, wholesome, commonplace girl transfigured and
become an angel in the eyes of a boy, a little romance that makes the
world a little cleaner and less mercenary, a romance that ends in a
beautiful comradeship and children. There was a glint of green in the
goddess’s eyes, and that soft red mouth could scream and utter
soul-killing blasphemies. The plain woman saw it all so clearly.

Yet she was quite helpless, and she knew it. A sensitive girl cannot go
and tell a man that he is in love with a trashy woman, especially if she
happens to be in love with him herself. It was possible that Tod might
save himself. The affair might prove to be a mere incident, one of those
chapters in a young man’s life that is written in a summer night and
soon forgotten. Tod was so clean and wholesome, and, in spite of his
innocence, so shrewd, that she saw him tiring of this passion in a
petticoat and rushing off to wash himself in the sea. This was a girl
who held a man for a day, a month or a year, and Tod was a man who would
want to be held for life.

Someone else in Horrox’s shop was interested in Tod’s love affair.
Horrox’s junior employees lived in. The girls slept over the main shop,
the men over a sort of annexe across an alley; they met for meals in the
big dining room under the furniture department. Horrox’s had its
etiquette and its autocrats. The autocracy of Miss Harbord, the
housekeeper, dominated all the lesser tyrannies. She was a big woman
with a swollen, waxy face, a black moustache and a merciless and moral
eye; and Horrox’s—with some irony—had nicknamed her “Mother.” Her
duties were that of a Watch Committee, of a supervisor of morals. There
had been scandals in Horrox’s, and one of the partners, who subsidised
Missionary Societies and loved the Memorial Hall, had fallen into such a
pious and quite honest rage over it that Miss Harbord had been imported
to act as mother superior. Miss Harbord saw to it that the sex adventure
was not cultivated in the establishment. She was fearless and devilishly
sly, yet no unkind friend to girls and men who did not try to fool her.
Miss Harbord was at her best when someone was ill.

Miss Harbord and Millicent Hornblower did not appreciate each other.
There had been a breeze, and something more than a breeze.

“If this happens again, Miss Hornblower, I’ll report you.”

“It wasn’t my fault. Why don’t you speak to the man? It’s always the
girl who gets the blame.”

“She needn’t do, my dear. And the man hasn’t got off without hearing
about it.”

This breeze had blown over months ago, but it had been so blusterous and
menacing that the man in question had given up walking recklessly round
exposed corners, and he was no longer the lover of Millie Hornblower.
Another storm had blown them apart. But he was not the utter cad that
Miss Harbord thought him; he was just the warm-blooded, reckless sort of
being who took what he could from a woman who chose to give it, but the
sort of man who would have given a wounded pal the last drop of water in
a desert. A man will be straight and generous with men and crooked with
women. You can’t trust him in the sweetshop, but in the street he will
get knocked over stopping a runaway horse.

“Say!”

It was rather dark at the head of the stairs leading down to the dining
room.

“I told you not to speak to me.”

“That’s true,” said Bob Twine, “but it’s got to be. I’ll meet you down
beyond the White Cliff at nine.”

“I shan’t go.”

“Yes you will. It’s about young Tod.”

She went.

Sitting on the shingle under the shelter of an old black fishing boat,
they fought each other with words while the dusk came down.

“What are you doing playing with young Tod?” asked the man.

“I’m not playing with him. Besides, it’s no business of yours.”

“Oh, isn’t it?”

“You’re jealous, I suppose?”

Bob Twine kept throwing stones at an old tin that the sea had left on
the shingle. He was dour and menacing, but curiously cool.

“Nothing of the kind. I don’t care a snap of the fingers for you now,
and you know it.”

“Thank you.”

“Look here”—and he turned to her with a kind of pleading solemnity—“I
taught that kid to swim just here. I taught him to box. He’s a good kid,
one of the best. You let him alone.”

The girl looked at him haughtily. “What d’you mean?”

“Don’t put on airs; you know what I mean. You are not the girl for that
kid. He’s not like me, the sort of chap that knocks about the
seafront——”

“How dare you!” she said.

“Don’t put on airs. I know you a bit too well, old girl. You let Tod
alone, or I shall tell him.”

“Tell him?”

“Yes.”

“You cad!”

And then she burst into tears and sat leaning against the boat’s black
timbers, while Bob Twine threw stones at the tin and never hit it.

“What beasts men are! You—you made a—a fool of me, you—you know you
did. I had to find out what—what a cad you were, and now, when I’ve met
a naice boy, you want to drag me down in the—the mud.”

“Bosh!” said the man, affected in spite of himself.

“Yes, you do. It’s mean and beastly. He’s not like you; he’s a little
gentleman.”

Bob Twine leant over and grabbed savagely at her wrist.

“Look here, I know all that; but I’m not going to let you play with that
kid.”

“I’m not playing with him.”

“Sure?”

“I’m—I’m in love with him. I want him to marry me.”

Bob Twine twisted away from her, and sat with his knees drawn up,
staring at the sea. She was drying her eyes and smothering an occasional
sob, and waiting watchfully upon his silence. The man’s face looked
swarthy and dark and troubled.

“Yes, and what sort of wife will you make him?” he said at last.

“I’m in love with him,” she repeated, “and I know he’s in love with me.”

Twine picked up a handful of shingle and sent the stones scattering
savagely over the beach.

“Damn! Suppose I’m a silly fool! But, look here, if I ever catch you
running crooked——”

“Thank you,” she said haughtily, “you taught me the difference between a
cad and a gentleman.”

But Millie Hornblower was in a panic mood, and she was afraid of this
swashbuckler of a man with his swarthy passions and his moments of
generous rage. It was possible that he might change his mind, tell Tod
that most disastrous tale, and the stars would fall out of the sky of
her new romance. She wanted Tod. She was very much in love with him, and
the indiscipline of her love was growing greater than her shyness. She
had got to slip into Tod’s arms before Bob Twine or any other man could
interfere.

She met him next evening at the St. Margarets’ end of the Parade, a
rather anxious Tod who had watched for her in vain the night before.

“I missed you last night.”

He was concerned, tender, just a little shy.

“I wasn’t very well,” she said; “something happened, and I stayed in.”

It struck him that she looked strange and unhappy, and his heart was out
to discover the reason.

“I’m sorry. Perhaps you don’t care to walk far?”

“It’s not that. I’d like a walk; I’d like to get away from all these
people.”

“What about Summer Valley? The wild roses are out in the hedges.”

“Yes, let’s go there,” she said.

But she was not herself. She did not talk much, and was quiet and
strange and unsmiling, and now and again she would give Tod a shrinking,
yet half-appealing look. Something had happened, and in the Summer
Valley Tod watched her face and the sunset. It was very sweet and still
there, the wild roses white in the hedges, the long grass full of dim
light, the woods on the ground growing black against the yellow sky. A
June evening, soft, mysterious and fragrant, a lover’s evening, subtle
and strange and sad.

“I wish you would tell me,” said Tod.

“Tell you what, Tod dear?”

He groped shyly for her hand.

“What’s worrying you. You know I——”

She looked frightened, piteous.

“I—I can’t. It’s all so horrid. It has made me feel so cheap and
beastly, and not fit to talk to a naice man.”

Tod put a sudden arm round her.

“But I’m just the man you have got to tell. Let’s sit down here under
this may tree.”

She let him have his way, and, unpinning her hat, she sat and pulled
bits of grass to pieces, while Tod worshipped and waited for her to
trust in him.

“Tell me, dear.”

“It’s all that horrid shop life. A pretty girl gets persecuted in a
shop.”

“Some man has been saying beastly things to you,” flamed Tod.

She turned her head away.

“Yes.”

“Just tell me his name.”

“I can’t, Tod. It’s better that I shouldn’t.”

“I’ll kill him.”

“Oh, don’t! I don’t want it spread about that I’m the sort of girl men
say vile things to. Besides, I gave him his answer, the best answer a
girl could give.”

Tod was bending towards her, his young face stern yet glowing.

“What did you tell him?”

“I said I was—oh, I can’t! I can’t tell you what I told him.”

“Yes you can, because I love you, dear; I’ve loved you for weeks and
weeks.”

She gave him one quick look, and then hung her head.

“Oh, Tod, I told him——”

“Well?”

“That I was in love with a dear, naice boy, and that I didn’t want a cad
to make love to me.”

“Millie, is it me you love?”

“Tod, dear Tod, perhaps.”

His arms went round her, and her arms folded about his neck. They clung
close and kissed, looked in each other’s eyes, and kissed again.

“You’ll marry me?” said Tod, with his eyes alight and his heart beating
fast and hard.

“Yes, dear.”

“Then we’re engaged. You are coming out of that shop.”

 Chapter Eighteen

So Tod and Millicent Hornblower became engaged, and Tod bought a diamond
and ruby ring at Gildersleeve’s in King William Street. It was a very
handsome ring, and it absorbed all the money that he had saved for a
summer holiday; but since Shaston had become a place of delicious
enchantment Tod thought no more of holidays.

He had become absurdly and delightfully old of a sudden, a little,
solemn John Bull carrying new and pleasant responsibilities. Life had
undreamt-of problems and interests, and finance ceased to be a matter of
no importance. Tod took a survey of his finances, and he began to wonder
how much he would be worth to Mr. Rathbone Cragg at the end of another
year. In a month or two he would be the master of a thousand
pounds—those two mortgages that Uncle Remus had guarded so tenaciously.

“I’ve got to see about it,” said Tod.

He went solemnly into Mr. Rathbone Cragg’s inner room, and announced his
engagement.

“Thought I ought to tell you, sir. You have been very kind to me.”

Mr. Cragg looked embarrassed.

“Quite so, Western. And who is——”

“Her name is Miss Hornblower. She is one of the ladies in Horrox’s. I
knew her when I was a boy.”

“An old romance,” said Mr. Cragg, smiling as though he had a pain, and
wondering what Mr. Burgetrode would say about it. “And how old is the
lady, Western?”

“About my age, sir. I’m just on twenty-one. And I wanted to ask you
about the future.”

“Quite so, Western. Prospects, eh?”

“I have new responsibilities, sir.”

“Let me see, you are getting twenty-one shillings a week?”

“Yes.”

Mr. Rathbone Cragg found himself in a subtle situation. Mr. Julian
Burgetrode was the man who mattered, and Mr. Burgetrode was the god
behind the gods. He was not on the stage, and Mr. Cragg had no right to
call upon him, or to betray to Tod how much depended upon the great
man’s pleasure. Therefore he temporised, and decided to write a letter.

“I should like to consider your future, Western, if you will give me a
day or two.”

“Of course, sir.”

“I have nothing but praise for your work here.”

Mr. Cragg wrote to Mr. Julian Burgetrode and explained the situation.
When he went home to lunch he told his wife much that he had said to Mr.
Burgetrode, but he said it in a different and more domesticated way.

“That young fool has got engaged to a shop-girl. Of course it’s no
business of mine, but with his prospects it’s a disaster.”

“And B. was so fond of him,” said the wife resentfully; “you know that,
Edward?”

“She had better forget all about it—at once.”

“She has. I shan’t have the boy up here again.”

“Don’t be hasty, my dear; it never pays to be hasty.”

Mr. Julian Burgetrode replied by return to Mr. Cragg’s letter.

“Please carry on in exactly the same way,” he wrote, “and consider the
boy as your clerk and nothing else. Offer him his market value. I shall
be in Shaston very shortly.”

Mr. Cragg carried out these instructions, and his “market value” was a
shock to Tod. It suggested an addition of five shillings to his weekly
wage at the end of the present year, an income of sixty-seven pounds
twelve shillings.

Tod began to consider economies. The moment had arrived when he found
time to dawdle in front of furniture shops, and to understand Arthur
Mossup’s preoccupation over the quest of a house. He decided that he
would buy no more new clothes, that he was eating too much, and that his
room at Miss Truebody’s was needlessly expensive.

Meanwhile Miss Millicent Hornblower was very much engaged, and enjoying
the sensation. Nearly everybody in the establishment of Messrs. Horrox &
Horrox became aware of that ruby and diamond ring, and it was flashed in
the eyes of lady customers who made boring demands for reels of cotton
and hanks of silk. It was shown to Miss Harbord, and that grim woman’s
congratulations were characteristically frank.

“It’s the best thing that could have happened to you, my dear. And a bit
of plain gold will be worth more than all those pretty stones.”

Millie warned her friends and gossips that her sojourn would be short in
the house of Horrox and Horrox.

“He’s such a dear boy, and hates the idea of my being in a shop. He
thinks it isn’t naice. Yet, he’s got money, you know. I don’t see him
staying with the Craggs. Someone’s interested in him, a gentleman who is
supposed to be a millionaire.”

Her gossips listened, and agreed that Millie was becoming a snob.

As for economy, it was not part of the landscape. She had spacious ideas
and a brilliant sense of chiaroscuro, and little sumptuous ambitions as
to houses and clothes. Tod was going up in the world. He had capital and
influence, and Millie did not see herself making beds and sweeping
floors or washing up dishes in some little back kitchen. She was going
to be a lady and give tea parties, walk into Horrox and Horrox’s and
issue orders across the counter, and take her morning cup of coffee at
Judson’s café. Tod was a dear boy. She was sure that he would make a
lovely husband.

Various people congratulated Tod. He met Bob Twine one Wednesday
afternoon, and the swarthy man lounged in front of Tod and offered him
sheepish and glum felicitations.

“Fancy a kid like you getting married! Suppose you’re as pleased as
Punch?”

“She’s such a dear girl,” said Tod; “we are going to be very happy.”

Twine looked at him queerly and seemed to be struggling with some inward
pain.

“A long time yet, I suppose?”

“I want to get married this year.”

“Oh, cheese it! What’s the hurry?”

“I don’t like Millie being in a shop.”

Twine went for a long prowl, the savage prowl of a man who was at war
with the world.

“Damn! I ought to tell the kid, but somehow I can’t. Perhaps she’ll run
straight with him. God knows!”

Someone came out of Miss Truebody’s sitting-room one evening as Tod was
running down the stairs, and stood in the dusk-filled passage waiting.
It was Bertha Cragg.

“I want to congratulate you, Tod,” she said; “I’m so glad.”

Her voice was very steady and quiet, but she did not look at him, and
her eyes were set between bitterness and tears. Tod took her good wishes
as he would have taken an “Oh, well played!” at tennis. He was quite
unsubtle towards Bertha Cragg, and he never knew that he had lost the
greatest thing in the world.

“I’m awfully happy,” he told her ingenuously.

“I hope you will always be happy, Tod. I should like to have met your
fiancée, but I am going away.”

“Are you?”

“Yes. An aunt of mine is going abroad for a year, and she has asked me
to go with her.”

“I hope you’ll have a jolly time,” said Tod; “you deserve it.”

And Bertha Cragg passed out of his life.

Tod made several pilgrimages to Arthur Mossup’s green and white cottage
in the Old Town, and sat at the feet of those authorities on domestic
science. Mossup was rather worried about the little shop; business was
not as brisk as it might be, but he forgot all his worries in prompting
Tod. He had a great love of detail, kept most elaborate accounts, and
was ready to tell Tod the cost of anything, from a second-hand mangle to
a stair-rod.

“It’s all here, Western, every tintack in the house. Nothing like
knowing where sixpence goes. Olive can tell you all about the food.”

“How many tons of coal do you burn a year?” asked Tod.

“Here you are—five tons, three hundredweight. And you get a Peerless
oil-stove. Saves pounds. Ask Olive.”

Olive, a pleasant, dark little woman, agreed.

Tod made a note of the Peerless oil-stove. He made notes of everything
that Mossup told him. Never did a man have a more earnest and attentive
disciple.

“Bring your fiancée up here, old man; she and Olive can have a talk. My
wife’s a marvel, Western; there never was such a woman; I’m the luckiest
man that ever was. She does the hats and the house, and she’s prettier
to look at than ever she was.”

“I think she’s very happy,” said Tod.

Mossup blinked his short-sighted blue eyes.

“Do you, Western? I’m glad. I’m such a quiet sort of chap, and do you
know, I never thought she’d marry me. Amazing, wasn’t it?”

“You’re a brick, old man. May I bring Millicent up on Sunday?”

“Come up to tea; we have tea in the garden on Sundays. Come up about
four o’clock.”

But Miss Hornblower was not interested in the Mossups or in their way of
doing things. Tod took her down to tea, with ingenuous praise of Olive
and her cleverness, but Millie did not sit and listen. She led the
conversation, and led it to a level above the careful economies of a
Mossup’s day, touching upon such luxuries as parlour-maids, late dinner,
Turkey carpets, at-home days, and whether you should have a double bed
in your spare bedroom. She depressed the Mossups and impressed Tod.
Millicent had ideas; she knew how things ought to be done in a
successful household, and Tod felt challenged by her optimism.

She referred to the Mossups’ cottage as a “stuffy little place,” and in
such a horrid neighbourhood, too.

“And did you see her poor hands?”

“What was the matter with them?” Tod asked.

“They don’t keep a servant!”

To judge by her tone there was something mysteriously shocking in such
social degradation.

“Perhaps they can’t afford it.”

“Fancy a man letting his wife slave like that. I think it’s
disgraceful.”

And Tod—the lover—was too much in love to disagree.

Mr. Julian Burgetrode arrived in Shaston towards the end of the same
week. He did not come by train, but in a car which he drove himself, a
great grey car that slid along noiselessly, but could say “perr-rump” if
challenged, and go streaking up a hill like a huge grey lizard up a
wall. This car swung into Caroline Crescent and stopped in front of the
Craggs’ office. Mr. Burgetrode sent in a message by his chauffeur, and
Tod came out.

“Get in,” said Mr. Burgetrode, “in here.”

Tod got in.

“Shall I want a coat, sir?”

“No. I don’t need you, Griggs. Get back to the hotel.”

“Right, sir!”

Mr. Burgetrode swung the car into Church Road, went up the steep hill at
thirty miles an hour, squeezed through between a tram and a railway van,
scraped past a fool of a wagon that was crawling all over the road, cut
round Post Corner, and drove a group of loafers hurriedly on to the
pavement. Going up the steep hill from Post Corner to the Ridge they
overtook another car that was piqued at being hooted at and tried to
show its pace. Mr. Burgetrode’s car went by it with a rude snort from
the exhaust, crowded in front, and smothered its defeated rival with
dust.

Tod had spent a rather breathless five minutes. They had seemed on the
edge of hitting things, but had never quite hit them, and Mr. Burgetrode
sat there as imperturbable as sin, looking straight ahead and not
bothering about anything. He had not spoken a word, and he ignored Tod
until they were on the straight and swinging road that lept through the
woods along the ridge towards Bidhurst.

“What do you think of that bit of driving, young Ajax?”

“You made things move, sir,” said Tod.

“Beastliness, sheer beastliness. But Power can be beastly.”

“People have to get out of the way?”

“The sleepy fools. Power, young man, Power’s like this car; it can glide
along and leave everything behind, and so softly that the sleepy fools
hardly feel the power. Or it can drive them into the ditch, kick dust in
their faces. It depends how you use it. But—Power!”

Tod felt those blue eyes on him, and he was puzzled.

“Rather fine—to have Power, sir.”

“What for?”

“To keep bullies from bullying; to put wrong things right.”

“That’s the ideal, is it?”

“What’s wrong with it?” asked Tod.

“Nothing, so long as you keep fierce and fit. No sentimental slosh, mind
you. And don’t let any woman cut your hair.”

Tod gave a quick glance at Mr. Burgetrode.

“Like Samson?” he asked.

“Exactly.”

Tod cogitated.

“You mean—any woman?”

“There is only one woman in a thousand fit to be a comrade and use
Power. They make props and prigs; get round a man like ivy. So you’re
engaged?”

“Yes, sir,” said Tod.

“Young fool!” reflected Mr. Burgetrode.

Then he smiled at Tod.

“Dreams, eh? I had ’em myself, but the only one that was worth while
died. I married Power after that, Tod, and I have been married to her
ever since. You will come and dine with me to-morrow night?”

“Thank you, sir.”

“With your fiancée. Horrible word! Seven-thirty at the Majestic. Give
the lady my compliments and say I shall be charmed and honoured to meet
her. What’s her name?”

“Millicent Hornblower,” said Tod.

“A good self-advertising name! Don’t be a young fool and think yourself
offended. Only the little people strut on dunghills.”

Mr. Burgetrode turned the car at the Bidhurst crossroads and drove back
to Shaston with extraordinary mildness.

There is no more blind and obstinate creature than a young man in love,
and Tod did not foresee the coming crisis in his career. He failed to
appreciate Mr. Burgetrode’s power or his point of view, and his
infatuation was so serene and utter that it did not occur to him that
Mr. Burgetrode might not like Miss Millicent Hornblower. The possibility
of it never entered Tod’s head. He was so much in love with Millie.

It was a rather excited and confident Tod who met her that evening on
the Parade.

“Mr. Burgetrode’s here. I’ve been out in his car.”

The girl’s eyes were on the alert.

“What’s he want?”

“He asked us to dinner at the Majestic to-morrow evening.”

“Asked me?”

“Why, of course.”

Tod’s dear lady went very pink.

“How naice of him!”

And then she added breathlessly:

“I must get back at once, Tod—at once.”

“What for, dear?”

“My dress. I’ve got a dress, but it wants touching up for the Majestic.
You’ll be in your dress-suit.”

“Shall I?” Tod asked. “Mr. Burgetrode doesn’t bother.”

“Of course, we must dress. It would be rude to him if we didn’t.”

“Hang it!” said Tod. “Have I got to wear white gloves?”

“No, you dear silly. A dinner jacket and a black tie. Have you got a
dinner jacket?”

“A thing without tails? Yes.”

“And we must have a taxi.”

“I’ll see about the taxi,” said Tod.

Tod called at Horrox’s with his taxi at about twenty minutes past seven,
and waited at the side door that was used by the shop assistants. He
became aware of three merry and fluffy heads at an open window above,
the heads of three irresponsible young women who were interested in
life.

“Hallo, Tod!” said one of them.

Tod took off his hat and began to blush, being very conscious of his
white shirt front and black tie.

“Doesn’t he look sweet!”

“Hallo, here’s Majestic Millie!”

Miss Hornblower appeared, wearing a black velvet cloak, and a length of
white filmy stuff over her head. Her shoes were of yellow satin, and Tod
had a glimpse of a yellow silk stocking. The three young ladies at the
window sent up a cheer, but Miss Hornblower’s dignity refused to be
disturbed. Tod opened the door of the taxi for her, and they drove off.

“Teases, those girls,” he said.

“They are jealous, that’s all.”

Tod thought he had never seen anything more beautiful than the girl at
his side, and most certainly she was very lovely. She let her black
cloak float open, and showed a gleam of white shoulders and arms shining
out from a mass of black lace. She had pinned a yellow rose over her
bosom, a little point of glowing colour between her shiny hair and her
yellow shoes. Tod contemplated the complete and utter conquest of Mr.
Burgetrode, an immediate surrender to this most charming and seductive
young woman. It was fate.

“Do I look naice?”

“Lovely,” said Tod; “just perfect.”

She patted his knee and, looking critically at his tie, told herself
that Tod Western was quite a gentlemanly youngster. His dinner jacket
fitted him well, and his head looked sleek and wet.

“It’s rather jolly, isn’t it?” she said.

“What?”

“Being engaged. I hope your Mr. Burgetrode will like me.”

“Could he help it?” quoth the lover.

Mr. Julian Burgetrode was sitting in the white loggia of the Hotel
Majestic, sinfully comfortable in an old Norfolk jacket and a pair of
grey flannel trousers. He saw the taxi drive up, and Tod giving his
gallant hand to a very handsome, golden-haired young woman in black. And
Mr. Burgetrode was annoyed with himself.

“A lapse on your part, my friend,” he thought; “only a cad makes
children feel uncomfortable.”

He went and met them on the steps, the old sahib, suppressing all that
was queer and discordant in that masterful soul of his. Tod, looking
flushed and happy, presented Miss Millicent Hornblower.

“I really must apologise to you young people,” said the great man; “I
was late—too late to change.”

“Please don’t mention it,” said the girl, thinking that Tod’s friend
looked very snuffy, and not the sort of figure she had expected.

Mr. Burgetrode gave her a sharp stare of his blue eyes. He saw the whole
of her in five seconds—red mouth, satin shoes, golden hair, engagement
ring. Here was a hard young pippin with a rosy skin and a deceptive
promise of much juice and sweetness. Mr. Burgetrode became very polite.

“Supposing we go in? Smith, take the lady’s cloak, will you? What a
pleasant day we have had.”

“Yes, the weather’s quite naice,” said the girl, “specially when you’ve
got an autocar. What’s yours, Mr. Burgetrode?”

“My little thing? A Graham Rolt. I like speed, you know.”

He shepherded them into the dining room, and to a far corner where a
table had been laid for three. There were roses on the table, red and
white roses in a big bowl. The head waiter, treading softly, wished Mr.
Burgetrode a “good evening, sir,” pulled back Miss Hornblower’s chair
for her, and waited discreetly; with the wine list.

“Veuve Cliquot,” said Mr. Burgetrode.

He felt that Tod’s young woman was dressed and scented for champagne.

A sudden and unexplainable shyness had fallen upon Tod. He was a little
awed by his fiancée’s talkative sangfroid and by the bold way she sat
there and attacked Mr. Burgetrode. Tod had expected her to be rather coy
and mute, and perhaps—in his heart of hearts—he was just a little
disappointed. He knew that Mr. Burgetrode did not like women, but Tod
had never been able to discover what sort of women the great man did
like. Perhaps the shy, gentle, rather silent type? And Tod had an
uncomfortable feeling that Mr. Burgetrode was being extraordinarily
polite, that he would have been much less polite and much more the Mr.
Burgetrode as Tod knew him, if Millie had seemed a little frightened and
in need of help. But she was a young woman armed, out to attack, her
head full of the impression she intended to make, unable to forget that
the great man had thought of her as a “shop-girl,” that it was her
business to show him that she was no fool. Her blue eyes looked hard and
alert, and she was very careful in the ordering of her hands. Yet Tod
had a horrid feeling that Mr. Burgetrode was not pleased.

“Quite a crowd here to-night,” she observed, staring round the room.

“Quite—a number—of people,” echoed Mr. Burgetrode. “Shaston is
getting—fashionable.”

“It’s very dowdy this year, don’t you think?”

“Is it?”

“One doesn’t see such naice people; so trippery, you know. We have got
quite a naice band this year, but it’s quite spoilt by all the common
girls and knuts. I’m so glad Tod isn’t a knut.”

“No, it’s a great blessing; I’m glad you realise that, Miss Hornblower.
Though I have seen him in mauve socks.”

“Oh, Tod!” said his fiancée.

Tod blushed.

“I like old clothes,” he began; “my dad always looked shabby.”

“Some men can afford to look shabby,” said the girl.

Mr. Burgetrode smiled.

“Two points of view,” he remarked meaningly; “and what sort of music do
you like?”

“I can’t bear ragtime.”

“No?”

“Mendelssohn is quite naice, don’t you think?”

“Well, Tod, what do you say?”

“I don’t know anything about it,” said Tod, “but I like marches, and
music that makes you feel all funny inside.”

“Stick to that,” quoth the great man. “Don’t try and train yourself to
look in a mirror.”

Tod was glad when the dinner came to an end, and they went and sat
outside in the loggia and drank coffee. Mr. Burgetrode sent for
Millicent’s cloak, and offered Tod a cigarette. They watched the people
walking up and down the Parade, and Tod tried to feel that he belonged
to the Majestic, and that he had no fear of the hall porter. He wished
Millie would not talk quite so much, and all about things of which she
knew nothing. Mr. Burgetrode had lit a pipe. He was bored.

Tod had ordered the taxi at half-past nine. It was five minutes late,
and Tod had begun to fidget. Millie was talking politics to Mr.
Burgetrode—politics! It appeared that she was interested in the
Primrose League, that society cherished by old ladies in memory of a
brilliant and cynical Jew.

Tod got up.

“Here’s our taxi.”

Millie gathered herself, and did the “so charmed to have met you” to Mr.
Burgetrode. He glanced at Tod.

“I want to see you to-morrow, Western.”

“What time, sir?”

“Here, after dinner.”

Tod handed the amateur gentlewoman into the taxi, and had a glimpse of
Mr. Burgetrode knocking out his pipe irritably against one of the white
pillars. Mr. Burgetrode went up the steps and into the hotel, and did
not turn his head to look back. Tod felt that the great man was not
pleased.

“What a queer old stick! I did my best, Tod, didn’t I?”

Her warm hand snuggled into his, and Tod was swept by a gust of sudden
tenderness and chivalrous contrition. She had been making a brave show
for his sake, playing her best game, against that difficult old man!
Darn Mr. Burgetrode! This girl was worth twenty Mr. Burgetrodes.

Tod bent and kissed her hand.

“You were great,” he said. “I’m awfully proud of you, Millie.”

She smiled, for she was rather proud of herself.

Old Burgetrode had carried a chair on to the little balcony outside his
bedroom, and was refilling his pipe with the slow hands of a thinker. He
sat there for half an hour, straddling the chair, his arms crossed on
the chair-back, watching the sea. Once or twice he smiled, the smile of
a man who saw that the jest of life was against himself, and that his
power and his cleverness were as so much sand when the tide of Nature
came rolling in. You chose a boy, set him to work, planned all sorts of
great happenings for him, and one day some little yellow-haired chit
crooked a finger at him, and the boy ran wild, chasing a petticoat.

Mr. Burgetrode laughed.

“You old fool,” he said, “of course it was bound to happen. The child
has to pass through its mother’s womb, the man has to pass through the
womb of the world. We all start afresh, we all have to learn the same
old sorry things, we all make the same mistakes and have to pay for
them. Whoever listens, or takes advice from an old man? What chance has
Fate when a boy gets a glimpse of a girl’s bosom?”

Yet the inevitableness did not reconcile him to the particular fate that
had overtaken Tod. He had hoped that Tod might escape the sex lure, or
escape it until his sinews were set and his head involved in the great
adventure, when a woman might not matter. But this little shop-girl was
a tragedy. She was pulling Tod into a parlour, fastening him to the
grocer’s and to the draper’s bills, making a little middle-class
gentleman of him, a thing that lusted to live in a red villa, to grow
geraniums in the front garden, and to tottle out on a motor-bike with a
side-car on Sundays. She was clever, and she was cheap.

“Damn her!” said Mr. Burgetrode, “damn her—and her red mouth! The
succulent young hussy!”

And yet he was sorry for the shop-girl and sorry for Tod. He had a
shrewd glimpse of them making all the old sorry mistakes, floundering,
quarrelling, filling their lives with little sordid ambitions, envies,
pathetic make-believes. That girl was no mate for Tod. When he had grown
tired of her body——!

Mr. Burgetrode knocked out his pipe and went to bed.

Tod came to the Hotel Majestic about half-past eight next evening, a
square-headed and obstinate-eyed young man, vaguely aware of clouds on
the horizon. Mr. Burgetrode was waiting in the lounge. He took Tod up to
his sitting-room, made him sit down in the most comfortable chair,
offered him his cigarette-case, and lit his own pipe. The great man was
unusually reposeful and quiet, almost gentle. Youth does not react to a
scolding tongue, nor had Mr. Burgetrode any intention of trying to
coerce a thing that is stronger than any man’s philosophy. Tod should go
his way, but he should choose the path with his eyes open.

“So you are going to get married, Western?”

“I am, sir,” said Tod.

“Very well; that settles it.”

And then he told Tod all that he had planned for him, all that he
thought to make of him. He was more frank than any father to a son, and
his frankness was without prejudice or guile.

“That’s how matters were to shape, Western. I wanted you to have two
years at Cragg’s just to see how you shaped, and the rest would have
followed. I should have put you with an architect for a year, with a
firm of builders for two years, and then sent you on a tour through some
of the big industries to get an idea of the general scheme of things.
After that I should have sent you round the world. And what for? Because
you took my fancy—because I thought you had it in you to make a big man
of yourself. But I did not want to influence you in any way, tie you to
a stick, and warp your growth. As it is, your lady friend has
intervened.”

Tod sat there, very stubborn and grave.

“I couldn’t help it, sir. I didn’t know.”

“Boy, it was I who was the idiot to dream dreams for a young man.”

“But are—they—dead, sir?”

Mr. Burgetrode smiled queerly.

“Of course. My young superman was to spread his wings and fly. He had to
think of nothing but flying, climbing, getting to the peak, and of
getting there alone. A man can’t fly with a woman clinging to his
legs—a young woman who doesn’t want him to fly.”

“My fiancée——” Tod began.

“I am saying nothing, Western, against the girl. You are doing the most
natural thing in the world, both of you. I did it myself, once. But she
wants you to marry her and live in a little red villa, and go to the
pier concerts, and keep a cat and buy her a new hat every month. She
can’t help it, nor can you.”

“I asked her,” said Tod; “I’m in love with her. I suppose, sir, you’re
not interested in me any more?”

Mr. Burgetrode relit his pipe.

“There’s the old saying, that a young man married is a young man marred.
In this case, it seems to be applicable. You can’t do all that I had
planned for you. You can’t strap a knapsack on your back and go out to
conquer the world. A young man who is married has one hand tied behind
him, and cowardice trots at his elbow. He’s afraid of the big adventure,
the great risks, and that the dragon may devour his wife. It’s his
business to kill the dragon for the sake of his wife? Quite so. But it’s
so much easier to stay at home in a little house with thousands of
little people in thousands of other little houses, and not go out into
places where dragons rage. I’m sorry, but there it is.”

“So it’s all over?” said Tod, keeping a stiff lip.

“Exactly. You have to make your choice between a girl and a career, and
knowing what you are——”

“I can’t break a promise. I’m not a cad.”

“Boy, that was what I was going to say. You have promised to marry the
girl, and I know you will marry her. My young Ajax would marry her, if
it meant hunting for cabbage-stalks in dust-bins. There is nothing more
to be said. You have to live your life and to learn. No soldier is worth
his pay who hasn’t seen his own blood. I should like you to write to me
now and again.”

Tod got up, but his knees felt shaky.

“Am I to stay on with the Craggs, sir?”

“Of course. It’s a career, of sorts. I shall write to Mr. Cragg, and
arrange that for you. If ever you are a free man again, Western, you can
come to me.”

“Thank you, sir. I think I’ll be going now.”

“Good night. Don’t forget that most men and women cheat in the great
game.”

“I’ll never cheat, sir.”

“Give them a straight drive on the jaw, and good luck to you. Good-bye!”

Tod went down the great staircase of the Majestic, a very sober and
rather sorrowful Tod. He did not guess then that three years would pass
before he would see Mr. Julian Burgetrode again.

But he did not tell Millie that he had lost his great patron. He did not
think that it would be fair to tell her.

Mr. Burgetrode was walking up and down his bedroom.

“Damn!” he said, “the boy has grit. I knew it was hopeless. If that girl
only had a nose like Cyrano de Bergerac’s! Faces, faces, always faces! A
fine man sucked dry by a pair of red lips! Good lord, I think I’ll adopt
a eunuch!”

 Chapter Nineteen

A change had come over Tod’s career. He had ceased to be one of the
young gods of the world with the light of the morning shining in his
face. He became a serious and contriving Tod, a young man whose thoughts
were in shop windows and furniture catalogues, who refused to buy
himself new ties, and who was very careful of the pennies in his
pockets.

“I’ve got responsibilities,” he explained to Mr. Lavender; “no more
Turkish cigarettes, you know.”

Mr. Lavender, being an old bachelor, was a romanticist.

“Giving up is gaining,” he told Tod, “and yet nature is a spendthrift. I
always did believe in young people marrying in May, a bit of spring
recklessness.”

Tod was not feeling reckless. Every page of his day-book carried those
uncompromising figures—“£67 12s. a year.” The immediate and unfailing
retort of his youth was “Yes, and the mortgages make another forty. We
shall have to manage on a hundred or so to start with.” He was making
grim efforts to save money out of his weekly pay, money that was
necessary for the equipping of a little house, but Millie was so alive
and so gay, so fond of chocolates and sweet cakes and the best seats in
the Pier Pavilion! Tod had never forgotten the incident of that
German-silver ring. Besides, he had a lover’s pride and the generous
man’s hatred of miserliness. He was engaged to marry the prettiest girl
in Shaston, and if she liked chocolates and fine clothes—well, there
was some excuse for it, and even Tod’s shrewdness could not kill the
love of a fine and swaggering gesture.

“Two stalls, please,” or “A pound of the best in the shop.”

Before many weeks had passed Tod realised that Millicent had ambitions.
Her social ideas were not to be expressed by an income of one hundred
pounds a year.

“I want a suite like that for the drawing-room.”

They were standing in front of a furniture shop window and looking at a
mahogany suite, whose chairs were upholstered in pink brocade. Tod was
heard to say something about the price.

“Oh, your Mr. Burgetrode will help.”

That was her answer to everything, and Tod began to grow rather weary of
Mr. Burgetrode’s empty name. There were times when he wished that he had
told Millie that the great man had cast them off, and that he—Tod—had
had to choose between a girl and a career, but he shirked making the
confession. He did not like the idea of presenting himself as a martyr,
and perhaps his pride was piqued. Confound Mr. Burgetrode! He could
manage his own adventure without being haunted by that old man of the
sea.

“I couldn’t expect him to do that, you know.”

“Why not?”

“I want to be independent,” said Tod.

Houses inspired the same social dream. They used to walk about Shaston
in the long summer evenings, and look at houses, but not the sort of
houses Tod had seen himself inhabiting. Millie had a particular
affection for a little red villa called “Carnarvon” in Guildford Lane, a
villa with casement windows and a sort of little pepper-pot of a turret
stuck on at one corner, and gables that were filled with imitation oak.
There were two little lawns in front, with beds of geranium and
calceolaria, and six standard roses on each side of the gravel path.

“Isn’t it sweet?”

“It’s bigger than we should want, dear.”

“Oh, Tod, we don’t want a shabby, poky little place.”

Tod squeezed her arm.

“Nothing’s too good for you.”

She had begun to debauch him.

He showed a new touchiness and the faint suspicion of a swagger in the
office of Rathbone Cragg & Son, for it was evident to Tod that the
favour of the gods had changed. Old Cragg showed him a new curtness, a
peremptoriness that made Tod know himself to be an underling. Frederick
Cragg had long refrained from cultivating Mr. Julian Burgetrode’s
protégé, and his attitude towards Tod was one of casual and superior
indifference. Tod had lapsed. And Tod did not feel humble; he resented
the subtle insult to his personal pride. They had been obliging Mr.
Burgetrode, had they? Some day he would show them a thing or two. The
woman was teaching him to strut and to crow.

In August Tod became one-and-twenty, and the hands of Uncle Remus became
impotent and obsolete. Tod went over to see his uncle, and spent a night
at Burnt Farm. Uncle Remus talked a good deal about Tod’s “little bit o’
property,” what an asset it was, and how a wise man never gambled with
his capital. “Suppose you’ll be leaving the money where it is?”

“At present,” said Tod, “but I’m going to be married. I may want a
little of it for furnishing.”

Uncle Remus sat up stiff and straight in his chair.

“You’re goin’ to be married?”

“Yes.”

“And only just turned twenty-one.”

“Why shouldn’t I get married?” Tod asked.

“Who’s the girl?”

“A young lady in Shaston. She was in Horrox’s shop.”

“A shop-girl?”

“Yes.”

A cunning look came over Uncle Remus’s thin face. He leant forward, and
pointed the stem of his pipe at Tod.

“She ain’t drivin’ you, is she?”

“What do you mean?”

“You ain’t got her into trouble?”

Tod lost his temper, and said angry things to Uncle Remus, but Uncle
Remus was quite unmoved.

“’Tain’t no use losing your temper,” he said; “pity is it ain’t as I
hinted. Five bob a week’s cheaper than a wife. I ain’t no believer in
gels.”

“What about my mother?”

“Your mother was a pedigree,” said Uncle Remus; “you’ll never find a gel
like her.”

The heavens looked like falling when Tod made his fiancée realise the
truth, that they would have to live on about a hundred pounds a year.
She wept, and Tod behaved like a guilty fool.

“I don’t want to stay in a shop, Tod; they are all so horrid to me.”

“But you are not going to stay at Horrox’s, my darling; we are going to
get married quite soon.”

“I—I couldn’t ask you to keep me when you have only a hundred a year.
And I do want you, Tod—I want you awful bad at times.”

They were lying in the bracken of one of the little woods in Summer
Valley, and Tod took the girl in his arms.

“I want you—too.”

They kissed, and Millie pulled Tod over to her and lay with him very
close.

“Oh, I want to be married! Why can’t you get that old Mr. Burgetrode to
help us?”

“Because I can’t,” said Tod, obstinate even in her arms; “he expects me
to be independent, and not to cadge. We have got to manage somehow.”

“Perhaps he’s only waiting to see how we get on?”

“Perhaps.”

“Putting us on our trial.”

“Perhaps.”

“Oh, Tod, why can’t you have a business of your own?”

“Well, why not? What sort of business?”

“Let’s think. You have some capital. Lots of chaps have succeeded with
half your money and half your brains.”

“It’s an idea,” said Tod, sitting up rather flushed and excited. “Why
shouldn’t we do something on our own?”

Eve had suggested the adventure, and it was Eve who discovered the
inevitable inspiration. Tod lay awake half the night, thinking of all
the possible ways of making money, and of how he could apply that £1,000
of his so that it should give him a share of gold pieces to pour into
the lap of his beloved. Tod’s experience was very limited, but
imagination rushed in where his experience failed him. Farming? No, he
could not imagine Millicent living on a farm. A shop? But what sort of
shop? And his bile rose at the thought of a life behind the counter. He
might become an agent for something—what he could not quite say. He had
heard of men making quite a lot of money by starting a big
boarding-house or a small hotel, but he did not think that Millie would
approve of the idea. He knew a good deal about houses, and a little
about the building of houses, and he wondered whether any of the Shaston
builders would be willing to take an enterprising partner? There were so
many possibilities, but they were so vague and nebulous that Tod
continued to grope and to grasp nothing.

“I’m going to take you for a walk,” said Millie.

“Looking at houses?”

“No; just a walk.”

It was a Wednesday afternoon, a hot and sweltering day in the thick of
the Shaston holiday season. The town was full of young women in white
dresses, young women with red arms and necks, and sun patterns scorched
on their chests, and wearing white stockings that looked tired and
overworked. Youths lounged about in badly washed flannel trousers,
hatless, working up a manly tan. Perspiring people from London, fat
women with shiny faces, bored men in Sunday clothes carrying buckets and
spades, swarms of children, all looking for the joy of life that was
pappy and soft as a stale cucumber. Hawkers pushing barrows of fruit.
Dirty women with very white aprons selling shrimps. Motors and human
things that smelt. All the tawdriness and trash and pathos of a “holiday
resort” in August.

Miss Hornblower led her lover towards the railway station.

“What’s the idea?” he asked her.

“Nothing. Just watching the crowds.”

She looked very cool and comely in a white dress flecked with touches of
cerise. Tod compared her with all these overheated, sun-scorched,
unstarched women, and his pride in her challenged the spirit of
adventure. How could a man fail to succeed with such a charming young
woman to fight for? His love was yearning to take the world by the
throat and compel it to surrender happiness and riches.

A London train had just poured its crowd into Shaston station, not the
cheapest of crowds, but people who travelled by an afternoon train and
meant to stay more than a week. A couple of taxis were loading up
luggage, and three or four flies had been fought for by pushful fathers
of families. There were little piles of luggage and groups of impatient
humans dotted about the pavement. Even outside porters had an artificial
importance.

“I-want-a-taxi,” a fat man was saying to a porter, a fat man in grey
tweeds and a Homburg hat, an emphatic man, a swollenly self-important
man. “I tell you-I-want-a-taxi. What’s the matter with this confounded
town?”

“There ain’t no taxis, sir; all engaged.”

“I’ve seen two, just two,” said the fat man truculently, “two taxis to
meet a London train. It’s a damned scandal!”

Miss Hornblower and Tod overheard the man’s accusation, and his damning
of the enterprise of Shaston.

“Seems to feel the heat,” said Tod.

“He’s right. There ought to be more taxis.”

She watched Tod’s face, but her hero had not caught the inspiration.

They wandered back down the town, but there was a purpose in the girl’s
pilgrimage. She led Tod down Queen Street to where a certain Pumpelly
kept a shop and garage, a rather dingy place that always seemed packed
with a chaotic mass of cars, and where irritable amateur drivers wanted
to know how they were to get in or how they were to get out.

Six or seven private cars were lined up along the pavement. A very
greasy young man in dirty overalls was doing something to one of them.
One long, grey touring car was full of very superior gentlewomen who
were inciting their man to find out, and find out with vigour, why they
were not being attended to. A fat man, rather like the fat man at the
railway station, was standing at the garage entrance and making his
presence felt.

“Is there anybody in charge here?”

“Try the office, sir,” said a small boy.

The fat man tried the office, and met Mr. Pumpelly in person.

“I have been waiting here for ten minutes.”

“Sorry, sir, no more petrol till this evening.”

“No petrol! I call it a scandal in a town like this. Up north—we know
how to do things.”

“You had better get back up north, sir,” said Mr. Pumpelly.

Millicent Hornblower and Tod had more than a glimpse of all this
stultified prosperity, and Tod stood on the opposite sidewalk, with his
hands in his pocket, and his eyes at gaze. There was a very distinct
block in the flow of Mr. Pumpelly’s functions. It was obvious that he
wanted more enterprise, more men, more accommodation.

“Do you see it now?” asked the voice of the girl.

Tod turned on her with a flash of his blue eyes.

“Taxis—a garage—of course!”

And then, in that public highway, he pinched Millie’s ear.

“You clever darling; you’ve got a head! Why didn’t I think of it
before?”

 Chapter Twenty

So Tod plunged.

There was an empty shop in Duke Street, a long tunnel of a shop that had
been the home of a local iron-monger who had failed to meet his
creditors. The owner of the premises showed himself to be a very
adaptable and progressive person. He agreed to spend a hundred pounds on
the place, refit it, put in a new front with big glass doors, and knock
down part of a back wall so that cars could circulate by way of a
backyard and side lane. He added ten pounds to the rent—but Tod was
delighted; he did not grudge his landlord his ten per cent.

Western’s Garage was opened on New Year’s Day. The shop front was
painted red; and a big blue-and-white sign jutted out on an iron bracket
