and let everybody know that there was a garage in Duke Street. Tod,
under Miss Hornblower’s inspiration, had been very lavish with his gold
lettering on the plate-glass windows. Orders were “promptly attended
to”—“All trains met”—“Touring cars for hire.” The shop window was full
of lamps, horns, tools, an assortment of tyres, jacks, oil cans, and the
like. The garage held one taxi and one American touring car, both
obtained under a system of deferred payments. The staff consisted of
Tod, a driver-mechanic, and a boy.

Tod had learnt to drive a car. He had read all the automobile journals
with great diligence, and paid various visits to London and the
Midlands. Someone in Wardour Street offered him certain commercial
facilities, one of those keen gentlemen who are so clever at making
money out of other people’s enterprise and energy. Tod was “advised” by
this Semite in Wardour Street. He obtained various agencies, but they
were agencies for rather second-rate firms who built the cheaper and
more dubious sort of car. Old Pumpelly had snaffled all the others. But
the business moved; Tod’s taxi snorted about quite busily, took people
to the station and to theatres, and staggered home again without an
accident. The American touring car had a rather more gentlemanly and
luxurious life, but then it was a summer car, and Tod had no spare
driver. Miss Hornblower would commandeer it on occasions, and make Tod
drive her up and down the front. He had bought her a fur coat, and all
the young women in Horrox & Horrox’s were aware of Millie and her coat.

But the business moved. People came into the shop and bought things,
tyres, petrol, oil, grease, repairing outfits, French chalk, sparking
plugs, oil cans. Tod advertised himself as ready to undertake repairs,
and Love—his driver-mechanic—was put to the test. Love was a fat
little man, sallow, greasy, with a slight squint and an immense display
of superficial self-assurance. He talked Tod into believing him an
expert, pouring out streams of technical jargon with a sort of biblical
gusto. “The mag” needed attention, or the “brasspot had a bit o’ fluff
in its tummy.” Somebody’s “gears were bottoming,” or “she wanted her
big-end rebushing.” Mr. Love always spoke of a car as “she,” and with an
intimate and affectionate shrewdness that embraced the whole sex. Tod
got a little breathless in trying to follow Mr. Love through all these
feminine details and subtleties, poring over motor-manuals at night, and
going to bed with his head full of “crown wheels” and “short circuits,”
“lay shafts” and “forced-feed lubrication.” He did not desire this man
Love to be a Mumbo-Jumbo, a Witch-doctor, a Priest of all the Mysteries
in his business. He wanted to keep a grip on the greasy seat of Mr.
Love’s trousers.

The driver-mechanic appeared to perform quite creditably upon one or two
ramshackle machines that were entrusted to “Western’s Garage” for
repairs. They ran when they left the repair shop, and that was good
enough for Mr. Love.

“Good enough.” That was his great phrase, containing a philosophy so
dear to the heart of the lazier sort of English artisan. If a car came
back again at the end of a week, Mr. Love insinuated his greasy person
into its entrails, spat, rubbed his hands on his trousers, and was ready
with a learned explanation.

“Old, bag o’ bolts. She’d shake something loose every hundred miles.”

He talked the owners into dazed acquiescence.

“She wants ’er timing-wheels resetting, sir. Had ’em done by the village
blacksmith—what? Of course. And the universal joint wants looking to,
and her water-pipes are all fur and muck. There’s not much I don’t know
about cars.”

Tod was very busy and very happy, and business shaped so well those
first few months that Tod began to buy furniture and to think of
marriage. He wanted Milly, and he wanted her very badly, for she had
begun to provoke the natural man in him and spring was in the air.

She came hurrying into the garage one Wednesday afternoon, and found Tod
in the little glass box of an office, entering up his books. Last
month’s receipts were more than respectable, and Tod was remembering
that he needed a summer suit.

“Oh, Toddy, dear!”

“Hallo!”

“It’s free—it’s to let.”

“What’s to let?”

“Why, our little house—that ducky house in Guildford Lane.”

She was flushed and greatly excited, and her enthusiasm had all the
seduction of her sex.

“I’ve just seen the board up. The people are going out next week. I went
in and asked.”

Tod got off his stool and kissed her.

“You want it, dear?”

She snuggled in close, ignoring Mr. Love, who was deep in the “innards”
of a derelict car.

“Of course I want it. Don’t you, Toddy?”

“I want all sorts of things, and I want them so badly——”

“Oh, naughty boy! But it will be rather nice, won’t it, just you and me
in that dinky house?”

“Let’s go and look at it,” said Tod, putting business behind him.

Needless to say, they took “Carnarvon,” with its pepper-pot turret, mock
oak, casement windows, gravel path, and standard roses. Tod prepared to
be married in June. The sun shone. All the pessimism of Uncle Remus and
Mr. Rathbone Cragg seemed like so much senile snivelling.

About a week before Tod’s marriage Bob Twine strolled up Guildford Lane
and slouched self-consciously through the gate of “Carnarvon.” It was
six o’clock and he found Tod pottering about the house. Tod was in his
shirt-sleeves, and his hair was rumpled, and Bob Twine caught him on his
hands and knees in the kitchen, nailing down linoleum.

“Hallo, kid.”

Tod looked hot and happy, but quite pleased to see Twine.

“Hallo, Bob. What do you think of this pattern?”

“Classy. All alone, are you, and learning to be the handy man?”

“Come and look at the house,” said Tod, getting up with all the
enthusiasm of a house-proud husband; “I’ve got the carpets down and most
of the furniture in.”

“Hire purchase, system, old chap?”

“Well, it’s easier to begin with. I hope to wipe off the lot by next
Christmas. Now look at this dining room suite. Millie chose it.”

“She did, did she? Mind if I light a cigarette?”

“Have one of mine. Aren’t those chairs jolly? And I think the carpet’s
just splendid.”

They strolled over the house, and even the obtusely happy Tod could not
help noticing that Bob Twine seemed glum and uneasy. The generous and
innocent Tod explained his friend’s depression by wondering whether he
was rubbing his own happiness into the raw of some discontent, and
whether Bob Twine yearned greatly for a home of his own.

“Why don’t you get married and settle down?”

Twine stared, swallowed something, and laughed.

“I’m not made that way, old chap, and I haven’t the money.”

“But you’ll want to get married some day.”

“Talk about something else,” said Twine flippantly. “Wish I was in the
army, somewhere east of Suez. God, that chap Kipling knows a bit.”

He threw the fag end of his cigarette into an empty grate, and became
boisterously cheerful.

“Well, kid, you’ll be the solemn father of a family in ten years. Taking
’em down to paddle, and teaching ’em to swim. Queer, isn’t it, and
you’re only a kid yourself. But—damn it—I wish you the best of luck,
and I hope the girl knows you deserve it. Think I’ll be toddling. So
long.”

Tod, holding a hammer in one hand and a box of tin-tacks in the other,
watched the swarthy man slouch down the path.

“Poor old Bob,” he thought; “he wants a home and won’t say so.”

Twine was cursing women and himself.

“I ought to have told him, but, damn it—how could I? He’s like a
blessed cherub blowing about with a hammer and a box of nails. Happy? My
God! Oh, you little devil, I hope you’ll play game by him.”

A week later Tod was married. It was a quiet wedding, not nearly so
decorative as Millie Hornblower would have desired. Her mother was the
only relative who attended it, and Tod wondered why Mrs. Hornblower
smelt of drink. The two bridesmaids came from Horrox’s, and Mr. Mann of
the Furnishing Department gave Miss Hornblower away.

Old Tom Lavender served as Tod’s best man. Millie had said that he was
far too old for it, but Tod had proved obstinate, for Tom Lavender had a
younger heart than most young men. Uncle Remus was not there, and he
sent no present. And away in the north the great Mr. Burgetrode was as
silent as death.

The honeymoon lasted four days, Tod not caring to leave the business to
Mr. Love and the garage boy. They spent the days at Maidenhead, Millie
Hornblower’s choice, and they did much lolling in a punt on the river, a
punt that refused to humour Tod’s first activities with a punt pole. But
who cared about progress? There were over-hanging and shadowy trees,
cushions, and a girl, a girl like a soft, white, sensuous flower with
lips tinted red.

For the first six months of his marriage Tod was supremely happy. He had
married a highly sexed young woman, a young woman who woke the dormant
man in him and taught him to delight in new physical experiences. During
those months Tod was the most passionate and uxorious of young husbands,
and his wife’s sensual frankness rather pleased him. She wanted him and
she wanted him with passion. His habits changed. His early morning swim
became a thing of the past. He ceased to play games, for sex was the
game of the moment, and Millie played it in all its variations. But she
did not want children.

“Just you and me together—for a while—Toddy. Besides, we can’t afford
them.”

They couldn’t.

The fact became very obvious to Tod. “Carnarvon” was a thirsty little
house, and Tod had begun to glimpse his wife’s social ambitions. He was
astonished at the number of things that appeared to be required for the
efficient functioning of a genteel house. They had no piano, but a piano
seemed to be a sign and symbol of grace, even though Millie could not
play it. People were beginning to call, and Millie gave little afternoon
bridge parties, and the cakes these people ate, and the cost of them,
worried Tod. His wife’s clothes became a problem. Two good suits were
sufficient for Tod, but the hunger of a woman’s wardrobe appeared to be
insatiable. She wheedled Tod into sundry extravagances; but that was not
the end of the tale, for when the finery had been bought, it had to be
displayed. Tod found himself taking stalls at the local theatre and
going there in evening dress. He even discovered himself in church,
walking up the aisle with a young woman who rustled, a young woman who
would never have set foot in the place had she been dressed in
sackcloth.

That red mouth and those plump white arms dominated him during the first
months of his marriage. He was proud of his wife, and rather proud of
“Carnarvon,” and the calceolarias and the dog, and the maid in white cap
and apron.

At the end of six months Tod began “doing figures.” The business was
prospering, but Tod realised that its prosperity was not commensurate
with Millie’s ideas upon expenditure.

Tod saw that he would have to expand.

He travelled up to town and saw the Semite of Wardour Street, produced
figures, and stated his reasons for believing in a possible expansion of
his venture. The result was the addition of two more taxis to Tod’s
stud, supplied on a system of deferred payments; and the hiring of two
more drivers. Tod had a weekly advertisement in the _Shaston Gazette_.
The red-fronted garage in Duke Street had an air of industrious
prosperity, and the garage boy began to call him “sir.”

But the surest proof of Tod’s success was the coming of Mr. Pumpelly. He
was a succulent and cheerful old Christian with a facial resemblance to
Father Christmas, and a partiality for loud check suits. Mr. Pumpelly
had been the principal jobmaster in Shaston before the birth of the
autocar, and he looked more the man of the horse than of the machine.
Geniality was his great weapon. “Good old Josh—one of the best, do
anything for anybody.” That was how Mr. Pumpelly glimpsed himself, and
there was a bland and worldly cunning under his rosy good humour, a
shrewdness that stood broad-bellied in the sunlight, and smiled.

Pumpelly strolled into Tod’s garage, and looked round with an air of
obvious approval, and then discovered Tod, Tod who had just booked an
order that spelt fifty pounds.

“Morning, Mr. Western. Nice little place you’ve got here.”

“It’s not bad,” said Tod.

“I was passing, and I dropped in to see whether you happened to have a
couple of 700 by 85 Avons in stock. I’m out; they’re wanted urgently.”

“Afraid I haven’t. I could let you have a pair of Speedwells.”

“No thanks. Don’t touch ’em. This your office, is it? Mind me strolling
in?”

Mr. Pumpelly strolled in with the genial casualness of a friend. He
looked at everything with interest, even at Tod’s stool. Then he lit a
cigarette.

“I’ll wire my London agents. Yes, a snug little place, and quite handy.”

And suddenly Mr. Pumpelly’s face beamed with the light of inspiration.

“Look here, Western, here’s an idea. Why shouldn’t we two fix up Shaston
between us? We’d tap different ends, and we’d keep other people out.
It’s an idea, ain’t it?”

“You mean we should go into partnership?” said Tod.

“That’s it. Why not?”

There was something in Tod that did not like Mr. Pumpelly, and he made
one of those swift decisions that was to prove catastrophic so far as
his future was concerned.

“Thanks, I prefer being on my own, but I’m obliged for the suggestion.”

“Think it over,” said Mr. Pumpelly with fatherly impressiveness; “it
would be a good thing for you.”

Tod thought it over, and two days later he wrote a private letter to Mr.
Pumpelly, refusing the offer of partnership. Mr. Pumpelly showed the
letter to a nasty, dark, dapper little fellow who had become interested
in Mr. Pumpelly’s business and was acting as manager.

“He’s one of the independent sort, Britt.”

Mr. Charles Britt passed a dirty forefinger across his little black
moustache.

“If he wants competition he’ll get it. I didn’t work five years with the
Solomons for nothing.”

 Chapter Twenty-one

So Tod’s boyhood died. It just faded away and left him, and he hardly
noticed that it had gone, and that it lay in the grave of his dead
mother. With it had passed all that was fine and adventurous and free in
Tod, that light of the morning look, the spirit of the boy bathing in
the sea.

He was a preoccupied young man, sometimes with a frown on his face, and
an air of sulky puzzlement. The business had begun to be irritating; it
absorbed the whole of Tod, was never satisfied, kept him awake at night,
and yet—like a fractious baby—was none the better for all the
fussiness lavished on it. All sorts of exasperating things happened, and
they happened in a way that made Tod feel very angry and very helpless.

Tod had become commercialised. Delilah had shorn the young Sampson, and
he had become the slave of the Philistines. The curse of the
civilisation of the West was upon him, the superstition that man was
born to make things, sell things, and to accumulate money, that nothing
mattered but money. And Tod was getting himself tangled up in the system
just as thousands of other young men get entangled in it and come to
believe that modern commercialism has been and is and will be for ever.

“Carnarvon” drove him daily into the scuffle with little half-petulant,
half-wheedling cries.

“Oh, Toddy, we must have a Chesterfield sofa.”

Or “Clara Brown has got one of those new musquash coats.”

Display had meant expansion, and expansion had brought Tod into the
presence of the economic god—Competition.

Mr. Julian Burgetrode could have lectured Tod on the subject of
competition. He would have said that it was part of Nature, a heritage
inherent in man, even wrapped up in his subconscious soul. It was
emulation, virile pride, the spirit of adventure, the beads and feathers
of the amorous savage, the jewel in the hilt of the sword of the hero.
He would have told Tod that the wrong sort of people had got hold of the
competition spirit. In other days there had been the Competition of
Prophets, the Competition of Knights, the Competition of Poets, Painters
and Scholars, the Competition of Cities over something finer than a herd
of swine, the Competition of Builders in the soaring spires of their
churches. These were noble emulations, emulations that the commercial
man had trodden into the hog-wash of his sty. Mr. Julian Burgetrode had
a ferocious contempt for the purely commercial man, and for the spirit
of industrialism of nineteenth-century England. He had fought his way to
the summit of things, he was a great captain of industry, he had
explored the great barrack from cellar to roof. And that is what he
called it: “A barrack.” “It won’t last,” he wrote to an American friend,
“and if we try to make it last, it will come down and bury us. And I’m
not so sure that I shall not be one of the first to get at the rotten
brickwork with a pick.”

There were other sayings of Mr. Burgetrode’s that might have reminded
Tod of his own father.

“We have created another sphinx and another riddle. The industrial
revolution and our concentration on purely material things made us
overpopulate ourselves with a swarm of slaves. The slaves are waking up,
only to find that we are still the slaves of the god we have created.
Too many millions of people—and food to be got for all! Our materialism
is a chain round our necks. No wonder the Sphinx grins like a cynic.”

“Competition, yes—it’s necessary—as things are. It may be brutal and
sordid, but it is the plain man’s only defence against greedy
commercialists. These gentlemen have to use their knives on each other.
Thanks to competition, they cannot give all their attention to cutting
their pounds of flesh off the poor ass.”

“Trusts and Combines. There should be no trust or combine that is not
state-owned for the benefit of everybody. I would have all trust
magnates treated as highwaymen or pirates.”

“The joy in the job. We have got to get back to that, or perish.”

Tod Western was just beginning to butt his head blindly against all
these problems, like a young man who was conscious of obstacles—and
rivals. Man does a lot of butting before he learns to stand still, to
reflect and to think. Most men can do nothing but butt all their lives,
till Death comes behind and kicks them into oblivion. And Tod was
getting well down and into the thick of it; but he played fair; that was
his great disadvantage.

He was generous to his wife, and she wasted his money and worried him
with her petty extravagances.

He trusted Love his mechanic, and Love robbed him in ingenious little
ways that Tod was too big to notice.

He trusted his two new drivers, and they too robbed him, filching
petrol, tools and oil, for Tod was an “employer,” and there is a certain
sort of new citizen who thinks that it is his right to perpetrate any
meanness upon the man who employs him.

These were minor matters. Tod did not notice them, for his consciousness
was absorbed in sensing a vague obstruction that baffled the expansion
of his business.

He began to remark all sorts of queer things.

None of the leading hotels ever ’phoned to Western’s garage for a car.

He had been unable to get himself appointed as agent by any of the
bigger manufacturing firms, and he inferred that old Pumpelly had sucked
that melon dry.

Nor was Tod satisfied with the work his taxis did at the station. So
dissatisfied was he during the second summer that he played private
investigator, and made the curious discovery that the porters appeared
to hail a Pumpelly taxi before one of Western’s. His own drivers loafed
in their seats, and did not seem concerned over the possible loss of
fares and tips, but Tod saw that it was bad business if Pumpelly did
three journeys to his two. And Pumpelly’s cars were more smartly kept
than Tod’s; there was no doubt about it all.

Tod tackled his men. He was generous and hated accusing other men of
slackness, but he had yet to learn that there is a type of man that
responds only to necessity and fear.

“Look here, you chaps, I want you to be brisker. What about that dirty
radiator? Take out your cushions, Smith, and let’s have a look at them.”

Smith’s cushions were a disgrace, and he roused Tod’s bile by being
insolent over it.

“Look here, Smith, if you’ve got no conscience, find one, or I’ll sack
you.”

Smith found a lick-and-a-scrape apology for a conscience, but he made up
his mind to do Tod down. He was that sort of man.

Tod happened to be out on the London Road one evening, and he saw a big
new boarding standing on a bank above the road. It warned tourists to
Shaston that Pumpelly’s Garage was the best, and the only garage usable
by a gentleman who respected his car. In fact Mr. Pumpelly lifted his
leg over Tod and any other rival in the town. He was the leading agent
for everything; he kept expert mechanics, even a consulting engineer.
And Tod was annoyed.

He had an even larger board erected higher up the London Road, so that
Western’s should be read before Pumpelly’s.

About that time Tod had his first quarrel with his wife, and like most
first quarrels it was an absurd affair. Millie had indulged in some
particular bit of selfish extravagance, and the arrival of the bill
coincided with the rise in the level of Tod’s financial worries.

“It isn’t sport,” he told her hotly. “Haven’t I enough worry without
this gorgeous waste of money?”

Millie passed from sulkiness to a fine, ranting fury, and from fury to
tears, and the fool man was beaten when that dear dew ran down into her
red mouth. It was the first time she had used the device against Tod,
and he surrendered instantly on that occasion.

There was a reconciliation of sorts, and they sat on the sofa with Tod’s
arm round his wife’s waist, and he tried to explain to her that he was
puzzled and worried. And Millie was vaguely alarmed. She had regarded
the business as booming, and Tod had never hinted at the malign
influence that appeared to be checking its expansion.

“Of course it’s old Pumpelly,” she said.

“But there is plenty of room for both of us. I can’t see myself going
out to spoil another man’s pitch.”

Tod’s wife had far more cunning than her man, and far more of the
suspicious business spirit, the spirit that applauds honesty when
honesty happens to pay.

“It’s business,” she told him; “any fool could tell you that. I’ll find
out.”

She was as good as her word, and she discovered more in a week than Tod
would have discovered in a year. Intuition helped her, the intuition of
a young woman who had no false ideas on public morality, and who could
put a plump forefinger unerringly on the sore that was eating into
Western’s prosperity. Moreover, she had certain gossips and friends, and
among them the wives of the local station-master and of a manager of one
of the smaller hotels. They told her just what she had expected them to
tell her. It was all so simple and so obvious that she thought Tod a
fool for not having used the same methods of persuasion, and she was
amazed with him for his lack of “smartness.”

“You are a kid,” she said a little contemptuously; “you have got a lot
to learn, Toddy.”

“What’s wrong?” he asked good-temperedly.

“You don’t look after your own interests. Suppose you’ve gone round the
hotels?”

“I sent them my card.”

She tittered, a vulgar little expression of scorn.

“Your card! You ought to have gone and seen all the hotel porters.”

“What for?”

“Who rings up for taxis? Why, the hotel porters, of course! Old Pumpelly
tips them all, and that’s why he gets the orders.”

“I don’t believe it,” said Tod.

“Well, if you are so green as all that——! And I’ve found out that old
Pumpelly has a ten-pound note divided up among the station porters each
Christmas. You see, they are smarter than you each time.”

“It depends on how you look at it.”

“Does it? I thought you wanted to make the business a success! I
shouldn’t be surprised, too, if Pumpelly hasn’t got at your drivers.”

“What d’you mean?” said Tod, with the battle-light in his blue eyes.

“Bribes ’em to be slack and let you down. Don’t be so simple. What
you’ve got to do is to out-tip old Pumpelly. Go round to the hotels and
tip the hotel porters a quid a-piece. You’ll get orders, and it will
pay. And I’d sack Love and the others.”

Tod flared up as his father had flared up before him.

“Go round and bribe people! No, thank you. I’m hanged if I’ll do it.”

“You’re a fool,” she said tartly; “it’s money that counts.”

 Chapter Twenty-two

Mr. Charlie Britt, the nasty, dark, dapper little fellow, now junior
partner of the firm of Pumpelly & Britt, had set out to smash Western’s
Garage. He had no spite against Tod as Tod, but Britt was combative and
greedy, and he belonged to the cut-throat school of business men who
believed in showing no mercy, and in tolerating no rival, if that rival
could be eliminated. Charles Britt’s methods were the methods of the
garrotter. Even political economists have admitted that business is
non-moral and that the man who sets out to be nothing but a trader has
logic on his side when he shuts up his Sunday conscience in his safe and
uses his wits as a wrestler uses his cunning. But Britt was one of those
men who work in the dark. He met Tod once or twice and was particularly
pleasant and polite to him, and Tod’s nature was too generous and open
for him to suspect of deliberately fouling him.

Western’s Garage had a run of bad luck. Two of Tod’s taxis were out of
action during the summer’s busiest month, one with a seized engine, the
other with a badly broken back axle. Smith was the offender in the
matter of the seized engine, a deliberate offender perhaps, and Tod
sacked him, but the mischief was done. Then Tod had trouble with a
private owner whose car was said to have lost a couple of electric
headlights in Western’s garage. The owner was so positive and so
combative that Tod paid him the value of the headlights, but the
incident worried him more than the loss of the money. He hated
suspecting people, especially men who worked with and about him every
day of the week. He spoke to Love about it, and Love’s self-assurance
frothed like beer.

“I have an idea where they went all right. No, it wasn’t Sid Smith.
D’you remember a tourin’ car with a dirty little Belgian chofer—stayed
’ere two nights? I guess the Belgian had ’em.”

Tod had to be satisfied with that.

The hoped-for expansion did not develop, and Tod began to realise that
he was living beyond his means. He had his monthly payments to make to
the Semite of Wardour Street, and these payments competed with the
demands of “Carnarvon.” Tod saw that the obvious and sensible thing to
do was to cut down his domestic expenses, concentrate everything on the
business, and to bide his time.

He walked home one evening, having made up his mind that Millie would
have to economise, and for the first time since his marriage he found
himself driven into a critical attitude towards his wife. Something
within him foresaw a “scene,” and Tod hated scenes, and in hating them,
asked himself why they should be necessary. Surely it was his wife’s
duty to back him up, and not to pout those red lips of hers whenever he
tried to talk about money?

And suddenly he became aware of the stranger in his wife, a woman whom
he did not know. Tod had been very much the slave of the business during
the past year; he had met Millie across the table, behaved as the
natural man to the natural woman, and yet he felt that they had drifted
apart. He had glimpses of Millie as a golden-haired young woman floating
about in a blue or cerise-coloured knitted coat, playing tennis, eating
chocolates, reading cheap novels, gadding about to other women’s houses,
and receiving other women into hers. She was out a very great deal, and
knew people whom Tod had never met. There were phases of her life and of
her day of which he was completely ignorant; he had never troubled his
head about how she passed her time or where she went, not because he did
not care, but because the world seemed good when she was happy. Yet, of
late, there had been less sunshine at “Carnarvon,” less of that
rhapsodic childishness and those melting exclamations of “Oh, Toddy, how
lovely!” Clouds? Yes, Tod had been conscious of clouds, but he had not
realised their significance.

He left that serious talk till after supper, and then lighting his pipe,
began with the jocular air of a man who felt uncomfortable.

“I say, Old Thing, I want to have a talk with you.”

Millie was instantly on the alert. When a man pretends to be cheerful he
is about to speak on those horrid subjects, retrenchment and reform.

“Oh? What about?”

“The business and the house. Come and sit on the sofa.”

But Millie preferred to stay where she was, and to hold sentiment and
emotion in reserve.

“I knew you were going to be a sport, dear. The fact is we have had a
run of bad luck, and I have got to go easy until I have paid off old
Goldstein. What we must do is to live quietly until we have the business
solidly established, and all the cars paid for.”

Millie asked him a question.

“What’s living quietly? Aren’t we living quietly now?”

It was a flank attack, an ambuscade, and Tod was thrown on the
defensive.

“We seem to spend a lot. There must be some things we can cut out.”

“Supposing you tell me what they are.”

“You ought to know more about it than I do. Besides, I don’t want to be
mean, dear; but you ought to be able to help.”

She held aloof and left him the responsibility of suggesting economies,
knowing that she held Tod at a disadvantage.

“If you think I’m extravagant, I’d like you to say how I might do things
differently.”

“We seem to spend a lot on cakes and stuff like that,” Tod blurted.
“Couldn’t you make them at home?”

“With only one servant?”

“You might make them yourself, dear.”

“And my clothes, too, I suppose?”

“Some of them. Hang it all, it has got to be done, Old Thing. I’m saving
every penny I can in the shop.”

She regarded him coldly.

“Have you been round the hotels yet?”

“No, and I don’t mean to.”

She shrugged.

“There you are; it’s always the way; a man expects a woman to do all the
dirty work and the scraping.”

“Don’t,” said Tod. “Don’t talk like that, dear. It hurts.”

She had meant it to hurt, but she let him kiss her.

“All right; we’ll cut things down in the house, but don’t grumble
if——”

“I shan’t grumble.”

He didn’t, even though his wife advertised her economies, and served
them like a bad egg under his nose. Millicent’s theory was that if you
make a man uncomfortable and deprive him of his little luxuries, he will
develop energy and show a more aggressive spirit in the acquisition of
money. She cut off Tod’s egg at breakfast, served him up unsavoury sorts
of hashes, limited him to milk puddings, and warned him against wasting
the gas. She made herself a dress, a flagrantly ugly and dowdy dress,
and wore it like a martyr when Tod was there to see. Once or twice when
he came home in the evening he found her lying on the sofa and
complaining that she had a headache, and Tod was the tender husband.

“Poor wifey! How did it happen?”

“I’m tired. It’s the work in the house. I’ve been doing the washing.”

She had washed three handkerchiefs and a camisole; Bella—the maid—had
dealt with the rest. But Tod was impressed, and he accepted his reduced
rations and all the main discomforts of his wife’s economies, and he
never murmured. His patience and his good temper annoyed Millie. The
primitive woman in her would have had more respect for a man who got up
and raged, and then rushed out with violence to spoil his neighbour. She
did not understand Tod; she began to think him a fool.

But Millie’s personal economies were not prominent. She did not curtail
her own pleasures, her gossips and her gaddings about, the little social
excitements of which Tod knew nothing. She ate her sweet cakes at tea,
when Tod had a sloppy cup and a couple of dry biscuits at the shop; she
bought her chocolates, went to the theatre and the cinema, and ran bills
at the drapers. She did not wear that home-made dress on any of her
expeditions, and her vanity found Shaston seafront a mirror of delight.

Tod had friends in Shaston who were wiser in the ways of Western’s wife
than the husband who stood grimly to business in his garage. Mrs.
Western was “fast.” She was not fastidious, and not too proud to amuse
herself with “pick-ups” on the front, strange young men who were out for
the sex adventure during their holidays by the sea. Millie bathed. She
went to the beach concerts, and struck up an acquaintance with the funny
man of the troupe, a swarthy person with a look of sensual
self-assurance and a prominent lower lip. Millie thought him
“frightfully funny and so smart.” Also, he was rather deliciously bold
and gave her guilty thrills.

People noticed her. It was part of her tragedy that she could never
escape notice, with that red mouth and that shining head of hair. It was
not the girl’s fault. Nature had designed her to be a woman of pleasure,
a creature of strong appetites, and, provoking the same appetites in
others, richly coloured creature to be captured and embraced. In
Millie’s case it was marriage that was wrong. She was a provider of
pleasure, not for one man, but many.

Bob Twine had taken an interest in Tod’s marriage, and he was one of
those who beheld the ripening of Tod’s wife. Twine hated her as a man
often hates a woman when he has had his will of her and then sees her
fool his friend. Twine was a moocher, a “parade monger,” an expert in
his knowledge of “girls.” He avoided Millie, and yet held her in view,
and that roving soldier’s eye of his saw all that was happening.

Bob Twine had yet another man inside himself. He was a lovable
reprobate, amusing, cynical, generous, an admirer of the virtues that he
himself would not cultivate. He had an affection for Mossup and a clear
admiration for Mossup’s wife. “She’s straight,” that was all he had to
say of her, “and it takes something for a pretty woman to be straight.”
He went often to the Mossups’ cottage, and sometimes they talked of Tod
and Tod’s marriage, seriously and like good friends who wished Tod
nothing but happiness.

“The kid’s made a mess of it,” Twine confided to Mossup. “How do I know?
Because I’m a bad lot myself, and you want to be a bad lot to know
anything about women.”

“How about me, Bob?”

“You! You know as much about women as I know about the Bible. One of the
few good ones took you by the collar and you ought to be grateful.”

“I am, Twine, I’m very grateful,” and his weak eyes grew moist; “but do
you think that Tod’s wife is—is immoral?”

Twine gave one of his short laughs.

“Same as me. I can’t keep away from the women, old chap; they’re so
gorgeous. And Millie Western is just like me—with a difference. She
can’t keep away from the men, the men who can bite. But she’s a liar,
I’m not a liar.”

Arthur Mossup looked worried, and worry had tended to become a fixed
factor in his life during the last six months. Like Tod, he was
struggling in the world of unbridled competition, and the little
business was like a half stifled plant making a pathetic struggle to get
at the light and the public. Mossup was hard up, desperately hard up,
and Olive was expecting a baby. The good fellow was not only a much
harassed draper but a household drudge, and his wife adored him.

“Arthur’s such a dear,” she told Twine. “If only we could have a little
bit of luck!”

“You deserve it,” he said, and added savagely, “some day I’ll go round
with a red flag and a hatchet.”

He left half a sovereign in Mossup’s pocket after they had come near
quarrelling over that piece of gold.

“Take it, you silly blighter. What do I want it for? It would only be
thrown away.”

“You’re a good chap, Twine; you don’t do yourself justice.”

“Don’t I? You go and ask some of the girls.”

Now poor Mossup’s worries culminated in a crisis that caught his pale
pride shivering like a half starved saint in a winter wind. Olive had
given birth to a boy; she had been very near death in the anguish of
that night, and her husband had about five shillings in his cashbox.
They were in debt, too, to the tradesmen, and Olive wanted coal and
delicacies. Poor Mossup’s heart was sore.

Tod was smoking his pipe in his den at “Carnarvon” and reading a trade
journal, when he heard the door-bell ring. It was the maid’s evening
out, and Millie was posing on the sofa with one of her headaches, so Tod
went to the front door. He found Arthur Mossup standing there, a
melancholy figure in the raw murk of a November evening, his spectacles
wet with rain and his seedy old black mackintosh flapping open at the
throat.

“Hallo, Arthur! Come in.”

Mossup was nervous and confidential.

“Can I have a few words with you, Western—alone, I mean?”

“Come along to my den, old chap.”

The drawing-room door opened while Mossup was making a great business of
wiping his boots on the door-mat, and Tod’s wife came to see to whom Tod
was speaking in the hall. Mossup looked frightened directly she
appeared.

“Oh, it’s you!”

“Good evening, Mrs. Western.”

“Arthur has just strolled in for a chat. We are going to have a smoke in
my den.”

Tod carried him off, and Tod’s wife sat in the drawing-room and listened
to the sound of their voices. It was Mossup who did most of the talking,
and in a jerky, excited and yet apologetic way, Tod’s voice breaking in
sympathetically from time to time. Mossup did not stay long. Millie
heard the two men come into the hall, and Tod opened the front door
while Mossup was putting on his mackintosh.

“God bless you, Western, God bless you, old man.”

Poor Mossup gulped out his gratitude, made a blind grab at Tod’s hand,
shook it, and blundered out into the night.

Tod was the son of his father, and perhaps he remembered that evening so
many years ago when Mr. Jacks had taken away those crisp notes from No.
13 Spaniards Row. Tod turned back with a glow at the heart and found his
wife standing in the doorway of the drawing-room. Her part was to play
the part of Tod’s mother; but her hands were not the hands of Nancy
Western, but the greedy paws of a selfish and sensual young woman.

“What did he want?”

Her voice was sharp and suspicious.

“Olive has just had a baby, and the poor old chap’s hard up.”

“I thought he had sneaked in to cadge. You didn’t give him anything?”

“Of course I did,” said Tod.

“How much?”

“Ten pounds.”

“You fool!” she said, “you silly fool!”

She was furious, and her mean fury astonished Tod.

“Chucking money away like that, and then expecting me to scrape in the
house! You soft-headed idiot! He’ll never pay you back.”

“I don’t expect him to,” said Tod. “I suppose I can do what I like with
my own money.”

The glow had gone out of his heart, and there was a frost in his blue
eyes. He remembered suddenly how his mother had kissed his father on
that night. And the pride fell out of Tod’s enterprise, his pride in the
business and the home. He walked past his wife, slammed the door of his
den, lit a pipe and smoked furiously. The woman had revealed herself,
and the revelation was the beginning of Tod’s disillusionment.

 Chapter Twenty-three

That winter saw the beginning of an unlovely home.

Tod’s wife had called him a fool for being generous to an old friend,
and those bitter words of hers were never recalled. Millie had all the
obtuseness of the little egoist. She did not know how deeply she had
wounded her man, that she had challenged and affronted all that was best
in him, and that such an insult is not forgotten. Tod might have
forgiven her, but she did not seem to realise that she had sinned
against the man in him, and that forgiveness was necessary.

And so they entered upon that most damnable of all states, a state of
silent and gradual hostility and of spiritual estrangement. The sex
spell had passed; the glamour of it had ceased to blind the man’s eyes,
and there was no beautiful comradeship to take its place. The boy and
girl affair was dead, and Tod found himself living with a woman who
began to betray a moral obtuseness that shocked his secret soul. The
“Giggles” of Pentworth had reappeared, and Tod stared at the apparition
with eyes of profound dismay.

She had called him a fool because his heart had gone out to poor old
Mossup, who wanted coal and food for a sick wife!

There were moments when Tod struggled passionately against this
disillusionment. He was lonely, worried, troubled about the future, and
he tried to get the woman in Millie back into his arms, but she had
grown strangely cold, and Tod was sensitive.

“Oh, shut up; I don’t want to be worried.”

So he left her alone, wondering why she had changed towards him, and
searching his own heart on the chance that the fault might be his own.
He could not help dating her change of temper from the time when the
business had ceased to thrive and money had become difficult. He had
forced economies upon her, and he remembered so well that the “Giggles”
of Pentworth had scorned the boy who had no pocket-money to spend.

Tod did a foolish but lovable thing. He went out into King William
Street one afternoon, bought a set of silver fox furs, and took them
home with him that night. He would show her that, though he had to be
careful, he was not mean, and that he had not forgotten her economies.

He hid the box under the sofa, and played the lover’s game.

“Someone has left a parcel,” he told her, with a touch of his old
affectionate shyness.

“Oh, what parcel?”

“You had better look.”

She glanced round the room, and then at Tod’s mock solemn face.

“What’s the joke?”

“I think it’s rather a nice joke.”

“I’m not good at guessing,” she said tartly.

Tod winced, and made haste to see something under the sofa.

“What’s that—a cardboard box?”

He had expected her to dash at it like a child, tear off the string, and
turn back the tissue paper; but she put a slow hand under the sofa, drew
out the box, and her face was the face of a woman preparing to tell a
lie.

“Gifford’s! I haven’t bought anything at Gifford’s for six months.”

“But I have,” said Tod.

And then she understood.

“Oh, Toddy!”

She had the fur stole round her throat, and her hands in the muff, and
she was looking in the mirror. She ran and kissed Tod.

“Aren’t they just lovely! You are a dear boy.”

That night they were lovers again, and her red mouth gave him kisses.

Tod’s fragment of a St. Luke’s summer lasted just three days. He was
opening his letters in the office when he came across one that made him
feel strangely sick at heart. It was a bill from Horrox & Horrox’s for
thirty-three pounds five shillings and sixpence, and it was accompanied
by a curt note that galled Tod’s pride. “We beg to remind you that this
account has been outstanding for over a year, and has been rendered to
Mrs. Western thrice previously. Unless it is settled immediately we
regret to say we shall be compelled to take other measures.”

Tod pulled out his cheque book, set his teeth, wrote a cheque for the
amount, and sent it round to Horrox & Horrox’s by the hands of the
garage boy. The paying of that bill necessitated his drawing upon money
that he had been scraping together to pay his quarterly tribute to the
Semite of Wardour Street. Tod was angry, and with the anger of a man who
felt that he had been deceived. Why had she hidden that bill? Why hadn’t
she told him?

He did not go home till late in the evening, for he had not the heart to
go home. And yet, after all, it was such a petty thing. Perhaps she had
forgotten, perhaps she had been saving money out of her housekeeping
allowance in order to pay that bill. Tod walked five miles before he
turned in at his own gate; he had been trying to invent excuses for
Millie, to save that little warmth of summer that had come back into
their lives.

Millie had been playing bridge at a house across the road. He found her
sitting in front of the fire, her shoes lying by the fender, and a box
of chocolates in her lap. She had been annoyed by something that had
happened during the evening, and Tod’s glum and worried face suggested
bad news.

He pulled up a chair and sat down.

“Tired, little girl?”

He was quite gentle, hating that bit of paper in his pocket, and hoping
with all heart that she would be able to explain it away.

“Where have you been? I thought you were coming to the Spencers’ with
me?”

“I’ve been rather worried, and I stayed late at the shop.”

“You always are worried,” she said peevishly.

And then Tod pulled out Horrox & Horrox’s receipted bill.

“I got this to-day, Millie. It rather upset my calculations.”

“What is it?” she asked him sharply, with a narrowing of the eyes and
one hand outstretched.

“A bill.”

Her whole face hardened.

“Nothing very wonderful in that, is there?”

“I didn’t expect it, that’s all. You might have told me, Millie.”

He passed her the bill, and she sat up and stared at it, pulling at her
lower lip with a crooked forefinger. She was angry—angry with Horrox’s
and angry with Tod.

“The fools shouldn’t have sent it to you.”

His eyes brightened.

“You were going to pay it?”

“Me?”

Her hard and sulky eyes killed the light in Tod’s.

“How was I going to pay it? Save farthings out of the grocer’s bill! I
wanted it kept back till things were going better.”

“Well, I’ve paid it,” said Tod grimly, “but it hasn’t made things easier
for me.”

And then he softened to her, hating to see her face so hard and sullen.

“I wish you’d tell me things, dear. I haven’t kept anything back from
you. Besides, it’s not good for the business to be threatened for not
paying your bills. Horrox’s letter made me mad.”

She gave him a quick, appraising glance, and then stared sulkily at the
fire.

“It’s not the only one,” she said; “you may as well know.”

He looked at her blankly, mastered a sudden spasm of anger, and became
more quietly and grimly serious. Tod was no whimperer. Games had taught
him not to squeal when he was hurt.

“You had better tell me. How much do you owe?”

She jigged a foot.

“About seventy pounds.”

“As much as that! It’s got to be settled.”

She expected him to explode, make a scene, and she would have understood
such a storm and known how to meet it; but Tod did not explode. He
seemed to grow quieter. And his self-control irritated his wife and
baffled her. It was like storming at a man who kept reading a book.

“I want you to realise what all this means.”

He pulled his chair close to hers, picked up a book, and, using the back
of Horrox’s bill, he began to jot down figures. His deliberation and his
calmness frightened her a little. She had no complete understanding of
the real Tod in her husband, and she noticed his compressed lips and the
intent steadiness of his blue eyes, and almost hated him for his
self-restraint. There was no flurry, no jerking movement of the hand
that was pencilling figures on the back of Horrox’s bill. In the words
of the vulgarly undisciplined: “I could have screamed.” That was the
effect Tod’s patience had upon his wife. What a silly, serious fool he
was! Why didn’t he stamp about and swear? Why didn’t he hit her in the
face—master her with his man’s strength? Her sensual nature would have
reacted to physical violence. She might have fought back at him like a
wild cat, but she would have ended by wiping his shoes.

Tod was too finely made to be the mate or master of such a woman, but he
succeeded in frightening her, and she hated him for the grave and
dispassionate way in which he did it.

“I want to go through these figures with you, Millie.”

“Oh, all right!”

He bent sideways towards her, using the pencil as a pointer.

“This shows what we have in hand, and what the business brings in
roughly each month. This column here shows what I owe, and what the
business and the house cost each month. It makes a pretty serious sum on
the wrong side. I had kept a hundred pounds out of the thousand as a
reserve in case of emergencies. I shall have to use it to pay these
bills.”

She stared at the pencilled figures, and swung a restless and rebellious
foot.

“I thought you were going to make such a success of it,” she said.

Tod held his temper fast by the throat.

“And so I may. This is going to cripple me for a time. Can’t you
realise, dear, that a young business has to be nursed, that it can’t
carry bills like this? We are sailing on the edge of bankruptcy. If
matters don’t mend we shall have to give up this house.”

He had frightened her, and frightened her so seriously that she began to
hate him. She was sullen, and quite untouched by his gentleness, the
gentleness of a man who was utterly in earnest. But she did not want to
give up “Carnarvon.” Her fear roused in her a strange spirit of
comradeship, the comradeship that unites two people who are in danger of
being swamped in an open boat. She began to look round for something
that she could use as a baler, while Tod stuck to the oars and kept the
boat’s nose towards the shore.

It is more than probable that Tod’s wife could have saved the business
if she had been given full authority and allowed to use her cunning. She
had an instinctive appreciation of what “business” meant in a town like
Shaston, and she had none of Tod’s scruples. Messrs. Pumpelly and Britt
would have found her a very notable rival, a handsome young woman with a
complete and natural knowledge of how to bribe men and make them serve
her. Had Tod been a man with a commercial imagination and a cynical
determination to make money, he would have stuck to the office and the
garage, put Millie in a car and let her play the part of out-of-door
partner. No sly women touting for votes at an election would have shown
more ingenuity. And she would have had no mercy on the men whom she
employed. Tod was too generous and too straight.

She even proposed some such adventure, seeing herself as a golden-haired
young fascinatress, interviewing hotel managers and dropping smiles and
half-sovereigns into the hands of persuadable porters.

“I bet I’d make things hum.”

But Tod would not have it. He was not inclined to see his wife turning
herself into a business tout.

Then Tod caught “flu,” and lay worrying himself to death over the
quarterly tribute that was due to the Semite of Wardour Street. He was
short of the money by ten pounds, and he could think of nothing else.
Worry and aspirin scuffled together over the question of his
temperature, and the drug had the worst of the squabble.

He kept babbling about selling things, and in the weakness of his fever
he allowed the truth about Mr. Burgetrode to slip into his wife’s hands.
She had wanted him to write to Mr. Burgetrode.

“I can’t. He cut me off, you know.”

“When you married me?”

“What’s it matter? I want an orange, dear. I want to suck an orange.”

The woman in her was touched for the moment, but much of her tenderness
was swallowed up in her anger against Mr. Burgetrode. “Thought I wasn’t
good enough, did he? Mean old beast!”

Tod talked delirious nonsense half the night.

“Ten pounds—I want ten pounds. Arthur had it; can’t ask Arthur. Beastly
Jew. Sell the piano and send him a cheque. Oh, darling, my head aches
so!”

Listening to his child’s anguish, Millie came by an idea. Tod was better
next morning, but utterly miserable and weak.

“I am going up to see that man in Wardour Street,” she said.

He protested. “But you can’t.”

“Of course I can. What’s his name—Goldstein?”

“Yes—Maurice Goldstein. But—Millie——”

“Don’t you worry, old dear. I’ll talk him around and explain things. I
dare say he’s not a bad sort, some bald-headed old blighter——”

“He’s quite young,” said Tod, “not more than forty. If he’d give me
another month——”

“You leave it to me,” laughed the young woman.

So Millie dressed herself in those silver fox furs and her most
fashionable hat, scented herself, gave her eyelashes the faintest of
touches, and travelled up to town to interview Mr. Goldstein.

 Chapter Twenty-four

Tod’s wife found Mr. Maurice Goldstein sitting in a revolving chair in
front of a rolled-top desk. She had been kept waiting in the outer
office for half an hour before Mr. Goldstein troubled to see her, and he
did not look up when the clerk showed her into his private room.

Maurice Goldstein was writing a letter.

“Excuse me a moment.”

The politeness was purely perfunctory. He sat at the desk like a
gourmand absorbed in a meal, his chin thrust forward with a queer
suggestion of truculence, the penholder looking lost in his fat white
hand. Millie, the woman, collected her impressions. The man was
prematurely bald, a ring of curly black hair running like a wreath round
an egg-shaped head. His neck was the neck of middle age, with an
incipient overflow where it met his collar. He wore white spats, and his
thighs and buttocks were huge. Millie had only a partial view of his
face, a glimpse of thick pouting lips between the full curve of nose and
chin.

He was deliberate. The letter was put into an envelope, addressed, and
the pen laid on its brass holder before his fat thighs swung round in
the pedestal chair. He had begun to speak while the chair was still
swinging and before he had seen Millie’s face.

“What is it you want? I’ve got an appointment in ten minutes.”

And then his sensual brown eyes seemed to set in a surprised stare.

Millie, the woman, smiled at him.

“I’m sorry to trouble you, Mr. Goldstein, but I have come to see you for
my husband.”

The Jew looked and said nothing. The sex in her was flowing into him
like wine.

“My husband is ill with influenza. He owes you a cheque or something. I
don’t know anything about money; and he was worrying, so I said I would
come and see you.”

Mr. Goldstein’s eyes filled suddenly with an amused, brown light.

“So many people owe me money, but it is not often, madam, that I see
their wives.”

“Do they? How jolly to be owed money.”

He made a fat movement of his chin.

“And not to get it—what!”

“Oh, you get it all right,” she said audaciously.

The Jew was pleased.

“My husband’s too easy, you know,” she went on with confidential
friendliness. “I know you helped to start him, but he wants a few
straight talks from a man who can make money. I’ve tried——”

He was rubbing one big fist in the palm of his other hand.

“And you said you knew nothing.”

“Did I?” and she laughed. “Anyhow, I do the economising. No, I don’t
like it.”

“You wouldn’t,” he said with an insolent and intimate gleam of the eyes.

He sat there and basked while she told him of Tod’s difficulties. In
fact, Tod cut a sorry figure in Maurice Goldstein’s office, having all
his money assets exposed to the cynical eyes of the Jew, and his moral
parts figured and discussed by a young woman who did not know the
meaning of the word loyalty. Goldstein was amused, more than amused. He
was astride of a great advantage, and he made the most of his mount.

“Excuse me. I’ll look up the transaction.”

He did not ring for a clerk, but turned up “Western’s Garage” in a
private ledger of his own. The figures seemed to amuse him, for Millie
saw him smiling over the book.

“You come from Shaston-on-Sea, Mrs. Western. I happen to be interested
in Shaston.”

“Oh?” said Millie. “How?”

Goldstein pulled out his watch.

“Lunching anywhere?” he asked curtly.

“Somewhere, by myself.”

“I’d like the honour of giving you lunch.”

“What about that appointment?”

His brown eyes held hers.

“It’s off. Will you come?”

“It’s very nice of you, Mr. Goldstein, but I——”

“Please yourself,” he said, with a sensual smile and a bluntness that
she understood. “I want to have a talk about Shaston and your husband’s
business.”

“I really am rather hungry,” she allowed, looking up at him under
mischievous lashes.

He rang the bell.

“Smith, get a taxi. I shan’t be back till three.”

Tod’s wife returned to Shaston like a cat that has been lapping cream.
It appeared that Maurice Goldstein was interested financially in the new
Regent’s Hotel that was to be opened on the seafront. It was a very big
hotel, one among half a dozen that were being floated by a syndicate in
various south coast towns. Goldstein had his big hands in the business;
he had his big hands in many businesses, and the smell of his money had
entered Millie’ nostrils. He was the sort of powerful and cynically
sensual man who can be very attractive to a sensual woman. The Assyrian
bull in him was more than symbolical.

Tod was eating an orange when his wife opened the bedroom door. He
looked gaunt and grey in the gaslight; his head an untidy mop, his chin
all brown stubble. And, somehow, he looked poor.

“I settled it,” she began, vaguely patronising. “Goldstein’s not a bad
sort. He won’t press you.”

Tod’s big eyes filled with relief.

“That’s good. I’ve been lying here all day, worrying my head off. How
did you manage it?”

“Just common sense. Besides, you old silly, it’s the first time you have
been late with the money, and Goldstein’s out to back you, and not smash
you up. It’s just common sense.”

She did not tell Tod that she had lunched with Goldstein, or that the
man was interested in the new Regent’s Hotel. It was no business of
Tod’s to know that Goldstein was coming down to stay at Shaston. Millie
foresaw possibilities in the situation, a chance of dabbling with her
own hands in financial experiments. She knew that men could be very
useful, but Tod was too touchy a fool to be told about such diplomacies.
She fancied she knew how to manipulate Maurice Goldstein.

Of that very sinister period in Tod’s life there is little that is worth
the telling. Its culminating incident collected the whole glare of the
affair’s significance. Some three months passed, and in spite of all his
efforts Tod still owed the Semite of Wardour Street some sixty pounds,
but the summer season was at hand, and Tod forecasted a new briskness in
the business. He had not been swamped by any more unexpected bills, and
Millie appeared to be spending less money. She had developed a new
penchant for going up to town once a week and buying her groceries and
household necessaries at the Stores, and Tod had nothing to say against
it, since she could prove that it saved money. Also, she seemed more
contented, more satisfied with something.

Early in May Tod went North to take the delivery of a client’s new car
at the Sangster Company’s works, and to drive it down to Shaston. He
told Millie that he expected to be away for two nights, as the new car
was rather an expensive production, and he had no intention of pushing
her through a speed trial on the way down. He meant to stay a night in
the North, and break the journey somewhere near Oxford. Millie said that
she envied him the drive, gave him a packet of sandwiches to put in his
little black handbag, and then darted out and sent a telegram.

The Regent’s Hotel had just opened for the summer season, and she dined
there that night with Maurice Goldstein. The Jew had a big car with him,
and after dinner they went out in the car, Goldstein driving himself,
and they got lost in the lanes among the Bramber woods. The lights were
out when they drove back to Shaston.

Now if Tod had kept to his programme, and not bustled the new “Sangster”
southwards on the afternoon of his arrival, he might have remained the
blind man in the home for months to come. “You can take her away at
once,” said the works manager, “she’s O.K., and won’t want touching for
a hundred miles.” So Tod took her away, knocked a hundred and fifty
miles off the journey before putting up for the night, and at five
o’clock next afternoon he was within ten miles of Shaston and speeding
along the London road with the woods all green under a clean, spring
sky.

“Wonder if she’d do Furnace Hill on second?” thought Tod.

The experiment challenged him. He swung the car to the left at the next
lane, and saw an A. A. scout peddling up the hill, looking very hot and
thirsty.

The man got off his machine and signalled to Tod to stop.

“Not going Shaston way are you, sir?”

“I am,” said Tod.

“I just got a message from a boy that there’s a car broke down the other
side of Furnace Hill. I’m going to ’phone up a garage.”

“I’m your man,” said Tod. “I’m Western of Western’s Garage. I’m going
round by Furnace Hill, and I can stop and see what’s wrong.”

“Then I can leave it to you,” said the scout; “I want my tea. Get it at
Lamb’s Corner.”

Tod drove blindly towards his disillusionment. The new car soared up
Furnace Hill on second speed, and Tod let her “free wheel” down the
opposite slope, delighting in the noiseless glide of her and the taut
silence of her youth. Furnace Hill had formed part of the old forest,
yet had remained a wilderness of gorse, broom, bracken and brambles,
with thickets of Scots fir and groves of straggling oaks. Tod knew it
well, and by the bend where a famous old yew sent its black plumes up
into the blue Tod came upon a yellow car standing derelict at the side
of the road. Tod’s car had come coasting silently round the corner. He
pulled up, got out, and looked about for some sign of the owner of the
yellow car.

Then he heard voices, and a woman’s laugh—a laugh that ended in an
ecstatic and giggling scream. There was a little wood here, full of
bluebells and surrounded by masses of yellow broom. Tod pushed through a
gap in the brushwood, and came upon a grassy space, a smooth trickle of
green that lost itself like water in the shadows of the wood. He saw a
girl and a man here. The man was bending over the girl, kissing her
throat with a sort of savage violence. Her bright hair and face were
visible above his dark and gluttonous head, and her eyes were closed
like the eyes of a dreaming, sensual cat. Her arms were round the man’s
neck.

And Tod recognised his wife.

He remembered making some sort of noise in his throat. He remembered the
two figures disentangling themselves, the man getting up clumsily on his
hands and knees, and turning a savage and sheepish jowl. The woman sat
up, dead white, patting her hair with her hands, and staring at nothing
with expressionless blue eyes.

Tod recognised Maurice Goldstein.

“What do you mean by kissing my wife?”

Even in the thick of his anger he felt the paltriness of it all, the
sordid smear on the face of his wrath.

The man grinned. He looked yellow, but there was a savage tawniness in
his eyes.

“Because she asked me to.”

“You cad!” said Tod.

He felt that nothing mattered—nothing but the necessity for hammering
this bulk of blackguardism with his fists. And hammer him he did, though
Goldstein made a fight of it with a heavy, blundering ferocity that
panted and got out of breath. Tod smashed him in the face till the white
bulge of it was all blood, and his big mouth a dripping thing full of
teeth and redness.

Tod’s wife stood and watched, pulling at her lower lip with her
forefinger, her nostrils quivering. There was something in Tod’s eyes
that fascinated her, a blue glare, a quick, calculating fury. She heard
the smashes he landed on the other man’s face.

Goldstein went down, dazed, his teeth showing in a bloody, snarling
grin.

Tod’s wife fawned suddenly upon her man.

“Oh, Tod, you licked him.”

Tod just turned his head slightly, and gave her an oblique stare.

“Come on,” he said, “come on, damn you!”

She followed him with extraordinary meekness, but loitered a moment by
the yellow car.

“My coat’s in here.”

She reached for a fur coat, but Tod’s eyes had become the merciless eyes
of a man who had been shocked out of all illusions.

“Leave that alone. I didn’t give it you.”

She left it alone, and, following him into the other car, sat staring
stiffly in front of her.

Tod drove down the hill, that blue glare still in his eyes, and
Goldstein’s blood showing on his knuckles. He felt that he had come to
the end of the world, that all the conscious pride had gone out of his
life, and that there was nothing left to him now that he had thrashed
the man who had debauched his wife. He had trusted her, and the reaction
was all the more fierce.

“How long has this been going on?” he asked her suddenly.

He was the master for the moment, and there was a whimpering note in her
voice.

“Oh, Tod, don’t be so horrid! I couldn’t help it. He took me by
surprise.”

“How?”

“I’d been out with him in the car, and we had a breakdown, and we were
just waiting for help. He seemed to go mad suddenly just before you
came.”

Tod did not believe her.

“Did he give you that fur coat?”

“He made me take it. I didn’t want to offend him.”

“But what were you with him for at all? It can’t have been the first
time.”

“How beastly you are! I was trying to help you and the business. He’s
got such influence. Don’t you see?”

“Good God!”

His nausea was complete. Her moral obliquity, the coarse cynicism of her
confession shocked the very deeps of his manhood. And he did not believe
her. He could not forget that look of sensual rapture he had seen upon
her face.

“Well, that’s the end of it,” he said.

“What d’you mean?”

“I have got to pay that blackguard what I owe him. I shall sell things
and give up the house. I don’t see that the business is much use—now.”

She said nothing. Tod’s face convinced her that there was nothing to be
said. But she began to hate him again, this inopportune, straightforward
fool who had blundered in and spoilt the cunning of her intrigue. She
would have looked at it as a “bit of fun,” fooling two men and getting
what she wanted. Lots of women did it.

She told him so, and he flashed round at her with sudden fury.

“Shut up—damn you! You don’t understand.”

He drove with cool recklessness into Shaston, and pulled up at the end
of Duke Street.

“You’d better go—back to the house.”

He had refused to say “home,” and she noticed it.

“All right. Are you coming?”

He drove on as though he had not heard her.

 Chapter Twenty-five

Tod drove the new car into his garage and switched off the engine, and
when the steel heart of the machine ceased to beat, something in Tod
gave up also. He felt tired, unstrung, yet curiously callous. He mooned
vacantly about the place for five minutes, like a man on the edge of
some great sickness, and then habit caught him and sent him into the
little office to see if there were any letters.

He was opening an envelope when he noticed Maurice Goldstein’s blood on
his hands.

“Filth!”

Strangely nauseated, he went out and shouted to Love, who was changing a
burst tyre on one of the taxis.

“Get me a bucket of water and some soap, will you?”

He stood there and washed, and the act of washing touched some live
memory in him, a memory of his father’s bloody knuckles and all the
passionate idealism of his own childhood. He remembered his mother and
the white flame of her scorn and her anger, and how that other
blackguard had been thrashed and paid. A sudden surge of emotion rose in
Tod’s throat. He straightened and stood wiping his clenched hands on his
handkerchief, realising this money-shop of his and the things that it
had made him suffer. He seemed to see everywhere the fat white face of
that Jew, the face he had punched into a bloody pulp. The white cans of
oil on the shelf had the colour of it; the greasy smears on the floor
were like the slimy marks of a beast’s track.

“Good God!” said Tod; “it’s his, it belongs to him. I wouldn’t touch it
again.”

He turned to Love.

“Here, you can leave that tyre; I want to lock up.”

Love scratched his nose with a black thumb.

“Jim’s out, at the station. And there’s a theatre to do at eight.”

“All right. That can finish the day.”

He walked back into the office and wrote a letter to the owner of the
new car. He wrote also to a firm of solicitors in Shaston, Newton and
Smythe, and asked for an appointment with the senior partner at eleven
o’clock next morning. Later, he locked up the shop, posted the letters
at the general office, and loitered a moment as though undecided where
to go.

Some impulse sent him wandering into old Shaston, the Shaston of his
early days, the Shaston of the Flowerdews and of Kitty of the soft eyes.
And Tod wandered and dreamed. He knew that something tragic had happened
to his life, that all the little strivings and ambitions of the last
three years had left him at the crossroads, with a stick and a bundle in
his hand. “Carnarvon” had come tumbling down like a child’s house of
bricks. “Giggles,” the little minx of Pentworth, had proved herself the
mother of the woman.

Tod’s mind was like a room hung with many pictures—memories; memories
that were so vivid and so significant that he marvelled at his own
blindness. Had not his mother said to him: “Tod, never marry until you
have been in love ten times.” Yet what a wonderful comradeship it had
been, that marriage of his mother and his father, and Tod’s heart
yearned that night towards the memory of those parents who were dead. If
only his mother had been alive, if he could have talked to her, put his
head on her knees! Yes, he felt like that.

And Mr. Burgetrode! Mr. Burgetrode had told him the truth. What had he
got out of marriage, anyway? It had made a money slave of him, tempted
him to do mean and cowardly things, and ended by mocking the elemental
pride of his manhood. His woman had——! But Tod fought the vile
thoughts off and thrust them aside, realising even in the depths of his
disillusionment, that foul thoughts make a man cruel and a coward. He
did not mean to be either cruel or a coward, even if life had proved him
a fool.

Tod wandered down Ponsonby Place, looking up at Mr. Eli Flowerdew’s name
on the familiar shop. He found himself wondering whether he would have
been happy with Kitty, and some boyish instinct cried out that she would
have loved him as his mother had loved his father. Memories, memories;
they were big and heavy in this throat! Dusk was falling, and he saw the
White Cliff, like a great grey ghost watching over the black fishing
boats and the sea. Bob Twine had taught him to swim down yonder, and
under the White Cliff he had spoken his last words to Kitty Flowerdew.

Tod crunched along the shingle towards the blue hollow of the night,
past the black fishing boats, till there was nothing but the lonely
sweep of the beach and the swish of the sea. An old hull lay derelict
here with shingle banked high about its black rump, and Tod sat down
with his back to the boat and watched the tide come in. It was very dark
now, and darkness lay upon the waters, but a stealthy sheen of silver in
the east told him the moon was rising.

He did not want to go back to that house; he did not want to move; he
felt he could sit there all night. It was clean and beautiful and still,
and Shaston seemed far away. Good God! how he hated the place, how he
hated everything save the quiet sea and the rising moon. What beasts
people were! And then he thought again of his mother and little Kitty
Flowerdew. No, there were good people in the world, and Tod’s sturdiness
stiffened its back. He had got to make a fresh start, somehow, get out
of that doll’s-house. And Millie——? He felt a great loathing and a
great pity for his wife.

Tod had dreamed there for an hour, and the moon was a great white shield
over the sea, when he heard someone walking on the shingle. The
footsteps came his way; slow, heedless footsteps that seemed to take no
account of distance or of time. Tod looked up and saw the black shape of
a girl. Her face was turned towards the sea, and there was something
poignantly familiar to him in the slim shape of her, the slight droop of
her head, and the curve of her throat. She stood there a moment, a
figure of indescribable hopelessness and desolation, not seeing the man
sitting with his back against the boat, conscious of nothing but herself
and the sea.

“Oh, Mother, Mother!”

Tod heard the words quite distinctly, and the voice was the voice of
Kitty Flowerdew. She seemed to hesitate a moment, and Tod’s heart seemed
to stand still. It was Edwin Rook’s wife, Kitty of the soft eyes,
following in the footsteps of her mother, that mother who had drowned
herself on Shaston beach.

Suddenly, he saw her unpin her hat, drop it on the shingle, and walk
towards the soft swish of the incoming tide. Tod was up instantly, the
moonlight in his face.

“Kitty!”

She stopped dead and stood in an anguish of terror and helplessness, but
she did not turn to see who had called her by name. She heard Tod’s
footsteps on the shingle, and shivered like a woman who has borne a
child and is weak and cold with pain.

“Kitty. It’s Tod.”

She drooped, and suddenly began to weep, and Tod stood there, greatly
shocked, and wanting to hold her hand. The bitterness of his own tragedy
lost itself in her tears.

“Kitty, what’s the matter, dear?”

She wept unrestrainedly and with the frank anguish of a child.

“Oh—don’t—please—Tod.”

He remembered so well that little, helpless cry of hers. He had taken
her hand.

“Come and sit down, dear. It’s so quiet and peaceful.”

She let him take her to the old boat, and they sat down in its shadow,
Tod deep in the waters of pity, the girl still weeping out her heart.

“I want to drown myself—oh—I want to drown myself,” she said, with an
anguish that was naked and unconcealed.

Tod’s upper lip quivered.

“Tell me all about it—dear. I came here because I was miserable, and
you came here too.”

“I can’t bear it,” she said, “I can’t bear it any longer. Let me drown
myself, Tod—dear Tod. I’m only in the way.”

Tod felt torn between a sense of his own helplessness and a fierce
desire to go and smash somebody, the man who had been a brute to this
child.

“You mustn’t, dear. Try and talk to me. I’ve been very unhappy, Kitty.
It ought to make us friends.”

“I can’t talk about it,” she said brokenly; “they want me out of the
way.”

“Who are ‘they’?” Tod asked very gently.

“Edwin—and——”

“Don’t tell me if it hurts too much.”

“And—my sister.”

“Good God!” said Tod.

A second sex horror thrust itself upon him, a horror that was more
brutal than the tragedy of his own marriage. So Rook had tired of this
dark-eyed child, and found that vixen of a sister ready to intrigue with
him? Lena Flowerdew had always been jealous of Kitty, and Tod had
fancied that she had wanted Edwin Rook, but how had it happened, how
could two people have been so devilishly callous?

He did not know what to say to Kitty. There seemed nothing to be said.

“Are you sure, dear?” he asked her.

She caught her breath and shuddered.

“Yes, I saw them. Oh, I can’t talk about it! And Edwin hates me now; he
thinks me a fool, and he was angry when baby died. He was only
angry—but it broke my heart.”

“Poor Kitty!” said Tod, putting an arm round her.

She whimpered again, for she had been a lonely and frightened little
woman with no one to talk to.

“Of course he ought to have married Lena. Lena always wanted him, and
hated me because he wanted me then. She’s more suited to him; she’s
hard, and thinks of nothing but business and money; and she wouldn’t be
afraid of him.”

Tod’s teeth came together. So she was afraid of Rook; he bullied her. It
seemed like torturing a caged bird.

“I don’t understand,” he said; “I couldn’t understand anybody being
rough to you, Kitty. What’s to be done?”

“There’s nothing to be done,” she answered, with tragic simplicity. “I
ought to do what Mother did. If baby had lived I might have borne it,
but now——”

She broke down again, and Tod sat and stared fiercely at the sea.

“Have you told your father?”

“He wouldn’t believe me, and Lena’s his favourite.”

“Haven’t you any friends, dear?”

“Not real friends. I couldn’t talk to anybody as I’ve talked to you.”

He lifted one of her hands and kissed it.

“That’s the finest thing I have ever had said to me, Kitty. Well, I’m
going to be your friend, and you can always trust me, dear. I’ll think
it all over. Now I want you to let me take you home.”

“Oh, Tod, I can’t!”

“But, dear, try and bear it till something can be done. You know, I used
to be in love with you; I wanted to marry you.”

“I wish I had married you, Tod,” she said; “you wouldn’t have been
cruel.”

He tried to talk to her calmly and sensibly, and to make her realise
that he was in utter earnest when he said he meant to be her friend, and
so broken was she in spirit that she obeyed him with a meekness that
touched his heart. He led her back to Shaston along the shingle, trying
to speak to her as he thought his own mother would have spoken. He felt
her flinch when they reached the street in which she lived, but she went
on, and then paused a little way from the house.

“Don’t come any farther, Tod.”

“I must see you again, Kitty. I’ll walk out there every night till you
come.”

“Dear Tod,” she said, and slipped away in the darkness.

Tod walked home with the soul of him purged and strengthened. There was
no light in “Carnarvon,” and Tod let himself in silently, lit the gas in
the hall, and looked into the empty rooms. Millie had gone to her
bedroom; Tod could hear her moving about in the room above, and the
thought of her nearness and of her share in his life brought back the
vivid presence of his own problem. Kitty Rook’s misery had softened him,
but he felt that he could not go near his own wife. The physical man in
him rebelled.

But he went up and knocked at Millie’s door.

“Yes?” said her voice, toneless and sulky; “the door’s locked.”

“Good night,” Tod said mechanically.

He improvised himself a bed in the little spare room, and lay awake
thinking of the future. Poor Kitty’s tragedy outbid his own. She was so
helpless, so humiliated, so much the child hungry for affection, and Tod
wondered what he could do to help her.

But his chivalry was a thing of the air, a dream that was to die with
the news of the morning.

Kitty Rook had drowned herself, and in spite of Tod. Some fresh impulse
of despair had seized her after he had left her and when the comfort of
his comradeship had been withdrawn. She had gone back to the beach, and
some boys who were bathing found her body floating next morning under
the White Cliff.

 Chapter Twenty-six

Tod breakfasted alone. Millie had shirked meeting the expected challenge
of his face, and she took her meal in bed, and waited for the sound of
Tod’s going.

Habit kept him to the clock. He walked down to Duke Street, smoking his
favourite bull-dog pipe, and thinking over the things that he had to do.
Goldstein would have to be paid at once, some of the furniture sold, and
a prospective tenant found for “Carnarvon.” Tod was still in doubt about
the business, whether he should go on fighting, and trust to the summer
season to pull it through. Kitty’s eyes seemed to be watching him, and
he remembered that he had posed as the strong friend, and had urged her
to make a fight for it, while he—Tod—thought over the possibilities.
He could not tell a girl to make a fight of it, and then back down
himself.

Tod found a letter waiting for him from Newton and Smythe. Love handed
it to him with his usually dirty hand.

“A boy brought it.”

Tod was asked to see Mr. Newton at twelve o’clock. The senior partner
was engaged at eleven.

Then the owner of the new car called, a middle-aged person who was as
excited as a boy.

“She’s smart, Western, isn’t she? Nice lines, what? And she’s fast, I
suppose?”

“Climbed Furnace Hill on second,” said Tod, wearily.

“Some car! Let’s have a look at the engine. I want to take her out and
try her.”

It was a quarter to twelve when Tod walked down Duke Street on his way
to the office of Newton and Smythe. By the clock tower where Duke Street
met King William Street and Marlborough Crescent, Tod’s path crossed
that of a newsboy who was selling the early edition of a local paper.

“Lady drowned at Shaston,” shouted the boy.

Tod stopped with a jerk.

“Paper.”

“Right, sir.”

He stopped by the clock tower and read the news of Kitty’s death.

“We regret to announce that the body of Mrs. Kitty Rook was found in the
sea this morning near the White Cliff. Mrs. Rook was the younger
daughter of our well-known townsman Mr. Eli Flowerdew,” &c.

 * * * * *
