Tod was shocked, shocked to the deeps. So she had drowned herself after
all, poor, wounded, broken Kitty, even as her mother had drowned herself
fifteen years ago?

“There’s nothing else to do, Tod.”

Was she right?

Tod had a lump in his throat and the wandering steps of a man who saw
nothing for the moment. The driver of one of Mr. Pumpelly’s new
motor-buses swung the steering-wheel round and bawled at him.

“’Ere, wake up! Are you going to market?”

“Sorry,” said Tod.

“With blokes like you about a man’s life ain’t worth livin’.”

Tod laughed with laughter that was near to tears.

But somehow Kitty’s death had shocked the last remnants of commercial
vanity out of him. What a sorry game it was, a scramble with men like
Flowerdew and Edwin Rook, that fat old cynic Pumpelly, and such
money-beasts as Goldstein! There were some men who played the game
cleanly, but there were others who would foul you whenever they could do
it safely or with advantage, and Tod had an idea that the men who fouled
were the men who won. How was it that he had let himself be drawn into
this little back-street, swindling business, when he might have been
soldiering or sailoring or out in the wilds somewhere, living like a
man? Mr. Burgetrode was right, damnably right. He remembered his
father’s saying about people with penny-three-farthing souls.

So Tod walked into the office of Newton and Smythe, and presented a new
and steel-bright decision to a phlegmatic old solicitor whose creed in
life was compromise.

“I want to get out of this business and pay everybody, and I want to get
out of it at once,” said Tod, “and I want to pay this chap Goldstein
to-day.”

Mr. Sobersides tried to smooth Tod down.

“There is no need to do anything rash, Mr. Western.”

“I’m not doing anything rash,” said Tod; “I’m only having a wash and
getting rid of my dirty clothes. The furniture in my house is all paid
for; so is one of the taxis. Can’t you raise money on that?”

“Certainly, when the security has been looked into. Am I to understand
that you want us to act between you and this Goldstein, to wind up the
business, settle with all your creditors, and reserve as much of your
capital as we can?”

“I want everybody paid,” said Tod, “and the thing done cleanly. There
might be a clear hundred or two left over, but I’m not counting on
anything.”

Mr. Newton scratched his chin with the end of a pen.

“Look here, my young friend,” he said; “let me advise you to hold your
tongue for a week and say nothing about selling. Leave it to us. The
business is a going concern, and we can sell it as such. You can let us
have your books?”

“Of course.”

“And perhaps you would like us to hold the matter up for a day or two
while you think it over?”

“If I thought it over for ten years,” said Tod, “it wouldn’t make any
difference. It’s a question of conscience.”

Old Mr. Newton thought Tod an excitable and rather overinflated young
man, but there appeared to be a woman in the case, though Tod had said
very little about his wife.

“So you will let me have your books, Mr. Western, and all outstanding
accounts, and your pass-book, and the copy of any agreement with this
Mr. Goldstein?”

“You shall have them to-night,” said Tod.

He walked out of Mr. Newton’s office, feeling that he had flung
something off his shoulders and that the day was the better for it. He
wanted to be conscious of nothing but Kitty, Kitty who in her anguish
had refused to accept any compromise and who had carried her secret with
her to the sea. There was only one street in Shaston for Tod that
morning, the street where she had lived and where he had parted with her
only a few hours ago, and he walked down it, and saw the drawn blinds of
the house and a few people gaping at it with vulgar curiosity.

Tod stood a moment against the railings of the house opposite, and
wondered what Edwin Rook thought of his wife’s death. He felt that he
wanted to get Rook by the collar, and drag him into the most crowded
street of Shaston and thrash him as he deserved to be thrashed. And then
someone bumped against Tod, a silly old woman with a vacant face and a
claw-like hand clutching a bag.

“Beg pardon. She’s in there, isn’t she?”

“Who?” Tod asked.

“Why, the woman who drowned herself. Suppose she’d got into some sort of
trouble.”

Tod froze. The old woman was looking at the drawn blinds and smiling as
though death and life were a mere circus procession.

“They say she had all her clothes on, so it must have been sooicide.”

“You old beast,” said Tod and walked on.

But in spite of his anger, his heart was softened, and any thought of
dramatic and physical interference melted out of his mind. Kitty lay
asleep somewhere in that house, the long sleep that even the hands of
silly old women could not jeopardise. She was beyond all recriminations,
humiliations, anguish, shame. She had kept her secret, and if there were
a merciful God, Tod knew that God held her soul, and that these people
who had killed her would have to answer for it to Him. And Tod told
himself that he would hold her secret sacred. It was too tragic and
poignant a thing to be brawled over and advertised by using the toe of a
boot. What was the use of telling Rook and the Flowerdews that they were
swine? If Kitty’s death had not made them realise it, no human words
would ever do so.

Tod walked on along the beach to where the old black boat lay half
buried in shingle under the White Cliff. He sat down there just where he
and Kitty had sat the night before, and the sun shone on him full and
clear. It was an exquisite May day, a soft turquoise sky and a calm
grey-blue sea, with just the gentlest swish of little waves along the
shingle; and the fishing fleet was out, the brown sails dark on the
horizon. Far out an old tramp steamer trailed a plume of black smoke, an
old blackguard of a boat contentedly smoking its pipe.

Tod sat there in the sun, and suffered this tragedy of a girl’s life to
turn the edge of his own bitterness. Dead Kitty seemed to plead with him
for that other woman, and Tod’s heart was softened towards his wife.

“I married her,” he said to himself, “and perhaps I have been to blame.
People are made differently and want different things. I’m responsible
for her in a way. It’s no use making a worse mess of it, and being mean;
we have got to try and make a fresh start and be patient with each
other.”

In some such mood as this he returned to “Carnarvon,” where the little
red may tree and the two laburnums were in bloom. There were buds on the
standard roses, and Ginger the sandy cat was asleep in the sun on the
drawing-room window-sill. Tod realised “Carnarvon” as he had never
realised it before; it was a little chocolate-box of a home, the house
of a woman whose ideals were all wrong.

“It’s got to go,” said Tod; “we’ll start differently next time.”

He found Millie in the drawing-room, sewing aimlessly at some
superfluous bit of finery. She gave him a momentary and sulky stare of
her blue eyes, and Tod saw that she was afraid.

“Sorry I have been away so long. I had a lot to do.”

She gave him a second glance, a look of surprise. She had been puzzled
by the unexpectedness of Tod, and she liked men to be obvious.

“Don’t apologise, please.”

He sat down, and sat down with an air of a man who was in no hurry, a
man who had made himself master of the situation. His bull-dog pipe came
out. He filled it, lit the tobacco, and puffed with obvious enjoyment.

“I want to talk to you, Millie.”

She bent her head over her work, and remained in that attitude during
all the time that he was speaking.

And Tod was gentle, extraordinarily gentle.

“We seem to have got adrift, somehow,” he told her, “and I want to make
a new beginning. I don’t think that we have understood each other, or
made allowances for each other. I want to get away from the money idea,
the idea that money and show matter more than anything else in the
world.”

She remained with bent head, saying nothing, astonished by his
gentleness, waiting to hear what he had to offer her in the way of life
and a home.

“We shall have to leave this house and sell most of the furniture, and
the business will have to go too. I have put everything in the hands of
Newton and Smythe. We’ll take rooms and live quietly and I’ll get a job
somewhere; I might even go back to the Craggs.”

Her silence betrayed nothing.

“We shall have to face it, Millie, and the only thing is to try and face
it like pals. We’ve drifted apart these last months, and all sorts of
things happen when people drift apart. I’m thinking how happy we were
three years ago.”

“Three years is a long time,” she said.

And she despised him for his gentleness. If he had thrashed her as he
had thrashed that Jew, he would have regained all his physical
ascendancy, and she would have been his woman. Spiritual fineness had no
meaning for Tod’s wife.

 Chapter Twenty-seven

On the day that Kitty was buried Tod went to see Mr. Rathbone Cragg. He
coerced his pride, and walked with an air of briskness in the direction
of West Shaston, but when he reached the familiar doorway in Caroline
Crescent, Tod’s pride was taken sick and caused him a moment’s anguish
and indecision on the doorstep.

He was staring at the glass swing doors and at the notices of sales
pasted on the walls of the vestibule, when a girl’s figure appeared, and
pushing through, came face to face with Tod.

“Why—it’s Tod!”

And then she corrected herself.

“It’s a long time since we met, Mr. Western.”

Tod lifted his hat and looked into the fresh eyes of his old playmate.
Bertha Cragg had aged a little, not in any physical sense, for her
pleasantly plain face was still the face of a girl, but there was a
maturity about her, the purposefulness of a clever woman who had work to
do, work that she had learnt to love.

“It is a long time,” said Tod awkwardly. “Are you still in Shaston?”

“For a holiday. I work, you know; I’m helping to run a girls’ club in
London. Were you going in to see Father?”

“Yes,” said Tod, “I had thought of doing so.”

His eyes were the eyes of a man who was unhappy. Nor was he the Tod whom
she had known, the Tod of the sea and the tennis-court and the wild
tramps through Sussex gorse, but a troubled Tod, a man who was
shouldering his way through disillusionment, and looking for something
that he had lost.

She wanted to talk to him; she wanted him to tell her why he was
unhappy, but life had taught her the finer reticences of life, and to be
careful how she laid a meddling hand on other people’s pride.

“You’ll find Father in his room. I’ll take you in.”

It was a generous thought, a sensitive bit of intuition, a glimpsing of
Tod as a man who had fallen on evil times and who had come to ask some
sort of favour. She saved him the wait in the outer office, and all
those little paltry details that a proud man resents, and more
especially did she save him from falling into the supercilious hands of
her brother Fred. By her own judging “Fred had become impossible.” His
intellectual priggishness fitted him like a well-cut coat.

“Here is Mr. Western, Father. We just met on the doorstep. He wants to
see you.”

She smiled Tod into the inner office, closed the door on him, and walked
calmly past her brother, who was looking at her with the irritable eyes
of a man who had been too late to intervene.

Mr. Rathbone Cragg was embarrassed, but he was less embarrassed than
Tod.

“Sit down, Western. What can we do for you? Find you a house?”

“You can let mine if you like,” said Tod; “I’m getting out of it, and
selling the business.”

“Tired of it?”

“Partly that. I have private reasons for getting out of it.”

There was a pause. Mr. Cragg’s hands were fidgeting with his watch
chain, and Tod seemed to be interested in his own boots. Both men were
shy of each other, but Mr. Cragg was a tough old worldling and had
learnt not to mix sentiment with business.

“I was sorry when you left here, Western, but, of course, conditions are
different now. I should have liked you back in the office.”

Tod’s brightening eyes betrayed him to Mr. Cragg.

“I was very happy here,” said Tod, “and I was keen on houses, but I
suppose you have no room now?”

“None—none at all. Besides—I expect you are feeling too big for the
old terms, Western?”

“I couldn’t expect anything better.”

Mr. Cragg saw how it was with him. Old Cragg was a far better man than
his son, and he had mellowed of late years, but he was extraordinarily
cautious. Big Mr. Julian Burgetrode was still of considerable
importance.

“Why don’t you write to Mr. Burgetrode?” he asked Tod with sudden
directness.

Tod stared at him.

“What for?”

“Well, he was a little interested in you, Western.”

Tod’s eyes grew obstinate.

“I couldn’t do it; private reasons. I thought I would just come and see
if there was any chance.”

Tod got up, feeling himself mortified and superfluous.

“I don’t foresee anything at the moment, Western, but there is just a
chance that we may be opening a branch agency at Pentworth.”

“Pentworth! Why, I was born there,” said Tod.

“But it’s quite undecided,” Mr. Cragg went on, ignoring the sentimental
possibilities of Tod’s remark. “If anything materialises and there
happens to be an opening, I’ll write to you, Western.”

“Thank you,” said Tod, and made his escape, receiving a casual nod from
Fred Cragg as he passed through the outer office.

But old Rathbone Cragg was not suffered to go to bed before he had
reconsidered his attitude towards Tod. After dinner Bertha followed him
into the library, and sat on the edge of the table and smoked her
cigarette. Old Cragg was very fond of Bertha; also—he was a little
afraid of her. She was a young woman of surprise, a young woman with a
heart that was bigger and a head that was broader than the hearts and
heads of the Cragg family. She made her brother appear just a cocky,
conceited “swat” from school, and old Cragg loved her little finger
better than Fred’s whole body.

“What did Tod Western want, Father?”

Old Cragg tried to take refuge in the daily paper, but Bertha extracted
him from its paragraphs and made him pay attention. There were times
when she “managed” him, and the hard old boy chuckled in secret, and
rather liked it. He disapproved of that cigarette of hers; but then—it
was Bertha’s cigarette.

“Western? Oh, young Western? Business—that’s all.”

“I wondered—I had an idea—and I’m just wondering whether I was right.
He wanted to come back to you?”

Mr. Cragg gave a little heave in his chin, a heave that said, “Confound
the girl!”

“Of course, I could not take him,” he admitted; “Reeves is very
satisfactory, and there is no room.”

“So he _did_ want to come back! He’s very unhappy, Daddy, about
something.”

“I don’t wonder,” said her father; “they say he’s bankrupt. That fool of
a wife of his.”

Bertha’s memories winced.

“I’m going away to-morrow, Daddy, you know. It would rather please me if
you tried to take Tod back.”

Old Cragg showed a certain testiness.

“I don’t think it is your—your business—to interest yourself in
Western, my dear. After all——”

“After all, I used to be rather fond of him, Daddy. You see, I’m not
ashamed of it. He had the making of rather a fine man.”

“Well, he shouldn’t have married that little——”

“We all do things we shouldn’t do. What about that new office at
Pentworth?”

“My dear Bertha!”

“There”—and she went over and kissed him—“I’m not going to worry you.
We have always been such good friends, you and I, and you can be—rather
generous sometimes.”

Mr. Cragg disbelieved her. He was not generous, and never had been
generous, but he supposed he would have to reconsider his attitude
towards Tod.

“If Reeves goes to Pentworth I might take Western in.”

“I don’t think you would regret it,” she said.

“There’s Fred to be considered. I don’t think that Fred would approve.”

“Fred approves of nothing but himself, Daddy.”

Old Cragg chuckled. There were times when he disliked his son.

Tod, meanwhile, was in search of rooms. He had arranged to have his
furniture sold, and to leave “Carnarvon” at the end of the month, and in
his quest for rooms he appealed to Tom Lavender, sitting on one of the
cane-topped stools in Mr. Lavender’s little shop and talking to him as
to a friend who understood.

The big, blond lover of birds was the very man to lay a cooling hand on
Tod’s feverishness.

“What kind of rooms do you want?”

“Something simple and cheap, with a bit of garden to look at. We should
have to do our own cooking.”

Mr. Lavender smiled at Tod.

“I have a couple of rooms upstairs that I never use; they would want a
little extra furniture. If you care to look at them——”

Tod’s worried face lit up eagerly.

“It’s awfully good of you, Tom. I’d try not to be any trouble.”

“Come up and have a look.”

They were pleasant rooms at the top of Mr. Lavender’s old house, with
lilacs in bloom in the garden below, and all Mr. Lavender’s roses ready
to celebrate June. The carpets were worn and the furniture casual and
decrepit, but Tod saw the possibilities of such a home. The atmosphere
of the house was so calm and clean, for it held the soul of a man who
was happy.

“Your wife could use the kitchen. I’ll arrange that with Mrs. Jelf.”

“And what shall I pay you, Tom?” Tod asked.

“I don’t see that you need pay me anything; just your share of the coal
and gas.”

“That won’t do, old man,” said Tod.

“Well, I don’t know what to ask?” smiled the lover of birds.

“Let Mrs. Jelf settle it.”

Mrs. Jelf was Tom Lavender’s cook-housekeeper. So Mrs. Jelf was asked to
arbitrate, after Tod had explained to her she was not to be called upon
to do any extra work, and that if she disapproved of Millie using the
kitchen for a short time each day, he—Tod—would buy an oil-cooker.

“Beastly things,” said Mrs. Jelf. “I don’t mind a little company.”

For Mrs. Jelf happened to be a foolish old woman who thought Mr. Tom
Lavender one of the greatest men on earth. Everything he did was right,
and everything he said was the essence of wisdom, nor were Mrs. Jelf’s
prejudices wholly contemptible. “He’s always doing kind things,” she
said, “and I’ve never known him out of temper, never even cross over his
food.” Mrs. Jelf thought him a saint.

Tod walked out of Mr. Lavender’s shop a very much happier man. He had
had a long talk with Tom, and he felt as though he had been for one of
those long tramps through the green ways of Sussex or climbing the downs
with the sea wind in his face. Tom Lavender was a healer. He lived for
the love of living and the love of all living things, and not for money.

When Tod returned to the garage in Duke Street, he found a bumptious
little man very busy in his shirt-sleeves, standing by one of the taxis,
with his right hand all grease. It was Mr. Charlie Britt of the firm of
Pumpelly and Britt, who had been puddling in the car’s gear-box to
satisfy himself as to the state of the gears. Love was crouching at the
back of the car unscrewing the nuts holding the inspection plate of the
differential, so that Mr. Britt could take a look at the crown wheel.

“Morning, Western. Don’t mind me poking my nose into things, I suppose.”

“What’s the reason?” Tod asked.

“We may make an offer for your show. It’s for sale, isn’t it?”

“It is,” said Tod.

Mr. Britt wiped his hands on a lump of cotton waste.

“No pig in a poke for me, thank you. Those gears aren’t so bad, but your
man tells me one big-end wants seeing to.”

“She knocks awful,” said Love from behind the car.

Tod felt annoyed, and yet he realised the inevitableness of Mr. Britt’s
suspicions. Moreover, he was not pleased to think of old Pumpelly buying
up his business. The thing smacked of defeat and of failure, and Tod’s
teeth were on edge.

“There’s not much wrong with the cars. I’d tell you if there were.”

“Oh, no doubt,” said the little man, pushing rudely past Tod and joining
Love at the rear of the car.

Tod stood there listlessly, telling himself that it was sheer
foolishness for him to resent Charlie Britt’s casual scepticism. Britt
believed in disbelieving everybody and trusting to his own sharp wits;
and he had found that it paid. Especially was it necessary to believe
that most of the men who worked for you were liars.

“Yes, not so bad,” said the brisk voice from behind the car, “but those
back springs look a little bit wheezy. Rock her, will you?”

This was to Tod, but Tod did not move. Britt stood up and gave him a
quick look.

“Sulky,” he thought. “Well, we’ve smashed you, anyway.”

When Pumpelly’s partner had looked at the cars, he bustled round the
whole place with the air of a prospective proprietor. Tod went into the
office and sat down at his desk. Charles Britt was making him feel very
sore.

The little man hunted him out.

“You’ve let your chaps slack pretty badly, Western.”

“Have I?” said Tod, resenting the tone of patronage.

“Some of your petrol cans are only two-thirds full. A bit of private
trade been going on somewhere. And I should think half your tools must
be missing.”

“Well, you’re not buying the missing ones.”

Britt pulled out a pair of nail-scissors and cleaned his nails. He
believed in a smart exterior.

“There’s only one way to handle the English workingman, and that’s to
keep him under, Western, keep him afraid. Fear’s the only thing that
keeps ’em afraid. You’ve been too soft on your men.”

“Much obliged,” said Tod. “Have a cigarette.”

Britt polished the nails of his left hand by rubbing them on the back of
his right-hand fingers.

“Always calling themselves skilled men too, and their bally trade union
tosh! Why, I learnt more than most of ’em know and learnt it in two
months. Booze and no-brain-piece; that’s what’s wrong with most of them.
Good morning to you.”

“Good morning,” said Tod, realising in a dull sort of way why he had
been unable to compete with such an alert and cynical little devil.

At “Carnarvon” that evening Tod was able to tell Millie that he had
found rooms, and she received the news with the sulky bitterness that
had characterised her since his discovery of her intrigue with Maurice
Goldstein. She accepted all Tod’s plans for the future, apathetically
and without comment, as depressed as a cat that had been turned out into
the rain. A natural indolence made her surrender all the more easy. She
had no alternative to offer. She would sit at the closed window of her
fate till someone came and opened it.

“Tom Lavender’s such a sportsman, and they’re quite jolly rooms,” said
Tod, “a sitting-room and a bedroom, and the use of the kitchen.”

“I can’t cook,” said his wife.

Tod stared. He had thought that all women could cook.

“Well, you’ll soon learn, and I dare say Mrs. Jelf will help you to
begin with. We can live quite quietly there, while I look around. No
more building of top stories until we have made sure of the
foundations.”

His cheerfulness amazed her, even though she guessed that his
cheerfulness was artificial. To the sensual and richly coloured young
woman the picture that he painted of life in Shaston lodgings was
utterly dreary and banal. Making beds, emptying slops, messing in the
kitchen, going out with a basket to buy food, getting meals ready,
always meals with a hungry man waiting for them, and then the disgusting
business of washing up. It was plain that Tod expected her to do all
this and saw nothing strange in her doing it.

And then—her clothes. Where was she going to put them all? For her
wardrobe had been something of a Pandora’s box, and Tod was not supposed
to know the whole extent of her extravagance. And he would always be
messing about in her bedroom. Even at Horrox and Horrox’s she had had a
little room of her own.

“Beastly!” was her verdict.

But to Tod she said nothing. For the future she would sit like a cat,
waiting.

 Chapter Twenty-eight

Tod’s chief recollection of those first days in Tom Lavender’s house was
the badness of Millie’s cooking. The very execrableness of it was a
protest against the economies he had planned for her, and though Tod
tried to smile over some of her unsavoury messes, his interior refused
to retain one particular meal. Millie had had trouble with her
saucepans, or to be more correct she had not troubled about them at all,
and Tod was most gloriously sick.

“I told you I couldn’t cook,” she said stubbornly, when he reproached
her with the queer taste of that particularly poisonous and dirty hash,
“and that woman makes things as awkward as she can.”

“What, Mrs. Jelf? I thought——”

“She’s an old cat.”

“I’m sorry,” said Tod.

But Mrs. Jelf was equally emphatic in her opinion of Millie. Tom
Lavender hauled Tod into his little study, and with an air of disquieted
shyness, proceeded to make a suggestion.

“Mrs. Jelf wants to do your cooking. She says she can manage it better.”

“Very good of her,” said Tod; “the fact is—my wife——”

Tom Lavender nodded.

“She hasn’t got the hands for it. Supposing——”

“I’ll pay Mrs. Jelf so much a week, Tom. Besides—I was a bit doubtful
about interfering in your kitchen.”

“That’s all right, old chap. But that old lady is a born cook; she loves
it.”

So it was arranged that Mrs. Jelf should cook for the Westerns, and
Millie dispossessed herself of that piece of feminine responsibility.
But Tom Lavender could not tell Tod what Mrs. Jelf had said of his wife,
that she had called her “a dirty, lazy, stuck-up slut with silk
stockings and no conscience.”

The larger part of Tod’s life was running in smoother and more pleasant
fashion. He was back at Caroline Crescent, sitting in the familiar room
and working with enthusiasm, and continuing to ignore the impertinences
of Frederick Cragg. Father and son had had a skirmish over the return of
Tod; Fred’s opposition had roused the older man’s obstinacy and had made
Tod’s return more sure, yet Western did not suspect that a woman had
stretched out a hand to help him, and that Bertha had armed the father
against the son’s sneers. As to Western’s Garage, it had passed into the
power of Pumpelly and Britt, but Tod’s lawyers had salved a portion of
their client’s capital, and Tod found that he was the owner of about
three hundred pounds. He invested the money in New Zealand Government
stock. Moreover he was living in the same house as Tom Lavender. They
smoked their pipes together at night, sitting out under the laburnums
and lilacs, talking about life and books and birds. Tod was to go out
tramping with Tom on alternate Sundays; the other days he gave to
Millie.

Tod was trying very hard to understand his wife. The innate chivalry
that Nancy Western had given to her boy refused to let itself be seduced
into accepting a purely cynical and materialistic view of marriage. Tod
was affectionate, and he felt responsible, and he tried to put that
memory of Maurice Goldstein out of his head. There was always the
thought that he had been to blame, that he had let himself get absorbed
in the business, and that he had forgotten that his wife was a woman.

For Tod was cursed or blessed with ideals, and very sturdy ideals, and
poor Kitty’s death had opened a vein of deep tenderness in him. He
treated Millie as he would have treated a sick woman or a hurt child,
taking her out to occasional concerts, trying to persuade her to like
boyish adventures in the country, buying her chocolates once a week. He
had very happy memories of those Pentworth picnics, and how sweet those
simple country days had seemed to his father and mother. Nancy Western
was living in her son, shedding a soft radiance over him, inspiring him
to try and reach that wife of his to use the ways of a freer youth, but
Tod’s wife did not wish for wings, the innocent, unsmirched wings he
sought to give her.

She seemed apathetic, casual, bored.

She had not forgiven him for his failure to make money. She would never
forgive him for having blundered in upon her affair with Maurice
Goldstein, and Tod did not realise the elemental antipathy the incident
had roused in her. Tod had ceased to be sexually attractive to his wife.
She resented this stuffy life in a couple of rooms; she resented Tod’s
kindness. He was trying to fasten his ideals upon her and they galled
her like a girdle of thorns. Tod’s wife was no saint.

Admiration, colour, thrills, movement, pretty frocks! How could a young
man working in an estate office give her these? Tod was dull; and so
disgustingly kind and good tempered when she was ripe and almost
yearning for a row. She was a fierce young animal in her sex cravings,
and there was no fierceness about Tod. He kissed her as though he were
kissing his mother.

“It’s beastly!” was her favourite expression, uttered in the monotony of
her long silence.

And so it was, beastly for both of them, though a second blindness had
fallen upon Tod. His thoughts were set upon being kind to his wife, and
she detested him for it, and longed to use her claws. His good temper
threw her back upon a negative sulkiness, and smothered her when her
inclination was to scream.

A man who is trying to save a drowning girl is conscious of but few
things: the nearness of the shore, the sea, her struggles, her wet hair,
his efforts to calm her, perhaps the deadly weakening of his own
strength. Watchers on the cliff above have a more complete and a more
pregnant view of the struggle, and so it was in Tod’s blind effort to
pull his marriage out of the sea. Even casual spectators saw more than
he did.

Mrs. Jelf saw as much as anybody. She had her glimpses of Millie, the
Millie who put on her clothes and went out, “Just to show off,” as Mrs.
Jelf expressed it, “while her young man’s working.” And such clothes
too! “Pink stockings and white shoes, and an ’at for every day in the
week.” She knew, too, that Tod’s wife scuffled home in time to change
these clothes and lay the table for her husband. When Millie was out
Mrs. Jelf would make a tour of inspection, and her woman’s eyes saw all
the slackness of Tod’s home, the frowsy bed left unaired and unmade, the
slops not cleared, dirt everywhere.

“Dear me, what a fine lady we are!” said Lavender’s housekeeper. “Too
fine to be the poor boy’s wife.”

The ubiquitous Bob Twine saw Tod’s wife as the deckchair girl, sunning
herself on the parade and collecting the homage of casual males. Twine
wondered whether Tod knew of it. One man could not tell another man that
his wife was little better than a woman of pleasure, but he did attack
Millie one Wednesday afternoon, planting himself on the chair next to
her.

“Same old game, madam!”

“You shut up,” said the girl.

“Suppose Tod knows? I guess not! Why don’t you play the game by the kid?
He’s the best little sportsman you’ll ever meet.”

“He’s too good,” said the girl viciously; “he’s so good that he’s dull.
It’s no use you snarling at me, Bob; I never ought to have married him.”

Twine softened to her at this flash of sincerity.

“True enough. But, hang it all, why not have a shot at making him happy?
I never told him, you know.”

“You can tell him—if you like. I’m so sick of it, I don’t care.”

“Good lord!” said Twine, “is it as bad as that! I tried to tell him
once, and couldn’t. I’m not going to now.”

“Well, what are you fussing about? If you are afraid to tell him you had
better leave me alone.”

“I will,” said Twine. “Good day,” and he got up and left her.

Tod was making desperate struggles to get beauty and a sense of goodness
of things back into his life. He felt that he wanted to remain a boy,
the boy he had remembered at Pentworth, for Tod knew that he had changed
and that the soul of him had grown worried and wrinkled. Also there was
less of the smile of the shrewd and happy boxer. There were times when
Tod had a scowl, and he knew it, and there were times when he lost his
temper. Little things irritated him most absurdly. He could remember
smashing the little green-topped card-table that never would stand
squarely on four legs.

Millie had looked up at him quite hopefully.

“Oh, temper!”

“Sorry,” said Tod, contrite and flabby.

He had spent the rest of the evening trying to mend that table and even
Millie could have told him that some things are better broken, unhappy
marriages as well as rickety tables.

He talked to old Lavender on one of the Sunday rambles, but he was loyal
and said nothing about his wife.

“Do you remember a time, Tom, when it began to hurt you to live?”

Lavender lied.

“I remember it quite well,” he said.

Tod smiled.

“You have a queer, sore feeling inside you, and the things outside you
seem to take pleasure in going wrong. You are always knocking your
soul’s shins against something.”

“You’ll get over it,” said Lavender gravely, “you are bound to get over
it. Don’t fret. Work, work like the devil, and set your teeth when the
storm comes.”

“What storm?” Tod asked.

But Lavender hid himself in symbolical excuses. He knew that Tod’s
marriage was all wrong, and that the woman was hurting the soul of him.
And Lavender prayed that she would go off with some other man, some fine
Assyrian bull, and leave Tod holding the hand of his own youth.

Early in July Tod had a second vivid glimpse into the heart of his wife.
There was a new little bungalow town squatting beyond West Shaston like
a flock of geese on a village green, and to get to it you walked along
the sea wall with the shingle on one side of you and dyked meadows on
the other. Tod had the keys of two of the new bungalows, and he was to
make a list of the fixtures.

The blue sweep of the sky, the breadth of the sea and the meadowland,
and the curve of the white foam along the shingle, brought back a memory
to Tod’s heart. He could remember walking with his father in some such
place on a breezy day in the spring of the year, and his father had gone
along waving his hat and shouting the words of some song. Tod remembered
how they had thrown stones at an old tin can that his father had set
afloat, and Tod had sunk it. And suddenly the spirit of fatherhood
entered Tod. He wanted a boy, a boy of his own, something to play with,
something to work for, something that mattered.

A girl in a white dress and a cerise-coloured jersey was sitting on the
beach, reading. She looked up as Tod came along the seawall, and Tod
recognized his wife. Surely this was a God-given coincidence, the secret
key for the opening of a woman’s heart, the prattler’s gate leading into
the great playground!

Tod sprang down, forgetting all about the bungalows. His wife looked
with quick eyes at his suddenly radiant face. She had been expecting a
man, another man, not Tod.

“I say, dear, I’m coming to sit down for ten minutes. Isn’t it gorgeous
out here, so clean and big.”

“It’s quite nice,” said the wife, wondering why he had turned up, and
whether he suspected.

“Bungalow Town can wait for half an hour.”

“Oh, you are going there?”

“Making lists of fixtures. Oh, hang business! Millie—I want something;
it has just come to me; perhaps you have been wanting it too.”

He leant over towards her with bright eyes.

“What is it?” she asked him.

“Millie, I want a child, a kid, just what I may have been to my dad.
Perhaps all this time—you have been wanting one—too.”

Her face hardened. What a fool he was!

“Rot!” she said. “What are you getting sentimental about children for?”

“It’s a sort of hunger,” he began.

“We can’t afford that sort of hunger.” She sneered at him: “Fancy having
a kid and living in two rooms. We haven’t got the money. Be sensible.”

“But other people have children, with less money than we have.”

“Well, I’m not going to be one of them. Very nice of you, I dare say,
but who has all the pain and all the work, and the economising? I’m not
having babies on eighty pounds a year, thank you.”

Tod left her and went on his way, and all the joy had gone out of the
morning. Money, always money, and a woman who had to be bribed!

 Chapter Twenty-nine

A week later Tod met Mr. Burgetrode.

He was standing with his hands in his pockets on the edge of a little
crowd of holiday folk who were watching a Punch and Judy show on the
beach below the parade. It was a Saturday afternoon, and Tod was alone,
so much alone that he envied that busy old Blackguard of a doll who
appeared to spend his life hammering other dolls with a stick. Mrs. Judy
had just been dealt with, and tossed into oblivion on the end of the
aforesaid stick. The crowd laughed, but Tod was frowning, frowning over
the problem that was eating into his vitals, his thoughts arrested by
the broad and human cynicism of the puppet show. The moral of the thing
was as old as time. This hook-nosed, hunchbacked scoundrel had studied
the philosophy of the big stick. The only doll who scored off him was
the clown, the merry fool who was never serious.

Mr. Burgetrode saw Tod before Tod saw Mr. Burgetrode, and Mr. Burgetrode
watched Tod while Tod was watching the puppet show. He had a complete
view of the young man’s “thinking” face, of his faded tie and clean
collar frayed slightly in the wash. Tod’s blue eyes were sullen and
unhappy; he looked hungry, and the food that he lacked was not bread and
meat.

Mr. Burgetrode edged his way to Tod’s side.

“Curious, isn’t it,” he said as though he had been talking to Tod for
hours, “that the only really successful person is the clown?”

Tod’s chin gave a jerk. He looked into “Old Mischief’s” lean brown face,
and then looked away again.

“It’s a puzzle,” he confessed; “what is success?”

“Ah, there you have me,” said Mr. Burgetrode; “I have been looking for
it all my life. People say I have found it. Have I? Now, a merely happy,
unserious animal like that clown——!”

They stood side by side till the show was over, very conscious of each
other, yet part of the crowd.

“Suppose we go and see the place where I sprained my ankle,” said the
big man with a sort of fantastic irrelevancy.

Tod turned with him.

“I’m back in Craggs’ office, you know,” he said with a sensitive boy’s
abruptness.

“I—did—know. I’m boxing you to-day, Tod, and I’m hitting hard. What
about these wasted years?”

“Is anything wasted?” said Tod.

“Never.”

“I’ve learnt things. I couldn’t have learnt them in any other way. But
it’s when you get tied up in them like a rabbit in a ferreting net.”

“The great thing is to try and avoid being a rabbit. It’s better to be
the ferret, better still to be the man who owns and sets the nets.”

“But why nets, sir?”

“You used the word yourself, young man. There always will be nets. Your
net may cover a rabbit hole or half a continent, but it’s there.”

“Seems rather hopeless,” said Tod.

“Not a bit of it. Think of that clown. If your birdcage is the whole
world, and the stars are just gold blobs on it, isn’t it big enough? The
moral is, don’t be a canary.”

“Canaries may be happy,” said Tod.

“Oh, happiness!” quoth Mr. Burgetrode with scorn. “What happiness is
there for man but doing things and trying to do them well? Does Nature
go round asking the canary if it is happy, or request the Hottentot to
inform her if the climate is just what he desires? Nature doesn’t care a
hang. She’s got bigger things to do. If we don’t please her she scraps
us.”

“That seems rather hopeless too,” said Tod.

“Nonsense. Nature is always doing things. She sets the nets for us; she
wants us to learn. Think about it, and you’ll see it must be so. You
talk about Nature being cruel. She’s not; she’s big; she can’t help
treading on people sometimes. You have trodden on lots of ants and
worms, my boy, and never known about it. As for calculating cruelty, men
beat her at it every time. Nature’s always at work, improving things,
trying a new world or a new sort of beast. I suppose she’s been trying
man, and he’s not done so badly. Who spun the fable about the Garden of
Eden, a man having to go out and work? Adam must have been a dull dog
till God set him digging. And there is a sort of modern fool who thinks
it would be rather fine and Utopian to get back to the workless, loafing
Adam. Nature will tread on such people; snuff ’em out, that’s all.”

They swung along the parade together, Tod having to step out to keep
pace with “the Tiger’s” long, agile legs. There was something
invigorating about walking with Mr. Burgetrode. He made you think of an
east wind blowing on a cold sunny morning. No sitting under a banyan
tree, no Oriental quietism for Mr. Burgetrode.

“We’re northerners,” he told Tod, “we’ve got to work or die. Nature’s
way, my man; she has shoved us up against necessity, just a whim
perhaps, as an experiment. She may think that the future is with the
Patagonians, and we’re one of her amusing side-shows, her after-dinner
jest. Who knows? Anyway, we are here, and there is plenty of good work
to do.”

“Some things might be done better,” said Tod.

“Well, do them better; don’t talk about it.”

“If it wasn’t such a dirty scramble for money——”

“Get money, and then you won’t abuse it; not too much money, mind you.”

“That’s all very well,” said Tod, “but it seems to me that if you’re a
sportsman, the mean people have you beaten every time.”

“Not always. And if it is so, it’s your own fault, the fault of Tod
Western, citizen, and every other Tod Western in the world. If all the
Tod Westerns got together and proceeded to sit on the heads of the
Semites; if every decent Englishman, instead of praying or grumbling,
daily kicked the rump of some commercial tout—well, things might
happen. The whole trouble began when we mixed up work and money. Work’s
the great necessity, the blood tax we have to pay to Nature. It’s no use
offering her a five-pound note.”

“What about the rich people?”

“They buy other people’s work, some of ’em. But don’t imagine, young
man, that because a man is rich he is a slacker. He may be one of
Nature’s vitalisers, and she pays him well, either with the joy of work
he does or the power she gives him. Do you think Nature cares twopence
about a goods-porter, so long as he is nothing but a goods-porter? She
treads on such people as you tread on an ant. Let them strike for ever
and ever. Nature says, ‘Work—you slave.’ There’s nothing of the
ca’-canny about Nature. She wants energy, movement, change, not a pair
of sulky hob-nail boots and a mouth that blabs the stuff out of the
halfpenny papers.”

“Then we are slaves?” said Tod.

“Just so long as we feel like it. Damn it, boy, the wide world is not a
bad playground, and even your plain sailor man can see a good deal of
it. You can’t get off the earth, you know, but Nature has given the best
of us a pretty long rope.”

“I’d like to see all men big,” said Tod.

“They have got to learn to be big first. You are never big without work.
Shove that in the mouths of the trade union bosses. I’ve worked sixteen
hours a day in my time.”

“But then you had brains,” said Tod.

Mr. Burgetrode laughed.

“We are getting personal, are we? And here are our old friends playing
golf just as usual, though the European situation is what the
journalists call threatening. Let’s sit down under this thorn bush.”

They sat down, Tod looking morose and puzzled.

“Life’s queer,” he said slowly. “One seems to get shoved into things
without any plan. Oh—I’m sick, sick of the whole business!”

Mr. Burgetrode lay flat, his hands under his head, his hat flung aside
on the grass. Tod saw that his eyes were closed, and that his face had
the flicker of a smile hidden somewhere.

“Why did you throw up the motor business?”

Tod frowned. It was news to him that Mr. Burgetrode knew anything about
Western’s Garage.

“I had to,” said Tod; “self-respect. I wanted to pay people before I
smashed.”

“Didn’t it pay?”

“It paid, but it didn’t pay enough. You see——”

“Extravagance,” said the great man tersely.

And Tod could say nothing. He began to suspect that “the Tiger” knew
everything that was to be known.

“I dare say it was my fault,” he confessed.

“One of the oldest faults on earth, Tod. I warned you. But you had to
fight your first fight, and get blooded—and beaten. Nature again. One
of her ways of eliminating weaklings. Well, what about it, now?”

“About what?” said Tod.

“Ambition, the man’s game, or office life in a sleepy, sea coast town.”

Tod’s eyes flashed. He sat with his chin in his fists, thinking.

“You mean that what you said to me that night in the hotel, about my
training for some big job?”

“Exactly,” said Mr. Burgetrode, sitting up and watching Tod’s eyes and
mouth.

Tod said nothing for nearly a minute.

“It was a mistake,” he said gruffly, digging one heel into the grass.

“Where was the mistake?”

“My marrying. I have never said so before to any living soul, but it’s
the truth. I’ve known it for months. She killed the last bit of love in
me last week.”

“How?” asked the big one softly.

“I—I wanted a kid,” said Tod. “I’ve been so lonely, and she wouldn’t do
it. I know it means pain and work for a woman, but, after all, what’s
marriage for?”

Mr. Burgetrode smiled.

“I’ve often wanted a kid, Tod,” he said, “but the want didn’t come to me
till I was old. I’d been too busy doing things. Supposing—you think it
over.”

“What about my wife?” Tod asked.

“Will she complain if your income doubles?”

“I don’t think so,” said Tod.

They walked back to Shaston, and Tod had tea at the familiar old Hotel
Majestic. He sat on the balcony of Mr. Burgetrode’s sitting-room, saying
little, but looking over the sea with eyes that had sighted the sails of
a strange ship rising above the horizon.

“I’ve got him,” said Mr. Burgetrode to himself; “I thought I should get
him some day.”

When Tod went back to his rooms at Tom Lavender’s, he found Millie
sulking over a book. He had promised to take her on the pier, and he had
forgotten all about it.

“Where’ve you been?” she asked suspiciously.

“Walking with a friend?”

“A man, I suppose?”

“Yes, a man I used to know years ago; met him by chance.”

He did not tell her that it was Mr. Burgetrode, and his wife thought
that he had been philandering with a girl.

Tod stayed up late that night talking to Tom Lavender. He wanted the
cool hand of Tom’s wisdom to touch him after that strenuous bout with
Mr. Burgetrode, and he developed to Tom all the vigour of the great
man’s creed of work and the joy of work. The lover of birds had no
selfish net to spread for Tod, though he was sadder than Tod knew at the
thought of losing him.

“It’s a great opportunity,” he said, “and opportunity’s a horse so few
men ever get a lift on. I’d take it, old chap!”

Tod lunched next day with Mr. Burgetrode.

 * * * * *

Yet this plan of the great man was overset by circumstances that were
stronger than Mr. Burgetrode, and he—like Tod—was to be but a fierce
striving figure in the huge fresco of a world upheaval, a figure out of
the brain of Michael Angelo the great. Julian Burgetrode met the
inevitable, and smiled at it with some of the pride of a father. The man
he had chosen could but prove himself a man.

In the sunlight of an August morning these two met on the white steps of
the hotel; Tod with a deep light in his eyes and the tense fire of youth
touched with a new heroism; Mr. Burgetrode with the day’s paper in his
hand, and a kind of grimness about his mouth.

He looked at Tod, and he knew at once what Tod had come to say.

“Well, young Ajax?”

“I must go,” said Tod; “I wanted to come and tell you, sir. It’s right,
isn’t it, even if I have to throw up everything? I am going round to the
drill hall to sign on.”

Mr. Burgetrode held out a hand to Tod.

“Ajax couldn’t do anything else, boy. You’re right, utterly right. But
when you come back, I shall want you to come to me.”

“I’ll come,” said Tod, much moved; “but there’s just one thing.”

“Out with it, boy. What is worrying you?”

“My wife,” said Tod. “I have some money left, but it’s capital; and army
pay won’t keep a wife. If I handed you the money, sir?”

Mr. Burgetrode’s eyes had lost all their fierceness.

“I’ll see to your wife, Tod; she shan’t suffer.”

“God bless you, sir,” said Tod; “I don’t suppose I shall have much
trouble with the doctor.”

“But the Germans will with you,” said Mr. Burgetrode to himself. “Ave
triumphans, little Great Heart—and may thousands of the same breed go
with you. Why didn’t this happen when I was young!”


 PART TWO

 Chapter Thirty

The village of Myonville, like many of the old Picardy villages in what
had once been Ponthieu, hid itself in trees. You rode between the flat
fields and the orchards, as young Seymour, the billeting officer, rode
that May morning, and saw ahead of you a mass of towering elms and
beeches, with a bit of white plastered wall showing here and there like
a patch of light sky at the end of a wood. Even the church steeple was
invisible, when the leaves were out, and the roads leading into the
village were great green tunnels.

“It’s good,” said young Seymour to himself, “It’s damned good.”

And so it was.

For Myonville was a virgin village; troops had rarely been quartered
there during the three and a half years of the war. It had had its
sorrows, but they, like the village, were secret and beautiful, part of
the low, white-walled and red-roofed houses; part of its orchards and
bits of garden and great pensive trees. And the spring was in Myonville,
an indescribable greenness, a glimpse of lush blue-grass under the pink
and white of the apple trees, perhaps a clump of daffodils—goblin gold
under the blue sky.

Young Seymour found the secretary of Monsieur le Maire. He was the
schoolmaster, big, stately, with the baggy black clothes of a French
civilian, shabby, but rather proud.

“Billets? Yes. For a battalion, Monsieur le Capitaine?”

“Eight officers and seventy-three men and three horses,” said Seymour.

The schoolmaster raised his eyebrows.

“It is very small.”

“Well, you see,” said Seymour, “during the retreat the Boches were so
loving that there wasn’t much left of us. Now, where am I to put the
colonel?”

The placing of the colonel seemed to be the most sacred act in Seymour’s
ritual.

“A big house, perhaps, Monsieur le Capitaine?”

“The colonel does not love big houses. A little house—with a garden.”

“There is the Café de la Paix.”

“Noisy?”

“Very quiet, monsieur.”

“Any children? The colonel loves children.”

“There are plenty of children in Myonville,” said the schoolmaster with
an amused twinkle.

So young Seymour went off with his list, and called first at the Café de
la Paix. The choosing of the colonel’s billet appeared to be a very
serious business, but as a matter of fact, it was a labour of love.

“Madame Lorellard? Bonjour, madame. I want to place Monsieur le Colonel
in your house, with your good permission.”

Seymour was a pink and white boy, and very polite.

“Josephine,” called the old lady, “ici,” and she smiled like a red apple
upon Seymour.

Josephine, the black, handsome Josephine, Josephine the talkative, the
temperamental, came gliding out of the inner room.

“My daughter, monsieur. She manages everything.”

Seymour paid the colonel’s court to Josephine. She was put out, annoyed,
haughty, yet polite. Strange officers were a nuisance. Besides, André,
her brother, was expected home on leave, and she wanted the room for
him.

“It is a very small room, monsieur.”

“The colonel loves a small room mademoiselle. May I see it?”

Josephine took him upstairs, and was yet more annoyed when Seymour
looked at the sheets.

“Monsieur, you insult me!”

“Oh, mademoiselle, I do not; I was admiring the linen.”

Someone had shown some sense in selecting Seymour as billeting officer.

He chalked the outer door and went about the rest of his business.
Josephine was walking up and down the kitchen, talking in her excitable
way to her mother and Cousin Claire. Claire taught in the school. She
had been a solemn child, addicted to wild bouts of elfin mischief, a
child with brown gold hair and great grey eyes.

“A colonel! Oh, la, la, he will be old and fussy, and he will want hot
water at all hours, and baths, and he will stamp about the clean floors
and scold. I know all about English colonels. Gabrielle had one at
Doullens, cross and red as a lobster.”

“He can’t eat us,” said Claire, thrusting her needle into one of
madame’s stockings she was darning.

“No, but he will be cross, and he will be rude, and he will always be
calling for red wine.”

“Josephine, the stew is boiling over,” said Madame Lorellard.

Josephine dealt with the stew, and Claire’s eyes had a little
mischievous glimmer of light in them.

“What a monster you expect! But then, if he is old he will not be a
nuisance in other ways.”

“Tiens!” said the black girl. “They are worse than the young ones
sometimes. Old pigs!”

About three o’clock that afternoon Claire was washing lace in the little
backyard and splashing soapsuds from her tub on to the leaves of the old
pear tree trained against the wall. Madame had gone out into the fields
to help old Lorellard with his hoeing; Josephine was in the garden
picking herbs. Claire heard someone open the door of the café and walk
in, and then an English voice called: “Madame!”

Claire went through the kitchen with a ball of wet lace in her two
hands. The glass door leading into the café room was open. She saw an
officer standing there.

“Monsieur?”

He turned, smiled and saluted. Claire was strangely conscious of his
eyes; they were blue eyes, rather sad, rather tired, but brave, very
brave and so honest.

“I am billeted here, mam’selle.”

He spoke quite good French. She looked at his sleeve and Tod Western
looked at her arms. Somehow he saw something very beautiful and peaceful
and good in those plump arms of hers, the two strong little hands
holding the ball of wet lace.

“But monsieur is so young!”

And Tod laughed.

“Mam’selle, did you expect a grandfather?”

“But we thought—and the young officer was so careful.”

Tod was still smiling, and she understood of a sudden why the tired but
smiling eyes had brought an atmosphere into the house. As a woman she
was quick to feel, though very slow to like. She noticed that he was
wearing two ribbons, and that his left arm looked stiff.

“Monsieur would like to see his room?”

She was pressing the ball of lace between her two hands; a drop of water
fell on the floor.

“If you please,” said Tod, as though he were listening to some soft
voice speaking inside him.

She put down the lace and dried her hands.

“It is a very small room, monsieur.”

Tod was absorbing all the pleasant homeliness of the place, the table
scrubbed white as hollywood, the red-tiled floor, the black stove
standing out into the room with the inevitable coffee-pot warming on it,
the bright pots and jars, the plain wooden chairs. It was simple,
austere, exquisitely clean. The doorway opening on to the yard showed
Tod a garden and a great green orchard beyond it, and in the orchard a
blackbird was singing.

His silence made Claire glance around at him as she dried her hands. He
was looking out into the orchard and smiling, and his blue eyes were
full of light. And again she understood; this was a man who loved simple
things, children, dogs, a green tree in leaf, the singing of a bird, a
field of corn rippling in the wind, red apples on a bough, a warm place
by the fire.

“I beg your pardon, mam’selle,” said Tod abruptly, “but all this is so
very good.”

She crossed the kitchen and raised the latch of the door that opened on
the stairs.

“Monsieur has suffered,” she said.

She ran up the steep stairs, a sturdy, graceful little figure, full
throated, her old shoes making a merry clatter. Tod followed her,
noticing the cleanness of that carpetless house, a row of shoes and
sabots lined up on a shelf, a couple of mauve linen bonnets hanging on a
nail, a table with clean linen laid out on it on the landing above. He
had a glimpse of a room, a woman’s room with clothes hanging under a
curtain and a red _duvet_ on the bed. Claire had opened a door, and she
went before him into the room.

“It is a comfortable bed, monsieur, and the room is quiet, for, as you
see, the window looks out on the orchard.”

She was quite natural and unembarrassed, without a trace of coquettish
false modesty.

“It is a very nice room,” said Tod.

“Is there anything that monsieur desires?”

“A table—a table to write on, if it will cause you no trouble. My
servant will carry it in.”

“We will find you a table, and it is no trouble.”

She left him and met Tod’s servant in the kitchen, a shy, rosy-faced boy
playing at Atlas with the colonel’s brown valise on his back. She
pointed him up the stairs, picked up the lace, and went back to her
washing. She could hear the voices of the two men in the room above, and
a sound of devoted activity on the part of the boy with the rosy face.

“You’ll have some hot water, sir?”

“If you can get it, Cane. Don’t worry the people.”

“I’ve got out a clean pair of pyjamas, sir. And your slacks.”

“They have promised me another table, Cane. I shall want it to-night.”

“It will be here, sir,” said the boy.

He spoke the truth, for Tod was a man who was loved with a very great
love by all men who had faced death with him.

There were other voices now in the kitchen. Josephine had returned, and
so had the old people, for Père Lorellard had hoed all the weeds.

“He is quite young,” said Claire calmly, drying her hands on a towel.

“Oh, la la, the young ones can be a nuisance.”

Her cousin said nothing.

And then Tod descended upon them, a quiet man, very much at his ease, a
man without guile.

He bowed to _grand’mère_.

“It is very good of you to take me in, madame. I shall give you no
trouble.”

He bowed to Josephine, but a little more stiffly.

“My room is charming.”

That mercurial and handsome young woman became instantly the coquette.
Would Monsieur le Colonel take a cup of coffee or a glass of red wine?
Yes, the war, the terrible war! How much longer was it going to last,
and would the Germans ever come to Myonville? Claire was busy hanging
her lace on a little line near the stove. She appeared to be absorbed in
what she was doing, and she took no part in the conversation.

Tod sat down and drank a cup of coffee, and talked to Josephine, whose
elbows were on the table. She was leaning forward, her handsome and
expressive face upturned, her black eyes looking straight into Tod’s
blue ones. She liked homage, did Josephine; she liked to have a man
_impressione_, interested in her and in her alone.

Tod gave it as his opinion that the Germans would never come to
Myonville, and that they would go on fighting till the Germans were
beaten. Josephine became voluble and eloquent on what France had
suffered, how her brother André had been wounded three times, and how it
was impossible to buy new clothes. Claire went on hanging up her lace,
and saying nothing.

Tod looked at Josephine because he was a gentleman, but he looked now
and again at Claire because he wanted to. A ray of sunlight was stealing
through the window, and it fell upon her brown gold hair and white neck
and plain black dress. She was a little woman, _la petite_ Claire, but
Tod had a feeling that there was nothing little about her. He liked her
calmness, her quietude, those enigmatically frank grey eyes of hers, and
the way she went on doing things.

“The aeroplanes, we hear them each night now,” said the dark girl, “and
the bombs! Oh, la la! And Abbeville is being destroyed. I cannot sleep.
I walk up and down, or run into the fields. It is so much safer in the
fields.”

Claire spoke for the first time.

“If I am to be killed at night, I will be killed in my bed,” she said;
“it will just happen so.”

Tod looked at her.

“You will not be killed,” he said, “but you are very wise.”

Tod had work to do, a fatherly care for this battered remnant of his
battalion, part of the battered remnant of a division. No one knew what
they were doing in Myonville; Tod did not know, nor did the red men at
D.H.Q. They had been pushed about the country, thrust into any odd
corner, left on the rubbish heap so to speak, while over yonder men
fought with their backs to the sea. Rumour had it that they were to
train Americans; but neither the war, nor the Americans, nor the great
spring offensive concern this tale—this green season in the village of
Myonville in Ponthieu by the sea.

Tod spent an hour in the orderly-room, went round the men’s billets,
inspected the kitchen, and was taken by Seymour to the officer’s mess.

“Is your billet all right, sir?”

“Just what I wanted, Jack. By Jove, aren’t those beech trees fine?”

“Gorgeous!” said the boy.

Tod wandered out after dinner into the lanes of Myonville. He had two
letters in his pocket, but he did not want to read them yet. For Tod was
sad, sad with the brave sadness of a strong man who had seen defeat, and
whose heart was full of the thought of the men—his men—who were dead.
He was sick of the war, soul-sick and very weary; there seemed no end to
the bloody business, no end to the hopes that were dashed.

“We’ve got to beat them,” was all that Tod would ever say; “we’ll go on
till we do it.”

He was not a man of rhetoric, a man of phrases or of elaborate theories,
one of your clever little people who sit in chairs in London and spill
ink in wrangling over things they do not understand. Tod had no use for
the war news of the journalists.

“Just tosh for the old women.”

He left it at that.

But these beech trees and orchards—these hills and little old houses
and these rich fields! How good they were! Such gentle lights on the
green grass, such mystery in the dim alleys of the orchards, such a
smell of growth, such human tenderness! Tod’s heart had been made bigger
by the war. The change in him could be summed up in those few words;
while over in England! What had those damned “leaves” of his given him?
Nothing, nothing at all.

It was growing dark when he returned to the Rue de l’Eglise and the Café
de la Paix. Peasants were sitting at their doors, and Tod found the
Lorellards grouped in the doorway of the café. It was a plain old brick
house standing on the street, next door to the schoolhouse where Claire
Vignard taught her children.

The whole family rose.

“Please don’t get up,” said Tod.

He was introduced to Père Lorellard, a ruddy old man who kept an
everlasting chuckle under his waistcoat, and whose blue eyes were full
of mischief.

Tod sat down between Josephine and Claire. There was no embarrassment,
no English awkwardness about these people. They accepted Tod, treated
him with the exquisite and intelligent politeness of the French peasant,
found their chairs just as comfortable although he had joined them. And
Tod felt happily at home, the simplest of men chatting to two girls and
two old people.

They praised his French.

“I’m half a Frenchman,” he laughed, passing his cigarette-case to old
Lorellard.

Josephine was the ready coquette, making her dark eyes mysterious and
mischievous in the dark.

“Monsieur will stay in France after the war, perhaps?”

“I’m married,” said Tod, glancing instinctively at Claire.

The fair girl did not look at him, but she had noticed a quick change in
his voice.

“And how many children have you?” madame wanted to know.

“None,” said Tod, like a man rebutting an accusation.

“Oh, mon Dieu!”—this was Josephine—“but the lines on my hand say that
I am to have seven. Seven children, monsieur! Oh, la la!”

Little Claire broke in in that queer, abrupt way of hers.

“Why work if you are to have no children? What is the good of it—just
for yourself?”

Her eyes met Tod’s in the dark, but she turned her head away almost
immediately, and a silence fell upon Tod. A vague sadness seemed to
steal in at the open doorway; and Tod rose and said “Good night.”

“I have letters,” he said.

Josephine lit him a candle, and waited for him to smile at her, but Tod
did not smile.

“He is thinking of his home,” she said, when Tod had gone upstairs. “He
is not happy.”

But Claire had a feeling that Tod was not happy because of his home.

The colonel was sitting at his table, and he had pulled those two
letters out of his pocket. One was from Mr. Burgetrode; the other from
his wife. He read Mr. Burgetrode’s who had grown even bigger with the
war, a creative giant labouring to beat the Boche. It was a long letter,
an intimate letter, almost fatherly in some of its touches. Tod spent
half of each leave with Mr. Julian Burgetrode; they had grown fond of
each other for there was the same bigness in them both.

His wife’s letter brought a touch of grimness to Tod’s face. He was
happy with his men, but miserable with those other memories. Millie had
a little flat in town, and even a lieutenant-colonel’s pay was
inadequate to explain her palpable opulence. Those “leaves” of his had
worried Tod, sent him back to France with intolerance and suspicion in
his heart. He suspected her of slyness, and of more than slyness.

“Have her watched,” some very merciless Stoic had said to him.

“I can’t,” Tod answered. “How can I? I’m up against death out here. That
changes a man. Set some sneak to spy on her? Damn it—I can’t do it! I
couldn’t do it—somehow—even if I felt sure——”

A woman would have said that Tod was right.

 Chapter Thirty-one

The Lorellards owned a little shop, the café, and a farm. The shop was
next to the café, and you entered it by a separate glass-panelled door.
Tod had to pass through the public room every time he left or entered
his billet; it was a biggish room with a queer little bar, half a dozen
clean white tables, a set of shelves almost barren of bottles, a dozen
chairs, and a bracket clock. This room was nearly always empty.
Sometimes Tod saw two or three old Frenchmen sitting at one of the
tables; his own men never came there, for the Lorellards had no need to
be very enthusiastic about the café. The shop was a more serious
proposition, but the child of their hearts was the farm.

It lay beyond the orchard and a row of tall beeches, and Tod discovered
it in company with Madame Lorellard. There were two little meadows
surrounded with willows, a broad strip of arable land green with young
wheat, a small wood of poplars, and a little field beyond the wood where
old Lorellard grew potatoes and Indian corn. The barn and his stables
were near the house.

Old Lorellard was always working. The back of his neck was all seamed
with wrinkles, and the colour of leather. His hands were knotty and
strong. In his old blue breeches and striped shirt, with a hoe in his
hands, he was the very soul of France, shrewd, cheerful, ready with a
mischievous gleam of the eyes under their badger grey eyebrows. He was a
philosopher, and in his way—a thinker.

Old Lorellard astonished Tod. He could talk unexpectedly about so many
unexpected things.

“Socialism, monsieur? We do not need socialism in Myonville. Look, you,
we all work here; there are no rich in Myonville.”

This was true.

“Moreover, there are no factories. It is the factories that make the
trouble.”

And then he asked Tod a question.

“Is it true, monsieur, that in your war factories you pay the workers,
even girls and boys, very much money?”

“That’s so,” said Tod.

“Then you will have much trouble after the war,” said old Lorellard; “no
one will know the value of money or the value of good sweat. But in
France we shall not have so much trouble.”

“Why?” Tod asked him.

Old Lorellard pointed to his crops.

“We grow things. We live close to Nature, and we know that she does not
give if we do not work. And work is good. They say we peasants are
misanthropes; sometimes it may be true. Look you, this land is mine;
there are thousands of little farms like mine in France. We work; we do
not waste money on things that do not matter. Paris is not France,
monsieur.”

“Are you happy?”

Old Lorellard gave him one of his shrewd, smiling looks.

“Well, what would you say, monsieur?”

“I hope I shall be as happy at your age,” said Tod.

Madame was like her man, only she went to church and he did not. Her old
sun-bonnet shaded a contented face, the face of a woman who had borne
strong children, and who had a right to be proud. Even the war had not
shaken her. Her boy’s three wounds were like red badges of honour over
her heart.

She talked quite frankly to Tod, sensing him as a man without guile.

“My other girl is married, monsieur. They are good girls, both of them,
though Josephine is a coquette. We do not spoil our girls, monsieur.
They understand what is expected of a woman.”

“And your niece?” Tod asked with a certain shyness.

Madame’s eyes lit up. Her heart was very warm towards Claire.

“A good girl, a very good girl, and so wise! She is an orphan, monsieur;
her father was a railway official; she had a good education.”

“And she is not—not engaged?” Tod asked.

“Oh, no, monsieur. It is unfortunate, but her dot will be very small.
Not but that men have asked for her, but she is very hard to please, is
Claire. She looks with those grey eyes of hers and waits and listens. A
good girl, so very strong yet gentle, and warm of heart, though she
seems so quiet. Such a wife for some man.”

And Tod wandered off into the orchard with a sad and very thoughtful
face. Why hadn’t he had the luck to find such a mate? There was
something mysterious and fine and compelling about Claire.

The staircase of the house led down into the kitchen, and Tod had to
pass through the kitchen whenever he went in and out. The Lorellards
made but little use of the sitting-room. Tod would always be passing
Claire. The school took but five days of her week, nor were the hours
long, and she sat by the kitchen window with her workbox on the
window-sill, and some work or other in her lap. She wore a rather shabby
black skirt, a white blouse with a faint blue stripe on it, and cut low
at the throat. On Sundays she wore a blouse with a pattern of little
mauve flowers, and a black cotton apron covered with blue pansies.

Sometimes she smiled at Tod, sometimes she looked at him with a baffling
seriousness and did not smile at all. She was not talkative; and though
her fingers were always busy, she had an air of pleasant repose.

“Always working, mam’selle,” Tod would say to her.

Her replies were never obvious.

“I think while I work,” or

“A woman is not all fingers.”

“Do you ever go out?” he asked her.

She laughed.

“Has not Monsieur le Colonel seen me sitting in the orchard? And I walk
every evening. There are always the children to walk with.”

“I wish——” said Tod, and then smothered the wish.

She guessed that he had wanted children.

Sometimes her face would glow with sudden light, and she would grow
animated. She had a very charming and expressive mouth, and the most
perfect white teeth; and when her eyes lit up Tod thought her beautiful,
beautiful in a way that was new to him. There seemed so much behind
those eyes.

Yet she baffled him, this daintily shabby, silent, children-teaching
Claire. She had such a sturdy poise of her own, the soft mystery of
woodlands that do not rush to you but wait for you to come to them, and
still remain mysterious. Tod found her serene, practical, strangely
quick to understand him, ready to listen, slow to speak. She seemed to
hold him at a distance. She was always a little way off.

He did not understand it then, that it was her strength, the strength of
a Frenchwoman who knew the passion latent within her, not the
Frenchwoman of the English idea, a woman far finer and more humanly wise
than that. She had something sacred within her, a passionate spirit that
no man should touch—until—— Until! That was the soft aloofness, Tod
saw in her eyes.

Each evening Tod became part of the family. Sometimes they sat in the
doorway, sometimes in the kitchen, sometimes in the orchard. Tod liked
the orchard best. He lay on the grass and watched the last sunlight on
the green boughs of the old trees and upon Claire’s face and hair.
Josephine was the talker, and Tod could fly a gaiety that would match
hers, but he did not look at Josephine as he looked at Claire.

Josephine made him talk.

“Were you always an officer, Monsieur le Colonel?”

Tod explained that he had begun the war as a private.

“And such a boy! And what are the ribbons for?”

He told them, looking at Claire as he spoke.

“Just luck, you know.”

“And in England I suppose you are rich? I should like to be rich. I
should have breakfast in bed each morning and in the winter I should go
to Nice.”

“And you would be none the happier,” said Claire, with one of her quick
flashes of aggressive truthfulness. “Too much money is like having too
many aprons; you never know which one to wear.”

Old Lorellard chuckled.

“Did you ever hear such wisdom? Quite a female Solomon.”

Claire blushed.

“I’m a poor man,” said Tod; “I never expect to be anything else. I agree
with Mademoiselle Vignard.”

Josephine gave Tod a gleam of her dark eyes, the look of a woman who
says in her heart: “Ah, it is like that, is it?” But if Josephine was a
coquette she was no cat. She was jealous for Claire when the real heart
of her was touched. Good women hold together.

These evenings left Tod astonished and full of pleasure in his own
astonishment. These people were peasants, substantial peasants, it is
true, but the fineness of their manners and the refinement of their
culture were unlike anything he had known in England. They made most
middle-class English families seem vulgar and ignorantly boorish. There
was no ostentation, no swelling up of the social frog, no fidgeting, no
giggling. Tod knew that the average Englishman is apt to be either
servile or insolent, and inclined to bray like a jackass at anything
that startles his complacent ignorance.

“They must be more civilised than we are,” he said to himself. “I know
I’m an ignorant beast.”

He learnt many things concerning Claire. She had been to school at
Tourcoing, a good school. She could read English, but speak it barely at
all, perhaps because Tod made her strangely shy. She could play the
violin. “But I play it very badly,” she confessed; “the war broke the
strings.” Her reading flustered Tod, who had never been much of a
reader. He did not know anything about the French classics, and he had
never heard of Ronsard, Montaigue, Pascal, Chateaubriand, Daudet or
Anatole France. She was surprised that he was ignorant of Balzac, and
Tod made a desperate grab and pulled up Dumas and Zola to impress her.
He could think of no other names. She had the advantage of him even in
the English classics, and she could have taught him his own history. And
yet there was nothing of the prig about her, nothing of the
schoolmistress. She could cook, sew, manage a house, and she had a
practical knowledge of farming and gardening. She had ideas about spiral
nebulæ and electrons and Mendelism, but such erudition struck Tod as
charmingly and wonderfully ridiculous.

“What do you want to know such things for?” he asked her.

“I don’t,” she said, “but it does not do me any harm, monsieur, so long
as I love children. A woman knows with her heart, a man with his head.”

And Tod’s consciousness began to be full of the eyes and the voice and
the soul of Claire.

 Chapter Thirty-two

That month of May in Myonville was the most wonderful month in Tod’s
life, full of the sadness of beauty and the beauty of sadness, exquisite
and strange. The war had been with Tod for more than three and a half
years. He had lived only with men, a very simple and big-hearted
soldier, taking the dirt and the wounds with them, boasting no poignant
memories of home, a very lonely man in that inner life where only women
and children and a few friends matter. He had given up his soul to the
war and his heart to his men, and they adored him. The clever little
people who wriggled into the safe corners at home do not understand
these things. How should they? They would have criticised that word
“adoration,” and they would have been bored by Tod’s simplicity. They
remained clever, with all the meanness of cleverness, while simpler men
became great.

Tod had had nothing to sweeten the war, save his success as a soldier,
the love of his men, and the big heartedness of Julian Burgetrode. His
memories of his wife were sordid and painful, memories of an overblown
young woman living in a London flat, a young woman of expensive habits
and extravagant clothes, hard, restless and sinister. There was a smear
on the face of Tod’s marriage. It was a thing of suspicion, horrible in
its dubiousness, its sly suggestion of disloyalty, its too obvious
materialism. And yet something restrained him, a sensitive generosity, a
reticence that kept a grimly shut mouth, perhaps that spirit of
discipline that Tod had taught to his men, for Tod could be very stern
to the shirker and the slacker. There were a few bad men who hated him,
because he had made them afraid, and was their master. They did not
matter. The battalion had faith in Tod, and a kind of schoolboy delight
in his shrewdness and his exposure of tricksters. “He’ll sweat blood for
you,” was the saying, “raise hell if anybody tries to do you down, but
if you run crooked with him, look out.”

In Myonville Tod found all that the heart of him had hungered for, green
leaves and cloth of gold, the smell of lilacs in a garden, red thorns in
bloom, the dear grey eyes of a girl. It was all so good, so wholesome,
so beautiful. He loved the beech trees and the orchards, the little
house with its soul of the home; he loved old Lorellard and his wife; he
loved the children in the streets, the horses that went to drink at the
big pond, the pigeons, the chestnut trees with their wax white flowers;
and perhaps without realising the pain and the beauty of it he loved
Claire.

She was Myonville; she was the spring, the greenness and perfume, the
soul of all he desired. She began to fill his whole consciousness, this
dainty shabby little woman with her grey eyes and shining hair, her
white throat, her busy fingers and her wisdom. She roused in Tod all
that passionate tenderness that memory connected with his mother. It was
only when he realised how much he wanted her that he discovered the
tragedy of it all.

Claire still puzzled him.

One evening he had tried to make her read an English book. Their heads
were together, by the lamp on the table. Old Lorellard was smoking his
pipe; madame dozing; Josephine watching Tod and her cousin. It was a
moment of gaiety, Claire laughing, expostulating, hesitating, with a
little finger pointing at some difficult word.

“But, monsieur, it is so absurd, your English endings. Puff and trough,
and though and enough. There’s no system.”

“There never is a system, except in the army,” laughed Tod; “I’ll get
the Army Council to get it all altered. Supposing you write me a
letter?”

“Be serious,” said Claire, touching the crown on his sleeve for a moment
with the tip of her little finger.

“I am serious,” said Tod, with a look of his blue eyes.

And suddenly she grew shy, strange. She drew the book away, and went to
Josephine almost with the air of a child asking for protection. Tod was
puzzled. Josephine began to talk to him in her gay and eager way, but
Tod did not want to talk to Josephine. Old Lorellard, that kind old man
of the world, refilled Tod’s glass, and smiled at him from under his
bushy eyebrows.

“To the end of the war, monsieur.”

“With all my heart!” said Tod.

“And monsieur will go to his home.”

Tod nearly choked over that glass of wine. He had glimpsed something
that he had not glimpsed before. Claire was nothing to him, even though
he had realised with a sudden anguish that she was everything. Perhaps
these good people were trying to make him understand; their eyes had
been open to his blindness? But, after all, he had spoken no single
word! It was just his own silly fancy, the sensitive boy in him, nothing
more, and for some days he tried to believe it was that.

He tried to pretend to a happy gaiety, to accept these weeks in
Myonville as nothing more than a charming interlude, a drawing of the
breath before more effort. His officers noticed that Tod was growing
solitary and silent, not morosely so, but with the gentle and quiet look
