of a man who had worries. He was out much on his horse, and it was on
one of these rides of his that Tod came nearer to a realisation of the
truth.

He had ridden to Eu; and was trotting back in the evening from that very
French and pleasant old town, enjoying the rolling country and the blue
sky and the beech woods, when he came upon three little people resting
by the road. A girl was sitting on the grass, holding a weeping child in
her lap, and a small boy was standing beside her and looking with quaint
melancholy at his dirty shoes. He seemed undecided as to whether he
should laugh or weep. The girl was Claire, very pretty in her simple
Sunday clothes.

Tod dismounted. Her grey eyes looked up at him gladly.

“What’s this! Oh, mam’selle, why this grief?”

“I’m so tired, monsieur,” said the child.

Claire explained.

“We have been to Le Treport. I promised to take them to the sea; we
missed the last train. I think there are only two trains in the day,
monsieur.”

“So you started to walk?”

“And mam’selle has been carrying Marie,” put in the boy; “and Marie is
heavy.”

Tod looked into Claire’s tired eyes, and knew now how he loved her.

“Ma pauvre petite,” he said, “did no one stop to give you a ride?”

“A car of the Croix Rouge passed us, but the driver shouted that it was
forbidden.”

“Punch knows how to disobey orders, don’t you, old chap? Who wants a
ride? Come along, Marie; up in the saddle; he’s such a quiet old horse.
Now then, Thomas, up you go too.”

He lifted the children on to Punch’s back, and then turned to Claire.

“You are tired,” he said, and his eyes added: “Ma chérie, so tired.”

His man’s tenderness betrayed itself so utterly that she flushed and
looked confused.

“I am no longer tired, monsieur.”

“We have eight more kilometers.”

“I’m very strong. It was carrying the child.”

“I wish I could carry you,” said Tod.

There was a welling up of light into her eyes, a light that is seen but
rarely in the eyes of a woman. Then she laughed.

“Monsieur would soon be tired of it,” she said. “I am little, but so
solid!”

Tod’s coming changed that weary pilgrimage into a triumphal return.
Punch behaved like a gentleman, walking stolidly with his bridle on
Tod’s arm. The children, surmounting a brief awe of Tod, began to laugh
and chatter.

“Who are they?” he asked.

Claire looked straight ahead down the avenue of beeches. “The mother is
a widow, a widow of the war. And she could not afford——”

“So you stepped in?”

“Well, it is a pleasure.”

“It would be to you,” he said, “anything that is fine and good——”

“Monsieur!” she exclaimed softly, but with a touch of reproach.

Tod flushed.

“I beg your pardon,” he said, “I couldn’t help it. And you love
children?”

“Is it not nature?”

“I have none,” he said, “but I was very happy when I was a child. I
remember it all so well. My mother was the best woman in all the world,
and I should like to be to my boy what my father was to me.”

“They are dead, then?” said Claire.

“Yes.”

“It is sad for monsieur. But you have a home.”

“No, not a home like the Lorellards’. If I get killed it won’t matter
much.”

“You must not say that,” she said, “there are too few good men in the
world.”

The two children demanded a large part of the conversation, and Claire
and Tod drew apart to meet again in the chatter of the youngsters. Old
Punch looked at Tod now and again with a question in those soft eyes of
his. “Is this according to Army Orders, sir, and am I doing the job
correctly?” Tod rubbed Punch’s nose, and then pretended to tickle
Mademoiselle Marie’s leg.

“Naughty,” she said highly delighted. “Naughty man.”

And all the time Tod was conscious of Claire stepping along beside him
as though they were mated and these two children on old Punch’s
back——! But Tod was dreaming dreams.

When they came to the outskirts of Myonville Claire looked questioningly
at Tod.

“Monsieur le Colonel would prefer to ride in alone?”

“I? Why should I?”

“The English are so correct.”

He regarded her earnestly.

“I’m as happy as a king and as proud. But is it not allowed for you and
these children?”

“I was not thinking of myself,” she said, “but of the dignity of the
English officer.”

“Please God,” said Tod, “may I be kicked into the nearest ditch if my
dignity is so shabby a thing as that.”

She gave him a flash of her eyes.

“You are a good man, Monsieur Tod.”

And Tod went into Myonville with the happy face of a Sir Percival, past
many of his own men who saluted him with hand and eyes, past the sacred
doorway of his own orderly-room and the brisk face of the battalion
sergeant-major. Somewhere near the church a man of the new draft that
had just come to the battalion, a little literary gent or something
jerked in for the last phase, chose to be facetious in the hearing of
one of Tod’s veterans.

“The C.O.’s found a hot billet. These officer chaps——”

The back of a hard hand swung swiftly, struck him in the mouth.

“You b——y little blighter! He’s worth a brigade of you scribbling
rotters. Don’t you try that sort of stuff on us.”

The literary gent had a bloody lip, and he was frightened.

“Can’t a chap have a little joke——?”

“No, not you, when you want to put it on the colonel. He was out in ’14
while you were polishing the seat of a chair. You aren’t in the same
class.”

It had been a happy evening for Tod, happy in its innocence and its
simplicity and in his comradeship with Claire. She had talked to him
more intimately and with a freedom that had brought him very near to
her, yet perhaps because of the day’s happiness he was a little blind to
what it meant to her. Tod had not realised the woman under that calm and
courageous surface. He had not fathomed the depths of her, the
possibilities of passion that a weaker woman might have betrayed to a
weaker man. He had not divined what had happened to Claire any more than
he quite realised all that had happened to himself.

The Lorellards were sitting in the big kitchen when Tod returned from
the mess. There happened to be a Frenchman with them, an interpreter
attached to an English Division, and an old friend. He lived in the next
village and was home on leave.

Tod was introduced. He sat down and began to talk. The kitchen was in
semi-darkness, for there were no lights; they had to be careful in
Myonville because the German planes were searching each night for a big
ammunition dump somewhere near the mouth of the Somme. And then Tod
missed Claire from the pleasant friendliness of the family circle. She
was in the kitchen. She had taken a chair over to the far corner of the
door that opened into the shop, and she was sitting there alone in the
dusk, a little figure, almost invisible, save for her pale face.

Tod was puzzled.

She did not move, or join in the conversation, but sat there mutely,
isolated, wholly apart.

“Mademoiselle Vignard is tired,” he said to Josephine. “Is not that so,
Mademoiselle Claire?”

“Perhaps,” she answered him with an almost casual abruptness.

She did not speak again, but something emanated from her, a pathetic
sense of resistance, an obstinate sadness that challenged Tod. He did
not understand her silence and her isolation, and in groping for an
explanation, wondered whether he was in the way.

The interpreter did not stay long. Tod stood there awkwardly after the
Frenchman had gone. Claire was still detached and silent in her corner.

“Have I driven your friend away?” he asked them.

Josephine answered him.

“Oh, no, monsieur. He was just a friend of my brother’s. He is not very
acceptable to us and to Claire.”

Tod thought he had found the explanation of Claire’s aloofness, but he
was wrong.

When he said “Good-night” to the Lorellards she did not come out of her
corner, and her good night was almost inaudible. “Bon soir, monsieur.”

Tod went upstairs to his room, troubled.

“Have I offended her?” he thought, “and how?”

 Chapter Thirty-three

Two nights later Myonville was bombed. Some German airman, searching for
that monstrous dump, and somewhat at sea in his reckoning, dropped his
explosives on that great patch of blackness, Myonville and its trees.
There was much noise; some tense terror; but not a house was touched:
the German bombs fell in the gardens, and the orchards and the fields.

Tod was undressing when he heard the German plane overhead, and the
excited voices of people in the street. He put on his tunic, leant out
of the window and listened, seeing the orchard of the Lorellards all
black under the full moon. A door banged in the house. He heard
frightened footsteps on the stairs, and Josephine’s voice high pitched
and excited.

“Claire, Claire, come into the fields. Maman is waiting.”

“I shall stay here,” said a quiet voice.

Then a bomb crashed somewhere over by the church and Tod saw Josephine
and her mother rush across the yard. The girl turned at the orchard
gate.

“Claire, do not be so foolish!”

Tod returned.

“I will bring her, mam’selle,” he called; “go on with madame.”

He knew the door of Claire’s room, and he crossed the landing and
knocked.

“Are you dressed, mam’selle?”

“Yes,” she answered him.

“Then you will come out into the fields?”

“I am not afraid.”

“But—I—am afraid,” he said; “you will come out with me into the
fields?”

She seemed to hesitate; then she opened her door. Tod was standing there
with the moonlight on his face, and no man could have looked less
unafraid or more calm.

“Come,” he said, “it is wiser.”

She preceded him down the stairs, not hurrying, moving almost
reluctantly. He had dominated her for the moment, and his domination was
so sweet that it hurt.

They reached the orchard, with its mystery of moonlight and of shadows.
The grass was heavy with dew, and the trunks of the old trees looked
black as ebony.

“Why did you lie to me?” she said suddenly.

“I do not understand you.”

“You said you were afraid. You are not afraid.”

“I was not afraid for myself,” said Tod.

She looked at him sharply in the moonlight.

“Sometimes one does not care.”

And then she tried to smother the half-confessed cry of pain in those
words.

“I mean—that it is just chance, monsieur.”

But Tod’s face had the tense, white listening look of the soldier, of a
man grown cunning in the hell’s music of the war. They were close to the
orchard ditch, and Tod pulled the girl into it with a fierceness that
was too elemental to wait for words.

“Down, down on your face!”

It was like the plunge and crash of some meteorite. The ground shook;
the whole orchard was a sudden yellow glare; fragments of metal, sods,
shreds of splintered wood went seething through the apple bough and
through the hedge behind them. One heavy bit of steel whined low over
Tod’s head, and fell with a “phut” into the bank behind him.

He found himself holding Claire’s hand. She was trembling, and bending
low upon her knees.

“Thank God,” said Tod, lifting his face into the moonlight.

She drew her breath in deeply, and then suddenly withdrew her hand.

“I’m sorry if I was rough,” Tod said to her.

“It was necessary, monsieur. I understand now.”

“Perhaps you understand,” he said, “why I was afraid? Rather than
anything should happen to you——”

She stood up and astonished him with a sudden outburst of gaiety. Tod
had seen men laugh in a hysterical sort of way when they had escaped
some great danger, but the girl’s soft laughter was far more elusive and
subtle. It sounded very strange to him in that solemn orchard with the
German plane still droning overhead.

“And grandpère is still asleep. I am sure of it. We always say that he
will not hear the trumpet of the Angel Gabriel. I wish to see the great
hole in the ground.”

She went forward lightly between the orchard trees, a quick and graceful
little figure, sometimes in the moonlight, sometimes in the shadow. To
Tod she had become a tantalising and baffling creature, surprising him
with her silence or her laughter, her flashes of frankness or an
aloofness that left him puzzled and alone.

“Here, monsieur! Oh, the poor earth! the poor tree!”

He found her standing on the edge of a black pit, with all the wet grass
about her covered with sods and dirt. An old pear tree, splintered at
the base, had toppled sideways, and lay with its boughs trailing in the
crater like a dead thing with spread arms and tumbled hair. Claire was
standing like a child and looking down into the pit, a sudden solemnity
upon her and pity in her eyes.

“It was such a good tree,” she said, “and bore such sweet fruit.”

Tod looked at her with a great longing.

“Better the tree than you.”

She did not seem to hear him.

“André was so fond of this tree. We used to have a swing on that big
bough. And Uncle will be grieved. He loves all trees.”

Tod said nothing. He had seen so many dead men and so many dead trees.

They heard voices in the meadow beyond the orchard, the voices of women
and children and old men. Josephine’s voice was audible above all the
others.

“It fell in our orchard, I am sure. Oh, mon Dieu! supposing the house
had been struck!”

Claire turned suddenly and went in the direction of the voices.

“Josephine, ici, ici. We are safe. The old yellow pear tree has been
broken.”

They came pouring into the orchard with all the eagerness and the
sangfroid of the French, those wonderful peasants whom Tod had learnt to
respect from the bottom of his heart. Josephine ran and kissed Claire.

“Where is mon père?”

“Asleep, I believe.”

There was a chorus of laughter, exclamations. They gathered round the
black hole and the broken tree, observing everything with the simplicity
of children, immensely interested, sympathetic. A boy slid down into the
crater and began grubbing with his hands for souvenirs.

Tod had placed himself close to Claire.

“It is good luck,” he said; “you will never be so near to a bomb again.”

She smiled queerly, her two hands folded below her throat, her eyes
looking up at the moon. And then she slipped away, withdrawing herself
with an unobtrusive gentleness, and going to Josephine, who was looking
at the tree.

Something held Tod back. He did not follow her, for that little act of
hers had affected him profoundly. It was so symbolical, so delicate, yet
so strong.

Later they gathered in the kitchen, where old Lorellard had been found
quietly smoking his pipe.

“It is all over, is it?” he said.

“Mon père, the yellow pear tree is broken.”

“Well, it died for France,” quoth old Lorellard with a wink and a cock
of his chin.

Josephine, a flushed and tumultuous Josephine, had run to the cellar for
a bottle of red wine.

“It is a celebration, a great deliverance,” she said, “for the whole
family and Monsieur le Colonel.”

Tod bowed to her.

“France has taught me many things, mademoiselle, the great things that
matter.”

They drank the red wine, all save Claire, who refused to touch it, and
the old people went to bed. Tod and the two girls sat talking for half
an hour with the moonlight shining in upon the floor and glinting on the
buckles of Claire’s shoes. Her face was in the shadow, Josephine’s in
the light.

“Such danger is but child’s play to monsieur,” said the dark girl.

Tod seemed to be thinking.

“There are things that hurt one more than wounds,” he said.

And he heard Claire sigh.

Josephine lit the candles.

“It is safe now.”

She gave one to Tod and went up the stairs, Claire following her. Tod
came last. Josephine’s room was at the top of the stairs, but Claire and
Tod had to cross the big landing that was larger than any of the rooms.
Their two doors were opposite each other.

“Good night, Mademoiselle Claire,” said Tod, looking back.

And for one moment she turned her head and gave him the most wonderful
look in all the world, a shine of the eyes, a soft and splendid
tenderness, a flash of exaltation touched with despair.

Tod went into his room, walked up and down for half a minute, and then
knelt at the open window.

“God, bless her always and always,” he said, “and make me strong.”

 Chapter Thirty-four

It was washing day, and Josephine and madame were hanging out the linen
in the orchard. A pair of Papa Lorellard’s blue linen trousers flaunted
it in the sunlight between the two white sheets; and a red petticoat
completed the tricolour. From the schoolhouse across the lane came a
murmur of children’s voices. Old Lorellard’s geese gaggled in the
meadow.

“The colonel is in love with Claire,” said Josephine to her mother.

Madame Lorellard was pegging up a pair of lace-edged pillow slips.

“It is understandable,” quoth the old lady; “I have seen it for days.”

“It is a pity,” said the daughter.

“Why a pity? I do not think that any harm will come of it.”

Josephine grimaced.

“No; they are both too strong, and monsieur is not like other officers.
But it is a pity.”

“Well?”

“That he is married. He would have married Claire.”

“Ma chérie, what a romancer you are!”

“I am not, Maman. And Claire wants him.”

Madame Lorellard’s kind blue eyes grew sharp and questioning.

“Has she told you?”

“Would Claire tell me, or anybody? But she wants him as she has never
wanted a man, and he—he is hers—body and soul. They are in heaven, and
in hell, Maman.”

“How do you know?”

“I have sharp eyes and a heart. My dear Maman, I am a woman, a vain one,
a talkative one, but I love Claire. I am handsome, yes, and I have black
eyes, and a mouth that troubles most men, but this man is different; he
has fallen down at little Claire’s feet. He could not help it, I think.
These things just happen.”

Madame Lorellard went on hanging up her linen. Her ruddy face looked
impressive, untroubled, the face of an old woman who was not easily
shaken. Her blue eyes were proud.

“Yes, it is a pity,” she said, “and yet I clap him on the shoulder for
it. A man of sense, a good man, so merry, and so courteous. He will not
do her any harm.”

“But she will suffer,” said Josephine; “Claire is not like the vain,
light-hearted ones.”

“But it is a triumph,” said the old Frenchwoman; “such a man and an
English colonel, a good soldier. Let her look at it in that way.”

“Claire cares nothing for triumphs. She wants the man—the one man, and
his children.”

“Bless the girl,” said Madame Lorellard, “but if he had married her we
might have lost her. Is not that something?”

“You are growing old, Maman,” said her daughter.

Even the children at the school were aware of a change in Mademoiselle
Claire Vignard, or “la petite mère,” as they called her. She looked sad,
so sad at times; the class noticed it when it should have been absorbed
in its sums. There were moments when “la petite mère” was strangely
inattentive, and she could be caught gazing out of the window at the
white road and the beech trees and the great clouds and the blue sky.
She was like a child whose heart was not in her lessons, and who wanted
to be away somewhere; the class understood that quite well, but the
reason of it was beyond the understanding of the class. Claire had
always been so vital, so smiling, so shrewd, so calm, so full of
interest in her teaching, a little mother who had persuaded the lazy
ones to take trouble. Half the small boys were in love with her. She was
so much prettier, so much more charming than Mademoiselles Epinard and
Fleury, those two rather thin women who were so ready to scold.

And then came that morning when Mademoiselle Claire Vignard worked out a
sum incorrectly on the blackboard, and did not notice it. But Monsieur
Moreau, the schoolmaster, who happened to come into the room, noticed it
and was shocked.

Such a thing had never happened before in Claire’s career.

“Mademoiselle Vignard! It is incorrect!”

“Which, monsieur?”

“The example on the blackboard.”

The class maintained a grave silence. Claire, faintly flushed, had left
her chair and was looking at the chalked figures on the board. Then she
took a duster and a stick of chalk and made the proper corrections.

“I am sorry, monsieur.”

M. Moreau happened to be worried, and in a bad temper.

“Please be more careful in future.”

The class was indignant, scandalised. For any man to speak to “la petite
mère” in such a fashion! Little Emile, the chanticleer of the class,
protruded a contemptuous tongue behind M. Moreau’s back.

“Cochon!” muttered the infant.

Claire smiled at them all when the schoolmaster had marched out. She had
recovered herself.

“Did none of you see that I was saying my prayers on the blackboard?”
she asked them.

Emile stood up and replied with great solemnity. He was destined to be
an orator.

“The class would never confess that Mademoiselle Vignard could make a
mistake,” he said; “the error was in M. Moreau’s manners.”

Claire’s laughing eyes thanked this sententious little diplomat of
seven.

“Perhaps I shall never make a mistake again, Emile. If I do, the class
shall correct me.”

Tod, too, had touched a season of great sadness. The gods were warning
him that this happy sojourn in Myonville was drawing to a close, for
were not reinforcements arriving, and a temporarily depressed brigadier
showing a new sparkle and a more critical alertness. Some day their
marching orders would arrive with all the blind suddenness of a bolt
from Olympus. And Tod’s heart was in Myonville. He could not help it; it
had just happened so. This green and golden month had been a season of
strange beauty and tenderness, the happiest and yet the saddest month in
all Tod’s life.

He would never see Claire again. He had no right to see her again, and
he knew that he loved as he had never thought to love any woman, as his
father must have loved his mother. It was all so hopeless and so tragic,
and yet there was no bitterness in Tod’s heart; the romance was too
great for that. He was just a big man, profoundly touched, profoundly
sad, not a little afraid of hurting the heart of the woman he loved,
still more sad when he thought of that other woman.

Tod was tempted, but the Quixote in him kept its face in the sunlight.

“If you took the trouble to find out,” said the voice, “you might
discover things that would set you free.”

And Tod refused.

“It can’t be done,” he answered. “I can’t play the spy and the cad to
win her.”

But he wanted to pour out his heart to Claire, to express that love of
his with all the passion and the tenderness that cried out in him like
the joy of the spring. There was such an exultation in loving her as he
did, and yet it was the thought of the afterwards that made him pause
and remain silent. Tod knew that he would suffer most terribly when he
left her, but he would have a man’s work to do, other men to think of,
while Claire—would just have this little village and her children. She
had been contented before his coming, and Tod spent those last days in
wondering whether she cared, whether she cared as he did. He did not
want to suffer; his vanity was not concerned in it, and yet—— What a
memory to carry in his heart, what a splendid tenderness for a man to
die with after all the little meannesses of his marriage!

It was Tod’s last evening but one in Myonville, though he did not know
it then, and he walked over the fields to Gibas where another battalion
was quartered, and had tea with the officers who were billeted in the
château. The colonel was a ragged old ruffian of a Scot, lean, biting,
merciless, but with a heart of gold. He had a peculiar affection for
Tod; in a sense they were rivals. Both battalions swore by their
colonels.

“You will be staying to dinner, Western?”

And Tod refused.

“Sorry, Colonel, but I must get back.”

“Must—must! There’s no must about it! Just a wee French lassie
somewhere, my lad!”

Tod blushed.

“I’m nearly twenty years younger than you are, sir.”

“Ye soft-tongued young scamp, calling me sir! Well, get along with you.”

And Tod went, old Melville walking as far as the red-brick gate-house
with him, eager as some rough-haired terrier over the news that was
coming in.

“We’ll be at them again in ten days, my lad. They say we shall move
north to St. Omer to pick up some more men and spend a week knocking
them into shape.”

“I’m ready,” said Tod with a far-away smile as the old Scot turned back
at the gate, “though I had almost forgotten the war back here.”

“Yes, it has been a brave month, Western. I’m the better for a sight of
the children and those big trees.”

Tod walked back through the deep lanes of Gibas, with the elms and the
beeches meeting above his head and a small stream that had grown tired
of its proper bed winding as it pleased about the sandy road. The blue
sky was almost invisible, so thick was the canopy of leaves, and the
little white houses seemed lost and hidden in this world of green
growth. The road climbed slowly out of these woodland deeps like a snake
crawling out to bask in the sun. Tod had a sweep of fields before him,
sheeted with red sainfoin and wheat full of scarlet poppies. The distant
trees of Myonville cut the deep blue arch of the sky like the misty
towers of a distant town. A great iron crucifix stood on a mound just
where the road left the shadows of the trees.

Tod took a field-track that branched away to the left. It was a
favourite walk of his, a sort of pilgrim’s way that was in no hurry to
get to any particular place, turning aside and loitering when a tree or
a piece of orchard or a row of old trees pleased it. At times it cut
deep into the fields like the sunk bed of a stream. Tod knew that it
would bring him into Myonville close to old Lorellard’s poplar wood,
where he could turn into the meadows and so through the orchard to the
Lorellards’ house. He felt that he wanted to be alone and to escape the
salutes of his men.

Half-way across the fields the track skirted a little grove of aspens,
and sitting on the grass bank under one of these trees Tod saw Claire
Vignard. She was all alone there like some Joan of Arc under the trees
of Domrémy, and so absorbed in her own thoughts that she did not see Tod
or hear his footsteps, so that he came on her like a figure that steps
down out of a dream.

Tod had faltered for a moment, swept by a rush of great tenderness. She
looked so much alone with her dear self, a little lost, so unconsciously
sad that his heart misgave him. He wanted to go to her, reach out his
hands and meet the soft light of her grey eyes and utter those words
that haunted him day and night:

“Ma chérie, I love you.”

Yet when he saw her sitting there alone, gazing towards the far horizon
of her dreams, his own tenderness smote him in the mouth. There was
nothing that he might say to her, nothing that he had the right to say.

He moved on again, and suddenly she saw him, the very man of her
thoughts, the mate of her dear desires, but she remained still, still as
a bird whose startled heart beats rapidly. Deep in her eyes shone a soft
inward glow. She felt Tod loved her, and would never speak.

“No school to-day?”

“It is a holiday, monsieur.”

Tod stood looking down at her, the little woman with the sunlight on her
face and hair, her bosom moving with a suggestion of slight
breathlessness under the plain white blouse, her grey eyes shining—yet
faintly clouded, each hand clasping a tuft of grass. Tod trembled on the
edge of that tragic tenderness of his. He wanted her, and she, in her
dreams, had felt herself embraced by him.

“It is so beautiful and peaceful here,” he said.

“Not a leaf moves,” she answered, looking upwards.

“Still as an empty church.”

She closed her eyes for a moment and dreamed. Tod was so near, yet she
knew that he would come no nearer.

“You have been very happy in Myonville?” she asked him suddenly.

“Very happy.”

And then he added:

“I know that I shall never be so happy again.”

Those were the only words he could utter, but in the stillness of the
fields she took them into her heart.

“I am going back now, monsieur,” she said.

“May I walk with you?”

“If you wish it.”

And silence walked with them nearly all the way.

 Chapter Thirty-five

Tod knew next morning that the battalion was to leave Myonville within
twenty-four hours. A dispatch-rider brought him an early morning order
from brigade headquarters, and Tod’s orderly-room in Widow Dubois’ house
near the church began to function with great vigour. Tod was there
before breakfast, with both hands deep in a basketful of official
detail. A lorry arrived for the quartermaster and thundered off to
Abbeville to draw ordnance stores. The transport officer had horses to
collect. The battalion was to parade at nine, in battle order, for the
C.O.’s inspection.

Tod rushed his breakfast and was back in the orderly-room. He was grim,
brisk, a little feverish, conscious of a little woman with grey eyes
waiting for him beyond all that mass of mechanical detail, the one thing
in life that mattered. The war mattered, but it had become a habit, and
Myonville had broken the habit. The work irritated him, though it had to
be gone through with, and Tod pushed his way through as though he were
forcing his way through a wood.

Tod inspected the battalion, went round all the billets, spent another
hour in the orderly-room with his adjutant and felt himself nearer
Claire.

“We move at nine, James. It’s all quite simple. We are due at the
starting-point at nine-fifteen. Seymour can ride on for billets at St.
Riquier. We have an hour’s break on the road.”

“I’ll see to everything, sir.”

“I think I’ll go round and get some of my things together,” said Tod.

Then the office orderly rushed in.

“The general, sir, the Divisional general.”

“Damn him!” said Tod under his breath.

It took another hour to get rid of the Divisional general. Tod had a
reputation, and the great man was critical and inquisitive. He told Tod
that his horses were dirty, and then asked him to dinner.

“I’m awfully sorry, sir,” said Tod, “but I have arranged to spend my
last evening with some friends here.”

The general was sportive and no fool.

“That’s all right, Western; I quite understand.”

Tod saluted him with great affection and watched him ride round the
corner, and then started for his billet. The orderly-room sergeant
pursued Tod.

“Will you sign these letters, sir?”

“Get Captain James to do it, Murray. I don’t want to see any more of
these damned things to-day.”

Claire Vignard had just come in from the school. She was sitting sewing
by the kitchen window, with the familiar work-basket on the window-sill
beside her. She happened to be alone, for Josephine was busy in the
shop, and the old Lorellards were down in the fields.

She heard the door of the café open, and a man’s footsteps crossing the
outer room. It was Tod; she knew that quick step of his so well, and the
quiet way he had of closing the door.

“Back from school, mademoiselle.”

He came into the kitchen, and put his cap and stick on the table. She
could see that he was much moved, and pretending to be casual; but he
was flushed, and his eyes were restless. He walked to the window and
stood looking at the orchard, a tense figure, head drawn back, eyes at
gaze.

Suddenly he turned to her.

“Always working,” he said.

She did not raise her head.

“Yes, it is good to work.”

“I shall always think of you like that,” he said, “sitting by the window
sewing. We go to-morrow.”

“To another village, Monsieur Tod?”

Her voice was very steady.

“No, to the front. I’m glad it’s the front.”

He turned again to the window, heart-sick, but very determined.

Claire had gone white, and her eyes seemed blind for the moment. She
kept her head bowed and went on with her work.

“Ssst!”

Tod turned sharply.

“What is it?”

“Nothing, monsieur.”

He saw a drop of blood, very red in the sunlight, showing on the tip of
one of her fingers, but her hands moved steadily at their work, and
presently a red stain showed on the white linen.

Tod never forgot that little drop of blood on Claire’s finger. It
remained a vivid memory through the coming months.

“I am sorry it is the trenches,” she said, after that short silence.

“Where else could a soldier go?” he answered. “I am not afraid of the
trenches.”

“I cannot think of you as being afraid, monsieur. I can only wish you
bon chance.”

Tod was looking at the orchard trees, and in their greenness he saw
Myonville, the spring, all the beauty of that quiet month, and
perhaps—the heart of Claire Vignard.

“I have been very happy here,” he said slowly, almost like a man
praying; “it has taught me so much. I don’t know whether I shall ever
come back again; I should like to come back, some day. You have all been
so good to me.”

Claire’s head had dropped a little lower.

“It has been a pleasure to—us, monsieur.”

And Tod smiled, a far-away, wondering smile.

“Life is queer, isn’t it? Well, one does not forget. It’s for France and
you, anyway, if anything happens, I shan’t regret it.”

She gave him a brave flash of her grey eyes.

“I shall not forget, monsieur. It will be a good memory.”

“God bless you, ma petite,” said Tod, and went blundering up the steep
stairs to his room with the excuse that he had to look through his kit.
But he sat on the bed and stared at the wall.

“It’s all over,” he said to himself; “she’s—she’s——”

He could not say what Claire was, for his thoughts were a mere fumbling
tenderness; but Claire was staring at the little red mark on the white
linen, and wondering whether he knew.

Lieut.-Colonel Western spent his last evening with the Lorellards. They
took their chairs and a little table into the orchard, also five glasses
and a bottle of red wine. It was a sad evening, dying away into the soft
June dusk, and Tod and Josephine were the talkers. Old Lorellard puffed
at his pipe and appeared depressed; madame had been working in the sun
and was tired; Claire’s eyes were watching Tod as though she wished to
burn the memory of him into her mind. Tod was calm, very gay, smiling in
the face of his own sadness.

“I shall be fighting for all of you,” he said; “for madame and monsieur
and the little farm, for Mademoiselle Josephine and the shop, for
Mademoiselle Claire and the children.”

“I am not a religious man, monsieur,” said old Lorellard, “but I shall
think of you when I use my scythe and my hoe.”

“That’s as good as a prayer, my friend.”

“Good men do not need praying for,” said Claire Vignard suddenly; “it is
enough for us to remember them.”

“Thank you, mademoiselle,” said Tod.

The battalion was to move at nine, and Tod’s servant had orders to call
him at six, but Tod needed no calling. He could not sleep. He was
thinking of Claire, thinking of her all the night, and how absurd it
seemed that he might never see her again. He could not bring himself to
realise it, though the man in him knew that it was true. Old memories of
Spaniards Row came back to Tod. All that he suffered that night, the
silence and the helpless yearning of it made him think of the night when
his mother died. She had slipped away from him into the darkness, never
to return—but Tod’s mother had died; Claire was a living Claire.

And so wonderfully alive, so wholesome, so comely and desirable, such a
soft sweet thing to be embraced, that Tod could hardly believe that he
was losing her. It seemed so wrong, so damnably and ridiculously wrong,
almost as monstrous as the death of his mother had seemed to Tod—the
child.

When the dawn came he left that most comfortable of French beds in which
he had been unable to sleep, washed himself, put on his best tunic and
riding-breeches and his new field-boots, and went softly on to the
broad, white-floored landing. The house seemed silent and asleep, and
Tod slipped silently down the stairs, yet not so silently that he passed
unheard by someone else who had been unable to sleep. Claire, standing
at her window in her nightdress, saw Tod walk out towards the orchard.
He paused by the gate to light his pipe.

“Mon pauvre,” she said to herself, “for you too the dawn is a sad one
after a night of sadness. Will you call your pipe by my name, I wonder,
as French boys do? Why did you come to us—ah!—why did you come?”

She watched Tod disappear into the orchard, and then slipped back into
bed.

“I will not see him again,” she said. “I do not think that I could bear
it. I shall stay here till he has gone.”

Tod walked up and down the orchard for an hour. Perhaps—lover-like—he
had hopes of seeing Claire again and of speaking a few last words to
her, words that would remain after he had left, but it was not Claire
who came to him, but Josephine on her way to the woodstack to bring in
an armful of dry wood.

“Bonjour, monsieur. You are very early.”

Her dark eyes looked straight at Tod.

“I could not sleep,” he said, with brotherly frankness.

She paused by him, a woman who understood all that had happened to these
two people, but whose thoughts were with Claire.

“You have been too happy in Myonville, Monsieur Tod.”

“Happy!”

He felt that Josephine understood.

“But not now.”

“Mon ami,” she said, “you will go to your home when the war is over, and
you will forget.”

“I shall have no home,” he answered, “and I shall never forget. Some
day—perhaps——”

She looked at him pityingly with her dark eyes.

“It is serious with you; I know. It is very hard.”

Tod thought a moment.

“Josephine—I wonder if I might write to her?”

She answered him at once and very firmly.

“No, monsieur. It would not be fair.”

“Not even once?”

“If wounds are kept open——”

Tod looked her straight in the face.

“You are right, absolutely right,” he said, “it would not be fair.”

And Josephine kissed him.

“Claire will understand,” she said.

 Chapter Thirty-six

The battalion bugles were blowing and the men were falling in on the
square by the church. The transports had hooked in and were waiting in
the little Rue des Fermes. Half Myonville was there by the church,
crowded against the fronts of the little white houses and under the
pollarded limes, laughing and exchanging banter with the soldiers and
wishing them good luck.

“Where’s the C.O.?”

The adjutant, glancing at his watch and rather worried, threw this
question at Haines the doctor, who came up jamming a packet of
sandwiches into his haversack.

“He left me at the mess. Said he had forgotten something at his billet.”

“It’s three minutes to nine. Sergeant-Major!”

“Sir?”

“Send the colonel’s horse along to his billet, the café down by the
school.”

“Never known him late,” said James to the doctor, “not by ten seconds.”

Tod had gone back to his billet, for an overmastering impulse had taken
him there. Claire had hidden herself and he had not seen her to say
“good-bye” when he had left the house at half-past seven. And suddenly
the thought had shocked him, the thought of “I shall never see her
again,” and his heart had answered—“I must.”

The café door was locked. Tod went round to the gate that led into the
yard, and came face to face with Josephine.

“Mademoiselle.”

Josephine understood.

“She is in her room.”

“I have a minute, no more. Josephine, you can trust me.”

“I trust you,” she said, and walked away towards the orchard.

Tod stood in the little brick-paved yard, looking up at the house all
green with the leaves of the great pear tree. A little smoke rose from
one of the chimneys. Claire’s window, with its white lace curtains, was
half closed.

“Claire,” he called softly, “Mademoiselle Claire.”

Then he saw her at the window. She was dressed, and he had a glimpse of
her white blouse and the top of her black apron with the blue pansies.

“Monsieur——!”

She had gone pale at the sound of his voice, and she was trembling.

“It is au revoir, I had not seen you.”

“Je ne descende pas,” she said; “I cannot, monsieur.”

She looked down at him for a moment with a sudden tragic radiance that
was near to tears. And then her hand dropped something, a little book
bound with a slip of blue ribbon. It was a little Testament that had
belonged to her mother. Tod picked it up.

“God bless you for ever and ever,” he said.

And then he turned and ran.

He was buttoning that little brown book into the breast pocket of his
tunic, when an orderly met him with his horse.

“All right, Smith. Thanks. Now then, Punch, old boy.”

He swung into the saddle and rode down the street to the church. The
church clock struck nine as he reached the battalion, those brown men in
their fighting kit standing steady, eyes to the front.

And Tod smiled. It was a brave smile. He was the soldier again.

“Nearly late, James.”

“It never happens, sir.”

“All ready?”

“All ready, sir.”

“Then we’ll move off.”

So Tod rode out of Myonville at the head of his men, and Claire went to
the school and sat calmly through the morning under the eyes of the
children.

“Is it all true?” her heart kept saying. “Why did he ever come to me?”

And yet she knew that she would not have had it otherwise.

 Chapter Thirty-seven

Tod fought all through the summer months; in the autumn he fought at Le
Cateau and Landrecies, and even before he had reached that October phase
he was made to realise that he was not going to be killed. At
Wallincourt he had a house knocked down about his head, and beyond being
buried and smothered with plaster, he was not hurt. James, his adjutant,
was shot dead beside him in a street in Le Cateau. Somewhere near
Landrecies Tod missed the exploding of a “delayed action mine,” and
missed it with a margin of forty seconds. “The C.O.’s luck.” It was
proverbial, and the men agreed that he deserved it, but they warned
Cane, his servant, to look out. “You be very careful, Whacker, or some
bloomin’ shell that ’asn’t the cheek to touch him will blow you to
b——y blazes.”

Cane “looked out” until the Armistice intervened, and he found himself
tacking oiled linen over the broken windows of a villa in Landrecies,
and arranging old pails to catch the drip from the ceiling. Cane
believed in the colonel; he believed too in that little old French
Testament which he knew Tod always carried in the breast pocket of his
tunic. Cane was a religious boy, though the battalion had given him the
irreverent name of “Whacker.”

Tod felt lost, most strangely lost. The amazing business was over, the
high spirited tension of it relaxed after those years of inward
discipline. It was as though the world had struck work of a sudden, and
stood waiting at the street corners for God and the millennium to arrive
on earth. Tod did not quite know whether he was glad or sorry. He was
glad for these poor French folk, but he had a feeling that something
fine and heroic and simple had gone out of life, and that the great
adventure was at an end.

They had reached the top of the hill, and the dull misty November sky
was like a grey curtain showing the world nothing but a huge question
mark. What next? Whither? How?

Were they going back down the hill again, back to the old life, or would
they push on to other hills vaguely imagined in the grey distance?

To Tod it was a day of interrogation.

He remembered standing in the garden of the little rococo French villa
that had been hit by a couple of German shells. Through one big hole in
the brickwork the staircase leading to the attics was visible, hanging
derelict in mid-air, its banisters splintered and twisted. A broken lime
tree lay half across the iron gate, resting on one of the brick pillars.
The grass plot was covered with broken tiles and bricks, the flower beds
pulped and trampled. The front door hung by one hinge, a pathetic and
drunken door. But the villa was not dead. An old Frenchman in carpet
slippers was patiently raking up the rubbish and taking it off in a
basket to a far corner of the garden. Madame and her two daughters had
carried out some chairs and a settee and were dusting them with
affectionate care.

Tod was touched.

“They don’t understand these things in England,” he said to himself;
“they never will.”

He went across and spoke to the old Frenchman, who was his host.

“To-morrow, monsieur, I will get some of my men to help you. It was bad
luck, those two shells, after all these years.”

“Ah, Monsieur le Colonel, we are quite happy. Are we not free?”

“I admire your courage, monsieur,” said Tod, “and the courage of madame
and her daughter.”

The Frenchman pointed with his rake at the hole in the wall.

“Bricks, monsieur, only bricks; not the blood of my children. That can
be remedied. We shall get to work in France; we shall clean up all the
rubbish.”

“I wish England had had some of this,” said Tod thoughtfully; “the few
bombs we got were nothing. I suppose that is why you French are so much
finer than our people.”

“But your army, monsieur, is it not superb?”

“There was nothing wrong with our army; I’m thinking of some of those
people at home.”

Tod wandered out alone one afternoon, and passing through a battered and
slimy-streeted old town, broke away into the country that was strangely
still and quiet under the grey November sky. It was a land of little
farms and green meadows shut in by high hedges, and Tod followed the
track of a tank that had cut square gaps in the hedgerows, the bushes
lying flattened like crushed faggots in the ditches. There were shell
holes everywhere, turf and scattered earth where the barrage of the
English guns had advanced across the fields. The farmhouses still seemed
to be deserted, and a great silence reigned over those little secret
meadows.

But Tod was not concerned with the war and its last dramatic phases. He
had come to the edge of the “Afterwards,” an afterwards that was as
sudden and strange as the sea to a man who has marched for months
through a forest. Tod wanted to think. He had been thinking in jerks and
spasms during the war, disconnectedly, rather vaguely, though certain
stark and fundamental facts had emerged from the chaos. He had learnt to
know men. He had learnt that they were utterly unequal. He loved them,
even though he knew that most of them were good-hearted sluggards, born
to be led and managed. He had discovered the bad man, who was often
clever and dangerous, and who responded to nothing but fear and the
voice of a master. Tod had been a master. No man less of a bully than
he, but he had learnt the great lesson of mastery. Character; that was
the one thing that counted. And because of his ideals, his love of men
and his passionate vision of what life should be for them in the future,
he was torn within himself, puzzled, challenged. He had had dreams of a
new leader and a new earth. Socialism—perhaps? But what of the
master-men, the leaders? Was not good staff-work as important in a State
as in an army? Was not civil discipline as necessary as military
discipline? Could a committee of battalion cooks have schemed and
carried through a big offensive? Were not the best talkers often the
worst fighters, and the least dependable of men? Tod had been living
with realities. He had learnt the great lesson, that the very nature of
man has a way of upsetting the most elaborate and beautiful theories.
You can use workmen to build a cathedral, but you cannot use the
workmen’s backs in place of stones. You can set a hundred men running a
race, but the whole hundred will not breast the tape together. Life was
still a race. Penalise the speedy men, and the thing would become a
farce, a disgraceful strolling match for loafers. Nature would put up
her starting pistol, and go in search of a better strain, of new men.
Civilisation’s business was to see that there was a fair start for all,
and no fouling.

In wandering over those wet fields Tod came upon a little orchard close
to a long rambling red-roofed house whose eyes still seemed to be
staring in a blind and frightened way at certain horrors that had been
enacted close to it. The orchard had been a German howitzer position. A
scattering of poplars and willows in the surrounding hedgerows had
screened it well, but chance or some English airmen had spotted those
squat black cannon, and death had come out of the grey sky.

The orchard was all brown with shell holes and scattered earth. Half the
trees had been smashed. One big howitzer lay wallowing in a crater like
some huge black beast dead and swollen. A second had been caught in the
act of making off towards the road, and stood where it had been left,
its snout cocked in the air as though it had been squealing in terror.
The ground round about was littered with wicker cases, fuse boxes,
shells, German helmets, overcoats, gas-masks, a torn and bloody tunic.
Two or three dead Germans still sprawled under the hedge. Tod could
picture the fierce intensity of that battle scene. The sudden plunging
shells, the crash of the explosives, the spurts of dirt and smoke, the
shrinking desperate men drifting away from their smothered guns, the
dead left lying. What a glimpse of the last judgment on the men who had
conceived and created the war!

Tod sat down on a wooden case. He was used to such scenes. This smashed
German battery was a thing of the past, and Tod’s thoughts were in the
future.

He had a packet of Mr. Burgetrode’s letters in his pocket, and he wanted
to read them again at his leisure. Some of them were in answer to the
scrawls he had sent to this big friend, the curt, frank outpourings of a
soldier to a man who understood.

There were many passages in Julian Burgetrode’s letters that spoke as an
answering voice to Tod’s thoughts.

“Am I an idealist? Of course I am an idealist, but I prefer good
concrete for my foundations, to building in a bog of sentimental and
Utopian slush.”

“If you think you are coming back to a new heaven and a new earth, my
son, get rid of that idea at once. We are going forward, but I think
that we shall go forward in a way very different from what most people
imagine. Read French history as it concerns a period like our own. And
don’t think that England has been chastened, as the best of you have
been chastened out there. It hasn’t. The grocer is still stronger than
God.”

“Keep away from the people who are always calling on the State to do
this and to do that. Don’t be befogged by that word ‘State.’ Be ready to
take your coat off and fell your own trees.”

“After all, what is the ‘State’ to which these good people are always
appealing? Such a State is nothing but officialdom. And who is your
official? Just old Mr. Peasgood Nonsuch, a rather dull and phlegmatic
old gentleman who goes back to Surbiton or Ealing each evening, and
grumbles over the paper, with his feet in the fireplace. Mr. Peasgood
Nonsuch, and whole cohorts of Nonsuches are not going to do your
business for you.”

“The country that sits down and waits for someone to hand it its spade,
bring it its dinner, and wipe its unsweated forehead, is going to the
devil.”

“Tod, my boy, you must know that men have to be managed and to be moved.
Most of them are just marionettes. Necessity is the great wire. Cut it
and you will stop the vital current to the whole complex scheme. And an
intoxicated mob of fools will be fighting like starving dogs.”

“We are still creatures of necessity, not democratic demigods. We have
created our scheme of civilisation, and we have got to live with it;
it’s our Sphinx, our Master. Nature is not going to be cheated.”

“For the average man needs fear, the fear of hunger and no fire. The
truth is as old as Adam. Have we suddenly become automatic angels?”

“Tod, my man, don’t sink yourself to the level of the pap-mongers and
visionary journalists. Don’t be quixotically unselfish. Not one man in
ten thousand really cares about the big dream. He’s thinking of his
coal-cellar and the income tax. The grocer will beam you his blessing
and do you behind your back. It’s in the blood.”

“Nature is not going to let us all wallow in a social elysium just yet.
We have got to be up and doing, every man of us. Those who sit under the
apple tree and smoke and wait for Mr. Peasgood Nonsuch to climb up and
throw down the apples won’t get ’em. They don’t deserve to get ’em.”

“Get power. Be big. Keep away from the clever little people. Character
and courage are worth more than cleverness. As you say—the war has
taught us that.”

“Get power. Use it benignantly, wisely, but get power. Why should a man
be afraid to be big? What sort of world would it be if we were all like
a field of cabbages. Refuse to be lopped and dragged down by the people
who haven’t the heart to be big.”

“Is it wrong to say that all the great work of the world has been done
by adventurers, pioneers, students, individuals? The mob of little men
comes trailing along through the gaps cut by the pioneers, and you will
hear the little men shouting: ‘How well we are doing this job!’”

“I’m not talking ‘superman stuff.’ It is that other damned creed that
I’m attacking, the cry of ‘all in the trough together,’ the mob spirit
that lusts to pull down because it has not the grit to climb up.”

“You seem to write at times, Tod, as though you wondered whether I ever
felt afraid of the power I happen to hold. I don’t. I created what I
have created; no other man has a grip of the great machine as I have.
I’m not modest about it, because it is a fact.”

“Why should one be so absurdly sensitive? I’m fair to the men who work
for me; I try to do all I can for them; I try to push up the keen and
the good ones.”

“I can almost imagine the day when the Proletariat will say to God:
‘Look here, you come down out of that. We’re fed up with your swank. We
can run the bally show ourselves.’”

“And supposing God agreed?”

“Take it another way. Supposing a general of a division was seized with
a hypersensitive false modesty. ‘I must really apologise to you men for
having the impertinence to be in authority over some fifteen thousand of
you. I suggest you elect a committee and fight the division. Of course,
if I might tender a little innocent advice—now and again, but believe
me I shall not intrude upon your authority!’”

“No, I’m not going to abdicate. I’m not senile just yet. The man who
knows that he can lead is a coward if he abdicates.”

“I often think there is too much open-mindedness about our young men of
brains. A little more backbone and a little more battle-axe would be
better.”

Tod smiled over these letters of Mr. Burgetrode’s. They were so fresh,
so pungent, so vigorous, and they echoed many of the grim facts that Tod
has discovered during the war. He had learnt how to father a battalion,
that little world of men in miniature.

And then he re-read Julian Burgetrode’s last letter.

“So the Bloody Business is nearly over. It has been the most religious
thing in all history. The German organisation should have won. It was
beaten by something higher and finer than mere organisation. Character,
the good heart of the plain man.

“I want you back with me, and I shall agitate when the time comes. I am
getting a little old. I want you to help me to manage men.”

Tod put those letters away, and began to think of other things. In this
battered orchard under the grey of the November sky, his thoughts went
back to Myonville and Claire—sturdy, grey-eyed, deep-bosomed Claire. He
had carried the little book she had given him, still tied about with a
bit of blue ribbon, a warm memory next to his heart. He unbuttoned the
flap of his breast pocket, drew out the worn, brown Testament, and,
looking at it, thought of her with a tenderness that was as sacred as
the memory of his mother.

That other life was claiming him again. He saw himself going back to
England, to the woman who inspired in him nothing but a profound
distrust. He had no letter from Millie in his pocket. It was three weeks
since he had received one from her, and perhaps the silence between her
letters had been a relief to him. He did not want to remember his wife.

Walking home towards the pale winter sunset, Tod tried to hold fast to
some of Julian Burgetrode’s words.

“Be big. Work is more lasting and more satisfying than love.”

And in Landrecies Tod happened to see a French soldier, home on leave in
Landrecies after more than four years of its occupation, being embraced
by his weeping wife.

“Oh, Jean, mon mari!”

“Damn!” said Tod; “Burgetrode’s wrong.”

 Chapter Thirty-eight

The Armistice was not more than three weeks old when Tod walked into Mr.
Burgetrode’s private room in the Hotel Europa. Mr. Julian Burgetrode had
been the Controller of Something during the latter part of the war, and
his department inhabited the whole of the hotel. A certain notorious
daily paper had said ferocious things about Mr. Burgetrode, referring to
him as the “Autocrat with the Red Tie,” yet nothing had happened. Many
people had discovered that it was impossible to hurt Mr. Burgetrode; he
walked over them with a grim and humorous smile, and his reply was his
silence.

“Hallo, Tod! I didn’t know you were on leave.”

He got up briskly out of his leather chair, his blue eyes a-shine, that
strong, long-fingered hand of his out to be grasped. Julian Burgetrode’s
eyes became quite different when he looked at Tod, for Tod had given him
the great thing that he had lacked.

“I’m just off the train, sir.”

Mr. Burgetrode’s eyes absorbed Tod in a keen stare. This was not the
normal Tod, but a strong man inwardly excited, burning from some blow or
shock of shame, a man whose mouth had been shut tight over words that
could only be uttered once or twice in a lifetime.

“Sit down.”

Mr. Burgetrode turned to his private secretary, but that discreet man, a
student of the Burgetrode climatology, an observer of cyclonic pressures
and weather-cocks, was slipping out of the room.

“I see no one for half an hour, Mr. Jukes.”

“Quite so, sir.”

Tod had not sat down, nor had Mr. Burgetrode. They stood facing each
other.

“Special leave, Tod?”

“Special leave.”

“What’s happened?”

Tod pulled a letter out of his pocket-book.

“Read that.”

Mr. Burgetrode sat down in his chair, but Tod walked up and down,
looking at the books, the maps and diagrams on the walls, restless,
excited, one great emotion locked up within him. Mr. Burgetrode seemed
to take a very long time reading that letter, and Tod kept giving him
quick and impatient glances.

But he had read it at last, and laid it down on his desk.

“Well?”

His blue eyes betrayed nothing.

“Why didn’t she tell me before?” said Tod, with sudden passion. “Good
God, why didn’t she tell me before!”

Mr. Burgetrode’s eyes gave a faint gleam. There was a very human cry in
Tod’s voice, an emotion that was deeper than a disillusioned man’s anger
and humiliation.

“Well, she has told you,” said Mr. Burgetrode quite gently.

Tod faced about.

“Yes, but this has been going on for months. She says so, she admits it.
If only she had been honest with me—months ago.”

“What does it matter, man, if you are free now?”

“It may have made all the difference in the world to me,” said Tod. “I
wanted to be free then, but I couldn’t behave like a cad.”

And then Mr. Burgetrode understood. His face seemed to grow a little
older, a little thinner, the face of a man who found it difficult to
adjust his patience to youth’s sudden passionate surprises.

“So that is what has happened,” he said quietly; “sit down and tell me.”

But Tod remained standing with his hands in his pockets, staring at the
fire. The war had broadened his sympathies and quickened his
understanding. Years ago he would not have thought it possible for this
fierce old man to be hurt, but now he knew that it was otherwise. And
Tod was touched.

“I have never told anyone else.”

“Is that so?”

“I wanted to come straight to you. It’s not because I feel that I owe
you debts that I can never repay—it’s because——”

He hesitated, and gave Mr. Burgetrode a queer, shy, human look, a look
that made something cry out in the older man’s heart.

“Because of what, Tod?”

“Because you matter. Because you have been the only friend in England
that mattered. You have been such a big man to me. It’s like coming to a
father.”

Mr. Burgetrode seemed to sink more deeply into his chair.

“Is that how you feel, Tod?”

“Yes.”

“Tell me about it. Who is she?”

Tod pulled out a pipe, filled it with quick and jerky movements of the
fingers, and looked again at the fire.

“She is French.”

“A Parisienne?”

“Oh, no; I met her in a village—last spring. She teaches in the village
school. Her people are peasants, well-to-do peasants, gentlefolk in all
that counts.”

“How old?”

“Claire—Claire must be twenty-four. The most exquisitely wise thing in
petticoats I ever met, and clean as a bit of white light.”

“So she teaches in the school, Tod.”

“Yes, she loves children,” said Tod; “she’s so much of a child herself.
And she can run a house, and cook, and mend things, and she has read
books that I never heard of. I don’t suppose she had had any new clothes
since 1914, and it suited her. She was a bit of France, a France that
our people don’t understand.”

Tod was looking at the fire, and Mr. Burgetrode was looking at Tod.

“And so it just happened?”

“Yes, it just happened.”

“And you went away and never told her?”

“I never told her, but I think she knew.”

Then Tod sat down by the fire with his back half turned to Mr.
Burgetrode. He was talking out of the deeps of his heart, and the old
man knew it.

“I couldn’t tell her, and yet it was hard, most damnably hard. But then
I had never had what other men have had, some woman to think of, and
perhaps—children. You don’t know how I wanted it at times. I only had
the men, the battalion. I suppose this was bound to happen to me, sooner
or later. It’s rather hard that it may have happened too late.”

“You haven’t written?”

“Never. It wouldn’t have been fair. Don’t you know how there are some
people you can’t trifle with; it would be like cheating a child.”

“I know,” said Mr. Burgetrode; “it would be like me cheating you.”

He got up and came and stood by Tod.

“So you will go back to this village?”

“I must, even if it’s to find that she’s no longer free.”

“And supposing she is free?”

“I shall ask her to marry me. I want her. You don’t know how I want
her.”

Mr. Burgetrode smiled at the fire.

“Tod,” he said; “I’m going to be honest with you. When you first told me
this I was angry. I was ready to say all sorts of clever and cynical
things, but somehow you always kill the cynic in me. Now that first
marriage of yours——”

“I know; it was just sex.”

“And now——?”

“I suppose it is sex; but it is something so different. She’s so
natural, so simple, and yet so clever. There is nothing smart about her
in the modern sense. She’s as natural as the peasant life they live out
there; she’d be natural anywhere, just like a flower or an unspoilt
child. You might call me the biggest fool on earth, but I know that I am
not a fool. To me it’s obvious; instinct—I suppose.”

“You are not a fool, Tod. The worst woman to manage is the greedy woman,
the superficially smart woman. The picture of your little schoolmistress
touches me. But supposing she refused to leave France?”

Tod glanced up sharply.

“I hadn’t thought of that. Besides, it’s all a dream as yet. I’m not
going near her till I’m free.”

“That may mean months.”

“I don’t care. I’m not going there with any excuses. She has eyes that
make any sort of excuse look mean.”

“I’m with you utterly. But to return to that question. Supposing she
refused to leave France? She’s a Frenchwoman.”

“I don’t think she would refuse,” said Tod; “if she cared for me enough
to marry me.”

“Would you turn Frenchman if she did refuse?”

Tod had to face that question, the question that mattered to Mr.
Burgetrode.

“I would,” he admitted, “but I don’t think the need would arise. A man
has to work where his farm lies, and my farm lies over here. Claire
would see that at once, she always looks straight at the heart of
things. But I’m dreaming again.”

He relit his pipe and stood up.

“I have had more than that half-hour, sir; I’m wasting your time.”

“This is my time, Tod. The things that concern you will always concern
me.”

He made a movement towards his desk chair, but a sudden little lift of
Tod’s head, a look of the eyes, half shy, half proud, but wholly
affectionate, held him where he was.

“Do you think I don’t appreciate all this? I’m not just a wild boy who
has got lost in his great romance.”

“I like the way you have come to me, Tod.”

“Of course I came to you. You have been my one friend at home through
all the war. Those letters of yours used to help me, give me a sort of
trumpet call when I was down. You are always telling me to be big. I
have tried to be big, and you have been big to me to-day.”

Mr. Burgetrode gave a queer and quiet laugh.

“Have I? Perhaps because you have taught me to swallow my own medicine.”

“I want to come back and work for you,” said Tod; “I have been thinking
of that—for years. And if Claire came into my life, she would not spoil
your work and mine. She’s big in her simple, wise way. I think you would
get rather fond of Claire. She’s like my mother—behind her man all the
time, not meddling or fussing, but helping with all her heart. There we
are, dreams again!”

“I accept the dream,” said Mr. Burgetrode, with strange softness, “and I
thank you for letting me share it.”

He turned sharply and went to his desk. His emotion translated itself
into action, and found self-expression there. He began to write a
letter. Tod remained by the fire.

Mr. Burgetrode finished his letter.

“Tod.”

Tod turned to him.

“This other affair had better be settled at once. Now—about the man?”

“Goldstein? Oh, he has plenty of money. He’s the sort of beast who would
have made a million during the war. I wish I could be sure that he could
never treat her like a cad. She—was—my wife, after all.”

“Tod,” said the big man, “don’t try and make yourself responsible for
anybody else’s bed. You cannot insure happiness for others. Now, I have
written this letter.”

“Yes.”

“It is to my own firm of solicitors. I have told them that this divorce
must be pushed through at the earliest possible moment. You had better
go to them, because what I have asked them to do will be done. There
should be no difficulty. She has gone off with the man and there will be
no defence.”

He handed Tod the letter.

“Now, one thing more—money.”

“I don’t want any money,” said Tod; “I have saved a little, and there
will be my gratuity. Besides——”

“You couldn’t take it? That’s true; not now, and for such a purpose.”

And then he smiled at Tod.

“Has Claire a dot?”

“I haven’t asked her. And we are still dreaming.”

“Perhaps not. I shall give Claire her dot. Now, what about to-morrow,
Tod? Where are you staying?”

“Nowhere—yet.”

“Go to the Melton. It is close to my club; and take my card. And
to-morrow—what about a long tramp in the country? I want to talk, and I
want you to talk.”

“What about?” asked Tod, with a smile.

“Well, if you can forget Claire for a moment, about what the war has
taught you.”

“You will have to include yourself in that, sir,” said Tod.

 Chapter Thirty-nine

For one day at least Mr. Julian Burgetrode ceased to control anything.
He put on the oldest clothes he could find, a shabby brown Norfolk
jacket, and a pair of old grey knickers and grey stockings. The athlete
in him showed when he put on such clothes, and he looked the long,
gaunt, iron-muscled Viking that he was, a man of many adventures and of
many victories. And yesterday he had won a victory over himself.

Tod came in from his hotel to breakfast with him, fresh-faced,
clean-eyed, carrying himself with a quiet yet boyish dignity. He did not
appear to notice Mr. Burgetrode’s clothes, for with Mr. Burgetrode
clothes did not matter. He would have dominated and effaced a collection
of rags, for the older his clothes were, the better he looked in them.

But he noticed Tod’s.

“Hallo, a new tunic and nice pink breeches!”

“They were waiting for me in town.”

Mr. Burgetrode’s eyes approved of Tod, and approved of him with fierce
affection. He was the finest type that England has produced,
big-hearted, shrewd, yet simple; very grim when thwarted, yet smiling
even when grim; big in the chest, with a fairish and well-squared head;
not too bulky like some of the Germans and Americans; a powerful, quick,
alert figure, never jerky or self-conscious in speech or movement. And
utterly frank; a man who could take blows and keep his temper.

“Porridge?” said Mr. Burgetrode.

“I feel like porridge. What a gorgeous day we’ve got!”

Mr. Burgetrode glanced at the ribbons on Tod’s tunic, the ribbons of the
Military Cross, the M.M. and the Croix de Guerre. He glanced too, at
Tod’s stiff left arm, and the crown and star on his sleeve. And Mr.
Burgetrode was proud, quite absurdly proud, prouder than Tod ever
guessed.

“And I daresay the clever little people in London would think him a very
ordinary sort of man,” he reflected, “so long as there was nothing but
talk. But let a mob get loose, and they would be chattering like
frightened apes behind this big boy’s back. Character—character’s
everything.”

“Where are we going to-day?”

Tod had left it to Mr. Burgetrode.

“What about your boots, Tod? I shall walk you twenty miles.”

“And we attack at the end of it?”

“My idea it to capture Leith Hill. The car drops us at a little place
called East Horsley. We find our way over the downs into Shere, strike
across to Abinger Hatch, lunch there, go on to Leith Hill, on to Ockley,
and then somewhere along the line of the old Staine Street back to
Dorking. The car picks us up there.”

“You won’t be jumping fences to-day, sir?” Tod asked.

“I believe I could beat you at it, my son. Those nice cord
riding-breeches would be something of a handicap!”

December had produced its jewel of a day, a softly clouded amethyst,
with rents of blue sky and splashes of sunlight. The hills were a grey
blue, the valleys full of violet shadow. The day had the soft eyes of a
woman, very gentle and very calm.

Here and there the bracken still had a touch of gold in its brownness,
and so had the fallen leaves. They were crisp to the feet under the
beech trees. The red-throated old Scots firs had a blue bloom on their
tops like the bloom on a grape. Nothing could have been more solemn than
the massed blackness of the spruces.

Old Burgetrode walked with his head up, keen as a boy, smelling the
smell of the woods and finding it good. The fall of the leaf always
stimulated him; there was nothing melancholy about it to a man who was
destined to die in harness.

Tod had been telling him about the last battles, and how he had been
thrilled and touched by all that he had seen in those redeemed French
towns and villages.

“It was worth all those years of slogging, to win back the poor, shabby
old places—with the poor, shabby, plucky people. It made me feel good.
It’s a thing I shall never forget.”

“The most epic thing that ever happened on earth,” said Mr.
Burgetrode—“that and the return of the Serbs. But I’m going to
catechise you, Tod.”

“Right. What in?”

“How did you run your battalion?”

Tod gave a short laugh.

“Towards the end it seemed to run itself. I never had any bother.”

“A complete and self-regulating organism, every cell functioning
automatically? Not quite that, I think! I’m a vitalist, and I always
look for the vitaliser. You were the central stimulus, what?”

“I got things done,” said Tod.

“Exactly. That’s a very simple definition of a vitaliser. And the men?”

“They were good chaps.”

“How did you manage them?”

“If I found a man running crooked I had him up alone and talked to him
absolutely frankly as man to man. I tried to be the father to him; I
gave him that chance.”

“And if he remained bad after that heart to heart talk?”

“I broke him,” said Tod simply; “that was the only thing to do.”

Mr. Burgetrode walked on, head in air, smiling in the deeps of his eyes.

“That is what you are going to do with me—manage men.”

“It’s one of the most interesting games in the world,” said Tod. “I’m
fond of men.”

“You won’t find is so easy in civil life.”

“I don’t suppose I shall.”

“You’ll have no machine behind you save the machine that some of our
visionaries want to smash. All the discipline will have to come out of
yourself. There will be days when you will be tempted to use ferocious
blasphemy, when you feel you could smite some fool on the mouth. If you
try to discipline some blackguard you may find a thousand men on strike.
The solidarity of labour, which may be just organised stupidity.”

“There is a lot of common sense in the average man,” said Tod.

“Yes, when he uses it and doesn’t let himself be tricked by being
tempted with a carrot. Socialism is the carrot just at present—not the
ordered and scientific Socialism that you and I might try to evolve—but
a sort of beautiful dream in which you do half the work you used to do
and get twice the money, and you begin the dream by confiscating other
people’s superfluous possessions. For Socialism, extreme Socialism, to
work effectually, ordinary men would have to be either angels or slaves.
Can’t you see the discipline that would be needed? An army might have
arrived at that state of discipline, a mass of civilians—well, I’ll say
never.”

“You are an individualist?” said Tod.

Mr. Burgetrode laughed.

“Haven’t you glimpsed the real solution—what has happened and will
happen every time? Supposing I accept Socialism, the scheme is not
complete. Individualism will ride on the patient Socialist ass. The
socialised many will be led and managed by the individualised few,
enlightened and sympathetic individuals, no doubt. It is the most
obvious thing that ever there was.”

“I suppose it is. But have the workers ever had their fair share?”

“They haven’t. And in a very short time they will be wanting more than
their fair share. I’m not thinking in money, but in products, the sweat
we give and the sweat we live on—other men’s sweat. We live on other
men’s sweat, mental or manual; that ought to be obvious—even to your
common-sense man, but sometimes it isn’t. John Brown is not going to
sweat for you. Charge him more for his sweat and he’ll retaliate. In the
end the thing becomes ridiculous; there is a row in the tap-room, and
some big person has to restore order. Besides, all this is deep down in
the bowels of Nature. Some people say that we have superseded Nature,
taken over her job. Bosh! Don’t you believe it.”

They were climbing through the woods to Leith Hill, and the
brown-coloured tower showed against a patch of soft grey-cloud.

“Get up high, get a view,” said Julian Burgetrode; “leave your heart
down below to learn, but push your head up to the stars to think.”

They sat down on the great grassy mound and looked south over the trough
of the Weald. The Downs showed in the far south, and even Chanctonbury
Ring was visible, like a patch of smoke from some giant smithy.

“It makes me think of Cassel,” said Tod when he had gazed a long while
in silence.

“Why did they make you a lieutenant-colonel, Tod?” Burgetrode asked him
suddenly.

“Don’t know, I’m sure,” said Tod; “I suppose they knew I was keen.”

“Exactly. Do you know what your trouble is going to be?”

“What?”

“Managing a unit in which half the men think they ought to be
lieutenant-colonels, and want to know why they’re not. The answer’s so
simple, but we seem to be afraid of hurting the working man’s feelings.
He’s so sensitive, so susceptible, and if you offend his dignity he
throws down his tools.”

“I prefer the working man to the rich Jew,” Tod said.

“Tod, my friend, have I ever said that I loved the Jew type? I should
think the Assyrians were the most beastly people who ever lived. I have
a sort of silly fondness for the plain Englishman; but are you going to
play the game by him if you are afraid to stand up and tell him the
truth? People don’t like being told the truth, and the plain man is in a
very nasty temper.”

“What particular truth?”

“That the plain man has got to work damned hard, and that then some of
his dream may come true.”

“Yes,” said Tod.

“That all men are not equal; that all men can’t be colonels, not only
because they are not fit to be colonels, but because a world of colonels
wouldn’t work.”

“But do they want to be colonels?” Tod laughed.

“Ask the women what they want. What has the great cry been? ‘We want a
bigger share in running things.’ Ambition! Quite right. And there you
have Nature’s old primitive cry. These fierce and discontented young men
in Glasgow and Manchester and Sheffield and London—what do they want?
Get to the heart of it. Power! They want to run their own people. Let
them. But when they start the poor ignorant mass like a lot of Gadarene
swine straight for the precipice—what then?”

A sudden grimness came over Tod’s face.

“We had something like that once or twice over there.”

“And you had to stop it.”

“Yes. I was only a second lieutenant then.”

“And what did you do?”

“Used my revolver. It was beastly—the most damnable thing I have ever
had to do. But it stopped it.”

“Quite so. And if the big men fail at a crisis, Nature steps in. Adam,
who has been drunk with words, is scourged back to work. And Nature
shows no mercy. She sets the slave to clean up the mess he has made.”

Mr. Burgetrode paused suddenly and looked at Tod. And then he laughed.

“Beyond the Downs lies the sea, and across the sea is a bit of old
Picardy, where the peasants are at work in the fields and children are
sitting in the school.”

“Sorry, Sir,” said Tod, “I couldn’t help it.”

 Chapter Forty

Tod spent a week of his leave with Julian Burgetrode and then went to
Shaston. The town had a few pleasant memories for him, memories of Tom
Lavender, the Mossups, and of poor Kitty who was dead. He put up at the
Brunswick, a quiet hotel on the front, and spent his first evening with
Tom Lavender.

Old Lavender was the same tranquil soul, but he had aged, and his
clothes looked shabby, and the cuffs of his shirt were frayed. Tod
noticed these rather pathetic details, also a certain waxy whiteness of
his old friend’s face. Tod had seen that look in France—on the faces of
old Frenchmen who had not enough to eat.

“I can’t walk as I did,” Tom told him; “if I can get to Ashurst Wood I’m
satisfied. After all, you haven’t to go far to find beauty and a few
birds.”

“The beauty is inside you,” Tod said to himself, “and you ought to have
birds singing round your deathbed.”

But he contrived to sneak a secret interview with Mrs. Jelf, a Mrs. Jelf
who looked thinner and sharper about the face.

“Mr. Lavender isn’t well,” said Tod.

The old woman gave him an almost fierce look.

“If I could help it I would,” she said; “I can manage on potatoes and
porridge and tea, and a little bread and margarine. He’s proud—terribly
proud.”

Tod was shocked.

“Do you mean to say——”

Mrs. Jelf held his sleeve between a sharp forefinger and thumb. She was
on the verge of senility, but loyal to the last.

“He thinks I have meat,” she said with a cunning air, putting her face
close to Tod’s; “he thinks I have meat every day! Poor dear! I tell him
lies; I pretend.”

“So—he—has all the meat, Mrs. Jelf?”

“It isn’t much.”

Tod sat down and pulled out his pocket-book.

“He’s never got to know of this. You’ll promise?”

“He’ll never know,” said Mrs. Jelf; “I pretend I’m such a manager. He’s
just like one of his birds, you know.”

Tod handed her two five-pound notes.

“Money’s for use. I’ll send you something every now and again. Doesn’t
the shop pay?”

“It never did pay very much,” said Mrs. Jelf, “but then money never did
matter to him; he’s above money.”

And then she grew fierce again.

“And to see those sluts all dressed up and showing off their clothes on
the seafront! And the money some of the greedy beasts have been making!
It near drives me mad sometimes, and him—not enough to eat. I could go
out and pull the clothes off their backs, I could.”

“He shan’t go hungry in the future,” said Tod.

“You always was a sort of son to him,” said Mrs. Jelf, beginning to
weep.

It amused Tod to stroll round Shaston and look into the shops he had
known and register a second impression of some of the people who had had
the handling of his youth. He walked into Mr. Snape’s shop and bought a
dozen new razor blades. “Mr.-Any-think-Else” had disappeared, but Snape
