was there more pink and polite than ever.

He did not recognise Tod.

“Be-ootiful weather for the time of year, sir.”

“Splendid,” said Tod, and he could not resist pulling Snape’s leg. “Do
you happen to keep Bartrup’s Consumption Cure?”

The chemist looked a little puzzled.

“We do stock it. Three-and-ninepence the bottle, sir.”

“Is it any good?”

Snape pursed up his lips. He did not think it wise to advertise the
stuff to an educated customer.

“Well, sir, of course, as you know, phthisis is a bacterial infection.
Bartrups contend that their mixture contains a specific remedy, some
particular drug——”

“It used to be just ipecac, and sugar,” said Tod, “and was dear at half
a crown. Three-and-nine, now, is it?”

Mr. Snape stared at him.

“Yes, three-and-nine. War price, sir.”

“The war has been very useful to a lot of people,” said Tod; “no, I
don’t want that rubbish. Good morning.”

He left Mr. Snape still staring, and raking his mental kitchen-midden
for some lost memory.

“Seem to have seen him before—somewhere. Fancy his asking for
Bartrup’s. Sidey sort of man. I don’t like these army chaps.”

Tod walked down Ponsonby Place and rediscovered the establishment of
Flowerdew & Rook. It had been enlarged, and had refined itself into a
very superior concern, showing you in one window a room furnished in the
Jacobean style, and another in which Sheraton was reproduced with
meticulous accuracy in all its details. The place was full of antiques,
beautiful old pieces of furniture, china, glass, Sheffield plate, and
some big trays full of exquisite enamels. A grey four-seater touring car
was standing outside the shop, and just as Tod strolled by, Rook came
out and entered the car.

Rook happened to glance at Tod, and recognised him. The two men stared
back at each other for a moment, but it was Rook whose eyes flinched and
shrank away.

“The Majestic,” he said to the chauffeur, “and hurry up, I’m late.”

Tod walked on.

“Rook has made money,” was Tod’s reflection, “and poor old Tom Lavender
hasn’t enough to eat.”

Tod was in search of the Mossup family, and the family proved elusive.
The old white cottage with its green porch and green front door had
passed into other hands. “We’ve bin here two years,” the old woman who
opened the door told him; “yes, Mossup was the name. A delikit chap that
kept a shop and was bankrupt. You might try Friday Street down by the
gas works.”

Tod tried Friday Street, appealing to a good-natured looking soul who
was scrubbing her steps.

“Mossup? The Mossups live in Love Lane. First on the right. It’s number
six, I think.”

Tod turned into Love Lane, a little black backwater of a street with the
great red gasometers towering over it, and a high wall blinding the
lower windows. No. 6 was one of about thirty tightly compressed houses
with a door and a window on the ground floor and two windows above. The
door of No. 6 was grained in imitation of some imaginary wood, probably
oak. The doorstep was clean, and the black door-knocker had just been
repainted.

Tod knocked.

A little girl of five opened the door. She had pale eyes, a white face,
and a scar on her neck where glands had been removed.

“Does Mr. Arthur Mossup live here?”

The child looked at Tod with solemn wonder.

“Yes, that’s Father. Father’s in the yard, sawing wood.”

“May I come in? I’m a friend of your father’s.”

“Yes, come in,” said the child.

Tod stepped straight into the front parlour, and Miss Mossup closed the
door. Tod heard sounds of activity coming from the back of the house,
the rasping of a saw, and then the sound of a man coughing.

“Supposing I go through?” he said to the child.

He was smiling, and when Tod smiled at children they smiled back at him.

“Yes, you may. I’ll take you to Daddy.”

She took hold of Tod’s hand, and her little fingers felt cold.

The yard at the back of the house was about twelve feet long and eight
feet broad—a respectable tank. Someone had built a small shed in one
corner, using old egg-boxes, and squares of tin cut from biscuit tins
for the roof. In the other corner lay a pile of old wood, bits of old
fence poles and posts. In the centre of the yard a man in shabby black
trousers and an old white sweater was sawing at a length of wood, using
a packing case with notches cut in it as a sawing-tool. The back of the
man’s neck looked thin and yellow. He was wearing carpet slippers, and
he had not shaved.

“Hallo, old man!” said Tod.

Mossup turned on him, an aged, shrewish, thin-haired Mossup, with the
eyes of a man who slept badly.

“Bless my soul!”

He flushed up in his old shy way, and reached for Tod’s hand.

“Heard you were some sort of big pot, now. How did you find out——?”

“By asking. And Miss Mossup——”

The child was watching Tod.

“Run away, Nan,” said her father, with sudden irritability, “we’re
busy.”

The child went slowly into the house, and Tod felt uncomfortable, cooped
up in this tank with a man who was so obviously sick in soul and in
body.

“Mind if I go on working, old man?” said Mossup.

“Not a bit. I’ll light a pipe. Do you smoke, Arthur? You didn’t use to.”

“Can’t afford it.”

Mossup turned to his sawing-stool. The saw was working badly; it wanted
sharpening and setting, but the man in the dirty white sweater struggled
with it with a sort of evil and angry patience. He got out of breath,
and the veins grew big on his forehead.

Tod felt still more uncomfortable.

“Good exercise, that,” he said.

“Saves a little money,” panted the sawyer. “I got this lot cheap, and
it’s cheaper than coal.”

He paused to rest.

“Drat this saw!”

“You’ve been ill, old man,” said Tod abruptly, forcing his way to close
quarters.

“Ill? I’m always ill. When the business broke——”

He resumed his sawing.

“I went back to Horrox’s. No good for the army. Then I tried
munitioning. Got bronchitis. Now I’ve always got bronchitis or
something. No good for work. Oh, damn this saw!”

His chest was heaving, his lower lip thrust out. Then he began to cough.

“Let me have a go,” said Tod.

He almost pushed Mossup aside. The man in the sweater stood and coughed
and panted.

“Must do something. Olive manages the millinery in a new shop in King
William Street. She works like a horse. Food’s so dear; it’s damnable.”

He caught hold of Tod.

“Ninepence for a loaf, Western, ninepence! And if I want a new pair of
boots——”

He began coughing again, and the cough smothered his anger. Tod went on
sawing, bending his head over it, the man in him shocked and hurt. Poor,
kindly, simple old Mossup! He had been such a good chap, and in his
place Tod saw this querulous, sick, soured failure, this pathetic thing
that could not bear to be watched by its own child.

“You want a holiday, old man,” he said softly.

“Holiday!”

There was a snarl in Mossup’s voice.

“This is a holiday. This rotten body of mine isn’t fit for anything
else. What holiday does Olive get? I’m just a household drudge, an old
woman; that’s all I’m fit for.”

“She doesn’t think so,” said Tod.

Mossup gulped something like a sob, and his voice became more human even
in its querulousness.

“It’s so humiliating, old man. If you only knew how I——”

He sat down suddenly on an old sugar box.

“She’s an angel, an absolute angel. And I’m—I’m a cantankerous,
snarling beast. I always feel sore in the head; I make her miserable.
It’s sordid; it’s horrible. I see it all, and yet—somehow—I can’t help
it.”

Tod could not bear it any longer. He left the saw standing in the notch
and turned to his old friend.

“Look here, old chap——”

“I never paid you that ten pounds, you know,” said Mossup quickly; “I
meant to.”

Tod’s eyes were almost angry.

“I gave it you,” he said. “Now, look here, old chap, are you seeing a
doctor?”

“What good’s a doctor to me? It’s my self-respect that’s ill. He can’t
physic that, can he?”

“But I know somebody who can.”

And then Mossup showed the hysterical and cantankerous temper of a weak
man whom life had humiliated.

“Yourself, I suppose?”

“Perhaps.”

“That’s it, that’s just it. You’ve been a success, Western. Your people
didn’t fit you out with a rotten body, and now you come swanking around
here making me look like a miserable fool before my wife. I won’t have
it! I tell you——”

“Old man, shut up.”

It was the soldier who spoke in Tod, the man who had the instinct of
authority.

“You are not going to talk like that, Arthur. Do you understand?”

Mossup looked at him vacantly. He thought that Tod was angry, and
anger—other people’s anger—frightened him.

“I didn’t mean——”

“You’re ill, old man; that’s what’s the matter. You want your strength
back, and then you want work.”

“No one wants me,” began the derelict.

“Shut up! I can get you a job in half an hour, directly you are fit for
it, and a good job. I happen to have been lucky. Now, I want you and
Olive and Nan to have dinner with me to-night—”

“But I haven’t got any——”

“Go in and shave,” said Tod, “or by George, old man, I’ll do it for
you.”

And Mossup went. A clean chin was the beginning of his recovery.

Tod hired a horse one morning and rode over to Pentworth. He stabled the
horse at The George and walked by way of Spaniards Row to the little
cemetery beyond the railway station. No. 13 was the same queer old
lovable lunatic of a house, but the shop had become a saddler’s, and
someone had cut down all the sooty laurels and aucubas where Tod’s great
snake had lived. Pentworth smelt vividly of his childhood. The northern
end of Spaniards Row was littered with the leaves of the great chestnuts
and elms, and it had the same damp perfume. Tod could picture the lights
beginning to blink out in the little town when the dusk came down like
fog from the sea.

Tod found the grass and weeds long and rank over the grave of William
and Nancy Western. He had written to a local florist and gardener about
the grave and paid the man a yearly fee for the keeping of it. Tod was
hurt and angry, and he called at the florist’s shop on his way back to
The George.

The proprietor was a surly, off-hand, take-it-or-leave-it sort of
person.

“Can’t get the labour,” he said. “We’ve had something better to do than
bother about graves.”

“Is that so?” said Tod. “We’ve been rather busy, too, out there. But you
took the money, didn’t you?”

The man blustered.

“What money?”

“I paid you by cheques,” said Tod, “and I happened to notice that the
cheques went through the bank. That grave hasn’t been touched.”

“You can have the money and you can get out of my shop,” said the man.
“Because you’re a colonel or something you needn’t think——”

Tod looked at him very steadily.

“I’m not going to make a row with you about that grave, but I shall call
on the rector and tell him to expect a cheque from you and that he can
use it as he pleases. Will you have it that way, or shall I put the
matter into the hands of my lawyers? I call it fraud.”

And quite suddenly the man became polite.

“You needn’t be so rough about it, sir. I’ve been worried to death what
with labour and prices. I’ll look up the amount.”

“Right,” said Tod; “don’t forget. And I think you will send a man down
to cut that grass.”

“To-morrow if possible.”

“To-morrow? I think I may be in Pentworth again to-morrow. Good
morning.”

Tod had an early lunch at The George and rode back to Shaston, touching
at Burnt Farm on the way. He found Uncle Remus in bed—the old man had
been hemiplegic for a year—and the carter’s wife looked after the house
for him, her husband managing the farm. The woman, a very decent and
quiet little body, was glad of a talk to Tod.

“He’s that trying and cantankerous,” she said, “that me and my man
wouldn’t stay here if we hadn’t worked for him all these years. He’s
always thinking we are after his money.”

Uncle Remus had his iron bedstead by the window where he could look out
on the farm. There was no fire in the room and no carpet on the floor,
and the old man’s grey head and bluish face contrasted with the red
coverlet on the bed.

Uncle Remus was senile, and yet the one great inspiration of his life
still dominated him. He was pleased to see Tod, and he crowed at him
like a baby.

“What’s them things on your sleeve, Nephew?”

Tod had drawn a chair beside the bed. Uncle Remus had withdrawn one claw
of a hand from under the clothes, and was fingering Tod’s sleeve.

“That means I’m a colonel, Uncle.”

“A colonel!” And Uncle Remus opened his pale eyes very wide. “Ain’t we
grown? And you killed plenty of Germans?”

“A few.”

“What’s them bits of ribbons?”

He passed a wavering forefinger across Tod’s chest, showing a child’s
desire to touch and finger things.

“Medals I have won, Uncle.”

“My!” said Uncle Remus. “You must have killed lots of Germans! Little
Tod Western grown up to be a colonel! I’m proud of the boy.”

And then he grew cunning and sly, leaning over and leering at Tod and
taking him by the sleeve.

“I’ve left you all my money, every bit of it.”

“It’s very good of you, Uncle Remus.”

“Come here; I don’t want to speak loud. I’ve saved four thousand pounds.
You’ll get it.”

He swung back in the bed and sat looking at Tod with an air of sly
exultation.

“What d’you think o’ that?”

“I think I’d like you to have a fire in your room, Uncle.”

“Fire! What do I want with a fire? Four thousand pounds! Worth more than
a row of ribbons, my boy, though I won’t say I ain’t proud of ’em. I’m a
warm man; I don’t want no fire.”

He bent forward again, tapped Tod on the knee, and pointed to the floor.

“She thinks she’s going to get some of it,” he said, with an attempt at
a wink.

“Who? Mrs. Dodds?”

“She comes purring round me like a cat. I let her think I’ve left her a
hundred pounds.”

“And haven’t you?”

“Not a farthing!”

And Uncle Remus crowed and rocked in the bed as though he had
perpetrated the great jest of his life.

“Not a farthing! I’ll be even with her for trying to fool me. Uncle
Remus wasn’t easy to fool, was he, my boy?”

“No,” said Tod, thinking that if the old man’s money came to him, Mrs.
Dodds should have her hundred pounds, and that Tom Lavender and poor old
Mossup would not be the poorer.

He said good-bye to Uncle Remus, promising to come over and see him
again, and made westwards into the red glow of a winter sunset. From the
high ground above Redling he could see the sea, the sea that lay between
him and France. Tod’s thoughts were in old Picardy, in that village not
far from St. Valery at the mouth of the Somme, that village where Claire
Vignard taught in the school.

A great restlessness came upon him, a sense of something precious
slipping out of his life while he remained silent, waiting. Matters were
going forward; Julian Burgetrode’s lawyers asserted that the case was
beautifully simple—a mere bit of legal machinery. And yet——

“I wonder if I might write to her?” said Tod.

A voice within him answered, “No.”

Perhaps some Frenchman had returned to Myonville, and Claire was happy
and forgetting? Tod set his horse at a canter, his blue eyes staring
straight into the sunset.

“I wonder?” he said to himself.

And then:

“Why shouldn’t I write to Josephine? I’ll tell her not to breathe a word
of it to Claire.”

He wrote to Josephine Lorellard that very night.

Four days later he was back with his division in Belgium, schooling
himself to face the months ahead of him and the coming of his liberty.

In March Tod was demobilised. Julian Burgetrode met him on Folkestone
pier.

“You will be free in two months or so,” he said to Tod.

Tod said nothing.

 Chapter Forty-one

One June morning Tod walked out of his hotel in Abbeville and took the
train for Eu. He had reached Abbeville the evening before, and had spent
an hour wandering round the familiar old town that had been so savagely
bombed during his month at Myonville. He was very much the young English
civilian, brown lounge suit, brown boots, fawn-coloured velour hat,
wash-leather gloves. His old military cane was the only soldierly thing
left to him, save, of course, all that soldierliness that he carried in
his heart and head. It was not the Tod Western of Shaston thrown back
into civilian clothes, but a man who had the _bel air_ and the eyes of a
master.

Tod sat in the train and watched the country go by. He was excited, a
little afraid, the boy lover utterly absorbed in the life of the moment.
No one expected him in Myonville, nor did he know what he would find
there, how they would welcome him, whether the day of days would not end
in some tragic anticlimax.

“Claire—why Claire married Jules Loubet six months ago.”

Tod tried to keep such words as these constantly in his ears.

He tested his French by chatting to an old farmer opposite him.

“Monsieur was in the war.”

Yes, Tod was in the war. He found his French flowing freely, but he kept
an eye on the landscape, and watched for the green valley and the cliff
of trees that meant Myonville and romance.

The great flat fields, the poplars, the straight roads, the little
white-walled houses stirred him most profoundly. He was back in France,
France that was so unforgettable, so full of poignant memories; and then
he sighted that old sugar factory among the beeches on the road to
Abbeville. Myonville was less than a mile away, and Tod began to fidget.

The train ran into Myonville through fields of wheat, beetroot and
crimson clover. There was the familiar level-crossing, with the red
brick Café de la Gare, and its skittle alley in the garden. The church
spire seemed to hold up a beckoning finger above the elms and beeches.

The train stopped. Tod raised his hat to the old Frenchman and got out.
Myonville had always been a quiet station, and it was so quiet on that
June morning that Tod felt the one notorious person on earth, a stranger
to be stared at. The station-master took the tickets, and Tod wished him
good morning. He had once had a slight altercation with the
station-master.

“Bonjour, monsieur.”

He gave Tod a surprised look and then re-examined Tod’s ticket.

“You don’t remember me,” said Tod.

“No, monsieur. The English are so much alike.”

It was an unpromising prelude.

Tod walked up the dusty road from the station to the little Place de
l’Eglise. Myonville looked exactly the same, only there were no English
soldiers in it. Women were chatting under the shade of the limes. Tod
saw the old curé toddling across the cobbles to the church, the
identical, rather snuffy and grey-chinned old gentleman. The same brown
dog was lying in the same doorway. The fat woman at the baker’s shop had
her hair in curling pins, and was arguing as usual, her hands on her
hips. From the doorway of the Papillon d’Or the young demoiselle who
used much “Fleur de Trèfle” and was Parisian in her atmosphere, looked
out questioningly at Tod with eyes that were black and big and alluring.
She had troubled the hearts of one or two of Tod’s subalterns. Tod
raised his hat to her and blushed.

“Tiens, it is Monsieur le Colonel!”

He passed on and found himself in that quiet and familiar street running
softly westwards in the sunlight towards the high shade of an avenue of
beeches. And suddenly Tod was afraid, for so much hung on the happenings
of the next few minutes. All the dreams of a year were walking with him
down that sunlit village street, and for a moment Tod’s courage turned
to water. Supposing she had left the place, or was married, or his
coming should prove of no meaning to Claire Vignard?

The street curved slightly, so that the Café de la Paix and the
schoolhouse next to it were hidden from Tod until he reached the big
pond where the peasants watered their horses. He noticed that there were
no children to be seen. It was the hour for them to be at school.
Walking very fast Tod rounded the curve, and saw the schoolhouse with
its iron railings and queer little belfry, and next to it the house of
the Lorellards all brown in the sunshine, just as he had seen it when he
had walked for the first time to his billet.

Both the sidewalk and the roadway were deserted, save for a figure in
light blue standing at its ease outside the Café de la Paix. This French
soldier seemed to belong there. Bareheaded, a pipe in his mouth, and his
hands in his pockets, he had the air of a man in possession, a man
standing on his own doorstep and surveying the world.

“Damn that chap!” said Tod.

He was absurdly interested in the figure of the Frenchman, absurdly
afraid of it. Did the man belong to that particular house, or was he a
mere chance loafer? He was standing squarely in front of the café
doorway, and he stared hard at Tod as the Englishman came up, nor did he
offer to move.

It was obvious that the Frenchman did belong to the house of the
Lorellards, and his thick-set blue figure closed the doorway.

“Bonjour, monsieur.”

Tod came to a standstill. The Frenchman looked surprised. He was young
and rather sallow, the laconic and inexpansive type of peasant.

He took his pipe out of his mouth, but he did not move from the
doorstep.

Tod tried a sniper’s venture.

“It is Monsieur André Lorellard—perhaps?”

The Frenchman looked still more surprised.

“That is so, monsieur.”

And Tod laughed.

“We never met, Monsieur André. Your mother and your father were so very
kind to me a year ago. You may have heard them speak of me, Colonel Tod
Western.”

The French boy’s impassive face changed instantly. He stood aside; he
put out a hand, and his rather hard blue eyes became laughing and
friendly.

“Monsieur—but what a surprise! I have heard of you so often.”

Tod gave André’s hand an almost savage grip.

“We were friends before we met. You are on leave?”

“I am home to be married, monsieur.”

“Felicitations,” said Tod, seriously scared.

“Perhaps you may have met my fiancée, monsieur?”

“It is possible.”

“Mademoiselle Yvonne Frangonard, of the Petite Folie Farm.”

Tod could have kissed Master André. That gentleman’s figure had become
one of cloudless blue.

“Yes, I think I remember Mademoiselle Frangonard. Are your parents at
home, André? I have come to see them.”

André smiled queerly at Tod. Then he went into the café and shouted
excitedly.

“Josephine! Josephine! Un ami!”

He turned again and laughed in a brotherly way at Tod.

“Wait, monsieur. Such a surprise for them all! They will be so
delighted!”

Josephine came into the long room, polishing a glass with a cloth. She
looked at André and then at Tod, seemed lost for a moment, and then
exclaimed as only a Frenchwoman can exclaim:

“Monsieur Tod!”

She came forward and kissed him. André had disappeared.

Tod sat on one of the deal tables, left foot swinging, smiling shyly at
Josephine.

“Yes, I have come back. I said I might come back, you know. And how are
Monsieur and Madame?”

“Very well, monsieur,” said Josephine, sitting on the table opposite
him.

“And you?”

She laughed.

“It is for monsieur to say.”

“Always charming—and my friend?”

“Perhaps.”

Josephine was waiting the question that mattered, the question that was
burning there in Tod’s eyes.

“And Claire?”

Josephine picked up her apron and examined a frayed edge.

“She is—at the school, monsieur.”

“And well?”

“And well.”

“And?”

“She is still Claire Vignard,” said Josephine, dropping her apron and
smoothing it with her hands.

Tod got off the table with a flushed and intense face.

“Josephine, I’m free; I’m free. Do you understand?”

She raised her dark eyes to his.

“Monsieur has——?”

“It was not my fault,” he said quickly. “I waited till it was all over
before coming here. I have all the necessary papers to prove——”

“Do you think I do not believe you?” she asked, almost sharply; “of
course I believe you. Monsieur does not tell lies.”

She glanced over his shoulder at the clock.

“Claire will be here in ten minutes.”

Tod was also looking at the clock.

“Do you think that I have anything of a chance, Josephine?”

The French girl smiled.

“It is a good thing, monsieur, that Claire is not religieuse. It will
not matter.”

Tod pondered those words, and Josephine slipped from off her table.

“I will go to the schoolhouse and meet Claire,” she said, “if monsieur
will wait in the kitchen.”

“Could I see her——?”

“No one will come into the kitchen for an hour. I will tell her there is
a friend—just a friend.”

Tod went and kissed Josephine.

“I can hardly believe it all,” he said.

He sat in the Lorellards’ kitchen, in the place that had always been
his, between the stove and the window. Claire’s wooden workbox stood as
usual on the window-sill, and Tod lifted the lid and looked at all its
neatness with the eyes of a lover. He remembered the way she had pricked
her finger on the morning when he told her that the battalion was
leaving Myonville. Through the window he could see the neat red bricks
of the yard, a washing tub tilted to dry against the wall of the
wash-house, the green tops of the orchard trees.

Suddenly Tod stood up. She had come in by the orchard gate and was
crossing the yard, the Claire of a year ago, in her daintily shabby
black dress, and her hair like a pale brown wreath touched with
sunlight. It was the same calm, frank-faced Claire, with her mouth and
her grey eyes, more serious, perhaps a little sad. She looked quite
innocent and unsuspecting, just a woman who had her work to do, a woman
who had come to speak with some casual friend.

A pair of clean shoes were standing against the wall. Claire picked them
up, entered the kitchen, and saw Tod.

She dropped the shoes on a chair, and stood looking at him with round
grey eyes.

“Monsieur—you!”

And Tod, the man, did not move, for he saw something that his dream had
never shown him. Claire was afraid.

 Chapter Forty-two

She sat down suddenly at the table, her elbows resting upon it, her
little fists clenched under her chin. Her eyes looked straight at Tod.

“Why have you come back?”

There was a fierceness in her voice, an attack in the abrupt words she
threw at him.

Tod took his chair and sat down opposite her, his elbows also on the
table.

“Because I have a right to come back.”

“Why?”

“I am free. I am no longer married.”

The eyes opposite him gave a kind of flicker, like a light blown by the
wind. She did not say anything, but sat there, waiting.

Tod felt in his coat pocket, brought out the little brown Testament and
laid it on the table between them.

“You remember?” he asked.

She bowed her head slightly.

“I remember, monsieur.”

“I have carried it—always. I thought I should never come back, that I
should never see you again. I was very unhappy then; I knew things that
I could not tell you. It would not have been fair. Was I wrong?”

“You were never wrong.”

The words were almost inaudible. Her fists had unclenched themselves,
and her hands were curved against her cheeks. She was looking at the
table now, and not at Tod, and her eyes were heavy with something deeper
than fear.

“I thought I should never come back,” he went on. “Then life changed for
me. And yet—I had to wait. The woman I had married had given herself to
another man. It had happened before I met you, but I had only guessed it
then.”

She stretched out a hand and fingered the little brown book.

“You did not—not love her, monsieur?”

“I did not love her.”

“Never?”

“Once, when I was a boy and did not know.”

She bent her head a little lower, to hide the wondering and melting
tenderness of her face.

“It was very hard for you, monsieur.”

One of Tod’s brown hands reached out and covered her hand that was lying
on the brown book.

“Claire, I am free.”

He felt a little trembling in the hand beneath his. And the love in Tod
blazed out and over her. He half rose, and leant across the table.

“Oh, beloved! is it possible that you care a little? All these months I
have been thinking and waiting for this day and for you. It’s just a
year ago.”

“A year ago——”

She hung her head, a woman trembling on the edge of her exultation, the
exultation of surrender.

“I rushed back. I couldn’t help it. You wouldn’t come down.”

“I couldn’t,” she said.

“But you gave me this. Oh, ma chérie, I love you—I love you.”

She raised her head suddenly and looked right into his eyes.

“I went to the school—after you had gone. I do not know what I taught
the children. I did not care.”

“Claire!”

“Yes, it is true. I knew you would never come back to me again, that you
could never come back—unless—and I am a woman, Tod!”

Tod bent over the table and took her face between his hands. There was
something sacramental in the great tenderness with which he touched her.
And thus they remained for a moment, Claire’s hands folded over his
wrists, her face dreaming up to his, her grey eyes the eyes of a woman
who gives herself utterly and without fear.

“God bless you for ever and ever,” said Tod.

And then he drew back a little.

“Claire!”

Her eyes waited.

“Will you marry me? Listen. I am English, you are French; my work is in
England. Would you go with me?”

She smiled up at him.

“I can speak a little English, mon chéri.”

Tod kissed her.

 Chapter Forty-three

There were voices in the orchard, the voices of Josephine and her
parents. Madame Lorellard had the look of a woman who had lost something
and knew that she would never find it again. Old Lorellard was laughing.
He loved young people and the exciting and romantic things that happen
when a man is young. André, slouching along with his hands in his
pockets, regarded the family with eyes of amusement. André had seen the
world.

“Listen!” said Claire. “They are coming!”

And then the Lorellard family descended upon Tod. Claire had drawn aside
and was standing with her back to the window, her hands resting on the
sill—a softly radiant Claire, with shy eyes that looked at everything
but Tod. Tod stood squarely in the middle of the kitchen, showing
something of the air of a man ready to receive a deputation,
half-smiling, half-solemn, but wholly proud.

The Lorellards had no thought of being formal. They were warm-hearted
French folk, and they enveloped Tod with the vivacity of the French who
can be gay and intimate without touching a note that is familiarly
vulgar.

“Ah, Monsieur le Colonel!”

“What a charming compliment you pay us!”

“Welcome back to Myonville!”

Tod kissed madame just as he would have kissed his mother; it seemed the
natural and properly human thing to do. Old Lorellard shook hands with
joyous enthusiasm. Josephine was laughing and talking for all of them.
André had looked at Claire, made a grimace, and then gone to shake hands
a second time with Tod.

“But what a surprise, monsieur!”

“And you have come all the way from England?”

“Oh, la la! These soldiers are wonderful men!”

“Where are you staying, monsieur? At Abbeville? But Monsieur’s old room
is at his service.”

“And Claire—Claire——”

But Claire had slipped away, and no one had noticed it save André, who
was sufficiently uninvolved to notice everything. The Lorellard family
looked wisely and humorously puzzled.

“But she was here a moment ago!”

“You were all talking so hard,” said André, “and Claire seems to think
it is quieter in the orchard.”

The family looked at Tod, and Tod found himself faced with a public
declaration.

“My dear friends,” he said, “I must ask your pardon for being an
Englishman. I’m afraid that I have behaved in a rather English way. I
have asked Claire to marry me.”

The family had accepted the position of an audience, and Tod had to go
on.

“I came back to Myonville because, by the English law, I am to-day a
free man. I should have asked Claire to marry me a year ago if I had
been free then. Monsieur Lorellard, as is only right and fair, I have
brought all the necessary documents and proofs with me.”

Old Lorellard nodded, smiled and grunted something that was
indistinguishable.

“I wish for the attendance to-morrow of Monsieur le Maire, a notary and
an interpreter, so that everything can be examined properly.”

Then old Lorellard interrupted him.

“Monsieur, let the formalities wait. We trust you with all our hearts.
Josephine, a bottle of wine.”

Josephine went to the cellar for the wine while madame collected the
proper number of glasses. The three men were fraternising round the
table, the two Frenchmen smoking Tod’s cigarettes, when Josephine
returned with the wine.

Old Lorellard looked round the room.

“But what has happened to Claire?” he asked.

“I will go and find her,” said his daughter.

Josephine was away quite a long time, and the Lorellards and Tod talked
about the war. The Frenchmen still seemed fascinated by it, and by the
epic of justice of those last hundred days. They could not understand it
when Tod tried to explain to them that in England people did not wish to
speak about the war.

“But, monsieur, how is it possible?”

“It is very strange,” said Tod. “I could go on talking about it for ever
and ever. It has taught me so much. But the people at home are bored. It
is said that no magazine will publish a tale that mentions the war. The
people wish to forget.”

Old Lorellard was frowning.

“In France, monsieur, we cannot forget. Perhaps it is that your people
in England have not suffered sufficiently.”

“It may be that,” said Tod, “but I do not understand it. I feel like a
Frenchman. I think that you are much greater than we are, monsieur.”

“A nation that forgets so easily,” said Père Lorellard, “is easily
fooled.”

Tod’s thoughts had followed Claire into the orchard, and he was
beginning to wonder why the two girls were so long in returning, when he
heard footsteps crossing the yard. Josephine reappeared in the doorway.
She ignored the family and made a sign to Tod. Tod followed Josephine
into the yard.

“Claire needs you, monsieur,” she said. “Be very gentle with her.”

“Is she unhappy?”

“It is the reaction, monsieur, the panic that sometimes attacks a woman
when she has felt too happy. You will find her in the orchard.”

Tod went in search of Claire, so manfully sure of his own happiness and
of hers that he wondered what was troubling her. He found her sitting on
the wooden seat under one of the apple trees, quite calm, so calm that
no sign of suffering or of struggle betrayed themselves to Tod. She
looked up at him with her frank, grey eyes, a little pathetically
perhaps, but with a frankness that refused to let her love cloud his
future with the emotions of the moment.

“I asked Josephine to send you to me. I wish to talk to you, Tod.”

He sat down beside her on the seat.

“What is troubling you, ma chérie?” he asked.

She made a little gesture with her right hand, a gesture that was meant
to repulse, to hold the man in him aloof.

“Will you listen to me, please? A little while ago I let myself be
overcome without thinking. It was like a dream, a dream that one has
dreamt so often, suddenly coming true. Here—in the orchard—I awoke; I
began to ask myself questions. Do you understand all I say?”

“Every word,” Tod answered her.

“Let me be quite honest,” and she looked at him for a moment, and then
at the house beyond the trees. “I am a peasant girl, Tod, a
schoolmistress; I am very ignorant of many things. You—you were a
colonel, what in England you call a gentleman; your friends will be
gentlemen, men of affairs. It may be that I shall not be suited to such
a life—your life; that I could not help you as I should wish to help
you. Do you understand?”

Tod’s face was shadowed with an almost sacred tenderness.

“I understand everything,” he said. “I have thought about everything all
these months. And I will answer you, Claire, as it is right that you
should be answered, not by throwing my love at you, dear, but with the
truth, the truth that can be so convincing.”

She drew a deep breath.

“I am listening.”

He leant forward slightly with his elbows on his knees.

“I will tell you some of the things about my life. I am a child of the
people. My father kept a little shop, a shop where he made photographs.
My mother worked, just as you and Josephine work. I loved her very
dearly, Claire; she was the best woman in the world. I, too, have worked
in a shop; I have sold furniture and drugs, and I have helped to let
houses. I was just a boy behind the counter. Then I had a business of my
own, and it failed. Afterwards—the war came. I began the war as a
common soldier; I ended it as a colonel. Now, in England, I have a big
friend who wishes me to work for him; he employs many men; he wishes me
to help him with these men. I have accepted. He knows I am here, and why
I am here, and he has wished me good luck. To me he is like a father.”

Tod paused for a moment, and glanced at Claire. She was still the calm,
courageous little woman, holding aloof for his sake, but Tod saw that
her eyes had softened.

“So you were not a rich man?” she said.

“I have no thought of being rich,” he went on; “there are so many better
things in life than money. I shall get money, because it is necessary,
and because I do not want the woman I love to be worried. I have
ambitions. I want to be big, as a man, like my big friend. I am quite a
simple soul, Claire; the war has made me simple. I want a comrade, a
little comrade, a little woman whom I can take in my arms, a little
woman with clever hands and a warm heart. I want her and a home, dear; I
have never had a real home since I was a child. I do not want my wife to
be a great lady, a woman of fashion, a gad-about, an intellectual. I
want her to be my wife, just that.”

Claire gave a little shiver, a shiver of inward exultation.

“You would let her work for you, Tod, and perhaps—perhaps you would let
her have a child?”

“I should want a child—her child,” said Tod.

She sat looking at the sky, and then she spoke again.

“But your friends?”

“My friends will be friends who work. Life is so much more real with
people who work, people who are interested. You know far more than most
English girls.”

“How?”

“You went to a good school at Tourcoing, did you not? Then you studied
at Lille. You have read more than I have read; you know things that I do
not know. You can cook, sew, make clothes, manage a house.”

And then he laughed.

“But do you not know how charming you are, how all men would love you,
men who are weary of feverish women, women who rush about and think of
nothing but excitement and clothes? You are so quick, so witty, so full
of understanding; so wise, so——”

She, too, was laughing now, laughing tenderly with the lover in him. She
laid a protesting hand on his sleeve.

“I am not such a marvel, Tod; I am a little girl who has taught
children, and I love you—greatly.”

“Ma chérie,” he said, and drew her head on to his shoulder.

They sat there for a while, not speaking, like two children in a wood,
heads touching, hands clasped, breathing in the beauty and the mystery
of it all, the green leaves, the blue sky, the shadows, the patches of
sunlight, each other. It was simplicity, the simplicity of deep and
happy emotion, two lovers in the spring of the year, a man and a woman
without guile.

“My Tod,” she said presently, “I hope you will never be ashamed of me. I
am clever—in my way—and I shall try so hard.”

He turned and kissed her hair.

“You must not talk like that. Doesn’t God know what he has given me? Ma
chérie, I’m so happy.”

She looked at him with eyes that were all light.

“Do you know why I, too, am so happy?”

“Tell me.”

“Because I can trust you, it’s so much to me, this love.”

“And to me,” he answered.

And then a sudden playfulness seized him.

“Mon Dieu—there is the family, those dear people sitting solemnly round
a bottle of wine, and waiting!”

“For us?”

“Of course.”

“Let us go,” she said, jumping up, but still holding his hand, “let us
go and begin life, mon mari.”

 Chapter Forty-four

On the following day Tod transferred himself from Abbeville, taking a
room at the Hôtel Papillon d’Or, since etiquette and his own sense of
delicacy would have it so. André was in Tod’s old room overlooking the
orchard, and though Josephine had talked of putting André in the attic,
Tod had refused to have her brother evicted. Besides, André was to be
married on the Sunday. He was the most important man in Myonville for
the moment, though André’s romantic pre-eminence was soon usurped by
Tod.

“The English colonel had come back to marry little Claire Vignard.”

The mayor knew the secret and talked about it, and the whole village
imitated the mayor. It was a great sensation, a sort of fairy tale in
real life, a romance that delighted the women and turned Tod into the
Prince. Everybody watched him go in and come out. The children ran after
him in the street. The girls said that he was very handsome. Being
English, he was supposed to be immensely rich. There is no socialism
about a love affair, none of your cabbage philosophy in the eternal
“Once upon a time.”

Tod was very busy. He made old Lorellard put on his Sunday clothes and
go with him to interview Monsieur le Maire. He persuaded the mayor to
produce a lawyer and an interpreter. He arranged that there should be a
formal meeting at the house of the Lorellards while Claire was at the
school, for it was a whim of hers that she should continue to teach the
children until her marriage.

Tod was frank with Monsieur le Maire.

“You see, monsieur, I divorced my wife; the decree of the divorce was
made absolute, as we call it, a few weeks ago. My English lawyers have
given me all the necessary documents, and I want them examined for the
benefit of Monsieur Lorellard.”

The mayor patted Tod on the shoulder.

“Monsieur is a man of honour, a man of good sense.”

“I do not wish to see the documents,” grumbled old Lorellard.

“Monsieur, it is necessary,” said Tod; “I like things done properly,
honestly.”

“Monsieur is quite right,” said the mayor.

Old Lorellard was looking uncomfortable.

“Has Claire told you, Monsieur Tod?”

“What?”

“She has no dot worth speaking of.”

“I knew that long ago,” laughed Tod. “My foster-father in England is
presenting her with a dot.”

“Tiens!” said the mayor, “what generosity, what delicacy! Permit me to
say, Monsieur Western, that I am at your service in all ways and at all
times.”

The mayor seemed to be on the edge of an oration, and Père Lorellard
dragged Tod away.

“He is a good fellow, but a windbag. I have a field to hoe.”

They were walking down the street when the mayor rushed after them.

“Monsieur—Monsieur le Colonel Western!”

Tod turned back to meet him.

“Monsieur, pardon me, but the marriage—it will have to be a civil
marriage. Does monsieur understand?”

“Quite. You will do me the honour of performing it. Is not that so?”

The mayor bowed.

“Mademoiselle Vignard is not a bigot. It is a very great mistake to be
bigoted. Now in England, Monsieur Western——”

He held Tod fast for five minutes, and when Tod was released he found
that Père Lorellard had disappeared. The hoeing of his weeds was of more
importance than the eloquence and the information of Monsieur le Maire.

Jan van Eyck would have made a picture of that “family conference” had
he seen it as Claire Vignard saw it when she returned unexpectedly from
the school. Monsieur le Maire sat at the head of the table, a florid man
with his yellow hair standing up like the mane of a lion, very
attentive, very solemn. Old Lorellard was in his blue breeches and blue
and white striped shirt, and his grey, close-cropped head was the head
of one of Napoleon’s grenadiers. He looked tolerant and amused, but his
eyes brightened whenever he looked at Tod. The _avoué_, pince-nez
half-way down his nose, was busy with papers. The interpreter, who
fidgeted on his chair like a bird on a perch, was always trying to
explain things that did not need explaining, and getting himself snubbed
by the lawyer. André smoked a cigarette and looked bored. Madame
Lorellard sat with her knitting in her lap, clicked her pins and
listened. Tod, with his old leather dispatch-box open on the table in
front of him, seemed always to be insisting on certain documents or
certain figures being explained to Père Lorellard, and Père Lorellard
would scratch his head, smile tolerantly at Tod, and allow himself to be
convinced twice over.

For the moment no one saw Claire. Tod was making a little speech, and no
one interrupted him, but the impressionable and bored André began to
smile. Claire was standing behind Tod’s chair, with a mother-look in her
eyes, quite unembarrassed by all these functionaires.

“So the dot will be fifty thousand francs,” Tod was saying.

Then someone touched him on the shoulder. He turned and saw Claire.

“What is this?” she asked with an air of severity.

André began to laugh. Tod had jumped up, a little flushed. He was trying
to smile at Claire.

She looked at Père Lorellard.

“Have you not told him?” she asked. “There is no dot, no fifty thousand
francs? It is absurd!”

She was angry, a little humiliated. Père Lorellard scratched his head
and appeared embarrassed, but the mayor was ready to make a speech.

“Mademoiselle——” he began.

She ignored him and turned to Tod.

“I have no dot,” she said. “I thought——”

Tod got hold of her hand; she made a little struggle, but Tod would not
let her go.

“Messieurs,” he said, “supposing I leave you to conclude these
formalities. You have all the documents. I wish to speak to my fiancée.”

The mayor bowed in his chair.

“Certainly, monsieur. A few words of explanation.”

Tod drew Claire out of the room.

“Come into the orchard, ma petite.”

He put his arm round her.

“I knew you had no dot, ma chérie. For myself I did not desire it. In
England we do not worry.”

“Oh, my Tod!” she said. “I bring you nothing—a few hundred francs!”

They were in the orchard now where no one could see them, for the high
hedges were thick with leaf. Tod took Claire’s face between his hands
and kissed her.

“Look at me, ma petite, right into my eyes. Have you not given me your
love, and is not that all that I desire? But you overheard a little
secret, a secret that was to have been kept till after our marriage.”

“I do not like secrets,” she said.

“It is no longer a secret. My big friend for whom I work, is like a
father to me. He is very rich. It was his wish that he should give you a
dot. I consented, because it will be such a pleasure to him, and because
he has no children and is lonely.”

“But fifty thousand francs! What shall I do with such a sum?”

“It can be invested. You will have a little income of your own, for your
clothes and all that a woman wants. And you will be able to send little
presents to Josephine and Madame.”

Her face brightened.

“How good you are. And all those papers, and the mayor, what do they
mean?”

Tod smoothed her hair back with his hands and kissed her forehead.

“Claire, I know that these good people trust me, but I wanted to show
them everything, so that they could be sure. Remember, I am taking you
away to England; I want them to have no shadow left behind; it is only
right that I should leave them with no whisper of a doubt. Each year we
will come to France and visit them; we can take our holiday here, and
Josephine and your aunt can come to us.”

“Oh, how good you are,” she said, “my Tod, mon mari!”

He held her very close.

“To-morrow you will not go to school?”

“But why?”

“I have hired an auto for to-morrow. We go to Abbeville or Eu, whichever
you please. Josephine can come with us. We are going shopping.”

“But, Tod!”

“Listen,” he said. “I haven’t spent any money for such a long time, and
I am going to enjoy myself. We will go to the jeweller’s and the
costumier’s, and visit the ladies who sell chapeaux. Yes, I am going to
enjoy myself very much.”

“I have a little dress,” she began, “that will do for the marriage.”

“Of course you have,” he said, “but is that any reason why I should not
enjoy myself? Besides, I want you to choose presents for all my good
friends.”

She looked at him with sudden, mischievous eyes.

“So you realise that I want nothing?”

“Of course I realise it,” he said, “but it is I who want a very great
number of things. And what is more, I am going to get them.”

 Chapter Forty-five

The car was waiting at Monsieur Lorellard’s door; not a very brilliant
car to be sure, but what did that matter? It needed painting; the
upholstering had felt the war, and the chauffeur wore a cloth cap and an
old blue military overcoat. Tod looked at the tyres, and half Myonville
looked at Tod.

“They are going shopping.”

“To Paris?”

“Paris! Listen to Nanette! The child’s head is always in Paris. No;
Abbeville, ma chérie.”

“He is going to buy her a ring, perhaps?”

“Of course.”

“Look, they are going alone! Mademoiselle Josephine is not dressed.”

“Well, it is the English custom, and monsieur is English.”

“I wish I could go to Abbeville and buy pretty clothes,” said Nanette.

Tod was handing Claire into the car. She wore a plain little black hat
and a black coat and skirt, and her shoes were not new, nor were her
gloves very much newer than her shoes. A white lace collar and a quaint
little tartan tie showed at her throat. Yet everything was dainty,
distinctive, sleek, indefinably French and charming.

“How pretty you look!” said Tod.

“These clothes? I bought them in Lille six years ago. Josephine, you
traitress!”

Josephine, in apron and slippers, laughed at them from the doorway.

“No, no, I never meant to come. I should have been so unnecessary.”

André gave a push to the old car as it smoked off.

“Au revoir. Remember me to all the charming girls at Printemps.”

They did not go to Abbeville; they went to Eu; for Claire had a greater
love for that more mysterious, more steeply-streeted, and more shadowy
old town. There were scars of the war too in Abbeville, raw places that
had not healed, memories of death and of terror. And Claire was happy,
happy with the beautiful completeness of a child who rushes out to
gather flowers on a morning in June. The woman in her took the joy of a
child and held it against her heart. It was her day of days, her first
adventure with her lover.

The old car creaked and panted along the upland road, threading the
shadows of the long avenues, and thrusting its unpolished snout into the
sunlight and the broad blueness of that June day. Tod was holding
Claire’s hand. The Frenchman in the front seat kept his eyes on the
road.

“It was just here,” said Tod, pointing to the grass bank under the
beeches.

“Yes, I remember.”

“And Punch came to the rescue. Poor old Punch! I wanted to buy him, but
he got lost at the Base.”

They dreamed awhile.

“Ma chérie,” said Tod suddenly, “what sort of house would you like to
live in?”

She was lost in a new languor, the delicious sense of her new intimacy
with this dearest of men, but she came out of her dreams and smiled at
him.

“A little house, just a little house.”

“You shall have it.”

“And a woman, a sensible woman to help me.”

“Will one be enough?”

“What should I do with more? I want the little house to be my house.”

“You are not always going to be in the house,” said Tod. “I shall want
you to come out with me. I shall want people to see my charming little
wife.”

“So you would like me to wear pretty dresses, sometimes?”

“It will be a weakness of mine, I’m afraid.”

The car sailed quite jauntily down the long hill into the valley below
Eu. Tod was feeling in the breast pocket of his coat; he brought out a
rather bulky pocketcase and looked half shyly and half tenderly at
Claire.

“Now you are going to let me enjoy myself?”

“What do you mean?”

“I am going to buy things, all sorts of things.”

She resisted, because a part of the subtle and exquisite joy of the day
was in resisting, and in the ultimate surrender. A Frenchwoman loves
interplay; it is born and bred in her, and she weaves it into all the
tender activities of her life. Her exclamation, her smiles, her
retaliations are more subtilised and less obvious than those of the
Englishwoman. She has not the mental nudity of the average woman across
the water. “She is always dressed,” says the critical islander, but when
that dress is charming and a little unexpected, why quarrel with it? All
civilisation hangs in the grace of a petticoat and the set of a hat.

Tod bent forward and spoke to the driver.

“We shall stay in Eu most of the day. Take us to the best hotel and put
up the car there.”

“Bien, monsieur.”

“And make yourself comfortable, monsieur; I wish it.”

The Frenchman smiled.

There was no haste in the mood of the morning. They left the car at the
hotel and set out to wander through the streets of Eu. Claire knew all
the shops, but she remained dispassionate, tenderly reticent, leaving
the spirit of the adventure to Tod. He should discover all the wonderful
things that he desired. If necessary she could guide him without
appearing to guide, resist by choosing. But she went with him as no cold
creature, no prig, no careful woman seizing an opportunity. She was just
as excited, just as passionately happy as Tod, but she would keep her
head because this dear man had lost his heart.

“We will go in here,” said Tod, after a glance at the rings in the
_bijoutier’s_ window.

It was a great affair, the buying of that ring, but a most subtle
victory for Claire.

“Look, it is exquisite, this little one. I love it.”

The simple little hoop with its single ruby, like a soft flower with
drops of brilliant dew hanging about its petals, might not have
satisfied Tod. Nor did a difference of two hundred francs satisfy the
_bijoutier_.

“This other is a very beautiful ring, monsieur.”

“It is,” said Tod.

“Look, then!”

She put it on, closed her fist, and made a quick revelation of the
thing’s hardness, its artificiality. Then she pulled it off, and let her
hand lie softly on Tod’s sleeve.

“It suits me,” she said; “it is so simple.”

She was right. There was a spirituality in her choice.

“Your taste is better than mine,” he said.

Her eyes lifted to his.

“But does it not please you better, on the hand of this little woman who
loves you so?”

“You most exquisite soul,” he said.

They ignored the _bijoutier_; nothing mattered that day. But the
_bijoutier_ still had ambitions.

“One moment, mademoiselle; here!”

“I have chosen,” was all she said, with an air of extraordinary yet
gentle finality.

The man said no more. He had studied women.

They wandered out again, Claire glancing at her ring.

“It is so sweet,” she said, “but there is yet another sweetness in it.”

He smiled down at her, with a question in his eyes.

“Well?”

“You let me choose it, you let the woman in me choose.”

“I could not help it,” said Tod.

Twenty yards farther on he wheeled and drew up outside a costumier’s. It
was one of those particularly French shops, small, piquant, restrained,
showing a few exquisite things in the window, a dress, two hats, a
rose-coloured sash, two little grey slippers standing demurely apart.
Claire knew this little shop. It was a fragment of Paris, very
fashionable, charming, but expensive.

“I think this should do,” said the man.

She admired Tod for choosing that shop.

“My dear, it is very expensive.”

“A few good things are better than a lot of cheap ones.”

She had no answer to that.

They entered the shop, and it was Tod who spoke to a swarthy little
Frenchwoman with bright eyes and a merry smile. He stood in the middle
of the square of green carpet, his hat in his hand, his blue eyes
vaguely amused.

“Madame, have you any Paris models?”

“Everything is from Paris, monsieur.”

“Good,” said Tod. “May we see everything? My fiancée wishes to choose.”

The two women looked at each other. Claire smiled.

“A simple costume, madame, that I can wear at my marriage, and
afterwards.”

There was some subtle interchange of sympathy between the two women.
Tod, the man, was put gently on to a chair in a corner and persuaded to
be a spectator. Madame and Claire began to chatter like old friends.

“Yvonne!”

A pretty girl, like a dark pansy, appeared from behind a glass door.
Madame spoke to her with an air of mystery; and the dark girl
disappeared, to return with an armful of Parisian creations.

They were displayed, commented on, discussed as though Claire were a
daughter.

“What about that pink thing?” said Tod from his corner, the blatant and
enthusiastic male.

Madame was very emphatic.

“Oh, no, monsieur—never! Yvonne should not have brought it.”

Tod lapsed and became again the devoted spectator. And yet he was
enjoying himself intensely, watching Claire and the two women fingering
these beautiful things, Yvonne spreading the costumes over her own
figure for Claire to judge them like an impressionist. He beheld a new
Claire, vivacious, critical, yet ever charming, smiling at him from time
to time, quite capable of holding her own with these experts, betraying
an instant and instinctive knowledge of what suited her and what did
not.

“That pleases me.”

She appealed to Tod.

“But it is black,” he objected.

Madame suppressed him with motherly impressiveness.

“Black, monsieur, is the most refined, the most restrained, the most
cunning of colours. A charming woman never looks better than in black.”

“Supposing you try it on?” said Tod meekly.

The two women, the costumes, and Claire disappeared into a room behind
the shop, and Tod waited for the completion of the mystery. Half an hour
passed, and then Yvonne came hurrying out.

“Mademoiselle wishes to know if monsieur is ennuyé?”

“I am quite happy, mademoiselle.”

She smiled at him archly and hurried out into the street. Something
seemed to be lacking, some little refinement, a detail that was
necessary for the completion of a picture. Yvonne returned with an
armful of cardboard boxes, but she did not tell Tod what they contained.

Tod heard much pretty chattering in French, the blending of three
women’s voices, exclamations of delight and of admiration. Tod felt
thrilled, just as excited as the women, for was there not something of a
sacramental mystery about all this feminine ritual, this dressing of his
most dear lady? And then she appeared to him; vaguely shy, smiling in a
way that was new to her, a little, subtle, tender smile, her soft lips
ready to ask a question.

“Dear heart!” said Tod.

He stood up and gazed, and his eyes saw nothing but this little woman in
a black chiffon velvet gown cut low in the front, threaded about the
wrists and neck with twisted threads of cerise and silver, and held at
the waist by a belt of silver. On her head was a close-fitting black
velvet hat, carrying a little halo of silver filigree work, and
underlined with cerise. Grey silk stockings and little black shoes with
silver grey uppers showed below the hem of the black dress.

“Well?” her eyes asked him with a little tremble of light.

“Dear heart,” said Tod again, “it’s—it’s perfect.”

She gave him a strange melting look.

“What beauty there may be in me—is yours,” it said.

Madame clapped her hands softly, and Yvonne smiled.

“Monsieur is convinced! Mademoiselle has the beauty of simplicity.”

“It’s—it’s perfect,” said Tod once more, looking into Claire’s eyes.

And he refused to let her change that costume. It was so simple and so
charming that he felt that she could have met both Myonville and Paris
in those dainty and gentle clothes.

“The other dress can be packed, madame, and we can call for it.”

“Certainly, monsieur.”

“And now,” said Tod, “what about the other things? I’m going out for a
short walk.”

He handed his pocket-book to Claire.

“Tod—I——”

“I won’t be long,” he said, escaping out of the shop.

Claire looked at Tod’s pocket-book and then at Madame.

“I do not require anything more,” she said softly, “but a pair of
gloves, a petticoat or two, and a simple blouse.”

“But if monsieur——?”

Claire smiled with her grey eyes at the other woman.

“It is sufficient—if I am happy.”

Madame smiled back at her.

“I understand. It is an admirable feeling. Yet, mademoiselle, if you
drive in an auto——”

“Yes,” said Claire with a sigh, “I shall need a cloak. A very simple
one, one that I could wear too in the winter.”

“We have a black velour, and quite inexpensive. It will harmonise with
that costume.”

“It must be quite inexpensive,” said Claire with that softly final air
of hers.

Tod returned in half an hour, and wanted to know what she had bought.

“All that I need,” she told him, handing him back his pocket-book. “I
have paid madame.”

Tod did not examine his pocket-book until the evening, and then to see
whether he needed another cheque cashing at Abbeville. He found that he
had more money left than he expected. Claire had refused to seize an
opportunity.

From the costumier’s Tod carried her off to lunch at the hotel, a
happily proud Tod who looked all Eu in the face with eyes that said “See
what an exquisite little woman I have here. And she is going to marry
me! Wonderful, isn’t it?” They had a bottle of Pommard for lunch, Claire
drinking just one glass of it and looking as though she had been born in
that charming frock. She was rather quiet with Tod, perhaps because she
was so happy, and thought of the future without a moment of dismay.

“We have more shopping to do yet,” he told her.

“For whom?”

“For you. And there are the presents for the family.”

She resisted gently.

“Ma chérie, you have bought me so much. For the others—yes, if it
pleases you.”

“Have you a travelling trunk?” asked the practical Tod.

“A little one.”

“You will want something bigger. Let us run through the list. Answer
correctly like a good child.”

She pretended to be extremely solemn.

“Have you an umbrella?”

“Yes.”

“A raincoat?”

“No.”

Tod made a note of it.

“House shoes—pretty ones, I mean?”

She shook her head.

Tod made another note.

“Gloves?”

“Yes.”

“A little handbag for the vanities of women?”

“No.”

“That is essential.”

She held up a hand.

“Please, Tod, no more.”

There was something in her eyes that touched and restrained him.

“Ma petite,” he said, “you have let me enjoy myself. It has been the
happiest day of my life. I thank you, with all my heart.”

The day drew to a close, and the shabby old blue auto improvised a
finale when they were within half a mile of Myonville by blowing a big
burst in one of its back tyres. There was no spare wheel, only a spare
tube and tyre. The chauffeur, who had lunched very well, smiled at Tod
and asked them if they would remove themselves from the back seat as the
tools and the jack were under it.

Tod got out.

“Why shouldn’t we walk? The car can bring on the parcels.”

They decided to walk.

The sunset was behind them, and they came into Myonville with a yellow
sky at their backs and the long shadows of the trees falling across the
village. One of the high avenues of beeches led them to the end of the
village, and from the darkness of this avenue they seemed to step at
once into the evening brightness of the wide and open street. The French
folk were sitting at their doors, and the passing of Claire and Tod was
the passing of a romance.

Myonville behaved like the warm-hearted and simple little place it was.
Claire had nothing to fear from these good people. She went past the
doorways with shining eyes, the little lady of Tod’s life, so frankly
happy that she looked for no evil in the heart of this little world, nor
would she have found it had she looked.

“Bon soir, Mademoiselle Vignard; bon soir, monsieur.”

There were smiles, whisperings, a bobbing out of heads when they were
past, but not a grudging word, not a sneer, no English gawking, no
English giggles. These people were civilised, and Claire Vignard had
taught their children.

“Tiens! but what pretty clothes!”

“The Englishman has been spending his money.”

“Is she not looking charming?”

“Yes, Claire Vignard deserves it. It will not spoil her.”

“Ah, what a romance! Did you notice how he looked at her? They say the
English make good husbands.”

“No man could help being good to her.”

And then a child ran out and into Claire’s arms.

“Oh, mam’selle! Pretty, pretty!”

Claire kissed her.

“Am I pretty, ma petite, or is it the dress?”

“It is you,” said the child.

Tod bent down in turn and kissed Nanette.

“We think the same, Nanette; we are very wise people, you and I.”

The Lorellards were sitting in the doorway of the café, and it was
Josephine who first caught sight of Claire, and Josephine’s good heart
betrayed itself.

“They are coming. The auto must have broken down. Ma foi! she is lovely!
He has turned her into a little Parisian angel!”

Even the bored André leant forward over his sister’s shoulder.

“It is Printemps,” he said; “Monsieur Tod has discovered Printemps.”

Claire was embraced, exclaimed over, kissed, while Tod stood and watched
the pleasure and the playful tenderness of these dear people, a Tod who
could not be disconcerted by André’s mischievous smile. Claire had to
show her costume, her _chapeau_, her little shoes, even her silk
stockings. They were like children, very lovable, grown-up children, and
Tod knew that he was one of them.

“Ah, Monsieur Tod, but you are très, très gentil, très généreux.”

“Am I?” said Tod. “But why? I have never enjoyed myself like this
before.”

He was looking at Claire with eyes of wonder and of tenderness.

“You will spoil her, monsieur,” said André roguishly.

Madame snubbed him.

“Love never spoilt a good woman, my son. Take that to heart.”

Then Claire escaped.

“Tod is hungry,” she asserted.

“I’m not,” said her man.

“But you must be, because I am,” and she laughed back at them from the
doorway. “I shall be back in one moment, and I will help to get some
supper.”

“It is ready,” Josephine called after her: “we waited for you.”

Tod wandered out into the orchard, because the sunset was there and a
blackbird was singing, and all his happiness seemed part of the sunset
and the green leaves and the blackbird’s song. Tod had never thought
that life could be so good. He idled to and fro under the apple trees,
filled to the lips with a precious and innocent tenderness, a simple man
whose very simplicity was his strength.

Presently he heard Claire calling him.

“Tod! Tod!”

He turned and met her where a red rose bush grew by the garden gate, and
Tod stood still. She had changed her new frock and put on her simple
black working skirt, a white blouse and the little apron with the blue
pansies. It was the Claire of all his memories, the Claire of the
schoolhouse, the Claire who had won him in her daintily shabby clothes.

“Dear God!” said Tod, “nothing will ever be better than that. It’s the
woman, not the clothes.”

 Chapter Forty-six

One summer afternoon Mr. Julian Burgetrode met the Channel boat at
Folkestone.

Standing behind a crane, a friend unseen, he watched the crowd on the
boat’s deck and their sheep’s progress towards the gangway. Mr.
Burgetrode had to look elsewhere for Tod and for Tod’s wife, for they
were not among the people who pushed and struggled to extricate handbags
and suit-cases.

“Silly fools,” he said, “and to think we had an army!”

Then he caught sight of Tod, Tod standing a little apart and absorbed in
an affectionate argument with a little woman in black, a charmingly
sturdy little woman who looked up into Tod’s face and maintained a grip
of her handbag.

“I _will_ carry it,” Claire was saying; “I am not so helpless.”

It was Tod who gave in, and like a big, amused, and a devoted boy, but
Mr. Burgetrode was not interested in Tod; his attention was absorbed by
Tod’s French wife. The Colossus had very keen sight, and he watched this
little woman in black while she was wholly unconscious of being
observed. He had an impression of a full round face, pale, but not with
the pallor of Paris, a wreath of soft brown hair, two big grey eyes, a
mouth that was both generous and shrewd, and a very firm little chin.
She was dressed quite simply in that costume that had been bought at
Lille before the war, and the black velour cloak, and the hat that had
come from Eu. She was one of those women who combine animation and
repose, who look both fragile and strong. Mr. Burgetrode had a full view
of her face as she talked to Tod, and it made him think of soft white
light, pure and yet sensitively passionate.

“French,” said Mr. Burgetrode to himself, “but yet not the France of
Paris. A little fair girl with a white throat and a passion for one man
in those grey eyes of hers. A very great passion perhaps. And she
understands.”

The crowd poured off the boat, and Tod was shepherding Claire towards
the gangway, making a sort of sacred space about her. Mr. Burgetrode saw
him glance over his shoulder, and then set his back squarely and
forcibly against the energetic person of a bullet-headed Englishman who
was thinking of nothing but a seat in the luncheon-car. Tod held the man
back till Claire was on the gangway. Mr. Burgetrode liked the squareness
and the determination of Tod’s back.

He went forward hat in hand to meet them.

“Hallo, sir! I hadn’t seen you. It’s very good of you to come and meet
us.”

Mr. Burgetrode was smiling.

“So here you are. And this is my adopted daughter? I am very charmed to
welcome you, madame.”

Mr. Burgetrode spoke excellent French, and for the moment he had
something of the manner of a Frenchman. Standing there bareheaded, he
held out a hand to Claire.

“It is Mr. Burgetrode,” said Tod.

She gave him a little smile, and then looked straight at Julian
Burgetrode. Their hands met. Claire’s eyes and face were as open as the
light to him; he was Tod’s big friend, and hers, a cleverer man than
Tod, she knew that at once; but great with the same greatness, so she
thought. She showed no fear of Julian Burgetrode, no knowledge of the
necessity in an attitude that should please him. It was possible that he
might be jealous of her. She smiled that aside.

“I have wished so much to see you, monsieur.”

Mr. Burgetrode still held her hand. He liked her simplicity, her
unaffectedness, her dignity that was like the dignity of a child. She
had come to a strange land, bravely, without tears, trusting a man. And
he, too, was trusted. The bigness of him offered its homage.

“Well, now you see me, Madame Claire. Is it a surprise for both of us? I
think I have the right to the greater pleasure.”

The woman in her answered the more subtle man in him.

“You will have no cause to be afraid of me, monsieur.”

“My dear child!” said Mr. Burgetrode with unusual emotion; “Tod, you
scoundrel, why didn’t you tell me?”

Tod looked puzzled.

“What, sir?”

A sudden smile passed between Claire and Mr. Burgetrode.

“Of course you told me! I beg your pardon. I didn’t visualise it, that’s
all.”

They followed the porter and the luggage along the pier, Claire walking
between the two men, and looking with eyes of amazement at all those
people who seemed in so savage and helpless a hurry.

“How droll,” she said with a glance at Mr. Burgetrode, “I expected
England to be so calm.”

“Calm!” echoed the great man; “no, we are not calm. We are much too
discontented.”

Tod walked on to speak to the porter.

“My husband is not discontented,” said Claire.

Mr. Burgetrode’s fierce blue eyes softened when he looked at this little
woman.

“He is a very unusual man.”

“He is the best and most generous man in the world,” she said, and then
with a passionately serious light in her grey eyes, “it is my affair
that he shall always be happy.”

Tod came back to them.

“There’s a big crowd in the Customs.”

“No hurry,” said Mr. Burgetrode. “I booked three seats on the
luncheon-car.”

“Will they want to open my box and disarrange all my pretty things?”

“Smile at them, madame,” said Mr. Burgetrode with an air of mischief.
“Tod, you had better leave it to your wife.”

Julian Burgetrode had yet another opportunity of watching these two
children of his while they were dealing with a bored and rather
irritable official. Tod chose to act as spokesman. Claire stood at his
side, looking up at him with eyes of adoration, motherliness, pride.

“I’m asking you if you have anything to declare, sir. It’s no use——”

“I haven’t anything; but my wife——”

“Open the lady’s trunk,” said the man brusquely.

Claire looked at Tod.

“What does he say?”

“He wishes to see your trunk?”

“Quelle horreur!” said Claire.

She took a little bunch of keys out of her vanity-bag and turned to the
official.

“Monsieur,” she began, using for the first time her quaint academic
English, “I am married, one week. I have one Paris costume, three silk
petticoats, my lingerie——”

The man stared at her. She was preparing to unlock her trunk.

“You will not disarrange all my pretty things, monsieur, please?”

Mr. Burgetrode saw that something had happened to the official. His
bored face broadened to a smile. He looked at Tod and chalked the
luggage.

“That’s all right, sir. I won’t bother the lady.”

“You can put your keys back, ma chérie,” said Tod.

Claire thanked the official.

“It is very agreeable of you, monsieur. I have told you the truth; it is
just my little trousseau.”

“Don’t mention it, madam,” said the man.

Mr. Burgetrode swept them away. He was laughing somewhere in the depths
of those fierce eyes of his.

“I was wrong,” he said to himself, “quite wrong. I shall never be
jealous of this child.”

Misspelled words and printer errors have been corrected. Where multiple
spellings occur, majority use has been employed.

Punctuation has been maintained except where obvious printer errors
occur.
